Capitation Mechanics: Decoupling Rural Solvency from RVU Volume

######## community-health-worker-rural-equity-model-2026 | The Community Health Worker as Rural Health’s Most Cost-Effective Equity Intervention: Evidence, Economics, and the Implementation Model That Scales
If there is a single intervention that fits rural health's specific constraints, it is the community health worker (CHW). Rural America's problems — distance, scarce clinicians, deep social need, and a deficit of trust in distant institutions — are precisely the problems CHWs are built to address, and they do it at a cost rural systems can plausibly bear. This analysis makes the rural-specific case for CHWs: why they fit rural geography and culture, the evidence, the financing shift that could sustain them, and the workforce realities of building a rural CHW model.
Why CHWs Fit Rural Health Specifically
The CHW model maps onto rural constraints almost point for point. Distance: a CHW who lives in the community can reach patients a centralized clinic cannot, doing in-home and in-community work that substitutes for visits a patient would otherwise skip. Workforce scarcity: CHWs extend a thin clinical workforce by taking on the outreach, navigation, coaching, and follow-through that do not require a clinician, freeing scarce physicians and nurses for what only they can do. Trust: rural communities, often wary of distant institutions and outside experts, respond to a known neighbor in a way they do not respond to a stranger in a white coat — and that trust is the CHW's irreplaceable asset (Center for Health Care Strategies).
This is why CHWs recur in nearly every rural-health strategy in this analysis layer — they are the people who close the SDOH referral loop where resources are thin, do the registry outreach that prevents admissions, reach the at-risk mother, and bridge patients to the telehealth and specialty care that distance otherwise blocks. In a rural context the CHW is not one program among many; it is the connective human infrastructure that makes the others work.
Rural health's defining problems — distance, scarce clinicians, deep social need, distrust of outside institutions — are the exact problems community health workers are designed to solve. In rural America the CHW is less a program than the human infrastructure other programs depend on.
The Evidence in Rural Context
The general CHW evidence — randomized trials showing prevented hospitalizations and savings on the order of $2,500 per Medicaid enrollee per year, with better chronic-disease control and primary-care engagement — is strong, and its mechanism is, if anything, more powerful in rural settings where the alternative to CHW outreach is often no contact at all. When the nearest clinic is far and clinicians are few, the marginal value of a trusted person doing proactive outreach is higher than in a resource-dense city. The cost-effectiveness case is also sharper: CHWs are far cheaper than the clinicians rural areas cannot recruit anyway, so they substitute for capacity that is not merely expensive but unavailable.
The honest caveat mirrors the broader CHW literature: rigorous rural-specific outcome studies remain limited, and the strongest trials were not all conducted in rural settings, so the rural case rests partly on extrapolation plus mechanism. But the extrapolation is reasonable — the features that make CHWs effective (trust, proximity, follow-through) are exactly the features rural care most lacks from other sources.
The Financing Shift
The historical killer of rural CHW programs was funding: grant-dependent programs that collapsed when the grant ended. The spread of Medicaid reimbursement for CHW services — now available through state plan amendments or other mechanisms in 24+ states and growing — is the development that could finally make rural CHW programs durable (Milbank; NASHP). For rural systems, where Medicaid is often the dominant payer, a Medicaid pathway is especially consequential because it reaches the population CHWs most serve.
But the rural financing story has a specific catch: reimbursement rates set for statewide or urban cost structures may not cover the higher per-encounter cost of rural CHW work, where a single home visit can consume an hour of driving each way. A rate that pencils out in a town may not in the countryside, so rural CHW sustainability depends not just on whether a state reimburses CHWs but on whether the rate accounts for rural travel time and low density. Where it does not, the financing pathway exists on paper but the rural program still cannot break even — the same low-uptake problem seen broadly, intensified by rural geography.
[{"Factor":"Fit","Detail":"Distance, workforce scarcity, social need, and trust all favor CHWs"},{"Factor":"Evidence","Detail":"~$2,500/enrollee/yr savings; mechanism stronger where alternative is no contact"},{"Factor":"Financing","Detail":"Medicaid reimbursement (24+ states) — but rural travel may exceed urban-set rates"},{"Factor":"Pipeline","Detail":"Must grow locally; trust can't be recruited from outside"}]
CHWs and rural health
Building a Rural CHW Workforce
The rural CHW workforce has to be grown locally, which is both its strength and its constraint. The qualifications that matter — being a trusted member of the specific community, sharing its language and culture, knowing its informal networks — cannot be imported; a CHW recruited from a city to a rural county loses the very asset that defines the role. This makes rural CHW development inherently community-based and slower than recruiting clinicians off a national market, but it also means the workforce, once built, is rooted and harder to poach. The implication for policy is that rural CHW programs need patient, place-based development support — local training partnerships, career pathways, and supervision — rather than top-down staffing models.
Integration Without Institutionalization
The deepest design tension in a rural CHW model is integrating CHWs into the health system enough to be funded and effective, without institutionalizing them so far that they lose their community character. CHWs need supervision, training, documentation, and a place in the clinical workflow to bill Medicaid and coordinate with clinicians — but each of those institutional ties pulls them toward becoming junior clinical staff, and away from being trusted community members who happen to work with the health system. A CHW who looks and acts like a clinic employee forfeits the outsider-insider position that lets them reach people the clinic cannot.
Rural programs that get this right keep the CHW's feet in the community — hiring from within it, basing them in community settings rather than only the clinic, and protecting time for the informal relationship-building that is not billable but is the source of effectiveness. They treat the institutional requirements as scaffolding for a community-rooted role, not as a remodeling of it into a clinical one. This is the same tension the certification debate raises, but it is sharper in rural settings, where the supply of trusted community members is small and the cost of alienating them by over-clinicalizing the role is correspondingly high.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether states set CHW reimbursement rates that account for rural travel time and low density, or whether urban-calibrated rates leave rural programs unable to break even; whether rural systems deploy CHWs where the evidence is strongest (chronic-disease management, SDOH loop-closing) rather than diffusely; whether local training and career pathways develop to build and retain a rooted rural CHW workforce; and whether rural-specific outcome evidence accumulates. The opportunity is unusually well-matched to rural needs; realizing it depends on rate adequacy and patient, place-based workforce building.
Bottom Line
Community health workers may be the single best-matched intervention for rural health, because rural America's core problems — distance, scarce clinicians, deep social need, and distrust of distant institutions — are exactly what CHWs address, at a cost rural systems can bear and with evidence of both better outcomes and savings. The spread of Medicaid reimbursement could finally make rural CHW programs sustainable, but only if rates account for rural travel and density, and only if the workforce is grown from within the communities it serves. Get the financing and the local pipeline right, and CHWs become the durable human infrastructure that makes rural population health, SDOH, and access strategies actually work; get them wrong, and a near-ideal rural intervention stalls for want of a workable rate and a local workforce. Of all the rural-health interventions examined across this analysis layer, the CHW is the one whose fit to the problem is closest and whose cost is most bearable — which makes the unglamorous work of rate-setting and local workforce development unusually high-leverage. Few rural-health investments offer this combination of strong fit, proven savings, and low cost; the barrier is not evidence or even money so much as the patience to build it right and the willingness to set a rate that reflects rural reality. For policymakers weighing where to put scarce rural-health dollars, that combination should put CHW financing near the top of the list.

This analysis makes the rural-specific case.
The Community Health Worker as Rural Health’s Most Cost-Effective Equity Intervention: Evidence, Economics, and the Implementation Model That Scales

######## difficult-decisions-vermont-hospital-leaders | Difficult Decisions Await Vermont's Hospital Leaders
The era of preserving every service at every location is, in Vermont, mathematically over. With multiple hospitals operating in the red and commercial premiums approaching the ceiling of what employers and individuals will pay, the state's health system faces a forced restructuring. The question has shifted from whether services will be consolidated to how those decisions get made — and whether they are made deliberately, with the right analytics and governance, or chaotically, through closures forced by insolvency.
The Financial Reality Driving the Decisions
The pressure is concrete and documented. In the most recent reporting cycle, six Vermont hospitals lost money on operations, according to the Green Mountain Care Board. The losses are not trivial: Gifford Medical Center in Randolph lost over $10 million on patient care, and its operating margin deteriorated from roughly negative 8.3 percent in 2023 to negative 18.2 percent in 2024; Northwestern Medical Center and Brattleboro Memorial also booked multi-million-dollar operating losses. Much of the deterioration traces to higher-than-expected expenses — contract and traveling-nurse labor, and rising drug prices — colliding with revenue that regulators are deliberately holding down to protect premium-payers.
This is the vise. Hospitals cannot raise commercial prices indefinitely, because premiums have already risen to the edge of affordability and regulators are capping budgets to keep them there; yet their costs keep climbing. When a hospital cannot raise price and cannot easily cut cost, the remaining lever is the scope of what it does — which services it offers, at which sites, at what volume. That is why service-line decisions, long avoided, have become unavoidable, and why the quality of the decision-making process now matters enormously.
Why the Simple P&L Is a Dangerous Tool
The intuitive way to evaluate a service line is its own profit-and-loss statement: if obstetrics or inpatient pediatrics loses money, cut it. In the current crisis this blunt instrument is dangerous, because a service's standalone P&L ignores the downstream revenue and clinical value it generates. A money-losing service line often feeds profitable ones — a maternity unit drives imaging, lab, surgical, and primary-care volume; an emergency department is the front door for admissions. Cutting the visible loss can destroy more contribution margin than it saves, leaving the hospital worse off.
The disciplined alternative is contribution-margin analysis that accounts for downstream effects: what revenue and volume leave the system entirely if a service is cut, not just what the service bills directly. A service line that loses money on its own ledger but anchors a web of profitable downstream activity may be worth preserving on pure financial grounds, while one that genuinely consumes resources without feeding the rest of the system is a cleaner candidate for consolidation. Making these calls on standalone P&L alone is how a hospital cuts its way into a deeper hole.
The financial reality forcing the choices: six Vermont hospitals lost money on operations in the latest GMCB reporting; Gifford's operating margin fell from ~−8.3% (2023) to −18.2% (2024), losing over $10M on patient care, driven largely by contract-labor and drug costs against regulator-capped revenue. With price increases foreclosed and costs rising, the scope of services becomes the only remaining lever — making the quality of these decisions decisive.
Access Is Not Just a Financial Variable
Service decisions in a rural state cannot be made on contribution margin alone, because access has a value the ledger does not capture. Closing a rural maternity unit may improve a hospital's finances while forcing laboring patients into hour-long drives with documented increases in adverse outcomes. The right framework weighs the financial contribution of a service against the access consequences of losing it — recognizing that some money-losing services are essential because no alternative exists within a safe distance, and that the cost of losing them is borne by patients, not balance sheets.
This is why Vermont has moved to put a public check on these decisions. As of late 2025, the Green Mountain Care Board has enhanced authority to review — and potentially block — hospital service cuts, a power it began testing in its scrutiny of Rutland Regional Medical Center's decision to close its inpatient pediatric unit. The legislation arose precisely because earlier rounds of regulator-ordered budget reductions had prompted hospitals to announce patient-facing cuts. The message is that service-line decisions are no longer purely internal financial choices; they are public decisions about access that the state will now weigh in on.
The Workforce Constraint Behind the Numbers
A large share of Vermont's hospital losses traces to a single cost category: contract and traveling-nurse labor. When a hospital cannot recruit and retain permanent staff, it backfills with agency clinicians who cost a multiple of staff wages, and those premiums flow straight to the bottom line. The deterioration is therefore partly a workforce problem wearing a financial mask — and it is compounded by Vermont's demographics, where roughly one in three primary-care physicians is over 60 and half the state's nurses are over 48, so the permanent workforce is contracting through retirement even as demand from an aging population rises.
This matters for how leaders frame the difficult decisions, because some of the loss is addressable without cutting services at all. Investments in retention — competitive compensation, working conditions, housing support, and training pipelines — can reduce the contract-labor premium that drives much of the red ink, easing the financial pressure that forces service cuts. The sequencing insight is that workforce stabilization and service restructuring are complementary, not alternative: a hospital that attacks its contract-labor cost may preserve services it would otherwise have to consolidate, while one that ignores the workforce problem will keep bleeding regardless of how many services it cuts. Honest decision-making separates the losses that reflect a genuine capacity mismatch from those that reflect a fixable staffing failure.
How Good Decisions Get Made
A defensible service-restructuring process has several elements that distinguish it from panic cuts. It uses contribution-margin and downstream-revenue analysis rather than standalone P&L, so the financial picture is accurate. It explicitly quantifies access impact — drive times, available alternatives, affected populations — so the human cost is on the table alongside the dollars. It engages the community and regulators early rather than announcing faits accomplis, both because the Green Mountain Care Board now requires it and because community trust is itself an asset. And it considers regionalization — concentrating a service at one site that serves several communities — as an alternative to outright elimination.
Regionalization deserves emphasis because it is often the least-bad option. For services where volume is too low to sustain safe, high-quality care at multiple sites — and where low volume itself is a patient-safety risk — concentrating the service at one well-resourced regional site can preserve access within the state while fixing the economics. It is not painless; it lengthens travel for some patients and is politically fraught. But it is frequently superior to the alternatives of preserving unsafe low-volume services everywhere or eliminating a service entirely, and a serious restructuring process treats it as a primary tool rather than an afterthought.
The Cost of Avoiding the Decisions
The temptation, always, is to defer — to preserve every service for one more budget cycle and hope conditions improve. In Vermont's situation that is the most dangerous choice of all, because deferral does not prevent restructuring; it merely converts deliberate restructuring into involuntary collapse. A hospital that preserves every loss-making service until it becomes insolvent does not save those services; it loses all of them at once, along with the profitable ones, when it closes. Orderly consolidation, however painful, preserves more access than disorderly failure.
This is the hard truth Vermont's hospital leaders face: the choice is not between cutting services and keeping them, but between cutting some services deliberately and losing many services chaotically. The leaders who confront this directly — with rigorous contribution-margin analysis, honest access accounting, early community and regulator engagement, and regionalization where it fits — will preserve the most care for the most Vermonters. Those who defer in the hope of avoiding hard choices will find the choices made for them, on worse terms, by insolvency. Difficult decisions, made well and soon, are the most pro-access path available.

With price and cost levers foreclosed, service scope is the only remaining lever. Decisions must use contribution-margin/downstream-revenue analysis (not standalone P&L), explicitly weigh access (drive times, alternatives), engage the GMCB (which can now block cuts, as in the Rutland pediatric case), and favor regionalization. Difficult Decisions Await Vermont's Hospital Leaders

######## economics-of-prevention-2025 | The Economics of Prevention: A 2025 Perspective
Prevention is health care's most appealing idea and its most frustrating one: stopping disease before it starts should be both better for patients and cheaper for the system, yet prevention persistently struggles to attract investment proportional to its promise. This analysis examines the economic case for prevention, the evidence on its return, why the appealing 'prevention saves money' slogan oversimplifies, the structural barriers that under-fund it, and what would let the system invest in prevention rationally.
The Case for Prevention
The economic logic of prevention starts with where the money goes: in the United States, chronic disease accounts for more than 85% of total health expenditure (CDC). Since chronic conditions — diabetes, heart disease, hypertension — are substantially preventable or manageable, the potential savings from preventing or controlling them are enormous, and prevention also generates broader economic value through improved workforce productivity and reduced disability. The intuitive case is powerful: catch and prevent the chronic diseases that drive 85% of spending, and the savings should be vast. This is why prevention is invoked in nearly every cost-control discussion as the obvious lever.
The evidence does support real returns when prevention is well-targeted and well-timed. Modeling of chronic-disease management finds ROI exceeding 20% when interventions sustain patient adherence and slow disease progression, with the crucial caveat that timing and delivery efficiency matter as much as the magnitude of behavior change — early, adaptive interventions that maintain adherence yield the highest returns (PMC; ASPE). Prevention works economically; the question is the conditions under which it does.
Chronic disease drives over 85% of U.S. health spending, and prevention can deliver ROI above 20% when well-targeted and sustained. But the appealing slogan 'prevention saves money' oversimplifies — some prevention saves, some costs, and the returns depend heavily on timing, targeting, and who captures them.
Why 'Prevention Saves Money' Oversimplifies
The slogan that prevention always saves money is, despite its appeal, an oversimplification that economists have repeatedly flagged. Some preventive interventions do save money (they cost less than the disease they prevent), but many cost money even when they are highly worthwhile — they improve health and extend life at a reasonable cost per quality-adjusted life year, but they do not pay for themselves in pure dollar terms. Screening a large population to catch a relatively rare disease, for instance, costs a great deal across everyone screened to benefit the few who have it, so the aggregate may not save money even though the screening is valuable. The honest framing is that prevention should be judged on cost-effectiveness (is the health benefit worth the cost?), not on whether it saves money (does it pay for itself?) — and many valuable preventive services are cost-effective without being cost-saving.
This distinction matters enormously for how prevention is justified. Advocates who oversell prevention as a money-saver set it up to disappoint when the savings do not materialize as promised, which can undermine support for genuinely valuable prevention. The defensible case is not that prevention always saves money but that targeted, well-timed prevention can save money, and that even when it does not, much of it delivers health benefits worth the cost. Treating prevention as a cost-effectiveness question rather than a savings guarantee is both more honest and more durable.
The Structural Under-Investment
Even where prevention clearly pays, the system systematically under-invests in it, and the reasons are structural. The first is the wrong-pocket problem: prevention's savings accrue over years and often to a different payer than the one paying for the prevention now — an employer pays to prevent a disease whose costs would fall on Medicare decades later, or an insurer pays for prevention whose savings appear after the member has switched plans. The payer making the investment cannot capture the return, so it rationally under-invests. The second is fee-for-service incentives: a system that pays for treating disease, not preventing it, financially rewards the sickness prevention would avert — under fee-for-service, prevention that reduces billable disease is revenue-negative for providers. The third is the time horizon: prevention's returns are slow and uncertain while its costs are immediate and certain, which disadvantages it in budgeting that favors near-term results.
These barriers explain the paradox of prevention being universally praised and chronically under-funded. The problem is not that prevention does not work but that the system's payment structure does not reward the entity that pays for it — exactly the wrong-pocket and fee-for-service failures that recur across health economics. Prevention is under-funded not because its value is doubted but because the system is structured so that the payer who invests in it rarely captures the return.
The Public-Health Dimension
The most cost-effective prevention often happens furthest from the clinic, which complicates the economics further. Population-level public-health interventions — tobacco taxes and control, clean air and water, vaccination programs, food and nutrition policy, injury prevention — are frequently the highest-return prevention investments available, yet they sit almost entirely outside the health-care payment system that this analysis has focused on. A tobacco tax that prevents thousands of cancers and heart attacks delivers enormous health and economic returns, but no insurer or hospital pays for it or captures its savings; it is funded, if at all, through public budgets that compete with everything else government does. The highest-value prevention is thus often the least connected to the entities that benefit financially from health spending.
This widens the wrong-pocket problem into a wrong-sector problem: the prevention with the best returns is public-health and policy work whose benefits accrue diffusely across society and over decades, captured by no single payer and funded by chronically under-resourced public-health budgets. The clinical prevention that payment reform could fund (screening, chronic-disease management) is real and valuable, but the largest prevention returns lie in public-health investments that the health-care financing debate often ignores entirely. A complete economics of prevention has to recognize that the most cost-effective prevention may require public investment outside the medical system, not just payment reform within it — and that under-funding public health is among the costliest false economies in the entire health enterprise.
What Would Let the System Invest Rationally
Fixing prevention's under-investment requires aligning who pays with who benefits. Value-based and capitated payment models are the most direct fix: when an organization bears the total cost of care for a population over time (an ACO, a capitated plan, an integrated system), it captures the savings from prevention and therefore invests in it rationally — which is why the strongest prevention programs tend to exist inside risk-bearing organizations. Longer-term member relationships and policies that let payers capture cross-payer savings would also help, as would direct public investment in prevention whose societal returns exceed any single payer's. The common thread is that prevention will be funded proportional to its value only when the entity paying for it can capture the return — which means prevention's future is tied to the spread of payment models that internalize long-term, population-level savings.
[{"Fact":"Chronic disease share","Detail":">85% of U.S. health expenditure (CDC)"},{"Fact":"ROI when well-done","Detail":">20% with sustained adherence; timing and targeting decisive"},{"Caveat":"Cost-effective != cost-saving","Detail":"Much valuable prevention costs money but is worth it"},{"Barrier":"Under-investment","Detail":"Wrong-pocket + fee-for-service + slow returns"}]
The economics of prevention
What to Watch
Key signals: whether value-based and capitated payment models spread enough to let payers capture prevention's returns and invest rationally; whether prevention is framed honestly as cost-effectiveness rather than oversold as guaranteed savings; whether policies emerge to address the wrong-pocket problem (cross-payer savings capture, longer member relationships); and whether the chronic-disease management interventions with proven ROI (early, adaptive, adherence-sustaining) are deployed at scale. Prevention's chronic under-funding is a structural payment problem, so its future tracks the spread of the payment models that would solve it.
Bottom Line
Prevention is health care's most appealing idea and its most under-funded, and the gap between its promise and its investment is structural, not evidentiary. Chronic disease drives over 85% of spending, well-targeted prevention can deliver strong returns (ROI above 20% when sustained), and even prevention that does not save money is often cost-effective — but the system under-invests because of the wrong-pocket problem (savings accrue to a different payer later), fee-for-service incentives (that reward treating the disease prevention would avert), and prevention's slow, uncertain returns against immediate, certain costs. The honest framing is to judge prevention on cost-effectiveness rather than overselling it as guaranteed savings, and the structural fix is to align who pays with who benefits — through value-based and capitated models that let the investing entity capture the long-term, population-level return. Prevention does not lack evidence or value; it lacks a payment structure that rewards the payer who funds it, and its future depends on building one.

PMC — What to Expect in 2024: Health Economics and Outcomes Research Trends

Prevention is health care's most appealing and most under-funded idea. Chronic disease drives 85%+ of spending and well-targeted prevention delivers ROI above 20% — but 'prevention saves money' oversimplifies (much valuable prevention is cost-effective without being cost-saving), and the system under-invests due to the wrong-pocket problem and fee-for-service incentives. This analysis covers the evidence, why the slogan misleads, and the payment fix.
The Economics of Prevention: A 2025 Perspective

######## end-of-all-payer-dream-act-167 | The End of the All-Payer Dream: Transitioning to Global Budgets Under Act 167
Vermont spent a decade pursuing one of American health care's most ambitious experiments: the all-payer Accountable Care Organization model, in which every payer would align behind a single value-based framework. That dream has largely given way to a different one — global hospital budgets under Act 167. This analysis examines what the all-payer ACO model tried to do, why it fell short of its promise, and what Vermont's pivot toward global budgets reveals about the limits of the ACO approach and the next phase of state payment reform.
The All-Payer ACO Dream
Vermont's All-Payer ACO Model, launched under an agreement with the federal government, was a genuinely bold idea: align Medicare, Medicaid, and commercial insurers behind a single accountable-care framework so that providers faced consistent value-based incentives across all their patients, not the usual patchwork where each payer pulls in a different direction. The theory was powerful — if every payer rewarded the same things (managing population health, controlling total cost), providers could finally reorganize around value rather than straddling conflicting payment models. Vermont was widely watched as the test of whether all-payer alignment could work at state scale, a step toward the holy grail of coherent, system-wide payment reform.
The model ran through a state ACO and aimed to bring a large share of Vermonters into accountable-care arrangements with shared financial accountability for cost and quality. For years it was held up as the leading example of multi-payer value-based reform in the United States — the place where the fragmented incentives that doom most payment reform might finally be unified.
Vermont's all-payer ACO model chased health care's holy grail: getting every payer to reward the same things, so providers could organize around value instead of straddling conflicting incentives. Its quiet retreat toward global budgets is a lesson in how hard that alignment really is.
Why the Dream Fell Short
The all-payer ACO model did not fail catastrophically so much as fall short of its transformative promise, for reasons that illuminate the limits of the ACO approach generally. Aligning all payers proved harder than hoped: commercial insurers and the federal government have different goals, time horizons, and constraints, and sustaining true multi-payer alignment against those frictions is enormously difficult. The ACO mechanism itself — shared savings against a benchmark — has well-documented limits: benchmarks are hard to set fairly, savings can come from favorable patient selection rather than better care, and the model asks providers to take financial risk that many, especially small rural ones, are ill-equipped to bear. And the underlying problem Vermont most needed to solve — the structural unsustainability documented later by the Oliver Wyman report — was a hospital-finance and demographic problem that an ACO overlay did not fix.
In short, the ACO model is an incentive layer placed on top of the existing fee-for-service plumbing; it nudges behavior but does not change the fundamental economics of a hospital whose revenue still depends on volume in a shrinking population. That structural mismatch is why Vermont concluded it needed something more direct.
The Pivot to Global Budgets
Act 167 represents the pivot: rather than continue layering accountable-care incentives on top of fee-for-service, Vermont moved toward replacing the payment base itself with global hospital budgets — fixed annual revenue that directly addresses the volume-dependence problem the ACO model could only nudge. This is a meaningful philosophical shift. The all-payer ACO tried to change incentives while leaving the fee-for-service chassis in place; global budgets change the chassis. For a state whose core problem is hospitals that cannot survive on declining volume, the more direct intervention — guaranteeing revenue regardless of volume — addresses the disease rather than the symptom.
The continuity matters too: the all-payer alignment work was not wasted, because global budgets also require all payers to participate, so Vermont's years of building multi-payer infrastructure feed directly into the new model. The pivot is less an abandonment than an evolution — from aligning payers behind ACO incentives to aligning them behind fixed budgets, a recognition that the alignment was necessary but the ACO mechanism was insufficient.
[{"Phase":"All-payer ACO model","Mechanism":"Align all payers behind shared-savings accountable care"},{"Phase":"The limit","Mechanism":"Incentive layer atop fee-for-service; didn't fix volume-dependence"},{"Phase":"Act 167 pivot","Mechanism":"Replace the base with all-payer global hospital budgets"}]
Vermont's payment-reform evolution
The Federal Dependency
One structural feature shadows both the all-payer ACO and the global-budget pivot: Vermont's reforms depend on the federal government's participation, because Medicare is too large a payer to align with state policy on its own. The all-payer model ran under a special agreement with the federal government that let Medicare participate in Vermont's framework, and global budgets likewise require Medicare to pay Vermont hospitals on the state's terms rather than standard national rates. This makes Vermont's experiment perpetually contingent on federal willingness — a change in administration, in CMS priorities, or in the terms of the agreement can reshape or end what the state has built.
This dependency is both Vermont's enabling condition and its vulnerability. It is enabling because federal participation is what makes true all-payer reform possible at all — without Medicare, any state model covers only part of the market and loses much of its power. It is a vulnerability because the state does not control the largest payer at the table, so even a well-designed Vermont reform can be undone by federal decisions made for national reasons. Any state contemplating Vermont's path has to reckon with the same reality: ambitious payment reform at state scale runs through Washington, and that is a dependency, not a detail.
What It Reveals About Payment Reform
Vermont's arc carries a national lesson. The accountable-care model — the dominant value-based-care approach in the U.S. for a decade — is powerful but limited precisely because it is an overlay: it adjusts incentives without changing the fee-for-service foundation, so it can improve behavior at the margin but cannot rescue a hospital whose underlying economics are broken. For systems facing structural rather than behavioral problems — declining volume, fixed costs, demographic decline — the ACO nudge is not enough, and more direct interventions like global budgets become necessary. Vermont's pivot is an early, instructive case of a state concluding that it had to move past the ACO paradigm toward something more fundamental.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether global budgets succeed where the all-payer ACO fell short, validating the pivot; whether other states drawing on Vermont's experience skip straight to global budgets or attempt the ACO path first; whether the multi-payer alignment Vermont built proves transferable to the global-budget model; and whether the federal government's posture (which underwrote the all-payer agreement) supports the budget evolution. Vermont's transition is a real-time test of whether the ACO era is giving way to a global-budget era for states with structurally challenged hospital systems.
Bottom Line
The end of Vermont's all-payer ACO dream is not a failure story but an evolution one: the state learned that aligning payers behind accountable-care incentives, while necessary, was not sufficient to fix a hospital system whose problem was structural rather than behavioral. Act 167's pivot to global budgets is the logical next step — changing the payment base itself rather than overlaying incentives on fee-for-service. The national lesson is that the accountable-care model, for all its value, is an overlay with real limits, and states facing structural hospital crises may need to move past it toward more direct interventions. Vermont, having pioneered the all-payer ACO and now pioneering the pivot to global budgets, remains the laboratory where the future of state payment reform is being worked out — and its conclusion that the ACO was not enough is a finding the rest of the country should heed. There is a humility in Vermont's arc worth absorbing: a decade of sincere, sophisticated effort on the most-watched value-based experiment in the country ended not in triumph but in a pragmatic pivot to something more direct. That is not a story of failure but of learning — the kind of honest course-correction that health reform rarely permits itself. The states that learn fastest from Vermont will be the ones willing to admit, as Vermont did, that an elegant incentive model is no substitute for fixing the underlying economics, and that sometimes the more radical intervention is also the more realistic one. For a country whose rural hospitals are sliding toward the same cliff Vermont reached first, that lesson is not academic — it is a preview.

The End of the All-Payer Dream: Transitioning to Global Budgets Under Act 167

######## equity-broadband-digital-determinants | The Broadband Gap: Solving the Digital Determinants of Health (DDoH)
Healthcare has quietly become a bandwidth issue. As telehealth, patient portals, remote monitoring, and online health resources move to the center of care delivery, the patients without reliable broadband are increasingly invisible to the digital health system — unable to attend a video visit, manage a chronic condition remotely, or participate in the portal-mediated workflows that value-based care depends on. Broadband has become, in effect, a social determinant of health: a 'digital determinant' whose absence produces measurable disadvantage.
The Scale of the Gap
The digital divide is large and sharply rural. As of 2022, roughly 28 percent of rural residents — and about 24 percent of Americans in tribal areas — lacked access to fixed broadband meeting the FCC's 100/20 Mbps benchmark, compared with a far smaller share of urban residents. More specifically for care delivery, the FCC has estimated that nearly 14.5 million Americans lack broadband with upload and download speeds adequate to support telehealth or other virtual-care modalities. These are not abstract connectivity statistics; they are a direct measure of who can and cannot participate in modern digital healthcare.
The consequence is visible in utilization data: rural counties have shown lower telehealth uptake than urban counties, a divide that maps onto the broadband gap. This matters because telehealth was supposed to be the great equalizer for rural access — the technology that dissolves distance to specialists. When the populations with the worst geographic access to in-person care also have the worst broadband, the tool meant to close the access gap instead risks widening it, delivering its benefits disproportionately to the already-connected. The broadband gap thus threatens to generate a new layer of health disparities on top of the geographic ones telehealth was meant to solve.
Broadband is a digital determinant of health: as of 2022, ~28% of rural residents (and ~24% in tribal areas) lacked broadband meeting the FCC's 100/20 Mbps benchmark, and ~14.5 million Americans lack speeds adequate for telehealth. Because rural counties already have the worst in-person access AND the worst connectivity, the broadband gap risks turning telehealth — meant to be the great equalizer — into a new source of disparity.
The Compounding With Other Disadvantage
The broadband gap rarely falls on otherwise-advantaged populations; it compounds disadvantage that already exists. The communities with the worst connectivity tend also to have older residents, lower incomes, higher chronic-disease burden, fewer local clinicians, and longer distances to care — so digital exclusion stacks on top of every other barrier rather than standing alone. A patient who is rural, older, low-income, and chronically ill is precisely the patient for whom telehealth could be most transformative and precisely the patient most likely to lack the broadband, device, and literacy to use it. The digital divide thus tends to widen gaps that are already wide.
This compounding is why broadband belongs in the social-determinants conversation rather than being treated as a standalone infrastructure issue. Just as housing instability and food insecurity cluster with and worsen poor health, digital exclusion clusters with other disadvantages and amplifies them. Addressing it in isolation — running fiber without regard to the affordability, device, and literacy barriers that accompany rural poverty — will leave the most disadvantaged still excluded. The equity payoff comes from recognizing that the broadband gap is one strand in a braid of disadvantage, and that closing it meaningfully requires addressing the strands together, in the same communities, as part of an integrated approach to the social and digital determinants of health.
Why 'Access' Is More Than a Wire
Solving the digital divide is not only about laying fiber. The research literature emphasizes that meaningful digital access has several dimensions beyond mere availability: affordability (a connection that exists but costs too much is no access), adequacy (speeds sufficient for video and data), acceptability (devices and interfaces patients can actually use), and the digital literacy to navigate them. A rural household may be 'covered' by a broadband map yet remain effectively offline because the service is unaffordable, too slow for video, or unusable on the only device available. Closing the gap requires attention to all of these, not just to coverage.
This broadens the equity task considerably. Treating broadband as a public utility essential to healthcare — the framing this analysis advances — means ensuring not just that infrastructure reaches a community but that the service is affordable, fast enough, and usable by the older, lower-income, and less digitally-literate patients who most need telehealth. A portal a patient cannot navigate or a video visit that buffers into uselessness is not access. The digital-determinants framing succeeds only if it addresses the full stack from fiber to literacy, because a failure at any layer leaves the patient just as invisible to the system.
The Policy Response and Its Limits
Substantial public investment is flowing toward the problem. The FCC and other federal programs have committed billions to rural broadband — including large awards aimed at extending service to millions of unconnected rural Americans and dedicated funds to support telehealth connectivity for rural healthcare providers. These are real and necessary steps. But infrastructure funding alone addresses only the availability layer; without parallel attention to affordability, devices, and literacy, the investment can build networks that the intended beneficiaries still cannot use for care.
The honest assessment is that broadband funding is a step forward but, on its own, not enough. The digital-determinants problem is solved only when a rural patient can reliably and affordably connect to a usable video visit and navigate the portal that manages their care — which requires coordinated investment across infrastructure, affordability programs, device access, and digital-literacy support. For health systems, this means treating connectivity as part of the care plan: screening for digital access the way one screens for transportation or housing, and partnering on the affordability and literacy supports that turn a broadband connection into actual access to care.
The Portal-as-Voice Problem
A specific and underappreciated dimension of digital exclusion is the patient portal. As health systems route appointment scheduling, test results, prescription refills, secure messaging, and increasingly the management of value-based care programs through online portals, the patient without portal access loses far more than convenience — they lose a channel of participation in their own care. A patient who cannot see their results, message their care team, or manage their conditions online is, in a portal-mediated system, effectively a patient without a voice. The portal has quietly become a precondition for full participation, and those without it are pushed to the margins of the system.
This matters acutely for the populations the digital divide hits hardest: older, rural, lower-income patients who are also, often, those with the most complex chronic conditions and the greatest need for engaged management. The result is a perverse alignment — the patients who would benefit most from portal-mediated, proactive care management are the ones most likely to be excluded from it. A health system serious about equity therefore cannot treat the portal as a take-it-or-leave-it convenience; it must provide alternative channels for the digitally excluded and actively work to bring them online, because allowing the portal to become a gate that locks out the most vulnerable patients converts a tool meant to improve care into a mechanism that deepens disparity.
Connectivity as a Care-Delivery Strategy
For a value-based health system, closing the digital divide is not charity; it is operational necessity. If a provider is financially responsible for a population's outcomes, the patients it cannot reach digitally — who miss virtual check-ins, cannot use remote monitoring, and fall out of portal-mediated follow-up — are precisely the patients most likely to deteriorate into expensive acute episodes. Digital exclusion converts directly into avoidable cost. Investing in patients' connectivity and digital capability is therefore continuous with managing population health, not separate from it.
The strategic conclusion is that digital determinants belong in the same category as the social determinants a serious system already addresses. A patient without broadband, like a patient without transportation or stable housing, faces a structural barrier to care that the system must help remove if it is to manage that patient's health. Treating broadband as essential healthcare infrastructure — and acting on the full availability-affordability-adequacy-acceptability stack — is how a health system keeps its most vulnerable patients visible, connected, and cared for in an increasingly digital model. Geography and connectivity should not determine the quality of a citizen's health, and closing the digital divide is how that principle is made real.
Sources
Broadband-gap and telehealth figures verified against FCC and peer-reviewed sources:

NIH/PMC — Redefining and solving the digital divide (availability, affordability, adequacy, acceptability)
State Health & Value Strategies — Federal funding opportunities to support universal broadband

Real access is more than a wire — it requires availability, affordability, adequacy, acceptability, and digital literacy. The Broadband Gap: Solving the Digital Determinants of Health (DDoH)

######## fiscal-impact-locum-tenens-rural-vermont | The Fiscal Impact of Locum Tenens Reliance in Rural Vermont
When a rural hospital cannot fill a permanent clinical position, it turns to contract labor — locum tenens physicians and travel nurses who fill gaps at a steep premium. For rural Vermont and rural hospitals everywhere, this reliance has become both a lifeline and a financial wound: it keeps services running but at a cost that erodes already-fragile margins and introduces care-continuity problems. This analysis examines the locum-tenens trap, its fiscal impact, the continuity costs beyond money, and why escaping it requires solving the underlying staffing problem.
The Contract-Labor Trap
Locum tenens (temporary contract physicians) and travel nurses exist to fill the gaps that permanent staffing cannot. For a rural hospital unable to recruit a permanent emergency physician, anesthesiologist, or nurse, contract labor is often the only way to keep a service line open — without the locum, the ER closes or the surgery stops. This makes contract labor genuinely indispensable in the short term: it is the difference between a functioning hospital and a closed service. But it is also a trap, because the premium cost of contract labor — significantly above the cost of an equivalent permanent employee — strains the budget, and the reliance can become structural, with a hospital perpetually filling permanent gaps with expensive temporary labor it cannot afford but cannot do without.
The trap tightens because the conditions that force reliance on contract labor are the same conditions that make permanent recruitment fail. A rural hospital in a hard-to-staff market cannot fill permanent positions, so it uses locums and travelers; but the high cost of that contract labor drains the budget that might fund the wages and improvements needed to attract permanent staff, perpetuating the cycle. The hospital is caught: it cannot afford to keep paying premium contract rates, but it cannot stop without closing services, and it lacks the financial room to invest its way to permanent staffing. Contract labor is a bridge that becomes a permanent, expensive fixture.
Contract labor — locum physicians, travel nurses — is the lifeline that keeps rural service lines open when permanent recruitment fails, but at a steep premium that drains the very budget needed to fund permanent staffing. It is a bridge that becomes a permanent, expensive fixture, perpetuating the cycle it was meant to bridge.
The Fiscal Impact
The financial drain is substantial and direct. Contract clinicians command rates well above permanent-staff costs — travel nurses, even after the post-pandemic normalization to roughly $2,100 per week, cost meaningfully more than staff RNs when agency margins are included, and locum physicians similarly carry premiums plus agency fees, travel, and housing. For a rural hospital operating at or below break-even, the gap between contract cost and permanent cost is a direct margin hit, and a hospital heavily reliant on contract labor can see that reliance push it from marginal to insolvent. In the Northeast Kingdom and similar rural regions, a meaningful share of clinical shifts being covered by temporary staff translates into a budget structurally burdened by premium labor costs.
The fiscal impact compounds the rural margin crisis. A rural hospital already squeezed by low volume, poor payer mix, and lagging payment can least afford the premium of contract labor — yet its staffing difficulties force exactly that reliance, so the hospitals least able to absorb the cost are the ones most dependent on it. Contract-labor spending is therefore both a symptom of the rural staffing crisis and an accelerant of the rural financial crisis, the two reinforcing each other. Reducing contract-labor dependence is one of the clearest financial wins available to a rural hospital — but it requires solving the staffing problem that creates the dependence, which is the hard part.
The Rural-Specific Severity
Contract-labor dependence is worse for rural hospitals than for urban ones along every dimension, which compounds the trap. Rural hospitals pay more for contract labor because clinicians are harder to attract to remote locations, so the premium over permanent cost is steeper. They rely on it more heavily because permanent recruitment is hardest exactly where they are. They can least afford it, operating on the thinnest margins. And the continuity loss hurts most, because rural medicine depends more on the community-embedded, relationship-based care that revolving temporary providers cannot deliver. Every disadvantage of contract-labor reliance is amplified in the rural setting, which is why the trap closes hardest on rural hospitals.
This rural amplification means contract-labor spending is both a larger share of rural hospital costs and a larger threat to rural hospital survival than the national averages suggest. A rural hospital covering a significant fraction of its shifts with travelers and locums is carrying a cost burden that an urban hospital with a deeper local labor pool simply does not face — and that burden, layered onto the rural margin crisis, can be the difference between solvency and closure. The contract-labor trap is, in this sense, a distinctly rural financial threat, and reducing it is a distinctly rural financial priority.
The Continuity Cost
Beyond the dollars, heavy reliance on contract labor imposes a clinical cost that is real if harder to quantify: the loss of continuity. Temporary clinicians, by definition, rotate in and out — they do not know the patients, the community, the local protocols, or the other staff the way permanent clinicians do, and they leave before relationships form. For a rural community where the relationship between patients and 'their' doctor or nurse is often deep and longstanding, a revolving door of temporary providers erodes the continuity of care that is one of rural medicine's strengths. Care delivered by a series of strangers is not the same as care delivered by a known, trusted clinician embedded in the community.
This continuity cost interacts with quality and trust. Temporary clinicians, however competent, lack the institutional and community knowledge that prevents errors and builds trust, and high turnover among them can degrade team functioning and care coordination. For the patient, the experience of never seeing the same provider twice undermines the relationship that supports good chronic-disease management and honest communication. The continuity cost is thus both a quality concern and an erosion of the community-embedded care that makes rural medicine work — a cost that does not show up on the balance sheet but is felt by patients and the remaining permanent staff alike.
Escaping the Trap
Breaking the contract-labor cycle requires solving the underlying staffing problem, because contract labor is a symptom. The levers are the same as for the broader rural workforce crisis: retention investment to keep the permanent staff a hospital has (every retained nurse or physician is one fewer contract gap to fill), creative recruitment and pipeline-building to grow permanent staff, care-model redesign (virtual nursing, team-based care, telehealth) that reduces the demand for on-site permanent staff, and the financial stabilization (global budgets, Medicaid expansion) that gives a hospital the resources to invest in permanent staffing rather than perpetually renting it. The key insight is that contract-labor spending and permanent-staffing investment are alternatives competing for the same dollars — and the hospital that can shift spending from the former to the latter both saves money and improves continuity.
[{"Element":"Function","Detail":"Fills gaps permanent recruitment can't — keeps service lines open"},{"Element":"Fiscal cost","Detail":"Premium rates + agency fees drain fragile rural margins"},{"Element":"Continuity cost","Detail":"Revolving providers erode the community-embedded care rural medicine relies on"},{"Element":"Escape","Detail":"Solve the staffing root: retention, pipeline, care redesign, financial stability"}]
The locum-tenens trap
What to Watch
Key signals: whether rural hospitals reduce contract-labor dependence as travel rates normalize and retention investment grows; whether the continuity costs of temporary staffing gain recognition alongside the fiscal ones; whether care-model redesign reduces the permanent-staffing demand that drives contract reliance; and whether financial stabilization gives rural hospitals the room to invest in permanent staff rather than renting temporary ones. Contract-labor spending is a useful barometer of the rural staffing crisis — falling reliance signals improvement, while persistent high reliance signals a hospital still trapped in the cycle.
Bottom Line
Reliance on locum tenens and travel nurses is rural health care's lifeline and its wound — indispensable for keeping service lines open when permanent recruitment fails, but carrying a premium cost that drains fragile margins and a continuity cost that erodes the community-embedded care rural medicine depends on. The trap is self-perpetuating: the staffing difficulties that force contract reliance are the same ones that make permanent recruitment fail, and the cost of contract labor drains the budget that might fund the permanent staffing that would end the dependence. Escaping it requires solving the underlying staffing problem — through retention, pipeline-building, care-model redesign, and financial stabilization — because contract labor is a symptom, not a disease. The rural hospitals that shift spending from renting temporary clinicians to investing in permanent ones will save money, restore continuity, and break a cycle that the most fragile rural hospitals can least afford to perpetuate.

######## global-budget-models-international-lessons-rural-us | What the U.S. Can Learn From Global Budget Models in Canada, England, and Japan for Rural Health System Reform
As fee-for-service strangles rural hospitals — whose finances collapse when low volume cannot cover fixed costs — policymakers are converging on an alternative: the global budget, which pays a hospital a fixed annual amount regardless of volume. The U.S. has two real laboratories for this idea (Maryland statewide and Pennsylvania's rural model) plus instructive international experience. This analysis examines how global budgets work, what the Maryland and Pennsylvania evidence shows, the international lessons, and why the model is especially suited to rural America.
How Global Budgets Work
Under fee-for-service, a hospital earns revenue per admission, test, and procedure — so revenue rises with volume, and falls when volume falls. For a rural hospital in a shrinking community, that is a death spiral: declining volume cannot cover the fixed costs of maintaining an ER, beds, and staff, producing the losses that drive closures. A global budget breaks the link: the hospital receives a predetermined annual revenue regardless of how many services it delivers, giving it predictable income and removing the incentive to maximize volume. In principle this both stabilizes fragile hospitals and discourages unnecessary care, since extra volume no longer means extra revenue (The Source; Saving Rural Hospitals).
The catch is the same as its appeal: setting the budget. A global budget requires someone — usually a state regulator — to decide what each hospital's revenue should be, balancing financial viability against cost control. Set it too low and the hospital cannot survive; too high and it locks in excess spending. Global budgeting therefore replaces the market's (flawed) volume signal with administrative judgment, which is only as good as the regulator making it.
Global budgets flip rural hospital economics: instead of revenue that collapses with falling volume, the hospital gets a fixed annual budget. That predictability is why the model is increasingly seen as rural America's most promising financial lifeline — and why setting the budget right is everything.
The Maryland Evidence
Maryland is the only state with a mature, statewide all-payer hospital global-budget system, making it the most important U.S. evidence base. The results on cost are real: the model was estimated to reduce Medicare hospital spending by roughly $550 million over three years — beating its target — and one analysis put total Medicare savings at about $975 million over five years, while slowing all-payer hospital spending growth (Mathematica; PMC). This is genuine evidence that statewide global budgets can bend the hospital cost curve, the strongest such evidence in the U.S.
The caveats are instructive. The Medicare savings were partly offset for commercial members by increased professional (non-hospital) spending — squeezing the hospital balloon pushed some costs elsewhere. And patient-experience scores in Maryland were lower than comparison hospitals (though they did not worsen under global budgets, suggesting a pre-existing difference rather than a budget effect). The lesson is that global budgets can control hospital cost but do not automatically control total cost or improve experience — the design has to account for cost-shifting to non-hospital settings.
The Pennsylvania Rural Test
Pennsylvania ran the most directly relevant experiment: the Pennsylvania Rural Health Model, a CMMI-approved global-budget arrangement specifically for critical access and rural hospitals, operating from 2019 through 2024. Its explicit goal was the rural one — improving the financial viability and stability of rural hospitals whose margins had collapsed with dwindling volume. The key finding is the one rural America cares about: global budgets provided predictable annual revenue that helped sustain these facilities, and some evidence showed increased resilience to volume loss during the COVID-19 pandemic, when fee-for-service hospitals saw revenue crater as elective volume vanished but globally-budgeted hospitals kept their fixed income (CMS; Saving Rural Hospitals).
That COVID natural experiment is telling: the very feature of global budgets that critics call a weakness (paying regardless of volume) became a decisive strength when volume collapsed for reasons outside the hospital's control. For rural hospitals chronically exposed to volume shocks, revenue stability is not a minor benefit — it is the difference between staying open and closing.
[{"Model":"Maryland (statewide)","Finding":"~$975M Medicare savings/5yr; slowed spending growth; some cost-shift to professional services"},{"Model":"Pennsylvania (rural)","Finding":"Predictable revenue sustained rural hospitals; resilient to COVID volume loss"},{"Model":"Vermont (Act 167)","Finding":"Statewide global budgets planned — all hospitals by FY2030"}]
Global budgets: the U.S. evidence
The Design Choices That Decide Success
Because the model is proven but the budget-setting is everything, the design choices are where global budgeting succeeds or fails — and they are worth naming concretely. The first is the base and trend: what revenue level a hospital starts at and how the budget grows each year. Set the base too low or the trend below cost inflation, and the budget squeezes the hospital toward insolvency; set them too high, and the model entrenches excess spending. The second is risk adjustment: the budget must account for changes in a community's size and acuity, or a hospital is penalized for a sicker, larger population it did not choose. The third is the handling of leakage — what happens when patients seek care outside the budgeted hospital — which Maryland's cost-shift experience shows must be anticipated.
The fourth, and most important for rural America, is service protection: a global budget should be structured so a hospital cannot improve its margin by dropping the essential, money-losing services (obstetrics, emergency, behavioral health) that are the whole reason the community needs the hospital. A budget that simply caps revenue without protecting essential services invites the hospital to survive financially by becoming clinically hollow. The states that get global budgeting right will be those that treat it not as a spending cap but as a deliberate instrument for sustaining the specific services a community depends on — which requires far more design sophistication than 'pay a fixed amount' implies.
The International Lessons
Global budgeting is unremarkable internationally — most wealthy countries fund hospitals through some form of fixed or capped budget rather than per-service fees, which is part of why they spend far less than the U.S. on hospital care. The instructive lesson from abroad is not whether global budgets work (they plainly can) but how to manage their failure modes: the risk that a fixed budget creates incentives to under-provide care or to push patients elsewhere, and the need for quality measurement to ensure budget discipline does not become rationing. Countries that pair global budgets with strong quality oversight and access guarantees avoid the under-provision trap; those that simply cap spending without those guardrails can ration care. The transferable lesson for the U.S. is that the budget must be paired with accountability for access and quality, not just cost.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether Vermont's statewide Act 167 implementation succeeds and adds a third major U.S. data point (especially a rural-heavy one); whether the Pennsylvania Rural Health Model, which ended in 2024, is replicated by other states; whether CMS's AHEAD model (which incorporates global-budget elements) scales the approach nationally; and whether the designs incorporate the lessons — guarding against cost-shifting to non-hospital settings and against under-provision — that the Maryland and international experience teach. The momentum is real, but global budgeting lives or dies on execution: the model is proven, the budget-setting is where it succeeds or fails.
Bottom Line
Global budgets are the most promising answer to the rural hospital financial crisis because they fix the core problem — revenue that collapses with falling volume — by paying a fixed annual amount instead. The evidence supports it: Maryland's statewide model saved Medicare roughly $975M over five years and slowed spending growth, and Pennsylvania's rural model gave fragile hospitals the revenue stability that proved decisive during COVID. The international experience confirms global budgets work and teaches how to avoid their failure modes through quality and access guardrails. The recurring lesson across all of it is that the model is sound but the budget-setting is everything — set fairly and paired with accountability for access and quality, global budgets can stabilize rural hospitals and control cost; set carelessly, they can starve hospitals or ration care. Vermont's Act 167 is now the experiment that will test this at full state scale in the rural conditions that matter most. If global budgeting is going to become the default answer to the rural hospital crisis — and the converging momentum from Maryland, Pennsylvania, CMS's AHEAD model, and Vermont suggests it might — then the policy community's attention should shift from debating whether the model works to mastering the design choices that determine whether any given implementation helps or harms. The era of asking 'should we try global budgets?' is giving way to the harder, more important question of 'how do we set them right?'

Maryland's statewide model saved Medicare ~$975M over 5 years; Pennsylvania's rural model gave fragile hospitals revenue stability that proved decisive during COVID. What the U.S. Can Learn From Global Budget Models in Canada, England, and Japan for Rural Health System Reform

######## glp1-employer-solvency-crisis | The GLP-1 Solvency Paradox: Employer Bankruptcy vs. Long-Term Value
GLP-1 drugs like Wegovy and Zepbound are the most clinically transformative — and financially destabilizing — medications to hit employer health plans in a generation. They genuinely work for weight loss and increasingly for cardiovascular and other outcomes, but at a cost and a scale that threaten to break plan budgets. This analysis examines the GLP-1 cost shock, the employer dilemma, the long-term-value-versus-near-term-cost tension, the access and equity stakes, and the rapidly shifting pricing landscape.
The Cost Shock
The financial problem with GLP-1s is the collision of high price and enormous eligible population. The drugs have listed for up to roughly $766 per month net of discounts, and because a large share of the adult population qualifies on obesity criteria, covering them for weight management is unlike covering a drug for a rare condition — the eligible pool is vast. The result is a budget shock: adding GLP-1 coverage for weight management can raise an employer's total pharmacy costs by about 2-5%, and in high-utilization groups by 10-15%, translating to an additional $10-25 per member per month, with some analyses projecting GLP-1s could drive up employer premiums by as much as 14% even with access limited to the highest-need patients (BCBS; WTW). For a self-insured employer, that is a direct hit to the bottom line.
GLP-1s collide high price with a huge eligible population: covering them for weight management can lift an employer's pharmacy costs 2-15% and premiums by as much as 14%. Unlike a drug for a rare disease, a sizable share of the workforce qualifies — which is what makes the budget math break.
The Employer Dilemma
Employers are caught in a genuine bind, and their responses show it. Coverage is growing — 52% of surveyed employers covered GLP-1s for obesity in 2024, rising to 57% in 2025 — but the cost is straining the model: about one in seven (14%) employers covering GLP-1s for obesity are considering dropping that coverage due to cost (WTW). To manage the expense, employers are layering on restrictions: lifestyle-modification requirements (69%), requiring patients to try lower-cost medications first (63%), limiting therapy duration (63%), and capping eligibility at higher BMI thresholds. These are rationing mechanisms in all but name — ways to capture some clinical benefit while containing the budget blow.
The dilemma is sharpened by employee demand and competition. GLP-1s are popular and effective, employees increasingly expect coverage, and an employer that drops it risks both employee dissatisfaction and a competitive disadvantage in recruiting — yet one that covers it generously risks an unsustainable cost trajectory. There is no comfortable position: cover broadly and absorb the cost, cover narrowly and face employee backlash and accusations of rationing effective care, or drop coverage and lose talent. Employers are improvising their way through a problem the system has not solved.
Long-Term Value vs. Near-Term Cost
The deepest tension is temporal. The case for covering GLP-1s rests heavily on long-term value: by treating obesity and its downstream conditions, the drugs may reduce future cardiovascular events, diabetes, joint replacements, and other expensive outcomes — and emerging evidence (cardiovascular outcome trials) strengthens that case. But that value accrues over years, while the cost is immediate, and it lands on an employer that may not even cover the same employee in five years given job turnover. This is a textbook wrong-pocket problem: the employer pays now for benefits that may materialize later, possibly for a different payer entirely after the employee changes jobs.
This misalignment distorts the rational decision. If an employer captured the full long-term savings, the investment might pencil out; because it likely will not — the employee may move on, the savings accrue diffusely and slowly, and some may never materialize for this payer — the near-term cost dominates the calculation. The result is that even drugs with genuine long-term value get rationed by payers who cannot capture that value, an outcome that is individually rational for each employer and collectively suboptimal for population health. It is the same structural failure that under-funds prevention generally, applied to the most consequential prevention drug in years.
The Offset Debate
A central dispute shapes every coverage decision: do GLP-1s pay for themselves through 'medical offsets' — the future costs avoided by treating obesity? Proponents argue that preventing diabetes, heart attacks, strokes, and joint replacements eventually saves more than the drugs cost, making coverage a sound long-term investment. Skeptics counter that the offsets are slower, smaller, and less certain than advocates claim, that patients often stop the drugs (and regain weight) before the offsets materialize, and that the upfront cost is large and certain while the savings are distant and probabilistic. The honest state of the evidence is that some offset is real, particularly for the cardiovascular outcomes now supported by trials, but it does not fully fund the drug cost on the time horizon and turnover an employer faces.
This unresolved debate is why employers cannot simply do the math and decide. If offsets clearly covered the cost, coverage would be straightforward; because they likely cover only part of it, and on a timeline that exceeds the average employee's tenure, the employer bears a real net cost in exchange for benefits that are partly societal and partly captured by future payers. The offset question is thus inseparable from the wrong-pocket problem: even where the offsets are real, the employer paying for the drug may not be the one who reaps them, which turns a potentially sound population-level investment into a money-loser for the specific payer making the decision.
The Access and Equity Stakes
The rationing has an equity dimension. As employers restrict coverage and the uninsured and underinsured face list prices few can afford, GLP-1s risk becoming a treatment stratified by ability to pay — available to the well-insured and the wealthy, out of reach for others, even though obesity and its complications fall hardest on lower-income populations. A genuinely effective treatment for a condition that drives enormous health disparities could, if access is gated by coverage and price, widen those disparities rather than narrow them. The 'miracle drug' becomes a miracle for those who can get it.
This is why the pricing landscape matters so much, and it is shifting fast. Manufacturers have begun offering direct-purchase options at lower prices (around $350/month, with commitments toward roughly $250), and in November 2025 the Trump administration announced deals with Eli Lilly and Novo Nordisk to lower GLP-1 costs for Medicare, Medicaid, and direct purchasers (CNN; AARP). Falling prices would ease both the employer budget shock and the equity problem simultaneously — the entire crisis is, at root, a function of price, and a substantially lower price would dissolve much of it. The trajectory of GLP-1 pricing is therefore the single most important variable in how this plays out.
[{"Dimension":"Cost","Detail":"Up to ~$766/mo net; vast eligible population"},{"Dimension":"Plan impact","Detail":"+2-5% pharmacy cost (10-15% high-util); premiums up to +14%"},{"Dimension":"Employer response","Detail":"Coverage 52%(2024)->57%(2025); 14% considering dropping; heavy restrictions"},{"Dimension":"Price shift","Detail":"Direct purchase ~$350->$250; Nov 2025 Medicare/Medicaid deals"}]
GLP-1s and the employer plan
What to Watch
Key signals: whether GLP-1 prices continue falling (the variable that most determines the whole picture); whether the long-term outcome evidence strengthens enough to shift the cost-benefit calculation for payers; whether employer coverage expands or contracts as cost pressure and employee demand pull in opposite directions; whether Medicare and Medicaid coverage (newly expanding via the 2025 deals) reshapes the market and pricing; and whether the access gap widens into a clear equity problem or is closed by falling prices. GLP-1s are a live experiment in how the fragmented U.S. system handles a genuinely effective but expensive drug for a common condition — and the answer is still being written.
Bottom Line
GLP-1 drugs are forcing a reckoning in employer health plans: genuinely effective medications for a condition affecting a huge share of the population, at a price that can lift pharmacy costs 10-15% and premiums up to 14%, creating a budget shock with no comfortable response. Employers are improvising — growing coverage but layering on rationing restrictions, with a notable share considering dropping it — caught between employee demand and unsustainable cost. The deepest problem is the wrong-pocket tension: the long-term value is real but accrues slowly and possibly to a different payer, so near-term cost dominates and even valuable treatment gets rationed, with an equity cost as access stratifies by ability to pay. The entire crisis is fundamentally a function of price, which is why the rapidly falling prices and new Medicare/Medicaid deals are the most important development to watch. GLP-1s are a test of whether the U.S. system can deliver a transformative-but-expensive treatment broadly and equitably — and so far the answer is that it is straining badly to do so. The GLP-1 episode is a preview of a recurring future challenge: as biomedical innovation produces more effective-but-expensive treatments for common conditions — the next obesity drug, the next Alzheimer's therapy, the next gene therapy that could apply to millions — the U.S. financing system will repeatedly face the same collision of high price, large eligible population, slow-accruing and wrong-pocketed value, and equity stakes. How the system resolves the GLP-1 dilemma, through some combination of falling prices, smarter coverage design, and public negotiation, will be a template for how it handles the wave of consequential, costly therapies coming behind it. In that sense GLP-1s are less an anomaly than a stress test — an early, large-scale trial of whether American health financing can absorb genuine medical breakthroughs without either bankrupting payers or rationing the breakthrough to those who can pay, and the system's halting, improvised response so far is not reassuring.
