The GLP-1 Solvency Paradox: Employer Bankruptcy vs. Long-Term Value

######## great-resignation-primary-care-firing-hospital | The Great Resignation: Why Primary Care is Firing the Hospital
American medicine has undergone a quiet ownership revolution: the independent doctor, long the archetype of the profession, has nearly disappeared into hospital systems and corporate entities. This analysis examines the scale and speed of physician consolidation, what is driving doctors to give up independence, the consequences for cost and care, the countercurrents (direct primary care, concierge medicine) of physicians 'firing' the system, and what it means for the future of the profession.
The Scale of Consolidation
The shift has been dramatic and fast. In 2012, roughly 60% of physicians worked in physician-owned independent practice; by 2024, that had fallen to about 42%, an 18-percentage-point decline, and at least 47% of physicians were employed by or affiliated with hospital systems (up from about 30% in 2012), with even higher shares when corporate (non-hospital) employers are included (AMA; GAO). The corporate acquisition pace is relentless: corporations acquired roughly 8,000 physician practices across 2024-2025, outpacing even hospitals, and private equity has been a major force — responsible for about 65% of physician-practice acquisitions from 2019 to 2023 (Becker's; GAO). The independent physician, once the norm, is now a shrinking minority.
The rural picture is even more consolidated: as of recent data, around 80% of rural physicians were employed by hospitals or corporate entities, and a large majority of rural practices were under hospital or corporate control — reflecting both the financial fragility of independent rural practice and the limited number of employers in rural markets. Consolidation, in other words, has gone furthest exactly where the alternatives are fewest.
In 2012 about 60% of physicians were in independent practice; by 2024 only ~42% were, with ~47%+ employed by hospitals and still more by corporate entities. In rural areas, roughly 80% of physicians are now employed. The independent doctor — long the archetype of American medicine — has become a shrinking minority.
Why Doctors Give Up Independence
The forces pushing physicians out of independence are economic and administrative. Running an independent practice has become financially punishing: stagnant or declining Medicare physician payment (which has not kept pace with inflation), the rising cost and complexity of EHRs and regulatory compliance, the administrative burden of billing and prior authorization, and the leverage advantage that large systems have in payer negotiations all squeeze the independent practice's economics. A solo or small-group physician faces the full weight of these pressures alone; selling to a hospital or corporation offloads the administrative and financial burden in exchange for a salary and the loss of autonomy.
The irony is that consolidation does not necessarily reward the physician financially — after acquisition, physicians have seen average income decline slightly, and independent physicians actually earn marginally more on average than employed ones. The driver is less higher pay than relief: escaping the administrative grind, the financial risk, and the negotiating disadvantage of independence. Physicians are trading autonomy not for money but for a reprieve from the operational burden that has made independent practice increasingly untenable — which is why the trend continues even though employment is not clearly more lucrative.
The Consequences for Cost and Care
Consolidation has measurable downsides, particularly for cost. When a hospital acquires a physician practice, the same services often get billed at higher hospital-based rates (the 'site-of-service' differential), increasing Medicare and commercial spending for identical care — a major driver of the price increases that consolidation produces (GAO; Fierce Healthcare). Provider consolidation more broadly is associated with higher prices, as larger systems gain the market power to negotiate higher rates. So the physician's move from independence to employment, multiplied across the profession, has contributed directly to rising health-care costs without a corresponding improvement in care.
The effects on care quality and the doctor-patient relationship are contested but real. Physicians in acquired practices report loss of clinical autonomy and pressure to meet financial and productivity targets — shorter visits, higher volume — which can erode the relationship and the professional satisfaction that drew them to medicine. The corporatization of medicine, critics argue, subordinates clinical judgment to financial management, while defenders note that scale can bring care coordination, data infrastructure, and stability that small practices lack. The honest verdict is mixed, but the cost increase is clear and the autonomy loss is real.
The Rural Dimension
Physician consolidation has a particularly acute rural dimension that compounds the rural access crisis examined elsewhere. With roughly 80% of rural physicians now employed by hospitals or corporate entities, the independent rural practice — historically a pillar of small-town health care — is nearly extinct, and its disappearance ties physician availability to the fate of the consolidating entities. When a rural hospital or corporate owner struggles financially or exits a market, the physicians it employs may leave with it, removing care that an independent practice might have sustained. Consolidation thus makes rural physician supply more fragile, not less, by concentrating it in institutions that are themselves financially precarious.
It also interacts with the site-of-service cost problem in a way that hurts rural patients specifically. When a rural practice is acquired and its services rebilled at hospital-based rates, rural patients — already traveling farther for care — face higher costs for the same services, even as the consolidation was often justified as necessary to keep the practice open. The rural physician's loss of independence is therefore not just a professional shift but a change that can raise rural patients' costs while making their access to physicians more dependent on the solvency of fragile rural institutions — a double disadvantage layered onto the existing rural-urban gap.
Firing the System: DPC and Concierge
Against the consolidation tide runs a countercurrent: physicians who 'fire' the entire third-party-payment system rather than join it. Direct primary care (DPC) practices charge patients a flat monthly membership fee (often around $75) and refuse insurance entirely, functioning as a subscription model that frees the physician from billing, coding, and payer negotiation — the very burdens driving others into employment. Concierge medicine takes a related path, charging patients a substantial annual retainer (often $2,000 or more) for enhanced access while typically still billing insurance. Both are reactions to the same pressures, but instead of escaping into a corporate employer, these physicians escape into a direct-pay relationship with patients.
These models are a telling response to the system's dysfunction, but they carry an equity cost. By opting out of insurance, DPC and concierge physicians reduce the supply of insurance-accepting primary care for the general population, and concierge models in particular cater to wealthier patients who can afford the retainer — so one physician's escape from administrative burden can mean fewer accessible primary-care slots for everyone else. They illustrate that physicians are fleeing the broken payment system in two opposite directions — into corporate employment and out into direct pay — both driven by the same underlying dysfunction, and both with consequences for access.
[{"Metric":"Independent practice","Value":"~60% (2012) -> ~42% (2024)"},{"Metric":"Hospital-employed","Value":"~30% (2012) -> ~47%+ (2024); ~80% rural"},{"Metric":"PE acquisitions","Value":"~65% of practice acquisitions, 2019-2023"},{"Metric":"Cost effect","Value":"Site-of-service differential raises prices for identical care"}]
The physician ownership revolution
What to Watch
Key signals: whether physician consolidation continues toward near-total corporate/hospital employment or stabilizes; whether site-neutral payment reform (paying the same rate regardless of setting) removes the financial incentive driving hospital acquisition of practices; whether Medicare physician payment is reformed to make independent practice viable again; whether direct primary care and concierge models grow enough to meaningfully affect access; and whether antitrust enforcement slows the consolidation that drives prices up. The independent physician's decline is one of the most consequential structural shifts in American medicine, with direct effects on cost, access, and the character of the profession.
Bottom Line
American medicine has undergone an ownership revolution — independent practice falling from ~60% of physicians in 2012 to ~42% by 2024, hospital and corporate employment surging, and rural physicians now roughly 80% employed — driven less by financial reward than by escape from the administrative burden, financial risk, and negotiating disadvantage that have made independence untenable. The consequences are real: consolidation raises costs through the site-of-service differential and the market power of large systems, while eroding the clinical autonomy that defined the profession. The countercurrents — direct primary care and concierge medicine — are physicians fleeing the same dysfunction in the opposite direction, into direct pay, with their own equity costs for access. Reversing or moderating the trend would require addressing its drivers: site-neutral payment to remove the acquisition incentive, Medicare payment reform to make independence viable, and antitrust enforcement to slow consolidation. Until then, the corporatization of medicine will continue, reshaping not just who employs doctors but what medicine costs and how it is practiced.

This analysis covers the scale, the drivers, the cost/care consequences, and the DPC/concierge countercurrents.
The Great Resignation: Why Primary Care is Firing the Hospital

######## health-economics-fundamentals-vermont-act-167 | Health Economics Fundamentals: Act 167 & The Vermont Case
Health economics offers a precise vocabulary for what Vermont is living through. The state's Act 167 hospital-sustainability investigation did not invent a new crisis; it documented, in unusually concrete terms, the collision between a demand curve that keeps rising and a financing structure that can no longer support it. Reading the Vermont case through the standard tools of the field — derived demand, price elasticity, cross-subsidization, and the distinction between cost-shifting and cost-saving — turns a set of alarming headlines into a coherent, and correctable, economic story.
Health as Capital: The Grossman Framework
Michael Grossman's foundational model treats health not as a good consumed for its own sake but as a stock of 'health capital' that depreciates over time and can be replenished through investment — medical care, but also nutrition, housing, and behavior. Demand for medical care, in this framing, is a derived demand: people do not want hospital visits, they want the health those visits restore. The model predicts that demand for care rises as the depreciation rate of health capital rises, which is precisely what aging does.
Vermont is one of the oldest states in the country. Roughly 22 percent of Vermonters are 65 or older, placing it near the top of the national rankings alongside Maine and Florida, and about 28 percent are 60 or older. In Grossman's terms, Vermont's population is depreciating its health capital faster than almost any other state's, which mechanically pushes up the derived demand for medical care even before any change in prices or technology. This is the demographic engine underneath the Act 167 findings: an aging population generates rising care demand as a matter of biology, not policy choice.
Inelastic Demand and the Affordability Trap
Price elasticity of demand measures how much the quantity demanded falls when price rises. For much of medical care — and especially for the chronic and acute needs of an older, rural population — demand is highly inelastic: a heart attack, a hip fracture, or insulin does not become optional because the price went up. The Act 167 community-engagement process, which reached more than 3,100 participants across all of Vermont's hospital service areas, surfaced exactly this dynamic. Rural Vermonters need access regardless of price, yet the price of that access — premiums and deductibles — has climbed far faster than incomes.
Inelastic demand has a hard policy implication: you cannot solve an affordability crisis by expecting patients to 'shop' their way out of it. When demand barely responds to price, rising prices do not reduce utilization; they reduce financial security, push people to forgo care they actually need, and eventually drive them out of the insurance pool altogether. That is the affordability trap, and Vermont is deep in it. On the state's individual exchange, Vermont Health Connect, premiums have roughly doubled or more over recent years, and payers have told the legislature that the regulated insurance markets have been losing money.
The Cross-Subsidy That Quietly Expired
For decades, American hospital finance rested on a cross-subsidy: commercial insurers paid well above cost, and those margins covered the losses hospitals booked on Medicare and especially Medicaid patients. Vermont hospital leaders describe this openly — they argue they must charge commercial insurers high rates because public-payer reimbursement is too low, with Medicaid payments among the lowest in the nation. Economically, this is a private tax levied on commercially-insured Vermonters to fund a public obligation the state and federal governments under-fund.
The cross-subsidy works only as long as the commercial base is large and stable enough to absorb the load. It is not anymore. As premiums double, employers and individuals drop or thin their coverage, shrinking the commercial pool that the whole structure depends on. In 2023, Blue Cross and Blue Shield of Vermont paid out roughly $1.11 in claims for every $1.00 of premium it collected — an unsustainable loss ratio that signals the commercial side can no longer carry the subsidy. The 'silent social contract' that premiums would rise forever to cover public-payer shortfalls has, in Vermont, audibly broken.
The economic core of Act 167: Vermont faces rising, inelastic demand (an aging population depreciating its health capital) financed by a shrinking commercial cross-subsidy. Those two curves cannot be reconciled by asking patients to shop harder or by raising premiums further — the first fails because demand is inelastic, the second fails because it accelerates the collapse of the commercial pool.
Cost-Shifting Versus Cost-Saving
A crucial distinction in health economics is between shifting a cost and eliminating it. The commercial cross-subsidy is pure cost-shifting: the dollar is simply moved from a public payer to a private one, and total system spending is unchanged. Genuine relief requires cost-saving — reducing the resources the system consumes to produce a given amount of health. Higher taxation, as the Act 167 report itself notes, mostly shifts costs onto residents and businesses (with the added risk of driving them out of state); it does not lower the underlying cost of care.
This is why Vermont's most-discussed reforms — hospital global budgets, reference-based pricing, and, until Vermont withdrew in July 2026, the multi-state AHEAD model — are structured to attack cost growth rather than reallocate it. A global budget caps a hospital's total revenue and forces it to live within a fixed envelope, converting the incentive from 'do more billable volume' to 'keep the population healthy at lower total cost.' Whether these tools deliver real savings is an empirical question, but their logic is correct: only cost-saving, not cost-shifting, can resolve a crisis in which every payer is already stretched.
The National Backdrop
Vermont's predicament is an intensified version of the national one. U.S. national health expenditure reached $5.3 trillion in 2024 — about $15,474 per person and 18.0 percent of GDP — after growing 7.2 percent in a single year. The United States spends roughly twice as much per capita as comparable wealthy countries yet posts a life expectancy of 79.0 years, about 3.7 years below the peer-country average of 82.7. The country, in other words, buys less health per dollar than its peers, and Vermont buys it for an older, more rural, more expensive-to-serve population.
Chronic disease is the dominant cost driver nationally, with projections that it could cost the United States as much as $47 trillion over the 2024–2039 period. For a state whose demographics guarantee a heavy and growing chronic-disease burden, the math is unforgiving: the same forces straining the national system bear down on Vermont first and hardest, which is precisely why Vermont has become a national test case for whether a small state can re-engineer hospital finance before the cross-subsidy collapses entirely.
Moral Hazard, Insurance, and the Limits of Cost-Sharing
Health economics also explains why the standard private-market remedy — making patients bear more cost through high deductibles — backfires in Vermont. The textbook rationale for cost-sharing is moral hazard: insulated from price, insured patients consume more care than they would if they paid directly, so deductibles are meant to restore price sensitivity. But moral hazard only bites where demand is elastic and care is discretionary. For an older, sicker, rural population whose utilization is dominated by chronic-disease management and acute events, the discretionary margin is thin, and high deductibles mostly deter necessary care rather than wasteful care.
The empirical literature is consistent on this point: blunt cost-sharing reduces high-value and low-value care roughly alike, because patients cannot reliably tell which is which. In Vermont's context, that means deductible-heavy designs translate directly into forgone primary care and medication non-adherence, which raise downstream costs through avoidable hospitalizations. The policy implication is that financial protection and cost control are not in tension here — protecting access to high-value primary and preventive care is itself a cost-control strategy, because it heads off the expensive acute episodes the system can least afford.
What the Framework Tells Policymakers
The health-economics lens yields a short, disciplined list of conclusions. First, demand is rising for demographic reasons that policy cannot reverse, so the lever must be the cost of producing health, not the quantity of health demanded. Second, because demand is inelastic, consumer-shopping and high-deductible designs will not bend the curve and will mostly transfer financial risk onto patients. Third, the commercial cross-subsidy is a depleting resource that cannot be stretched further without collapsing the pool that funds it. Fourth, only cost-saving structural reform — global budgets, payment redesign, prevention, and right-sizing of services — addresses the actual problem; everything else is cost-shifting dressed up as a solution.
Act 167 is best understood not as a doomsday report but as a forcing function: it translates the abstractions of health economics into a dated, dollar-denominated ultimatum. The framework says the crisis is real, the easy answers are illusions, and the hard answers — restructuring how care is financed and delivered — are the only ones the economics will actually support.

######## hospital-at-home-rural-implementation-guide-2026 | Hospital-at-Home in Rural Markets: Implementation Economics, Patient Eligibility, and the Operational Model That Is Working
Hospital-at-Home (HaH) is proven safe and cheaper than inpatient care, and the federal waiver that funds it now runs through 2030. But the national evidence comes overwhelmingly from large urban and academic systems. The harder question — and the one that matters for the communities with the fewest beds — is whether the model is actually implementable in rural settings. This analysis is a rural-implementation lens: the distinctive barriers, the eligibility math, the operating adaptations, and the honest assessment of where rural HaH works and where it does not.
Why Rural Is Different
The core HaH model assumes things that are scarce in rural America. It assumes a care team can reach a patient's home twice a day — but rural patients are spread across long, sometimes mountainous distances, so the routing math that is trivial in a city becomes the binding constraint in the country. It assumes reliable broadband for remote monitoring and video — but rural broadband gaps are exactly where HaH would be deployed. And it assumes enough clinical staff to run a parallel home service — but rural workforce shortages are the reason the local hospital is struggling in the first place (National Rural Health Association, 2024).
These are not reasons rural HaH cannot work; they are the design constraints any rural program has to engineer around. The systems attempting it adapt the urban template rather than copy it — leaning on community paramedicine for in-home visits, intermittent rather than continuous monitoring where bandwidth is thin, and partnerships that let a small hospital plug into a larger system's command center rather than build its own.
The national Hospital-at-Home evidence is overwhelmingly urban and academic. Rural implementation is not a smaller version of the same thing — it is a different engineering problem dominated by distance, bandwidth, and workforce, and the urban template has to be adapted, not copied.
The Eligibility Funnel Is Narrower in Rural Areas
HaH eligibility runs through a screen that combines clinical suitability with an assessment of the home environment — is the dwelling safe, is there a caregiver, can the patient's needs be met there. In rural settings that funnel narrows from both ends. Clinically, lower patient volume means fewer candidates in the eligible diagnosis bands at any given time, which makes it hard to keep a home-hospital census large enough to justify fixed program costs. Environmentally, rural housing and caregiver availability screen out a meaningful share of otherwise-eligible patients, and the long distances mean the 'rapid return to hospital' safety requirement is harder to guarantee.
The distance problem deserves emphasis because it cuts against the safety case. HaH's safety rests partly on being able to escalate a deteriorating patient back to the hospital quickly; when the patient is 40 minutes away on rural roads, that escalation window is longer and riskier. A rural program has to weigh that against the alternative — which, given rural hospital closures, may be no local inpatient bed at all. In some communities HaH is competing not with a nearby hospital but with a two-hour transfer, which can flip the risk calculus in its favor.
[{"Constraint":"Distance/routing","Effect":"Twice-daily visits costly; slower escalation back to hospital"},{"Constraint":"Broadband","Effect":"Remote monitoring/video degraded where signal is weak"},{"Constraint":"Volume","Effect":"Fewer eligible patients to cover fixed program cost"},{"Constraint":"Workforce","Effect":"Hard to staff a parallel home service amid shortages"},{"Constraint":"Capital","Effect":"Upfront logistics/monitoring investment vs. thin margins"}]
Rural HaH: the binding constraints
How Rural Programs Make It Work
The programs that succeed share a set of adaptations. The most important is the hub-and-spoke partnership: rather than a critical access hospital building its own command center, it connects to a larger system's HaH infrastructure, borrowing the monitoring, physician oversight, and logistics it cannot build alone. The second is community paramedicine — using Mobile Integrated Health paramedics for the required in-person home visits, which both solves the workforce problem and uses personnel already trained for and accustomed to covering rural distances. The third is patient selection discipline: rural programs tend to focus on the stable, predictable conditions (certain infections, heart-failure and COPD exacerbations) where the escalation risk is lowest, rather than pushing acuity.
Reimbursement realism is the fourth. Because the federal waiver only covers fee-for-service Medicare, a rural program's viability depends on its payer mix; where most eligible patients are in plans that do not reimburse HaH, the economics may not close regardless of clinical fit. The honest rural-implementation guide therefore starts not with technology but with two questions: can we reach these homes safely and often enough, and will we get paid for enough of the patients we could serve?
The Build-vs-Partner Decision
The single most consequential strategic choice for a rural system considering HaH is whether to build its own program or partner into someone else's. Building means standing up a command center, monitoring technology, logistics for medication and equipment delivery, and a dedicated clinical team — a substantial capital and operating commitment that the typical rural hospital, running thin or negative margins, cannot finance on volumes too low to spread the fixed cost. For most standalone critical access hospitals, building is simply not viable, and attempting it is a fast route to an expensive program that never reaches a sustainable census.
Partnering inverts the math. By connecting to a larger system's existing HaH infrastructure — its command center, physician oversight, and supply chain — a rural hospital can offer home-hospital care to its community while paying for marginal participation rather than fixed buildout. The trade-off is dependence on the partner's protocols and a smaller share of the economics, but for a hospital whose alternative is no HaH at all, that is usually the right trade. The emerging pattern, accordingly, is regional: a hub system extends HaH capability across a network of rural spokes, mirroring the hub-and-spoke logic that increasingly defines rural specialty access generally.
Where It Fits — and Where It Doesn't
Rural HaH fits best in 'frontier-adjacent' settings: communities far enough from a hospital that the alternative to home care is a long transfer or no admission at all, but close enough and connected enough that twice-daily visits and monitoring are feasible. It fits a system that already has a partner hub and a paramedicine capability to lean on. It fits the stable medical admissions where escalation risk is low. It does not fit the most remote frontier areas where distance makes twice-daily visiting and rapid escalation genuinely unsafe, nor the smallest standalone hospitals with no partner and no capital, nor higher-acuity patients whose deterioration could outrun a long ambulance ride. Pretending otherwise — treating HaH as a universal rural fix — is how programs fail and patients get hurt.
The Patient's Side of the Ledger
For rural patients, Hospital-at-Home's appeal is not abstract. The alternative to a local home-hospital admission is frequently a transfer to a facility one or two hours away — separating an older patient from family, support, and familiar surroundings precisely when those things aid recovery, and imposing travel and lodging costs on relatives who must follow. Home care keeps the patient in their community, lowers the delirium and deconditioning risks that hospitalization inflicts on older adults, and lets local family remain involved. In communities where the nearby hospital has closed, HaH can be the only way to receive acute-level care without leaving home at all.
Those benefits, though, presuppose a home that can support care and a patient who is not too sick for the distance — which loops back to the eligibility funnel. The patient experience is HaH's strongest selling point in rural America and a real driver of the high satisfaction scores the model earns; it is also why the eligibility and safety discipline matters so much, because a program that stretches to serve a patient it cannot safely reach trades that goodwill for harm.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether rural-specific outcome and safety data emerges to validate (or qualify) the urban evidence in low-volume, long-distance settings; whether broadband investment (BEAD) reaches enough rural homes to make monitoring reliable; whether Medicaid and commercial payers broaden HaH coverage so rural payer mixes can support programs; whether community-paramedicine models scale and gain stable reimbursement; and whether the post-2030 waiver question is resolved with a permanent benefit that justifies rural capital investment. Rural HaH will grow where partnership, paramedicine, broadband, and payment align — and stall where they do not.
Bottom Line
Hospital-at-Home can extend real inpatient-level care into rural communities, but only as a deliberately adapted model — never a copy of the urban template. Its rural viability is governed by distance, broadband, volume, workforce, and payer mix, and it is genuinely unsuitable for the most remote and highest-acuity situations. The rural programs that work are built on hub-and-spoke partnerships, community paramedicine, disciplined patient selection, and a clear-eyed payer-mix analysis. Done that way, HaH can be a lifeline for communities whose alternative is a closed hospital and a long transfer; done as a one-size-fits-all rollout, it courts the safety failures that distance makes uniquely dangerous in rural care.
Sources

hospital-at-home-rural-implementation-guide-2026
Hospital-at-Home is proven and funded through 2030, but its evidence is overwhelmingly urban. This rural-implementation analysis examines the distinctive barriers — distance, broadband, low volume, workforce, payer mix — the narrower rural eligibility funnel, the adaptations that make it work (hub-and-spoke partnerships, community paramedicine, disciplined selection), and the honest limits of where rural HaH fits and where it is unsafe.
Hospital-at-Home in Rural Markets: Implementation Economics, Patient Eligibility, and the Operational Model That Is Working

######## hospital-services-optimization-vermont-act-167-deep-dive | Hospital Services Optimization To Drive Vermont Health Care Reform and Meet Act 167 Requirements
Vermont's Act 167 investigation laid bare a paradox that conventional hospital management cannot resolve: commercial premiums have risen sharply over recent years, yet a substantial share of the state's hospitals still operate at a loss. Rising prices and falling margins are not supposed to coexist — and the fact that they do signals that the problem is structural, not managerial. Solving it requires treating the hospital system as an engineering problem: applying the tools of systems optimization to a care-delivery network that has grown misaligned with the population it serves.
The Paradox of Rising Prices and Falling Margins
Under ordinary conditions, a provider facing losses raises prices and recovers margin. Vermont cannot do this, for two reasons documented in the Act 167 work and subsequent reporting. First, commercial premiums have already risen to the edge of affordability, and the Green Mountain Care Board deliberately caps hospital budgets to protect premium-payers — so the price lever is regulated shut. Second, costs keep climbing for reasons largely outside hospital control: contract and traveling-nurse labor, drug prices, and the fixed cost of maintaining duplicative services across a sparsely populated state. Six Vermont hospitals lost money on operations in the latest reporting cycle even after years of premium growth, which is the paradox in numbers.
The engineering interpretation is that Vermont maintains more fixed service capacity than its population can fill efficiently. Health-services research is consistent on the point that splitting a fixed volume of complex care across many low-volume sites raises cost per case and can harm quality, because each site carries full fixed costs and none achieves the volume that drives efficiency and proficiency. A system optimized for an earlier, larger, younger population is structurally over-built for an aging, shrinking, rural one — and no amount of price increase fixes a capacity mismatch.
Applying Systems Engineering to Care Delivery
Health-systems engineering treats the delivery network as a system to be optimized against explicit objectives — access, quality, cost — subject to real constraints like geography, workforce, and safety minimums. Instead of asking 'how do we preserve every service at every site,' it asks 'what configuration of services across sites delivers the required access and quality at the lowest sustainable cost?' This reframes Vermont's crisis from an unwinnable budget fight into a solvable, if painful, optimization problem with a defined objective function and constraints.
[["Lever","Mechanism","Key constraint"],["Service regionalization","Concentrate low-volume complex care at fewer high-volume sites","Patient travel time / access floor"],["Volume-quality alignment","Stop splitting complex care below safe volume thresholds","Safety minimums for each procedure"],["Workforce optimization","Reduce reliance on high-cost contract labor via retention","Statewide clinician shortage"],["Capacity right-sizing","Match fixed capacity to actual demand at each site","Maintaining emergency/essential access"],["Care-setting shifting","Move appropriate care to lower-cost settings (home, outpatient)","Data and reimbursement infrastructure"]]
Optimization levers for a rural hospital network under fixed budgets
Each lever attacks the cost-per-case problem from a different angle, and the constraint column is what keeps optimization honest: this is constrained optimization, where the access floor and the safety minimums are hard limits, not variables to be traded away for savings. A configuration that minimizes cost while violating the access floor — stranding communities beyond a safe drive time — is not a solution; it has simply optimized the wrong objective. The engineering discipline is precisely in respecting those constraints while squeezing out the waste between them.
The Act 167 paradox — premiums up, margins still negative — is the signature of a capacity mismatch, not a management failure: Vermont maintains more fixed service capacity than an aging, rural population can fill efficiently. Health-systems engineering reframes the crisis as constrained optimization — minimize cost per case subject to hard access and safety floors — solvable through regionalization, volume-quality alignment, workforce optimization, and care-setting shifts.
The Volume-Quality Imperative
A point that elevates regionalization from a cost play to a quality imperative is the well-established volume-outcome relationship: for many complex procedures, higher-volume providers achieve better outcomes, and very-low-volume sites can pose genuine safety risks. When Vermont splits a small statewide volume of a complex service across several sites, it may be simultaneously raising cost and lowering quality — the worst of both. Concentrating that service at one site that reaches adequate volume can improve outcomes while fixing the economics, aligning the financial and clinical cases.
This reframes the most painful conversations. Consolidating a low-volume service is often portrayed purely as a loss of access, but where volume sits below safe thresholds it can also be a gain in quality and safety. The honest framing presents both sides: regionalization lengthens travel for some patients (a real access cost) while raising the volume — and often the safety — of the care they receive (a real quality benefit). A serious optimization weighs both, rather than treating every consolidation as pure loss or pretending the travel cost away.
Why This Requires the Data Infrastructure
None of these optimizations is executable without data. Right-sizing capacity requires accurate demand and utilization data by site and service. Shifting care to lower-cost settings — home, outpatient — requires the data and reimbursement infrastructure to manage patients safely outside the hospital. Even measuring whether a configuration is meeting its access and quality constraints requires system-wide data the legacy siloed environment does not readily provide. This is why Vermont's services-optimization agenda and its health-data-liquidity agenda are the same project viewed from two angles: you cannot optimize a system you cannot measure.
The dependency runs the other way too. Building data infrastructure without acting on what it reveals wastes the investment, and acting on service configuration without data invites error. The two have to advance together — data systems that make the network legible, and the governance and will to act on what they show. Vermont's transformation is, at bottom, the coupling of an engineering re-design of the service network with the data infrastructure that makes such a re-design measurable, safe, and accountable.
Sequencing the Transformation
Optimization is not a single decision but a sequence, and the order matters. The lowest-pain levers should come first: attacking contract-labor cost through retention, shifting appropriate care to lower-cost settings, and right-sizing obvious capacity excess generate savings without reducing access. The hardest lever — regionalizing or consolidating services that communities depend on — should come later, after the easier savings are captured and after the data exists to make and defend the decision. Leading with the most painful cuts before exhausting the painless savings squanders community trust and invites resistance that can derail the whole effort.
The data infrastructure should be built in parallel with the early levers, so that by the time the hard service decisions arrive, the system is measurable enough to make them well. A premature consolidation decided without utilization and outcome data is a guess; the same decision made after the data systems are in place is an analysis. This argues for a deliberate phasing — workforce and setting-of-care optimization first, data infrastructure built alongside, and service-configuration decisions taken last and on evidence — rather than an indiscriminate round of cuts driven by the current year's budget gap. The engineering mindset is as much about sequencing the transformation as about the final configuration it produces.
The Path Through Act 167
Act 167 is best read not as a verdict but as a mandate to engineer. It establishes that the status quo is unsustainable and that incremental budget fights cannot resolve a structural capacity mismatch. The constructive response is to treat Vermont's hospital network as a system to be deliberately re-configured — regionalizing where volume and safety demand it, right-sizing capacity to real demand, attacking contract-labor cost through retention, and shifting care to lower-cost settings where it is safe to do so — all subject to hard access and quality constraints and all enabled by the data infrastructure that makes the system measurable.
Done with engineering discipline and honest constraints, this preserves the most access and quality achievable within a budget the state can actually sustain. Done by avoidance — defending every service at every site until insolvency forces disorderly closures — it ends in the loss of far more. The choice Act 167 forces is between optimizing the system deliberately and watching it fail randomly. The tools of health-systems engineering, grounded in the economics of a rural, aging state and the empirical mandates of the Green Mountain Care Board, are how Vermont chooses the former.
It is worth naming what success would look like, because the engineering framing can sound coldly financial when the stakes are deeply human. A successfully optimized Vermont system is not one with the lowest possible cost; it is one that keeps essential and emergency services within a safe reach of every community, concentrates complex care where volume makes it safe, employs a stable permanent workforce instead of expensive transient labor, and operates within a budget that keeps premiums affordable for the Vermonters who pay them. That is a system engineered around access and quality as hard constraints and cost as the thing to be minimized between them — which is precisely the inversion of the failing status quo, where cost runs free and access erodes by accident. Act 167 is the mandate to make that inversion deliberate.

Hospital Services Optimization To Drive Vermont Health Care Reform and Meet Act 167 Requirements

######## impact-cost-shifting-rural-solvency | The Impact of Cost-Shifting on Rural Hospital Solvency
The conventional story of rural hospital finance rests on a comforting mechanism called cost-shifting: hospitals lose money on Medicare and Medicaid patients, the story goes, and make it up by charging private insurers more. It is intuitive, widely believed — and, according to the weight of modern evidence, largely wrong. This analysis examines the cost-shift theory, why the evidence refutes it, the crucial distinction between cost-shifting and cross-subsidization, and what the truth means for rural hospital solvency.
The Cost-Shift Theory
Cost-shifting is the idea that hospitals respond to underpayment by public programs by raising the prices they charge commercial insurers — a 'hydraulic' model in which squeezing one side of the balloon (low Medicare/Medicaid rates) makes the other side (commercial prices) bulge. It is the standard justification offered when hospitals defend high commercial prices: we have to charge private insurers more because the government underpays us. The theory implies that rural hospital solvency depends on having enough commercially-insured patients to subsidize the publicly-insured ones, and that a hospital with too few commercial patients is doomed.
Why the Evidence Refutes It
The problem is that rigorous evidence does not support dynamic cost-shifting as a major ongoing force. The Congressional Budget Office and prominent health economists (notably Austin Frakt) have found that the share of a provider's patients covered by Medicare and Medicaid is not associated with higher prices paid by commercial insurers — meaning hospitals do not measurably raise private prices to offset public underpayment (CBO; JAMA Forum). It has been nearly two decades since a rigorous study found evidence of substantial cost-shifting, and remarkably, the evidence runs the other way: when Medicare and Medicaid cut payments, hospitals tend to cut what they charge private insurers, not raise it (AJMC/Frakt).
The reason is that hospitals charge commercial insurers as much as their market power allows regardless of public rates — they are not leaving money on the table that they only collect when the government cuts them. A hospital with pricing power already extracts high commercial prices; a hospital without it cannot raise prices just because Medicare paid less. Commercial prices are driven by market power and negotiating leverage, not by public payment shortfalls. The 1980s-era studies that suggested cost-shifting reflected a different market, and the pattern has waned; today, commercial prices track consolidation and leverage, not the public payer mix (California Health Care Foundation, 2025).
The comforting story that hospitals make up Medicare/Medicaid shortfalls by charging private insurers more is, by the weight of modern evidence, a myth. Hospitals charge commercial payers what their market power allows regardless of public rates — and when public rates fall, they tend to cut private charges, not raise them.
Cost-Shifting vs. Cross-Subsidization
The crucial nuance — and the source of much confusion — is the distinction between cost-shifting and cross-subsidization, which are different things. Cost-shifting (largely debunked) is the dynamic claim that low public rates cause higher commercial prices. Cross-subsidization (real) is the static fact that the higher revenue hospitals earn from commercial patients enables them to provide services that lose money, including care for publicly-insured and uninsured patients. In other words, hospitals do use commercial profits to cover unprofitable services — that part is true — but they do not raise commercial prices in response to public underpayment; the high commercial prices exist independently, driven by market power, and happen to fund the losses.
This distinction matters enormously for rural hospitals. It means a rural hospital's problem is not that it 'can't shift costs' but that it lacks the commercial volume and market power to generate the cross-subsidizing surplus in the first place. A rural hospital serving a heavily Medicare/Medicaid population in a market with little commercial insurance simply does not have the high-margin commercial revenue that urban hospitals use to cover their losses — and it cannot manufacture that revenue by raising prices, because it lacks the leverage. The rural solvency problem is a cross-subsidy-deficit problem, not a cost-shift-failure problem.
Why the Myth Persists
If the evidence is clear, why does the cost-shift belief endure? Partly because it is intuitive — the hydraulic image of squeezing one side and another bulging is easy to grasp and feels true. Partly because it serves hospitals' interests: invoking cost-shifting justifies high commercial prices ('we have no choice — the government underpays us') and opposes public-rate changes, so the industry has every incentive to keep the story alive. And partly because there is a kernel of truth nearby — cross-subsidization is real — that gets conflated with the debunked dynamic claim, so people who correctly observe that commercial revenue funds losses wrongly infer that public underpayment causes high commercial prices. The myth survives because it is intuitive, useful to those who repeat it, and adjacent to a true fact.
This persistence has real costs. Policymakers who believe cost-shifting may resist reducing wasteful commercial prices for fear of 'forcing' hospitals to cut services, or may accept high commercial prices as a necessary subsidy — both based on a mechanism that does not operate as claimed. Disentangling the myth from the reality is therefore not an academic exercise; it changes what policies look sensible. If high commercial prices are driven by market power rather than public underpayment, the appropriate response is to address the market power (antitrust, price regulation, transparency), not to tolerate the prices as a necessary cross-subsidy — a very different policy posture than the cost-shift framing implies.
What It Means for Rural Solvency
Getting this right reframes rural hospital policy. If cost-shifting worked, the solution would be to ensure rural hospitals have enough commercial patients to shift costs onto — but since it does not work that way, the real issue is that rural hospitals lack the commercial-revenue base to cross-subsidize their unprofitable but essential services (the ER, obstetrics, care for the uninsured). This points the policy response not toward enabling cost-shifting but toward directly funding the services that cannot pay for themselves: enhanced public payment for essential rural services, Medicaid expansion to convert uninsured losses into paying patients, global budgets that guarantee revenue regardless of payer mix, and designations like Rural Emergency Hospitals that match payment to what rural communities actually use.
It also debunks a common hospital argument. When hospitals oppose Medicaid expansion or public-rate changes by invoking cost-shifting, the evidence suggests the mechanism they describe does not operate as claimed. The honest framing is that rural hospitals need adequate direct payment for the care they provide — not the ability to extract more from private insurers, which they largely cannot do anyway. Rural solvency is about whether the payment for the care rural hospitals actually deliver covers its cost, and the cross-subsidy model that sustains urban hospitals is structurally unavailable to most rural ones.
[{"Concept":"Cost-shifting (myth)","Detail":"Low public rates CAUSE higher commercial prices — not supported by evidence"},{"Concept":"Cross-subsidization (real)","Detail":"Commercial profit FUNDS unprofitable services — but prices set by market power, not public rates"},{"Concept":"Rural problem","Detail":"Lacks commercial volume/leverage to generate the cross-subsidizing surplus"},{"Concept":"Implication","Detail":"Fund essential services directly (expansion, global budgets, REH) — not enable cost-shifting"}]
Cost-shifting vs. cross-subsidization
What to Watch
Key signals: whether policy debates move past the cost-shift framing toward directly funding essential rural services; whether the evidence that commercial prices track market power (not public underpayment) strengthens the case for antitrust and price regulation rather than higher public rates as a giveaway to hospitals; whether Medicaid expansion and global-budget models address the real cross-subsidy deficit; and whether hospitals continue invoking cost-shifting in policy arguments despite the evidence. Clearing up this confusion is a precondition for sound rural hospital policy, because a solution built on a debunked mechanism cannot work.
Bottom Line
The cost-shift theory — that hospitals make up public-payer shortfalls by charging private insurers more — is largely a myth that modern evidence refutes: hospitals charge commercial payers what their market power allows regardless of public rates, and tend to cut, not raise, private charges when public rates fall. What is real is cross-subsidization: commercial profits (set by market power) fund unprofitable services. For rural hospitals, this distinction is decisive — their problem is not a failure to shift costs but a structural deficit of the commercial revenue and market power needed to cross-subsidize essential services in the first place. The policy implication is to fund rural hospitals' essential, unprofitable services directly — through Medicaid expansion, enhanced payment, and global budgets — rather than chasing a cost-shifting mechanism that does not operate as believed. Sound rural hospital policy starts with discarding the comforting myth and confronting the real problem: that the care rural hospitals must provide costs more than their payer mix pays for it.
Sources

The comforting story that hospitals offset Medicare/Medicaid underpayment by charging private insurers more — cost-shifting — is largely a myth; modern evidence (CBO, Frakt) shows commercial prices track market power, not public rates, and hospitals cut private charges when public rates fall. What's real is cross-subsidization. For rural hospitals the problem isn't failed cost-shifting but a deficit of commercial revenue to cross-subsidize essential services. This analysis untangles the two and the rural-solvency implications.
The Impact of Cost-Shifting on Rural Hospital Solvency

######## inflationary-scissors-rural-hospitals | The Inflationary Scissors: Rural Hospitals in Crisis
Rural hospitals are caught in a vise that economists call a 'scissors' — two blades closing on them at once. On one side, the cost of providing care rises relentlessly with inflation; on the other, government reimbursement, the dominant source of rural revenue, barely moves. When costs climb and payment stalls, the gap between them widens every year, and for hospitals running on razor-thin margins, that widening gap is an existential threat. This analysis examines the inflationary scissors, the fixed-payment problem, why rural hospitals are uniquely exposed, and what closing the gap would require.
The Scissors Effect
The scissors metaphor captures a specific economic trap: the cost of inputs (labor, drugs, supplies) rises with general inflation, while the price a hospital can charge for its services — dominated by government payers whose rates are set administratively — does not keep pace. The two trend lines diverge, and the space between them is the hospital's deepening loss. This is not cyclical; it is a structural mismatch between market-rate input costs and administratively-set output prices, and it compounds year after year.
The physician-payment data illustrates the blade most clearly. Medicare physician payment was cut 2.83% in 2025 — the fifth consecutive year of cuts — even as the Medicare Economic Index (the measure of practice-cost inflation) projected costs rising about 3.5% (AMA; AAFP). Over the longer arc, Medicare physician payment declined roughly 29% from 2001 to 2024 after adjusting for practice-cost inflation, while clinician costs are projected to keep rising more than 2% per year through 2033 (AMA; KFF). Payment falling while costs rise is the scissors in its purest form — and hospital payment, while structured differently, faces an analogous gap where Medicare updates lag the real cost of providing care.
The 'inflationary scissors': input costs (labor, drugs, supplies) rise with inflation while government payment — set administratively — barely moves. Medicare physician pay fell 2.83% in 2025 (5th straight cut) against ~3.5% cost inflation, and dropped 29% inflation-adjusted since 2001. The widening gap is the rural hospital's deepening loss.
The Fixed-Payment Problem
The heart of the scissors is that a large share of rural hospital revenue comes from payers whose rates do not respond to inflation in real time. Medicare and Medicaid — which dominate rural payer mix — set rates through administrative processes (annual updates, fee schedules) that have systematically lagged actual cost growth, especially during periods of high inflation. A hospital facing an 8% jump in labor and supply costs cannot simply raise its Medicare rates to match; it receives whatever update the formula produces, which has repeatedly fallen short. The hospital is, in effect, a price-taker on most of its revenue while being a price-payer on all of its costs — a position that guarantees margin erosion when inflation runs ahead of payment updates.
This asymmetry is why inflation is uniquely dangerous for hospitals reliant on public payers. A business that can pass cost increases through to customers weathers inflation; a hospital that cannot raise its dominant payers' rates absorbs the increase as a loss. The years of elevated inflation following the pandemic were therefore especially brutal for rural hospitals — input costs spiked while Medicare and Medicaid updates lagged, opening the scissors wide and driving the operating losses that pushed many toward insolvency.
Why Rural Hospitals Are Uniquely Exposed
Every hospital faces some version of the scissors, but rural hospitals are uniquely vulnerable for compounding reasons. Their payer mix is the most government-heavy, so the largest share of their revenue is subject to the lagging administrative updates. Their thin margins leave no cushion to absorb a widening gap — an urban system with diversified revenue and reserves can weather a bad year that bankrupts a rural hospital operating near zero. Their low volume means they cannot spread rising fixed costs across enough patients. And their limited commercial revenue denies them the higher-margin payer mix that helps urban hospitals offset public-payer shortfalls. The same hospital is maximally exposed to the cost blade (high fixed costs, labor-intensive, inflation-sensitive) and minimally protected from the payment blade (government-dominated, no leverage).
The Labor Blade Cuts Deepest
Within the cost blade, labor deserves special attention because it is the largest and most inflation-exposed component of a hospital's costs — and because of a phenomenon economists call Baumol's cost disease. Health care is labor-intensive and resists the productivity gains that let other industries offset wage growth: a nurse can only care for so many patients, and unlike manufacturing, you cannot automate most bedside care, so wages must rise to keep workers without a corresponding productivity increase to fund them. This makes hospital labor costs rise faster than general inflation in a way that is structurally hard to control, and labor is the dominant line item — meaning the cost blade is driven substantially by a wage dynamic the hospital cannot escape through efficiency.
For rural hospitals, the labor blade is sharpened further by the workforce shortage. They must pay premiums — travel-nurse rates, recruitment incentives, retention bonuses — to attract and keep scarce clinicians in markets few want to work in, so their labor costs rise even faster than the national average while their payment lags even further behind. The scissors, in other words, opens widest on its labor edge precisely where rural hospitals are weakest, which is why the nursing and workforce crises and the inflationary scissors are the same problem viewed from different angles: rising, hard-to-control labor costs colliding with stagnant payment.
Closing the Gap
Addressing the scissors requires acting on both blades. On the payment side, the most direct fix is reforming the administrative update formulas so that Medicare and Medicaid rates track actual cost inflation — for physicians, tying payment to the MEI rather than the political process that has produced five straight years of cuts; for hospitals, ensuring updates reflect real input-cost growth. Global budgets offer a different approach: by guaranteeing a hospital predictable revenue (with built-in trend factors), they can be structured to keep pace with costs in a way that volume-based fee-for-service does not, which is part of their appeal for rural hospitals. On the cost side, efficiency gains, shared services, and care-model changes can slow the rise of the cost blade, though they cannot reverse general inflation.
The fundamental point is that the scissors is a policy-created problem amenable to policy solutions. The cost blade is largely beyond any hospital's control (it is general inflation), but the payment blade is a choice — the lagging updates are the product of formulas and political decisions that could be changed. A payment system that updated rates to match cost inflation would close the scissors; the current system, which lets payment fall behind, opens it. Rural hospital survival depends substantially on whether policymakers fix the payment blade they control.
[{"Blade":"Cost (rising)","Detail":"Labor, drugs, supplies rise with inflation; clinician costs +2%/yr through 2033"},{"Blade":"Payment (lagging)","Detail":"Medicare physician pay -2.83% (2025), 5th straight cut; -29% since 2001 inflation-adjusted"},{"Element":"Rural exposure","Detail":"Government-heavy payer mix + thin margins + low volume + no commercial cushion"},{"Element":"The fix","Detail":"Update formulas to track cost inflation; global budgets with trend factors"}]
The inflationary scissors
What to Watch
Key signals: whether Congress reforms the Medicare physician payment formula to tie updates to inflation (ending the cycle of cuts), and whether hospital payment updates are similarly aligned with cost growth; whether inflation moderates, narrowing the gap, or persists, widening it; whether global-budget models (with their trend factors) spread as a way to insulate rural hospitals from the scissors; and whether the cumulative effect of years of lagging payment forces a structural reckoning with how administratively-set rates handle inflation. The scissors will keep closing on rural hospitals until the payment blade — the one policymakers control — is reformed to track the cost blade they do not.
Bottom Line
The inflationary scissors is the structural trap squeezing rural hospitals: input costs rise relentlessly with inflation while administratively-set government payment lags, opening a widening gap that hospitals on razor-thin margins cannot absorb. The physician-payment data shows the blade starkly — five straight years of Medicare cuts against rising cost inflation, a 29% inflation-adjusted decline since 2001 — and hospital payment faces an analogous lag. Rural hospitals are uniquely exposed because their payer mix is most government-dominated, their margins thinnest, and their commercial cushion smallest, leaving them maximally hit by the cost blade and minimally protected from the payment blade. The crucial insight is that the payment blade is a policy choice: the lagging updates are not inevitable but the product of formulas that could be reformed to track cost inflation. Until they are — or until global budgets with proper trend factors spread — the scissors will keep closing on the rural hospitals least able to withstand it, and the operating losses that follow will keep driving the closures that hollow out rural health care.

The Inflationary Scissors: Rural Hospitals in Crisis

######## masterclass-ai-governance | Masterclass: Implementing AI Governance in Clinical Settings
Most health systems are deploying clinical AI faster than they are governing it. The gap is not for lack of principles — there are no shortage of frameworks — but for lack of operational structures: the committees, policies, and protocols that turn 'be responsible with AI' into something a hospital actually does. This is a practical guide to standing up clinical AI governance: who must be at the table, the procurement discipline that catches problems before deployment, the monitoring that catches them after, and the emergency protocol for when an algorithm goes wrong.
Why Governance Fails
Clinical AI governance fails most often for a predictable reason: it is treated as an IT problem and run by IT. Technology staff can evaluate whether a tool integrates and performs technically, but they cannot judge whether its outputs are clinically appropriate, whether its failure modes are dangerous in practice, or whether it discriminates against a subgroup — and they cannot, by themselves, change clinician behavior. Governance that lives in the IT department produces tools that are technically sound and clinically ungoverned. The first principle of effective AI governance is therefore that it must be a clinical and institutional function, not a technical one — owned by people with the authority and the clinical judgment to decide what gets deployed and how it is used.
Clinical AI governance fails when it's run by IT. Technologists can judge whether a tool works technically; they cannot judge whether its outputs are clinically safe, whether it discriminates, or change how clinicians use it. Effective AI governance is a clinical and institutional function, not a technical one.
The Stakeholder Table
Effective governance starts with the right people. A Clinical AI Safety Committee should not proceed without, at minimum: a Chief Medical Information Officer or equivalent clinical-informatics leader to bridge medicine and technology; practicing clinical champions from the specialties where AI is deployed, because the people who will use the tool must judge it; legal and compliance, given the liability and regulatory stakes (including the Section 1557 anti-discrimination duty); a health-equity voice to scrutinize subgroup performance; data-science capability to interrogate how a model actually works; and patient representation where appropriate. The composition is the message: governance that includes only technologists and administrators will miss exactly the clinical, equity, and legal risks that matter most.
The committee's authority matters as much as its composition. It needs a real mandate — the power to approve, reject, or halt AI deployments — not an advisory role that operational pressure can override. A governance body that can be bypassed when a vendor's tool is attractive or a deadline looms is not governance; it is theater. The committee must own the decision of what AI the institution uses and under what conditions.
Procurement Discipline: The Model Card
The highest-leverage governance happens before deployment, at procurement, because it is far easier to refuse a flawed tool than to unwind one in clinical use. The key instrument is a 'model card' requirement: before buying an AI tool, the institution should require the vendor to disclose, in standardized form, what the model does, what data it was trained on, how it performs across demographic and clinical subgroups, its known limitations and failure modes, and its validation evidence. A vendor unwilling or unable to provide this is itself a red flag. The model card directly addresses the vendor-accountability gap that otherwise leaves the institution legally responsible for a tool it cannot see inside — it forces the disclosure that makes informed, defensible deployment possible.
Procurement discipline also means demanding subgroup performance data specifically, not just overall accuracy, because a tool can be accurate on average and dangerously wrong for a subpopulation — and under Section 1557 the institution has an affirmative duty to identify and mitigate exactly that. Building these requirements into purchasing contracts pushes accountability toward the vendor who controls the model, and gives the institution the evidence it needs to meet its own legal and clinical obligations. Procurement is where leverage over vendors is greatest, because the alternative — the institution's business — is what the vendor wants.
Post-Deployment Monitoring
Governance does not end at deployment, because AI models drift: their performance changes as patient populations, clinical practice, and data shift over time, so a tool validated at launch can degrade silently. Effective governance therefore requires ongoing monitoring — tracking the tool's real-world performance, watching for the specific failure modes identified in procurement, and auditing outputs against defined accuracy thresholds, all stratified by subgroup to catch emerging bias. This is the difference between treating AI as a static product (validate once, trust forever) and as a living system (validate continuously, because it changes). Many institutions do the upfront validation and neglect the ongoing monitoring, which is how a tool that was safe at launch becomes unsafe in use without anyone noticing.
The Red Button Protocol
Finally, governance requires an emergency stop — a 'red button' protocol for rapidly suspending an algorithm that is causing or threatening harm. When monitoring detects that a tool is malfunctioning, drifting dangerously, or producing harmful outputs, the institution needs a pre-defined, fast pathway to pull it from clinical use, communicate the suspension to affected clinicians, and revert to non-AI workflows — without the delay of assembling a committee to debate it in the moment. The red button is the operational embodiment of the principle that AI is an assistant under supervision: the institution retains the ability to switch it off. A governance program without a tested suspension protocol is like a building without a fire alarm — fine until the moment it is not.
[{"Component":"Stakeholder table","Detail":"CMIO + clinical champions + legal + equity + data science — with real authority"},{"Component":"Procurement (model card)","Detail":"Require vendor disclosure of training data, subgroup performance, limitations"},{"Component":"Post-deployment monitoring","Detail":"Continuous, subgroup-stratified performance auditing (models drift)"},{"Component":"Red button protocol","Detail":"Pre-defined rapid suspension pathway for a malfunctioning algorithm"}]
Clinical AI governance: the operational structure
Governance for the Resource-Constrained
The operational structures described here are achievable for a large academic medical center with informatics staff, a CMIO, and a compliance department. They are far harder for a small or rural hospital that may have no dedicated IT security staff, let alone a data-science team to interrogate a model's subgroup performance — yet these are exactly the institutions deploying vendor AI tools, often with the least capacity to govern them. This is the governance equity problem: sophisticated oversight concentrates where resources are, leaving under-resourced institutions to deploy ungoverned AI by default, importing risk they cannot see.
The realistic answer for resource-constrained institutions is shared and pooled governance. A small hospital cannot stand up its own Clinical AI Safety Committee with deep data-science capability, but a hospital association, a regional collaborative, or a state body could provide shared governance services — vetting tools, demanding model cards, and monitoring performance on behalf of many small members, the same shared-services logic that applies to HIE infrastructure. Just as no rural hospital should build its own data-exchange stack, none should be expected to build sophisticated AI governance alone. Pooling the capability is how the institutions with the least resources avoid being the ones deploying the least-governed AI — and it is the missing piece in most current discussions of clinical-AI governance, which implicitly assume a large-institution context that most of American health care does not match.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether standardized model-card and disclosure norms emerge so institutions are not each negotiating transparency from scratch; whether Section 1557 enforcement pushes health systems to build the audit programs the rule requires; whether governance maturity keeps pace with the move of generative AI into higher-stakes clinical tasks; and whether smaller and rural institutions — which lack the data-science capacity to run sophisticated governance — get shared resources or pooled governance models so they are not left deploying ungoverned AI. The principles of AI governance are settled; the operational capacity to execute them, especially outside large academic systems, is the gap to close.
Bottom Line
Clinical AI governance is an operational discipline, not a statement of principles, and it fails most often because institutions treat it as an IT function rather than a clinical and institutional one. Doing it right means a Clinical AI Safety Committee with the right people (clinical, legal, equity, data science) and real authority; procurement discipline anchored in model-card disclosure that forces vendor transparency and addresses the accountability gap; continuous, subgroup-stratified post-deployment monitoring because models drift; and a tested red-button protocol to suspend a tool that turns dangerous. These structures turn the abstract duty to use AI responsibly into something a hospital actually does. As clinical AI proliferates — much of it outside FDA regulation and into ever-higher-stakes tasks — the institutions that build this operational governance ahead of need will capture AI's benefits while containing its risks; those that deploy first and govern later are accumulating a liability that a single bad output can crystallize.
Sources

The Hastings Center for Bioethics — AI in Healthcare (governance)
