######## maternal-health-mandate-rural-ed-requirements-2026 | The New Maternal Health Mandates: What Rural Emergency Departments Must Have on Hand by July 2026
As rural hospitals shed obstetric units, a dangerous gap has opened: pregnant patients in crisis increasingly arrive at emergency departments that have no obstetric capability and no recent practice managing a birth or a hemorrhage. The policy response is to make every ED — even those without a labor-and-delivery unit — ready for obstetric emergencies. This analysis examines the readiness gap, the framework for closing it, what readiness concretely requires, and why a 'screen-stabilize-transfer' standard is the realistic floor for rural maternal safety.
The Gap: EDs That Aren't Ready
The collapse of rural obstetrics — more than half of rural hospitals no longer offer obstetric care — means a growing share of rural pregnant patients in emergencies present to a general ED rather than a labor-and-delivery unit. The problem is that these EDs are largely unprepared. In a recent assessment of rural critical access hospitals without obstetric units, none of the responding hospitals met the criteria for comprehensive emergency obstetric care, and only one met the criteria for even basic emergency obstetric care (PMC). That is a stark finding: the facilities now most likely to receive a rural obstetric emergency are the least equipped to handle one.
This is the downstream consequence of the maternity-desert crisis playing out in real time. When the nearest delivery unit is an hour away, an obstetric emergency — a postpartum hemorrhage, eclampsia, a precipitous delivery — does not wait for the drive; it arrives at whatever ED is closest. An ED that has not stocked the right blood, drugs, and equipment, and whose staff have not drilled obstetric emergencies, is a place where a survivable complication can become a fatal one. The readiness gap is, in effect, a maternal-mortality risk factor created by the closures.
In one assessment of rural critical-access hospitals without obstetric units, NONE met the bar for comprehensive emergency obstetric care and only one met the basic bar. As obstetric units close, the EDs most likely to catch a rural obstetric emergency are the least prepared for it.
The Readiness Framework
The response is led by the Alliance for Innovation on Maternal Health (AIM), the ACOG-implemented program behind the maternal safety bundles, which has developed obstetric-emergency-readiness resources specifically for non-obstetric, lower-resource, and rural facilities. AIM organizes its patient-safety bundles into four domains — Readiness, Recognition and Prevention, Response, and Reporting/Systems Learning — and the readiness work for non-OB settings adapts that structure to a facility whose goal is not to deliver babies routinely but to safely recognize, stabilize, and transfer an obstetric emergency (AIM/ACOG).
The framing matters. The goal for a rural ED without an L&D unit is not to become a delivery center — that ship sailed with the unit's closure — but to be able to do three things reliably: recognize an obstetric emergency, stabilize the patient (control hemorrhage, manage blood pressure, deliver if delivery is imminent), and transfer safely to a facility with comprehensive obstetric care. This 'basic emergency obstetric care' standard is the realistic floor, and the readiness assessments show most rural EDs are not yet meeting even that.
The EMTALA Dimension
Readiness is not only a clinical best practice; it carries legal weight through EMTALA, the federal law requiring any ED that participates in Medicare to screen and stabilize any patient with an emergency medical condition — including active labor — regardless of the facility's specialty capabilities. An ED without an obstetric unit cannot wave off a laboring patient; it is legally obligated to provide a medical screening exam and stabilizing treatment within its capacity before any transfer. That makes obstetric-emergency readiness a compliance issue as well as a safety one: a facility that has not equipped or trained its ED to stabilize an obstetric emergency is exposed not just to a bad outcome but to an EMTALA violation.
This legal floor cuts against the temptation rural hospitals might feel to simply declare obstetrics 'not our service' after closing the unit. EMTALA does not permit that for emergencies — the obligation to screen and stabilize follows from operating an ED at all, not from running a labor-and-delivery unit. The practical implication is that closing an obstetric unit does not relieve a hospital of obstetric-emergency responsibility; it heightens the need for ED readiness, because the same emergencies will still arrive and the legal duty to handle them safely remains.
What Readiness Concretely Requires
Obstetric-emergency readiness is specific and checkable, which is what makes it actionable. It includes a stocked obstetric-emergency cart aligned with the ACOG/AIM hemorrhage bundle — uterotonic medications to stop bleeding, equipment for the procedures that control hemorrhage, and neonatal resuscitation equipment. It requires immediate access to blood: ACOG recommends a minimum of 4 units of O-negative packed red blood cells with the ability to obtain 6 for obstetric emergency preparedness — a real challenge for small rural hospitals with limited blood banking. It requires at least one nurse per shift trained in obstetric and neonatal emergency response (such as ALSO or NRP certification). And it requires a documented transfer agreement with a comprehensive obstetric facility, with a defined transport pathway and time.
[{"Element":"Hemorrhage cart","Detail":"Uterotonics + hemorrhage-control equipment (ACOG/AIM bundle)"},{"Element":"Blood access","Detail":"≥4 units O-neg PRBCs, ability to obtain 6 (ACOG)"},{"Element":"Trained staff","Detail":"≥1 nurse/shift with OB/neonatal emergency training (ALSO/NRP)"},{"Element":"Transfer agreement","Detail":"Documented pathway to comprehensive OB care within defined time"}]
Obstetric-emergency readiness for a non-OB ED
The Implementation Challenge
Closing the readiness gap is harder than publishing a checklist. The same financial fragility that closed the obstetric unit constrains a rural hospital's ability to maintain blood inventory, keep emergency drugs stocked and unexpired, and fund the training and drills that keep skills current for emergencies that, by definition, are infrequent. Maintaining proficiency for a rare event is a genuine challenge — a nurse who delivers a baby or manages a hemorrhage once a year cannot rely on routine to stay sharp, which is why simulation drills and standardized protocols (the bundle approach) matter so much: they substitute structure for the repetition that low volume cannot provide.
There is also a coordination dimension. Readiness is not just a single hospital's responsibility but a regional system's: the stabilizing ED, the transport service, and the receiving comprehensive facility have to function as one chain, and a weak link anywhere — no available transport, no receiving bed — breaks the whole sequence. Effective programs therefore build regional obstetric-emergency systems, not just hospital-level checklists, recognizing that a rural obstetric emergency is inherently a transfer scenario.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether AIM's obstetric-emergency-readiness resources translate into measurable improvement in the share of rural EDs meeting basic emergency obstetric care standards; whether funding (federal grants, state programs) reaches rural hospitals to stock carts, maintain blood access, and fund training; whether regional obstetric-emergency systems develop so stabilize-and-transfer actually works end to end; and whether readiness requirements become a formal expectation (through accreditation or regulation) rather than a voluntary best practice. The trajectory of obstetric-unit closures shows no sign of reversing, so ED readiness is not a temporary fix but the new permanent floor for rural maternal safety.
Bottom Line
As rural obstetric units close, emergency-department readiness for obstetric emergencies becomes the last line of maternal safety — and the evidence shows most rural EDs are not yet ready, with assessments finding almost none meeting even basic emergency obstetric care standards. The framework to fix it exists: AIM's readiness bundles adapted for non-OB settings, with concrete requirements — a stocked hemorrhage cart, adequate O-negative blood, OB-trained staff, and a working transfer agreement. The challenge is implementation under financial strain and the difficulty of maintaining proficiency for rare events, which makes drills, standardized protocols, and regional emergency systems essential. This is not a substitute for the obstetric units that have been lost; it is the realistic floor for keeping rural mothers alive when an emergency arrives at a hospital no longer built to handle it. The uncomfortable truth is that ED obstetric readiness is a second-best solution to a first-order failure — the loss of rural maternity care — and it exists because the better answer (keeping obstetric units open) has proven economically impossible in much of rural America. Policymakers should pursue both: shore up the units that remain while ensuring every ED that will inevitably catch the overflow is prepared to stabilize and transfer safely. Treating ED readiness as a reason to accept further obstetric-unit closures, rather than as a backstop for closures that could not be prevented, would be exactly the wrong lesson to draw from it.

######## medicaid-dsh-rural-hospital-fiscal-collapse-2026 | Medicaid DSH Allotment Reductions and the Rural Hospital Fiscal Collapse: A State-by-State Impact Analysis for FY2026–2028
Editor's note (2026): This brief reflects the current statutory status of Medicaid Disproportionate Share Hospital (DSH) allotment reductions. The Affordable Care Act scheduled large annual DSH cuts on the theory that expanded coverage would reduce uncompensated care, but Congress has repeatedly delayed them — and, most recently, eliminated the near-term reductions. The figures below are drawn from the Congressional Research Service, the Congressional Budget Office, the American Hospital Association, and related public sources. Earlier drafts that stated the FY2026 cuts were 'legally in effect' were incorrect and have been corrected.
What DSH Is and Why It Matters
Medicaid DSH payments are supplemental funds that flow to hospitals serving a disproportionate share of Medicaid and uninsured patients. For safety-net and rural hospitals, DSH is not a marginal subsidy; it is a structural pillar of solvency, partially offsetting the losses these facilities book on low-reimbursement and uncompensated care. Because rural and safety-net hospitals operate on the thinnest margins — many already losing money on operations — a reduction in DSH falls directly on the institutions least able to absorb it, which is why the scheduled cuts have been treated as a potential trigger for closures.
The scheduled reductions are severe in aggregate. The ACA-era schedule called for DSH allotment cuts on the order of $8 billion per year, and recent analyses framed the combined near-term exposure as roughly $24 billion across multiple fiscal years. For a single rural hospital, the loss of DSH support can be the difference between a survivable loss and an unsurvivable one — which is precisely why the politics around these cuts have been so persistent and bipartisan.
The Cliff That Keeps Being Postponed
The defining feature of the DSH cuts is that they are scheduled, severe, and repeatedly postponed. For more than a decade, each looming reduction has prompted bipartisan intervention to delay it, because the consequences for safety-net and rural hospitals are politically intolerable. The pattern has continued: in the most recent action, Congress eliminated the Medicaid DSH reductions for FY2026 and FY2027, removing the near-term cliff that earlier drafts of this brief had assumed was in force. What remains in statute is the FY2028 reduction of roughly $8 billion, scheduled to take effect on October 1, 2027, unless Congress again acts to delay or eliminate it.
This history is the single most important fact for hospital financial planning, and it cuts two ways. On one hand, the cuts have never actually taken effect, so treating them as an imminent certainty has repeatedly proven wrong. On the other hand, the reductions remain in statute and require affirmative congressional action to prevent, so they cannot be ignored either. The rational posture is to plan for a structural risk that is real but recurrently deferred — neither panicking as though the cliff is certain nor assuming the perpetual reprieve is guaranteed.
Current status (2026): Congress ELIMINATED the Medicaid DSH allotment reductions for FY2026 and FY2027. Only the FY2028 cut of ~$8 billion remains in statute, set to take effect October 1, 2027 unless Congress acts again. The ACA scheduled ~$8B/year cuts (recently framed as ~$24B of near-term exposure), but they have been delayed for over a decade — a scheduled, severe, repeatedly-postponed cliff, not an active cut.
Why the Uncertainty Itself Is Costly
Even though the cuts have never taken effect, the perpetual uncertainty imposes a real cost on the hospitals that depend on DSH. A facility cannot confidently make multi-year commitments — to service lines, to capital projects, to staffing — when a significant share of its funding is subject to a statutory cliff that may or may not be delayed again at the last minute. Lenders and bond-rating agencies factor this instability into a hospital's cost of capital, and boards hesitate to invest against a funding stream that requires an act of Congress to preserve each cycle. The scheduled-then-delayed pattern, in other words, taxes rural hospitals through uncertainty even in the years the cut does not land.
This mirrors a pattern visible across rural health finance, where short-term extensions of critical funding — DSH, telehealth flexibilities, various rural payment add-ons — keep essential programs alive but in a state of chronic precariousness. The cumulative effect of governing rural health on a series of expiring cliffs is to make long-term planning nearly impossible for exactly the institutions that most need stability. The durable fix for DSH, as for those other programs, is not another last-minute delay but a permanent resolution that lets rural hospitals plan against a known funding base — replacing the recurring brinkmanship with the predictability that sound financial management requires.
Why the Cuts Were Scheduled — and Why the Logic Failed
The original rationale was coherent on paper: the ACA expanded coverage, which was expected to shrink the uninsured population and therefore the uncompensated-care burden that DSH exists to offset, so DSH could be scaled back. The logic failed in practice for several reasons. Coverage expansion was uneven — states that did not expand Medicaid retained large uninsured populations — so the uncompensated-care burden DSH offsets never fell as uniformly as assumed. And even in expansion states, rural and safety-net hospitals continued to serve high shares of low-reimbursement patients, leaving them dependent on DSH regardless of coverage gains.
This mismatch between the policy's assumption and the on-the-ground reality is why Congress keeps stepping in. Cutting DSH on the theory that uncompensated care has fallen runs into the fact that, for the specific hospitals DSH supports, the financial pressure has not eased — and in many rural markets has worsened. The repeated delays are, in effect, Congress acknowledging that the predicate for the cuts has not materialized. Until DSH is reformed to match actual hospital need rather than an outdated coverage assumption, the cycle of scheduled-cut-then-delay is likely to continue.
The Interaction With Other Coverage Pressures
DSH cannot be analyzed in isolation, because it operates alongside other forces that move the uncompensated-care burden it offsets. Changes to Medicaid eligibility, enrollment, and financing — including coverage losses from eligibility redeterminations and broader Medicaid policy shifts — directly affect how many uninsured and underinsured patients hospitals must absorb. When coverage contracts, uncompensated care rises, and the case for cutting DSH weakens precisely as hospitals' need for it grows. The DSH question is therefore entangled with the entire trajectory of Medicaid coverage: cuts scheduled on an assumption of expanding coverage become especially dangerous in any period when coverage is instead contracting.
For rural hospitals, this interaction is the real risk to watch. A DSH reduction that lands in the same window as coverage losses would be a compounding blow — less supplemental funding at the very moment uncompensated care climbs — and it is that combination, more than the DSH cut alone, that could push fragile rural facilities over the edge. This is why hospital advocates frame DSH not as a standalone line item but as one element of a larger Medicaid-financing picture, and why the prudent rural CFO models DSH exposure together with coverage and enrollment trends rather than treating the scheduled cut as an isolated event. The danger is in the alignment of pressures, and planning has to account for the whole field of force, not just the one statutory cliff.
What Rural Hospitals Should Actually Do
Given a real-but-deferred risk, the prudent strategy is preparation without panic. Rural and safety-net hospitals should model their specific DSH exposure — what share of their revenue DSH represents and what a reduction would do to their margin — so they understand their vulnerability concretely rather than abstractly. They should engage in the bipartisan advocacy that has repeatedly secured delays, since that political process is the main reason the cuts have not landed. And they should pursue the broader structural reforms — payment redesign, cost reduction, regionalization where appropriate — that reduce dependence on any single fragile funding stream.
The deeper lesson is that DSH dependence is itself a symptom. A rural hospital whose solvency hinges on a supplemental payment that Congress must repeatedly rescue is financially fragile regardless of whether this particular cut takes effect. The durable answer is not perpetual reliance on the next delay but structural reform that makes rural hospitals viable on a sustainable payment base. DSH cuts, scheduled and postponed in an endless cycle, are best understood as a recurring warning about that underlying fragility — one the FY2026/2027 elimination has quieted for now, but the FY2028 schedule keeps alive.

Medicaid DSH Allotment Reductions and the Rural Hospital Fiscal Collapse: A State-by-State Impact Analysis for FY2026–2028

######## nursing-workforce-crisis-economics | The Nursing Workforce Crisis: Retention Economics vs. Agency Spend
The nursing workforce crisis presents hospital operations leaders with a stark financial choice that shapes their entire labor strategy: invest in retaining permanent staff, or keep paying agency and travel premiums to fill the gaps. After the pandemic permanently reset the economics of nursing labor, this retention-versus-agency-spend decision has become one of the most consequential in hospital operations. This analysis examines the post-pandemic labor cost reset, the retention-versus-agency tradeoff, the operational levers that reduce agency dependence, and why the math favors retention.
The Permanent Cost Reset
The pandemic did not just spike nursing labor costs temporarily — it permanently reset the cost basis of care delivery. Nurses learned their market power during the crisis, agency and travel staffing became normalized as a structural part of the labor market rather than an emergency measure, and the baseline expectations for nursing compensation, flexibility, and working conditions shifted upward and did not return to 2019 levels even as the acute travel-rate bubble deflated. Operations leaders are therefore managing a labor environment that is structurally more expensive and more fluid than before, where reliance on external agency labor is a standing temptation and a standing cost rather than a one-time emergency expense.
This reset frames the central operational challenge: in a permanently tighter, more expensive labor market, how a hospital sources its nursing labor — permanent staff versus agency/travel — has become a major determinant of its cost structure. A hospital that defaults to agency labor to cover every gap operates at a structurally higher cost; one that invests in stabilizing its permanent workforce can operate leaner. The choice is no longer a marginal HR matter but a core financial strategy.
The pandemic permanently reset nursing labor economics — nurses learned their market power, agency staffing normalized, and baseline expectations rose and stayed up. The central operational question is now: invest in retaining permanent staff, or keep paying agency premiums to fill the gaps? That choice shapes a hospital's whole cost structure.
Retention vs. Agency Spend
The core tradeoff is straightforward to state and decisive in its implications: agency and travel labor cost substantially more per hour than permanent staff (once agency margins, travel, and premiums are counted), so every shift covered by agency rather than permanent staff is a more expensive shift. A hospital heavily reliant on agency labor bleeds margin on every such shift, while a hospital with a stable permanent workforce covers the same shifts at lower cost. The financial logic therefore strongly favors retention — reducing agency dependence by keeping permanent staff — because the cost difference compounds across thousands of shifts into millions of dollars.
The data on retention investment makes the case concrete. With turnover costing roughly $60,000 per nurse and agency labor costing a large premium over permanent staff, retention investments that prevent departures and reduce agency reliance return multiples of their cost. Some systems investing in 'internal agencies' or gig-work platforms for their own staff — giving permanent nurses the flexibility that drew them to travel work while keeping them in-house — have reported meaningful reductions in total labor spend, capturing the flexibility nurses want without paying the external-agency premium. The operational insight is that the flexibility nurses sought through travel work can often be provided internally at far lower cost than external agency labor.
The Internal-Agency Innovation
One operational innovation deserves emphasis because it directly captures the value that external agencies extract: the internal agency or internal float pool. The logic is elegant. Nurses left permanent jobs for travel and agency work largely to gain flexibility and premium pay — but the external agency that placed them captured a large margin in the process. An internal agency replicates the flexibility (letting a hospital's own nurses pick up extra shifts, work flexible schedules, or float across units at premium internal rates) while keeping both the nurse and the margin in-house. The hospital pays its own nurses a premium for flexibility instead of paying an external agency a larger premium plus margin, capturing the difference.
This reframes the agency problem as partly a value-capture failure: hospitals were paying external agencies for flexibility their own nurses wanted, when they could have offered that flexibility directly and kept the margin. Systems that have built internal agencies and gig-style internal platforms report meaningful reductions in total labor spend precisely by recapturing this value. The lesson is that much agency spend is not buying something a hospital cannot provide itself — it is buying flexibility the hospital could offer internally at lower cost, and the internal-agency model is the operational vehicle for capturing that value rather than ceding it to staffing firms.
The Operational Levers
Reducing agency dependence is an operational discipline with specific levers. Internal float pools and internal agencies let a hospital provide flexibility and surge capacity using its own staff rather than external agencies, capturing the premium that would otherwise go to a staffing firm. Self-scheduling and flexibility programs address the top driver of nurse satisfaction and reduce the appeal of leaving for travel work. Predictive staffing and analytics help match staffing to demand, reducing both costly over-staffing and the gaps that force agency fills. And retention investment — addressing burnout, workload, and career growth — keeps the permanent staff that agency labor would otherwise replace. Each lever reduces the agency dependence that inflates the cost structure.
Why the Math Favors Retention
Pulling the analysis together yields a clear operational conclusion: the math favors retention and internal flexibility over external agency reliance. Agency labor is structurally more expensive per shift, turnover is costly, and the flexibility that drives nurses to agencies can often be provided internally at lower cost — so the hospital that invests in retention and internal flexibility operates at a lower cost basis than the one that defaults to agency labor. The historical pattern of treating agency spend as an unavoidable cost while underinvesting in retention is, on the numbers, backwards: the agency spend is largely a consequence of failing to retain and failing to provide internal flexibility, both of which are addressable. The operations leader who reframes agency spend as a controllable consequence of retention and flexibility failures, rather than an inevitable cost, can substantially reduce a hospital's labor cost structure.
[{"Element":"Cost reset","Detail":"Pandemic permanently raised nursing labor cost basis; agency normalized"},{"Element":"Per-shift","Detail":"Agency/travel costs substantially more than permanent staff"},{"Element":"Turnover","Detail":"~$60,000 per departure feeds agency dependence"},{"Element":"Levers","Detail":"Internal float pools/agencies, self-scheduling, predictive staffing, retention"}]
Retention vs. agency spend
What to Watch
Key signals: whether hospitals shift from external agency reliance toward internal flexibility models (internal agencies, float pools, self-scheduling) that capture the agency premium; whether retention investment grows as operations leaders recognize agency spend as a controllable consequence of retention failure; whether the post-pandemic labor cost reset stabilizes or continues rising; and whether new care models (virtual nursing, team-based care) reduce the nursing demand that drives both agency spend and turnover. The retention-versus-agency balance is a barometer of operational maturity in managing the permanently tighter nursing labor market.
Bottom Line
The nursing workforce crisis confronts hospital operations leaders with a defining financial choice — invest in retaining permanent staff, or keep paying agency premiums to fill the gaps — in a labor market the pandemic permanently reset to be tighter, more expensive, and more fluid. The math favors retention: agency labor costs substantially more per shift, turnover is expensive, and the flexibility that drives nurses to external agencies can often be provided internally at far lower cost through internal float pools, self-scheduling, and internal agency models. The historical treatment of agency spend as an unavoidable cost is, on the numbers, backwards — it is largely a controllable consequence of failing to retain and failing to provide internal flexibility, both addressable. The operations leaders who reframe agency spend accordingly and invest in retention and internal flexibility will operate at a structurally lower cost basis than those who default to external agency labor, making this one of the highest-leverage decisions in hospital operations in the post-pandemic labor environment. The broader operational truth is that labor is the largest and fastest-growing cost in most hospitals, so how a hospital sources and retains its nursing workforce is not a peripheral HR concern but a central determinant of its financial health — and the organizations that treat workforce strategy with the same rigor they bring to revenue cycle or supply chain will find, in retention and internal flexibility, one of the few remaining large, controllable cost levers available to them.

######## policyAnalysis-clin-001 | Hospital-at-Home Programs: CMS Acute Hospital Care at Home Waiver Outcomes
Hospital-at-Home (HaH) delivers acute, inpatient-level care — daily physician oversight, nursing visits, IV medications, diagnostics, and continuous monitoring — in a patient's residence instead of a hospital bed. Once a fringe experiment, it became a mainstream care model under a COVID-era Medicare waiver, and in early 2026 Congress committed to it through the end of the decade. This analysis reviews the evidence on outcomes and cost, the operating model and its guardrails, the policy timeline that nearly ended the program in 2025, and the equity questions that determine who actually benefits.
From a 17-Patient Pilot to a National Model
Hospital-at-Home is not a pandemic invention — it is a thirty-year-old idea that the pandemic finally made payable. The model was formalized at Johns Hopkins in 1995, when geriatrician Bruce Leff and colleagues ran a small pilot — initially just 17 patients — to treat acutely ill older adults who needed hospital-level care but risked deterioration (delirium, deconditioning, infection) in a conventional ward. The pilot concluded the approach was safe, feasible, highly satisfactory, and cost-effective for selected patients (Knowable Magazine, 2025).
For the next twenty-five years the idea stalled on a single obstacle: Medicare and most private insurers did not pay for home hospitalization, so fewer than 50 programs existed nationally. COVID-19 broke the logjam. On November 25, 2020, under the broader 'Hospital Without Walls' initiative and Section 1135 emergency waiver authority, CMS launched the Acute Hospital Care at Home (AHCAH) initiative, which let approved hospitals bill Medicare for inpatient-level care delivered at home (AHA, 2020). With payment finally available, the number of programs grew from fewer than 50 before the pandemic to more than 400 approved hospitals today — one of the fastest delivery-model expansions in recent memory.
What the Evidence Shows
The clinical case for Hospital-at-Home is unusually strong for a delivery-model innovation. CMS's own 2024 evaluation of the Acute Hospital Care at Home (AHCAH) initiative found that mortality was lower for home patients across all of the top 25 MS-DRGs studied, and the difference was statistically significant for 11 of them (CMS, 2024). The agency also found that Medicaid enrollees treated at home experienced similar or better outcomes than those in brick-and-mortar hospitals.
That federal finding rests on two decades of prior research. The foundational Johns Hopkins model developed by Bruce Leff documented roughly 38% lower cost of care alongside lower complication rates, higher patient and caregiver satisfaction, and dramatically lower 30-day readmissions — on the order of 7% versus 23% for matched inpatients in early studies (AHRQ PSNet). Subsequent systematic reviews in heart failure and other conditions have generally reproduced the pattern: equal or better safety, shorter effective length of stay, fewer hospital-acquired infections and episodes of delirium, and improved quality of life at 6 and 12 months.
The convergence is what makes Hospital-at-Home credible: an independent line of academic trials and CMS's own administrative-data evaluation reach the same conclusion — for carefully selected patients, home-based acute care is at least as safe as the ward and materially cheaper.
Why Home Care Costs Less
The savings are structural, not the result of skimping on care. A hospital bed carries enormous fixed overhead — facility depreciation, 24/7 staffing of an entire unit, environmental services — that is allocated across every admission. Shifting an appropriate patient home removes most of that overhead while preserving the clinical inputs that actually drive recovery. Home patients also avoid two expensive iatrogenic risks of hospitalization: hospital-acquired infections and the functional and cognitive decline (especially delirium in older adults) that frequently follows days in an unfamiliar, immobilizing ward environment. Fewer complications mean shorter episodes and fewer downstream readmissions, compounding the direct overhead savings.
The Operating Model and Its Guardrails
Hospital-at-Home is not 'a nurse visit plus a phone.' Under the CMS waiver, a participating hospital must submit a patient-screening protocol for approval that combines clinical criteria with an environmental assessment of the home — confirming, among other things, that the dwelling is safe and that the patient's needs can be met there (CMS; American Nurse). The care model itself is intensive: 24/7 availability of both physician and nursing coverage, at least two in-person visits per day by a registered nurse or, where permitted, a Mobile Integrated Health paramedic, continuous or intermittent remote monitoring of vital signs, and on-demand audio/video connection to the care team. Medication delivery, mobile diagnostics, and meal support round out the package.
[{"Requirement":"Eligibility screening","Detail":"CMS-approved protocol — clinical criteria + home environmental assessment"},{"Requirement":"Physician oversight","Detail":"24/7 availability; daily evaluation (in person or via telehealth)"},{"Requirement":"Nursing visits","Detail":"≥2 in-person visits/day (RN or, if appropriate, MIH paramedic)"},{"Requirement":"Monitoring","Detail":"Remote vital-sign monitoring; on-demand audio connection to care team"},{"Requirement":"Rapid response","Detail":"Ability to escalate and return the patient to the hospital quickly"}]
Hospital-at-Home: core CMS requirements
These guardrails matter for interpreting the outcome data: HaH's strong results are the results of a tightly screened, intensively monitored program — not evidence that any patient can safely be sent home. The eligibility funnel is doing real work, and a program that loosens it without preserving the monitoring intensity should not expect the same outcomes.
Where It Works — and How Big It Has Gotten
In practice, Hospital-at-Home concentrates on a band of conditions that are acute enough to require hospital-level management but stable enough to monitor safely at home: heart failure, COPD exacerbations, pneumonia and other infections, cellulitis, and selected post-acute and chronic-disease flares. Mass General Brigham has built the largest U.S. program, running roughly 50-60 home patients a day with capacity around 70 and a stated goal of shifting 10% of its total inpatient volume — on the order of 200-300 beds — into the home (Becker's, 2025). Cleveland Clinic's Florida program treated about 1,000 patients in its first year, and Mayo Clinic and Kaiser Permanente co-developed a model and the Advanced Care at Home Coalition to push the approach forward.
The scale signal matters for policy: these are not boutique pilots but core operations at some of the country's most sophisticated systems, built on the assumption that the payment pathway will persist. That assumption is exactly what the waiver politics put at risk.
The 2025 Cliff and the 2030 Extension
For all its clinical success, Hospital-at-Home spent 2025 on the edge of disappearing, because its Medicare reimbursement runs entirely through a temporary waiver. Without an active AHCAH waiver, hospitals cannot bill fee-for-service Medicare or Medicaid for home-based acute care at all — they can only bill private and managed-care plans (Chartis, 2025). That single fact makes the waiver existential: it is the difference between a reimbursed service line and an unfunded one.
Congress let the initiative lapse on September 30, 2025 amid a government-funding impasse, briefly stranding active programs. The Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 then reinstated and extended the waiver through September 30, 2030 — the substance of the Hospital Inpatient Services Modernization Act (H.R. 4313 / S.2237) — giving the field roughly five years of funding certainty for the first time (AMA, 2026; Healthcare Dive, 2025). As of late 2025, roughly 419 hospitals across about 147 health systems in 39 states had been approved to operate AHCAH programs (CMS, 2025).
A care model can be proven safe, cheaper, and preferred by patients and still nearly die — not on the merits, but because its funding was attached to a waiver that Congress has to keep renewing. Durable adoption requires durable payment.
What Makes It Hard to Scale
If the evidence is so favorable, why is Hospital-at-Home roughly 1-2% of eligible volume rather than 20%? Several barriers beyond payment are real. The first is logistics: a home hospital is a supply chain — same-day delivery of medications, oxygen, infusion equipment, and mobile diagnostics, plus a routing system that gets nurses or paramedics to scattered homes for two visits a day. That coordination is operationally harder than walking down a hospital corridor, and it does not scale linearly. The second is workforce: the model needs clinicians comfortable practicing without the safety net of a unit, and in many markets nursing and paramedic capacity is already strained.
The third is the technology stack. Reliable Hospital-at-Home depends on remote monitoring that feeds a command center, video connectivity into the home, and EHR integration so the home episode is documented like any other admission — and that stack only works where connectivity does. The fourth is payer fragmentation: the AHCAH waiver governs fee-for-service Medicare, but Medicaid coverage is uneven and commercial coverage varies plan by plan, so a hospital's program economics depend on its specific payer mix. A system whose eligible patients are mostly in plans that do not reimburse HaH cannot run the model at scale regardless of the clinical case.
The Equity Question: Who Gets to Stay Home?
Hospital-at-Home's benefits are real but not automatically equitable, because eligibility depends on conditions that track existing disadvantage. The screening protocol's environmental assessment effectively requires adequate, safe housing; many programs also assume an available caregiver. Patients who are unhoused, live in unsafe or crowded housing, or lack a caregiver can be screened out — which means the model can systematically exclude some of the most vulnerable patients it might most help (PMC, 2024).
Technology compounds the gap. Remote monitoring and telehealth presuppose reliable broadband, which is precisely what rural and many low-income urban areas lack: rural residents report markedly lower broadband access, and in some areas connectivity is too weak to support monitoring devices at all (Commonwealth Fund, 2025; Johns Hopkins, 2025). The result is a risk that Hospital-at-Home, left to its default design, concentrates its benefits among well-housed, well-connected, caregiver-supported patients. Equity-minded policy responses — Medicaid coverage of HaH, broadband investment, and broader workforce models including community paramedicine — are aimed squarely at widening that funnel.
Hospital-at-Home's eligibility criteria — safe housing, a caregiver, working broadband — are also a map of social advantage. Without deliberate design choices, the model risks delivering its biggest benefits to the patients who already have the most.
What to Watch
Three things will determine whether Hospital-at-Home matures from waiver-dependent program into permanent infrastructure. First, payment permanence: the 2030 extension buys time, but a permanent Medicare benefit (with a defined payment mechanism rather than a renewable waiver) is what would justify the capital investment in logistics, monitoring, and staffing that scaling requires. Second, scope expansion: whether programs move beyond the stable medical admissions that dominate today toward higher-acuity and post-surgical care, and whether Medicaid and commercial payers broaden coverage. Third, equity instrumentation: whether programs and regulators track who is screened out and why, and whether broadband and caregiver-support investments measurably widen access rather than leaving HaH a benefit for the already-advantaged.
Bottom Line
Hospital-at-Home is one of the rare delivery-model reforms where the clinical evidence, the cost evidence, and patient preference all point the same direction, validated by both academic trials and CMS's own data. Its vulnerability has never been clinical — it is structural: a care model funded by a waiver Congress must keep renewing, an operational lift in logistics and workforce, and an eligibility model that can quietly track social advantage. The 2026 extension through 2030 stabilizes the payment problem for now; the operational and equity problems are unsolved and deserve explicit measurement.
For health systems, the strategic read is that Hospital-at-Home is now here to stay long enough to justify real capital investment — but the programs that endure will be the ones built for payment permanence rather than waiver renewal, engineered for the logistics of delivering a hospital to a doorstep, and designed deliberately to reach beyond the well-housed, well-connected, caregiver-supported patient. The single most important number to watch is not the count of approved programs but the share of a system's eligible admissions it can actually divert home — and whether that share grows for the patients who have historically been left in the ward.

######## policyAnalysis-clin-002 | Precision Medicine and Pharmacogenomics: Payer Coverage Policies in 2025
Pharmacogenomics (PGx) — using a patient's genetics to guide which drug, and what dose, will work safely — is one of the most clinically mature pieces of precision medicine. The science is settled for a meaningful set of gene-drug pairs, the guidelines are written, and the FDA flags many of them on drug labels. Yet adoption remains slow, and the reason is not the biology. It is payment. This analysis examines what PGx can do, what the evidence shows, why insurer coverage lags the science, and what would have to change for genotype-guided prescribing to become routine.
What Pharmacogenomics Actually Does
Many drugs are metabolized by enzymes whose activity varies genetically from person to person. A patient who is a 'poor metabolizer' of a given enzyme may accumulate a drug to toxic levels at a standard dose; an 'ultra-rapid metabolizer' may clear it so fast that a standard dose does nothing. PGx testing identifies these variants — most importantly in the CYP2D6 and CYP2C19 enzyme genes — so a clinician can choose a different drug or adjust the dose before the patient is harmed or fails therapy. The classic examples span psychiatry (antidepressants), cardiology (clopidogrel after stenting), oncology, and pain management.
The evidence governing these decisions is not vendor marketing — it is curated by the Clinical Pharmacogenetics Implementation Consortium (CPIC), which grades gene-drug pairs by strength of evidence. CPIC 'Level A' pairs are those where genetics should change prescribing; many of the same pairs carry FDA labeling. For CYP2D6 and CYP2C19 alone, CPIC assigns Level A status to multiple psychiatric drugs — citalopram, escitalopram, amitriptyline, atomoxetine, nortriptyline, paroxetine, and vortioxetine among them (CPIC; PMC, 2024).
The pharmacogenomics debate is unusual in medicine: the science is ahead of the system. For a defined set of gene-drug pairs the evidence is strong enough that both CPIC and the FDA say genetics should guide prescribing — yet most prescribing still proceeds blind to it.
Where It Is Already Standard of Care
It is easy to dismiss pharmacogenomics as speculative, but several gene-drug pairs are already woven into routine practice — proof that the model works when evidence and workflow align. In oncology, testing the DPYD gene before giving fluoropyrimidine chemotherapy (5-FU, capecitabine) identifies patients who cannot metabolize the drug and would otherwise suffer severe, sometimes fatal, toxicity; Europe has moved toward mandatory DPYD testing, and U.S. cancer centers increasingly follow. In HIV care, testing for the HLA-B*57:01 allele before prescribing abacavir to prevent a dangerous hypersensitivity reaction is unambiguous standard of care. In hematology and rheumatology, TPMT/NUDT15 testing before thiopurine drugs prevents life-threatening marrow suppression.
These examples share a common shape: a clear, sometimes catastrophic harm; a single decisive gene; and a test result that maps to an obvious action. Where those conditions hold, pharmacogenomics is not contested at all — it is malpractice to ignore it. The frontier debate is about extending the model from these clean single-gene, high-stakes cases to broader preemptive panels across more ordinary prescribing, where the benefit per patient is smaller but the population scale is vast.
The Evidence: PREPARE and Preemptive Testing
The most important recent evidence comes from the PREPARE trial, a large multi-country study of 6,944 patients across primary care, oncology, and general medicine that tested a preemptive panel approach — genotyping patients across a set of pharmacogenes before prescribing, rather than testing reactively after a problem. PREPARE found that acting on a 12-gene panel meaningfully reduced clinically relevant adverse drug reactions, providing the strongest real-world support yet for preemptive PGx (Nature Pharmacogenomics Journal, 2024).
The distinction between preemptive and reactive testing is central to both the clinical and the economic case. Cost-effectiveness analyses find that preemptive panel testing — done once and reused across a lifetime of prescribing decisions — is frequently cost-effective, whereas one-off reactive testing for a single drug often is not, particularly in cardiovascular care (Nature Pharmacogenomics Journal, 2025; PMC systematic review). The economics improve precisely because a single test informs many future prescriptions; reactive testing throws away that reuse value.
[{"Approach":"Preemptive (panel)","Description":"Genotype many pharmacogenes once, before prescribing","Economics":"Often cost-effective — one test informs many future decisions"},{"Approach":"Reactive (single-gene)","Description":"Test one gene after a drug problem or for one drug","Economics":"Frequently not cost-effective in isolation"}]
Preemptive vs. reactive PGx testing
The Psychiatry Cautionary Tale
Pharmacogenomics' biggest commercial market — psychiatry — is also where its credibility is most contested, and the distinction matters for the whole field. Combinatorial commercial panels such as GeneSight test dozens of psychiatric medications and produce algorithmic 'use as directed / use with caution / use with increased caution' recommendations. But the evidence does not support that breadth: the FDA has stated that only about 13 antidepressants have enough pharmacogenomic evidence to warrant therapy changes, whereas GeneSight evaluates roughly 57 medications, and independent reviews — including a VA evidence brief — have found that GeneSight-guided treatment did not produce statistically convincing improvements in remission over usual care (AAFP, 2021; VA Evidence Brief).
The lesson is not that psychiatric PGx is worthless — single-gene CYP2D6/CYP2C19 guidance for specific antidepressants rests on solid CPIC evidence — but that proprietary combinatorial algorithms extend well beyond what the evidence supports, and conflating the two damages the credibility of the rigorous core. Payers, reasonably, treat the over-broad commercial panels skeptically; the risk is that skepticism spills over onto the genuinely actionable gene-drug pairs. Keeping the evidence-based core distinct from the marketing is essential to the field's legitimacy.
The Diversity Problem
There is also an equity flaw baked into the reference data. Pharmacogenomic knowledge has been built disproportionately on people of European ancestry — who represent more than 63% of population-labeled individuals in PharmGKB, the field's leading database — even though the functional variants in drug-metabolizing genes differ by ancestry. Roughly half of all functional variants in drug-related genes are unique to a single population group, and standard tests can miss alleles common in African, Afro-Caribbean, and other underrepresented populations (PMC, 2025; ScienceDirect).
The consequence is that a PGx test calibrated on European reference data can be less accurate — even misleading — for patients of other ancestries, precisely the populations that already experience worse medication outcomes. Without deliberate inclusion of diverse genomes in reference panels, pharmacogenomics risks doing what too much of precision medicine does: delivering its benefits first and best to the already-advantaged while leaving others with a tool that works less well for them.
Pharmacogenomics carries two warning labels of its own: over-broad commercial psychiatric panels that outrun their evidence, and reference data skewed toward European ancestry. Both must be managed honestly, or PGx will repeat precision medicine's habit of helping the well-studied most.
Why Coverage Lags the Science
If the science is strong and preemptive testing is cost-effective, why is uptake slow? The binding constraint is reimbursement. Medicare took a meaningful step in August 2020, when its MolDx program issued local coverage determinations covering PGx tests where a clinically actionable gene-drug interaction exists — defined as CPIC Level A or B (American Pharmacogenomics Association). But commercial coverage remains patchy and conservative. Even for clopidogrel, where the FDA carries a boxed warning about CYP2C19 poor metabolizers and the cardiology stakes are life-and-death, the reimbursement rate for the relevant CYP2C19 test sits around 52% and has not improved much over time despite mounting evidence (PMC, 2024).
Several forces explain the lag. Payers favor single-gene, single-indication coverage over the panels that are most cost-effective, because panels look like 'testing for things we're not treating yet.' Outcome data, while strong for ADR reduction, is thinner on hard endpoints like mortality and total-cost-of-care that payers weight most heavily. And the savings from PGx accrue diffusely and over time — fewer adverse events, fewer failed medication trials — which is exactly the kind of long-horizon, cross-budget benefit that fragmented U.S. insurance is structurally bad at capturing.
Pharmacogenomics suffers from the wrong-pocket problem: the test costs one payer money today, while the savings — avoided hospitalizations, fewer failed drug trials — land later, in someone else's budget. A system that cannot see across pockets underinvests in exactly this kind of prevention.
Coding and Operational Barriers
Beyond coverage policy, the plumbing is clumsy. Single-gene tests use discrete CPT codes (for example, 81225 for CYP2D6, 81226 for CYP2C19), while multi-gene panels are billed through individual gene codes or proprietary PLA codes, creating documentation friction and inconsistent payment. Just as important is the last mile at the point of care: a PGx result is only useful if it surfaces inside the EHR as an actionable, persistent alert when a relevant drug is ordered. Where results sit in a PDF rather than as structured, decision-support-ready data, even a tested patient gets prescribed blind. Implementation, not just coverage, is a real barrier.
The implementation gap compounds the coverage gap in a self-reinforcing way. Because results are not reliably actionable in the EHR, clinicians do not order tests; because few tests are ordered, payers see low demand and limited outcome data; because outcome data stays thin, coverage stays conservative; and because coverage is conservative, health systems do not invest in the integration that would make results actionable. Breaking that loop requires moving on coverage and integration together, not sequentially — which is why the organizations most likely to crack it first are integrated, risk-bearing systems that control the payer, the EHR, and the clinical workflow at once.
What Would Move It
Three changes would accelerate genotype-guided prescribing. First, payer adoption of preemptive panel coverage rather than single-gene reactive coverage, aligning reimbursement with the approach the evidence actually supports. Second, EHR integration that turns a result into a real-time prescribing alert, so the clinical value is captured automatically rather than depending on a clinician remembering to look. Third, value-based contracting that lets the entity bearing long-term total-cost risk — an ACO, an integrated system, a capitated plan — internalize the downstream savings from fewer adverse events, solving the wrong-pocket problem that fee-for-service cannot.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether commercial payers expand from single-gene to preemptive-panel coverage in the wake of PREPARE; whether CMS broadens its MolDx framework or adds gene-drug pairs as CPIC elevates them; and whether large integrated systems and ACOs — which capture their own downstream savings — move ahead of fee-for-service payers in embedding PGx as standard practice.
Two more developments are worth tracking. The first is the FDA's own posture: the agency maintains a table of pharmacogenomic associations in drug labeling and has periodically signaled where the evidence is and is not sufficient — its push-back on over-broad combinatorial claims is itself a market-shaping force. The second is the delivery model: pharmacist-led pharmacogenomics services, in which a clinical pharmacist interprets results and advises prescribers, have emerged as a practical way to close the interpretation gap without requiring every physician to become a PGx expert. Where the test result is cheap but the interpretation is the bottleneck, that division of labor may matter as much as coverage policy. Adoption is likely to advance fastest exactly where payment, data integration, and interpretation support are aligned at once.
Bottom Line
Pharmacogenomics is a case where the medicine is ready and the financing is not. For a defined set of gene-drug pairs, the evidence is strong, the guidelines are explicit, and preemptive panel testing is cost-effective — yet conservative, single-gene-oriented coverage and weak EHR integration keep it from becoming routine. The lever is economic and structural, not scientific: align reimbursement with preemptive panels, wire results into prescribing decision support, build out interpretation capacity, and let risk-bearing organizations capture the downstream savings. The field must also keep its evidence-based core distinct from over-broad commercial panels and close the ancestry-diversity gap in its reference data, or it will squander credibility and widen disparities even as it grows. Until those pieces align, pharmacogenomics will remain a proven tool used far below its potential — precision medicine stranded by imprecise payment.

precision-medicine-pharmacogenomics-payer-coverage-2025
For a defined set of gene-drug pairs, pharmacogenomics is clinically mature — CPIC and the FDA agree genetics should guide prescribing, and the PREPARE trial (6,944 patients) shows preemptive panel testing reduces adverse drug reactions. Yet adoption lags because of conservative insurer coverage and weak EHR integration. This analysis examines the science, the economics, and what would make genotype-guided prescribing routine.
Precision Medicine and Pharmacogenomics: Payer Coverage Policies in 2025

######## policyAnalysis-clin-003 | Virtual Care Access and Quality: Post-Pandemic Telehealth in Clinical Practice
Telehealth went from a fringe convenience to a core delivery channel almost overnight during COVID-19, then settled into a durable but smaller role as the emergency receded. Five years on, the questions are no longer whether telehealth works but where it works, who it reaches, and on what payment footing it survives. This analysis covers the post-pandemic utilization picture, the precarious Medicare funding timeline, the evidence on quality, and the equity paradox at the center of the audio-only debate.
Where Utilization Landed
The pandemic spike was extraordinary and temporary. Telehealth surged from a rounding error to roughly half of outpatient visits at the April 2020 peak, then fell back as in-person care resumed. It did not, however, return to baseline: utilization has stabilized at a meaningful new floor, with more than one in ten Medicare beneficiaries — about 12.5% — receiving a telehealth service in the second quarter of 2025 (HHS ASPE; CMS). Importantly, research on Medicare fee-for-service from 2019-2024 found that the rise in telehealth was not associated with an increase in total outpatient visits — telehealth largely substituted for in-person care rather than adding utilization on top of it, easing the early fear that virtual access would simply inflate volume and cost.
The single most important pattern in the post-pandemic data is concentration by specialty. Telehealth did not spread evenly: it became dominant in behavioral health and common in a handful of cognitive specialties, while remaining marginal in fields that require physical examination or procedures. Behavioral health is the clear leader, with the highest telehealth share of any specialty — a fact that shapes both the policy debate and the equity questions below.
[{"Pattern":"Peak vs. settled","Detail":"~50% of outpatient visits (Apr 2020) → stabilized; ~12.5% of Medicare beneficiaries used it (Q2 2025)"},{"Pattern":"Substitution, not addition","Detail":"Telehealth largely replaced in-person visits rather than increasing total volume (2019-2024 Medicare FFS)"},{"Pattern":"Specialty concentration","Detail":"Behavioral health = highest share; physical/procedural specialties remain low"}]
Telehealth's post-pandemic shape
What the Pandemic Proved — and Didn't
The COVID experiment settled some long-running debates and left others open. It proved feasibility at scale: a health system could pivot a large share of ambulatory care to video in weeks, and patients would use it. It proved demand, particularly in behavioral health, where stigma, transportation, and provider scarcity had long suppressed in-person utilization. And it generated, for the first time, population-scale data on telehealth's effect on cost and total utilization — the evidence now central to the permanence debate.
What it did not prove is that telehealth is a universal substitute. The natural experiment was conducted under emergency conditions — care avoidance, waived rules, suspended cost-sharing — that do not reflect steady state, so some pandemic-era findings must be read cautiously. And it surfaced rather than solved the divide: the same emergency that demonstrated telehealth's reach also exposed how many patients could not fully use it. The post-pandemic task has been to separate telehealth's durable value (behavioral health, follow-up, specialty access, triage) from the emergency-specific surge, and to build permanent policy around the former.
The Funding Cliff That Won't Go Away
Telehealth's biggest vulnerability is not clinical but legislative. Most of Medicare's pandemic-era telehealth flexibilities — letting patients be seen at home, removing rural-only geographic restrictions, allowing audio-only visits, and permitting FQHCs/RHCs to act as distant-site providers — were authorized as temporary waivers, not permanent law. That has produced a recurring 'telehealth cliff': the flexibilities approach expiration, Congress extends them at the last moment, and the cycle repeats. They briefly lapsed during a 2025 government-funding impasse before being restored.
The current state, as of the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 (signed February 3, 2026), is a two-year extension of the general Medicare telehealth flexibilities through December 31, 2027 (CMS; Quarles, 2026). The crucial exception is behavioral health: the Consolidated Appropriations Acts of 2021 and 2023 made the core behavioral/mental-health telehealth flexibilities permanent — no geographic restriction, the home as an eligible originating site, and audio-only delivery, all codified in statute (Telehealth.HHS.gov). So the policy landscape is bifurcated: behavioral telehealth is durable, while everything else lives on a clock that runs out at the end of 2027.
Telehealth policy is now two-tier. Behavioral-health telehealth is permanent law; the rest of Medicare telehealth survives on a waiver that expires December 31, 2027. Any health system building telehealth into its model has to plan for that split — and for the possibility that the non-behavioral flexibilities lapse again.
What the Evidence Says About Quality
The quality evidence is broadly reassuring with important caveats. For many conditions — especially behavioral health, chronic-disease follow-up, medication management, and triage — telehealth delivers outcomes comparable to in-person care while improving access and convenience (RAND). It reduces travel burden, can lower no-show rates for some populations, and extends scarce specialists (psychiatrists, neurologists) into areas that have none. For these uses, the substitution is clinically sound, not a compromise.
The caveat is modality. Video and audio-only are not clinically equivalent: video consultations show higher diagnostic and decision-making accuracy than audio-only calls (PMC). That matters because audio-only is not a marginal slice of telehealth — it is the lifeline for patients who lack the broadband, devices, or digital literacy for video. The quality gradient (in-person ≥ video ≥ audio-only) thus runs in exactly the opposite direction from the access gradient, setting up the field's central equity tension.
The Cost Question
The fear that stalled permanent telehealth policy was cost: that easy virtual access would generate extra visits and inflate Medicare spending. The post-pandemic evidence has largely refuted it. A Michigan Medicine analysis found telehealth did not increase the total number of visits older Americans had — it substituted for in-person evaluation-and-management visits — while driving down post-visit costs (Michigan Medicine; Health Affairs Scholar, 2024). The per-encounter economics also favor virtual care: outpatient telehealth encounters average roughly $112.80 versus $161.40 for in-person, and Medicare costs in the 30 days after an initial telehealth visit ran about $82 lower per person than after an in-person start.
The larger savings come from diversion, not the visit itself. For patients enrolled in telehealth-supported programs, monthly emergency-room visit rates fell sharply — in one analysis from 8.5% to about 3.03% — as earlier, lower-acuity intervention prevented expensive escalations, especially for behavioral health and chronic conditions (TAFA). The mechanism is the same one that makes telehealth valuable to risk-bearing ACOs: catching problems early and cheaply avoids the hospitalizations and ED visits that dominate total cost. This is the strongest economic argument for permanence — telehealth appears to bend cost down by substitution and diversion rather than bending it up by induced demand.
The Cross-State Licensure Barrier
A structural constraint that rarely makes headlines quietly limits telehealth's reach: physician licensure is state-by-state, so a clinician generally cannot see a patient located in another state — even an established patient who is simply traveling — unless licensed there. For a technology whose entire premise is dissolving distance, a rule that re-imposes state borders is a fundamental friction. It blocks the most natural telehealth use cases, from a patient who moves between states seasonally to a rural region that could be served by specialists two states away.
The main mitigation is the Interstate Medical Licensure Compact (IMLC), an expedited pathway for physicians to obtain licenses in multiple member states, now enacted in 40 states plus the District of Columbia and Guam (Telehealth.HHS.gov; CSG). Parallel compacts exist for nurses, psychologists (PsyPact), counselors, and social workers. But the IMLC streamlines obtaining many state licenses; it does not create a single national telehealth license, and it does not cover physicians who want to practice only via telehealth in a state. So the barrier is lowered, not removed — and it remains one of the most consequential, least-discussed limits on telehealth's potential.
The Audio-Only Equity Paradox
Audio-only telehealth is simultaneously an equity solution and an equity risk, and honest policy has to hold both ideas at once. On one hand, audio-only visits are the critical access tool for patients without adequate technology or connectivity — disproportionately Black and Hispanic patients, dual-eligibles, rural residents, older adults, those with limited English proficiency, and lower-income patients (research consistently finds higher audio-only rates in exactly these groups). Eliminating audio-only coverage would cut off the patients with the fewest alternatives.
On the other hand, those same patients are then routed to the lower-accuracy modality, so a tool meant to close the access gap can widen the quality gap. The digital divide also shows up in attendance: studies link telehealth no-shows to patients who have only a cellular plan, only a smartphone, or no computer — and Black patients show the highest relative risk of cancellations and no-shows, with behavioral health (the highest-telehealth specialty) also having the highest no-show odds (PMC, 2024). The divide is not just whether you can get a visit, but whether you can complete it and how good it is when you do.
Audio-only telehealth rescues access for the patients with the least connectivity and then hands them the least accurate version of the visit. The fix is not to ban it but to invest in the broadband and devices that would let those patients reach the better modality.
What This Means for Stakeholders
For health systems, the strategic implications are concrete. Behavioral health telehealth can be built on permanently — it is the safest place to invest. Non-behavioral telehealth should be planned with the 2027 cliff explicitly modeled, including a contingency for partial or full lapse. And programs serving disadvantaged populations should treat audio-only as a necessary bridge while actively working to move patients toward video through device and connectivity support, rather than accepting the quality gap as fixed. For payers and policymakers, the case for making the non-behavioral flexibilities permanent rests on the now-solid finding that telehealth substitutes for rather than adds to utilization — undercutting the cost-inflation fear that has stalled permanence.
What to Watch
The decisive question is the 2027 cliff: whether Congress finally makes the general Medicare telehealth flexibilities permanent or extends them again at the deadline, and whether audio-only payment survives in particular. Watch also the CBO scoring fight — the cost estimates attached to permanence have repeatedly stalled action, and the substitution evidence may reshape them. On equity, watch whether broadband investment (BEAD and successors) measurably shifts patients from audio-only toward video over time, and whether payers maintain audio-only parity in the interim. And watch fraud-and-program-integrity rules, which have become the policy lever opponents of permanence most often invoke. Finally, watch the licensure compacts: whether more states join the IMLC and the nursing and psychology compacts, and whether any momentum builds toward a genuine national telehealth registration that would finally match the technology's borderless reach to a borderless license.
Bottom Line
Telehealth has matured into a durable, evidence-backed channel that works especially well in behavioral health and for follow-up and access-extension — but it lives on a divided foundation. Behavioral telehealth is permanent; the rest expires at the end of 2027 absent congressional action. The quality evidence supports telehealth while flagging that audio-only is genuinely less accurate, which collides with the fact that audio-only is the access lifeline for the most disadvantaged patients. The mature policy stance is to make the flexibilities permanent, preserve audio-only as a bridge, and invest in the connectivity that would let every patient reach the better modality — treating telehealth not as a cheaper substitute for care but as infrastructure whose equity depends on the broadband beneath it. The cost evidence now favors permanence, the cross-state licensure compacts are slowly lowering the borders that contradict telehealth's premise, and behavioral health shows what durable virtual care looks like. The remaining work is political will at the 2027 cliff and sustained investment in the connectivity that decides whether telehealth narrows or widens the gaps it touches.

Telehealth has settled into a durable post-pandemic role — about 12.5% of Medicare beneficiaries, concentrated in behavioral health, largely substituting for in-person care. But its foundation is split: behavioral telehealth is permanent law while the rest expires Dec 31, 2027, and audio-only is both the access lifeline for disadvantaged patients and the lower-accuracy modality.
