Virtual Care Access and Quality: Post-Pandemic Telehealth in Clinical Practice

######## policyAnalysis-clin-004 | Sepsis Protocol Standardization: SEP-1 Bundle Compliance and Mortality Outcomes
Sepsis kills more people in U.S. hospitals than almost any other condition, and for a decade federal policy has tried to standardize its treatment through SEP-1 — the Severe Sepsis and Septic Shock Early Management Bundle. In 2024 that measure crossed a critical threshold: it became pay-for-performance. This analysis examines what SEP-1 requires, what the evidence does and does not show about whether it saves lives, and why a measure now tied to hospital payment remains scientifically contested.
The Scale of the Problem
The stakes explain the policy intensity. The CDC estimates that at least 1.7 million U.S. adults develop sepsis each year and at least 350,000 die — sepsis contributes to more than a third of all hospital deaths, more than almost any other single condition (CDC). The economic burden is correspondingly enormous: sepsis care is among the most expensive reasons for hospitalization in the United States, with cost estimates ranging from roughly $52 billion to over $60 billion per year (AHRQ HCUP; PMC). Sepsis is also time-critical in a way few conditions are — survival falls with each hour of delayed recognition and treatment — which is precisely why a time-based process measure is so intuitively appealing, and why getting it wrong has real consequences.
What SEP-1 Requires
SEP-1 is a 'bundle' measure: to be compliant, a hospital must complete a specific set of interventions within defined time windows after sepsis is recognized. The elements include obtaining blood cultures before antibiotics, measuring serum lactate (and repeating it if elevated), administering broad-spectrum antibiotics within a target window, and delivering fluid resuscitation for patients with hypotension or elevated lactate. Crucially, SEP-1 is all-or-nothing — missing any single element fails the entire bundle for that patient, which makes the measure demanding to satisfy and sensitive to documentation.
The measure was introduced by CMS in 2015 as a reporting metric. National compliance has climbed over the years — from roughly half of cases at introduction to the high-50s/low-60s percent range more recently — but it varies sharply by hospital type, with lower-volume and critical access hospitals generally lagging large academic centers (CMS; CIDRAP).
The 2024 Shift: From Reporting to Payment
The consequential change came in fiscal year 2024, when CMS moved SEP-1 from a pay-for-reporting measure into the Hospital Value-Based Purchasing (VBP) Program, placing it in the program's Safety domain (HealthLeaders, 2024). That move matters because the VBP program redistributes a portion of hospitals' Medicare payments based on performance: SEP-1 compliance now has direct dollar consequences, not merely public-reporting ones. For hospital quality and finance leaders, a measure that was once a documentation exercise became a line item that can move reimbursement.
The policy significance of SEP-1 is no longer mainly clinical — it is financial. As of FY2024 it sits inside Hospital Value-Based Purchasing, so compliance rates now shift Medicare dollars between hospitals regardless of the unresolved debate over whether the bundle itself saves lives.
The Evidence Problem
Here is the uncomfortable core of the SEP-1 story: the evidence that bundle compliance actually reduces mortality is weak and contested. A 2024-2025 systematic review identified twelve studies examining the relationship between SEP-1 compliance and mortality — five found a statistically significant benefit, and seven did not. The review concluded there was no high- or moderate-quality evidence that SEP-1 compliance improves mortality, while allowing that a benefit might exist in certain subpopulations (Systematic review, PMC, 2025; 2 Minute Medicine).
This is not a fringe critique. The Infectious Diseases Society of America and allied groups formally urged CMS in 2023 to retire SEP-1, arguing that the measure rewards process compliance rather than outcomes and that a mortality-based sepsis measure would better serve patients (End Sepsis, 2023). At the same time, patient-safety advocates credit SEP-1 with raising sepsis awareness and driving earlier recognition — so the disagreement is genuine, not one-sided.
[{"Position":"In favor","Argument":"Standardized early recognition; raised institutional attention to sepsis; some subgroup mortality signal"},{"Position":"Against","Argument":"No moderate/high-quality evidence of overall mortality benefit; all-or-nothing scoring; rewards process not outcome"},{"Position":"Unintended effect","Argument":"May drive overuse of broad-spectrum antibiotics, working against antimicrobial stewardship"}]
SEP-1: the case for and against
The Science Underneath the Bundle
To understand why SEP-1's evidence base is shaky, it helps to trace where the bundle came from. Its intellectual ancestor is Early Goal-Directed Therapy (EGDT), the protocol from Emanuel Rivers' landmark 2001 single-center trial of 263 septic-shock patients, which reported a striking mortality reduction from aggressive, protocolized early resuscitation targeting specific hemodynamic goals (Rivers, 2001). EGDT reshaped sepsis care worldwide and seeded the protocolized, time-bound philosophy that SEP-1 later codified.
But when EGDT was tested rigorously at scale, it did not hold up. Three large multicenter randomized trials — ProCESS (US), ARISE (Australasia), and ProMISe (UK) — found that strict EGDT protocolization offered no mortality benefit over good usual care (NEJM patient-level meta-analysis, 2017). The nuance matters: the trials did not prove early sepsis treatment useless — they showed that once clinicians recognize sepsis and treat it promptly, the specific rigid protocol adds little. That is the crux of the SEP-1 debate: the bundle mandates the protocolized specifics that the best trials found unnecessary, while the genuinely valuable part — early recognition and prompt treatment — is harder to capture in a checkbox.
SEP-1 inherited its DNA from a 2001 protocol that three large trials later failed to confirm. The enduring lesson of those trials was that prompt recognition matters more than rigid protocol — yet it is the rigid protocol that SEP-1 scores.
The Antibiotic Stewardship Tension
One specific concern deserves emphasis because it pits two quality goals against each other. SEP-1's time-to-antibiotics requirement pressures clinicians to give broad-spectrum antibiotics quickly to anyone who might be septic. But sepsis is hard to diagnose at the bedside, and a measure that penalizes delay incentivizes treating possible sepsis as definite sepsis. The result, critics argue, is overuse of broad-spectrum antibiotics in patients who turn out not to have bacterial sepsis — directly undermining antimicrobial stewardship programs that are themselves a national patient-safety priority. A measure designed to improve safety can, at the margin, degrade it on a different axis.
SEP-1 asks clinicians to act fast on a diagnosis that is often uncertain. Speed is the right instinct in true sepsis — but a measure that scores speed alone can convert diagnostic uncertainty into reflexive broad-spectrum prescribing.
Why Compliance Varies So Much
SEP-1's all-or-nothing design has an underappreciated consequence: a hospital can deliver excellent sepsis care and still fail the measure on a technicality. Because every bundle element must be completed and documented within its time window, a single missed lactate redraw, a fluid order placed but charted late, or an antibiotic given just outside the window fails the entire case. A meaningful share of measured 'non-compliance' is therefore a documentation and workflow problem rather than a care-quality problem — which is why hospitals invest heavily in sepsis order sets, automated screening alerts, and abstraction support purely to capture the care they are already providing.
This design also explains the persistent gap between large academic centers and smaller or critical access hospitals. Larger centers have dedicated sepsis coordinators, mature electronic order sets, and chart-abstraction teams; smaller hospitals often do not, so they score worse even when bedside care is comparable. When SEP-1 was a reporting measure, that gap was an information asymmetry. Now that it sits in Value-Based Purchasing, the same gap becomes a payment penalty that flows away from exactly the resource-constrained hospitals least able to build the documentation infrastructure that compliance rewards.
Toward a Better Measure
The constructive response gaining traction is not to abandon sepsis measurement but to reorient it from process to outcome. A risk-adjusted, mortality-based sepsis measure — one that asks whether a hospital's septic patients survive at expected rates given their severity, rather than whether boxes were checked — would reward the thing that matters and would not penalize a hospital for treating sepsis effectively in a way the bundle does not recognize. It would also relieve the antibiotic-overuse pressure, since hospitals would be judged on survival rather than on speed-to-antibiotic in isolation. The technical challenge is real: sepsis is heterogeneous and hard to risk-adjust, and outcome measures can be gamed through coding. But the major infectious-disease and critical-care societies have judged those challenges more tractable than the conceptual flaw at the heart of a process bundle the evidence does not support.
What This Means for Hospitals
For hospital leaders, the practical posture is dual. Operationally, because SEP-1 now affects VBP payment, hospitals have to perform on it: that means reliable sepsis-screening triggers, order sets that make the bundle the path of least resistance, and documentation that captures compliance accurately (a meaningful share of 'failures' are documentation gaps, not care gaps). Clinically, the smarter programs treat SEP-1 as a floor, not a ceiling — using it to drive early recognition while layering on antimicrobial stewardship guardrails so the antibiotic-overuse risk is actively managed rather than ignored.
For policymakers, SEP-1 is a case study in the limits of process measures. It illustrates how a metric can become entrenched in payment policy faster than the evidence base matures, and why the field is pushing CMS toward an outcome-based sepsis measure — one that rewards risk-adjusted survival rather than checkbox completion. Whether CMS replaces or augments SEP-1 with such a measure is the open policy question.
There is also a credible federal model for what better looks like. In August 2023, the CDC released its Hospital Sepsis Program Core Elements — a framework that, instead of scoring a single time-bound bundle, asks hospitals to stand up a real sepsis program: leadership commitment and dedicated accountability, structures for identification and management, and measurement of local sepsis epidemiology and outcomes (CDC, 2023). It is a program-maturity model rather than a checkbox, and it points toward the kind of measurement that rewards building durable capability over passing individual cases.
The Core Elements also widen the lens beyond the acute admission. Sepsis is not over when the patient leaves the ICU: survivors face elevated risk of readmission, functional decline, and death for months to years afterward — 'post-sepsis syndrome.' A measurement regime fixated on a few hours of bundle compliance captures none of that downstream burden, which is where much of sepsis's true cost and mortality actually accrues. An outcome-oriented framework can; a process bundle cannot.
What to Watch
Three developments will shape the next phase. First, whether CMS responds to the IDSA petition by developing a risk-adjusted, mortality-based sepsis measure to complement or replace SEP-1 — the single most important signal. Second, whether the VBP financial incentive measurably changes compliance behavior and, more importantly, whether any compliance gains translate into outcome gains or merely more antibiotic use. Third, the equity dimension: because critical access and low-volume hospitals lag on compliance, a payment penalty attached to SEP-1 could disproportionately draw dollars away from the rural hospitals least able to absorb the loss — turning a quality measure into a regressive transfer if not carefully calibrated.
Bottom Line
SEP-1 is simultaneously the most influential and most contested sepsis policy in the United States. It has standardized early sepsis management and, since 2024, carries real financial stakes through Hospital Value-Based Purchasing — yet the evidence that compliance saves lives remains inconclusive, the measure carries a real risk of driving antibiotic overuse, and its all-or-nothing scoring penalizes documentation gaps and resource-poor hospitals as much as genuine care failures. The defensible institutional strategy is threefold: perform on SEP-1 because it now affects payment; manage its antibiotic-overuse side effect through active stewardship; and build toward the CDC Core Elements model of a mature, outcome-measured sepsis program rather than treating bundle compliance as the goal. Alongside that, the field's major infectious-disease and critical-care societies are right to press CMS for a risk-adjusted, mortality-based successor measure. The principle is simple: a measure that moves Medicare dollars should eventually have to prove that it moves mortality — and on that test, SEP-1 has not yet delivered.

######## policyAnalysis-clin-005 | Maternal Mortality Crisis: Evidence-Based Interventions and State-Level Performance
The United States has the highest maternal mortality rate of any wealthy nation, and the burden falls catastrophically unevenly by race. Yet the defining fact of U.S. maternal death is also the most damning: the large majority of these deaths are preventable. This analysis examines the numbers, the racial disparity that anchors them, why so many deaths happen after delivery rather than during it, and the interventions — safety bundles, postpartum Medicaid, doulas — with the strongest evidence behind them.
Where the Numbers Stand
The U.S. maternal mortality rate was about 17.9 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2024 (CDC NCHS final data) — down from pandemic-era peaks but still far above peer nations, several of which sit in the low single digits. Behind the national average lies a stark racial gap: Black women die from pregnancy-related causes at roughly three times the rate of white women, with Black maternal mortality around 50 per 100,000 against about 14.5 for white women (KFF; CDC). American Indian and Alaska Native women also face sharply elevated risk.
Two features of the disparity refute the easy explanations. First, it is not explained by income or education: Black women with a college degree experience higher pregnancy-related mortality than white women without one — a finding that points to factors beyond socioeconomic status, including differential quality of care and the physiological toll of chronic stress and racism (KFF). Second, the disparity widens with maternal age. Severe maternal morbidity — life-threatening complications short of death, such as hemorrhage and eclampsia — follows the same pattern and is far more common than death itself, so the mortality figures are the visible tip of a much larger burden of preventable harm.
The central scandal of U.S. maternal health is not only that the rate is high, but that more than 80% of pregnancy-related deaths are preventable (CDC) — and that the harm concentrates on Black and Native women regardless of their income or education.
A Necessary Caveat: The Measurement Debate
Any honest analysis of U.S. maternal mortality must address a genuine measurement controversy, because it affects how the trend should be read. Beginning in 2003 and completing in 2017, U.S. states added a 'pregnancy checkbox' to death certificates to better capture maternal deaths. It improved identification — but also introduced overcounting, especially for older women: NCHS found checkbox errors rise sharply with age, with more than half of errors on certificates of women over 45, some flagged as maternal deaths with no supporting cause-of-death information (NCHS; PMC).
This matters because researchers have argued that a substantial part of the apparent rise in U.S. maternal mortality over the past two decades reflects this surveillance change rather than a true increase in deaths; when maternal deaths are identified by requiring pregnancy to be mentioned in the cause-of-death fields, the rates look lower and more stable (AJOG, 2024). NCHS has since tightened its coding rules for women 45 and older to reduce the artifact. The responsible reading: the U.S. genuinely has high maternal mortality and a severe racial disparity that no measurement change explains away — but the precise level and trend carry real uncertainty, and confident claims about a smoothly 'rising' rate should be treated with caution.
How the U.S. Compares
Even accounting for the measurement debate, the international gap is undeniable and large. The United States consistently records the highest maternal mortality among high-income nations — multiples of the rates in Canada, the United Kingdom, Australia, and the Nordic countries, several of which operate in the low single digits per 100,000. The contrast is instructive because those countries are not uniformly richer or more medically advanced; they differ in system design. Most rely heavily on midwifery-led care for low-risk pregnancies, guarantee continuous postpartum coverage, and have less fragmented care — the very features U.S. reforms (postpartum Medicaid, doula/midwife coverage, safety bundles) are now trying to retrofit onto a fragmented system. The comparison reframes U.S. maternal mortality as a system-design failure rather than a clinical-knowledge gap.
When and Why Mothers Die
Maternal death is not concentrated at delivery, as intuition suggests. A large share of pregnancy-related deaths occur in the postpartum period — the weeks and months after birth — and a meaningful fraction occur late, up to a year out. The leading causes span the timeline: hemorrhage and embolism cluster around delivery, while cardiovascular conditions, mental-health-related deaths (overdose and suicide), and infection drive much of the postpartum and late-postpartum mortality. The CDC's finding that the majority of deaths are preventable applies across this whole window — meaning the system has repeated opportunities to intervene and repeatedly misses them.
This timing has a direct policy implication: coverage and care that end at six weeks postpartum — historically when pregnancy-related Medicaid eligibility lapsed — cut off exactly when many fatal complications emerge. Deaths from overdose and suicide in particular often occur after that coverage cliff, when a new mother loses her insurance and her care team simultaneously.
[{"Metric":"Overall rate (2024)","Value":"~17.9 per 100,000 live births","Source":"CDC NCHS"},{"Metric":"Black vs. white","Value":"~50 vs. ~14.5 per 100,000 (~3x)","Source":"CDC / KFF"},{"Metric":"Preventable share","Value":">80% of pregnancy-related deaths","Source":"CDC"},{"Metric":"Postpartum Medicaid (12-mo)","Value":"49 states + DC implemented","Source":"Milbank / CMS"}]
U.S. maternal mortality at a glance
Why the Disparity Persists
If the racial gap is not explained by income or education, what does drive it? The evidence points to two reinforcing mechanisms beyond patients' own characteristics. The first is where Black women give birth. Black mothers disproportionately deliver in a concentrated set of hospitals that score lower on quality across multiple indicators — a pattern shaped by residential and economic segregation and referral networks. Researchers estimate that if Black women delivered in the same hospitals as white women, Black severe maternal morbidity would fall meaningfully — in one analysis from about 4.2% to 2.9%, preventing roughly a thousand severe events (systematic review, PMC). In other words, a large share of the disparity is a site-of-care effect: unequal hospitals, not just unequal patients.
The second mechanism is what happens inside the encounter. A substantial literature documents implicit bias in maternity care — Black women's symptoms dismissed or under-treated, warning signs discounted, pain under-managed — contributing to the delayed and overlooked diagnoses that turn survivable complications into deaths. Studies even find negative or stigmatizing descriptors appearing disproportionately in the clinical notes of Black and publicly insured patients. This is why the CDC's 'Hear Her' framing — take women's symptoms seriously — is not a slogan but an intervention: much of the preventability of these deaths lives in whether a clinician acts on a Black mother's report of something wrong.
Two findings reframe the racial disparity as a system failure, not a patient failure: Black mothers are concentrated in lower-quality hospitals, and they are more likely to be dismissed within the encounter. Equalize the hospital and heed the patient, and a large share of the gap is preventable.
What Works: Maternal Safety Bundles
The most operationally proven intervention is the maternal safety bundle — standardized, evidence-based protocols for the obstetric emergencies that kill, deployed through the Alliance for Innovation on Maternal Health (AIM) and state Perinatal Quality Collaboratives. Bundles for obstetric hemorrhage, severe hypertension/preeclampsia, and venous thromboembolism give every unit a pre-agreed, drilled response — the obstetric equivalent of a code protocol. The model originated in California, whose Maternal Quality Care Collaborative (CMQCC) drove the state's maternal mortality far below the national rate over more than a decade, and AIM has since spread the approach to a large majority of states.
The logic is the same as in other high-reliability safety work: most obstetric catastrophes are not exotic; they are common emergencies handled too slowly or inconsistently. Standardizing the response — hemorrhage carts stocked and checked, hypertension treated within a target window, risk assessment on every admission — converts variable individual performance into reliable institutional performance. The evidence base shows bundle implementation reduces severe maternal morbidity, which is both the harm itself and the leading indicator of mortality risk.
What Works: Postpartum Medicaid Extension
Because so much maternal death is postpartum, extending coverage through the first year is one of the highest-leverage policy levers available. The American Rescue Plan Act gave states a pathway to extend Medicaid postpartum coverage from 60 days to a full 12 months, and as of early 2025, 49 states plus the District of Columbia had implemented the extension (Milbank Memorial Fund, 2025). Medicaid pays for roughly four in ten U.S. births, so this reaches the population at highest risk.
The mechanism is continuity: a full year of coverage keeps new mothers connected to care for the cardiovascular, mental-health, and substance-use conditions that drive late maternal deaths, precisely the period when the old 60-day cliff used to sever it. Professional bodies including ACOG and the Society for Maternal-Fetal Medicine have strongly endorsed the extension, and the near-universal state uptake makes it one of the rare maternal-health interventions with genuine bipartisan traction. The open question is robust implementation — coverage on paper does not help if enrollment and care delivery do not follow.
The 60-day postpartum coverage cliff was a policy designed around childbirth as an event, in a country where maternal death is increasingly a year-long risk. Extending Medicaid to 12 months realigns the coverage with where the deaths actually happen.
What Works: Doulas and Midwifery
A third evidence-backed lever is widening the maternity workforce beyond physicians. Doulas — trained non-clinical birth companions — are associated with better outcomes and experiences, particularly for Black birthing people, and a growing number of state Medicaid programs now cover doula services. Midwifery-led care for low-risk pregnancies is standard in most peer nations with far better outcomes than the U.S., and states are raising Medicaid reimbursement for midwives. These approaches address two failures at once: the workforce shortage driving rural maternity-desert closures, and the breakdown of trust and communication that contributes to the racial disparity, since the CDC emphasizes that recognizing warning signs and delivering respectful, responsive care prevents deaths.
What to Watch
Several indicators will show whether the recent progress holds. First, whether the overall rate continues its post-pandemic decline or plateaus, and crucially whether the Black-white gap narrows — the national average can improve while the disparity persists, as it did in 2023. Second, implementation quality of the 12-month postpartum extensions: enrollment, continuity, and whether late maternal deaths actually fall. Third, the rural dimension — maternity-desert closures (covered in the rural-access analysis) directly raise risk, so OB-unit losses and the spread of obstetric-emergency readiness in rural EDs are maternal-mortality variables. Fourth, durability of doula/midwifery Medicaid coverage and AIM bundle adoption as budget pressures mount.
Bottom Line
U.S. maternal mortality is a preventable crisis with a known toolkit. The rate is high and the racial disparity is severe and stubbornly independent of income or education, but more than 80% of these deaths are preventable, and the interventions that work are identified: standardized safety bundles for the emergencies that kill, 12-month postpartum Medicaid to match coverage to when deaths occur, and a broadened workforce of doulas and midwives to extend access and rebuild trust. Nearly every state has now adopted postpartum extension and most have adopted AIM bundles — the scaffolding is in place. The remaining work is execution and equity: implementing these tools well, sustaining them under budget pressure, and measuring success not by the national average alone but by whether the gap between Black and white mothers finally closes. Because the disparity is substantially a site-of-care and in-encounter problem, closing it also requires acting where the data points — improving the specific hospitals where Black mothers deliver and confronting the bias that turns warning signs into missed diagnoses. The knowledge to prevent four in five of these deaths already exists; the failure is one of delivery, equity, and will.

######## policyAnalysis-eco-001 | Value-Based Care Contract Performance: 2025 Shared Savings Results Across ACO Models
After a decade of skepticism about whether value-based care actually saves money, the 2024 results are in — and they are the strongest yet. The Medicare Shared Savings Program, the country's largest accountable-care program, delivered record savings, and the data reveals a clear lesson about what makes value-based care work: downside financial risk. This analysis examines the 2024 ACO results, why two-sided risk is the engine of savings, the gap between high and low performers, and what the evidence now says about the future of value-based payment.
The 2024 Results
The Medicare Shared Savings Program (MSSP) posted its best year on record in 2024. Of 476 ACOs, 75% — representing 80% of the 10.3 million assigned beneficiaries — earned performance payments, and the program saved Medicare $2.48 billion relative to benchmarks, the eighth consecutive year of savings and a new record (CMS; Accountable for Health). Per-beneficiary savings reached $637, and even after paying out shared savings to the ACOs, the program returned roughly $240 per beneficiary net to Medicare. Crucially, the savings came with quality: nearly every ACO met CMS's quality standard, with improvements on blood-pressure control, A1c control, and cancer screening — demonstrating that the cost reductions were paired with better, not worse, care.
This matters because value-based care spent years dogged by the question of whether it actually delivers — whether shared-savings programs genuinely reduce spending or just reward favorable patient selection. The sustained, growing, quality-paired savings of the MSSP now make a strong empirical case that well-designed accountable care does work. After a decade of building, the model has produced its proof of concept at national scale.
The 2024 verdict on value-based care: the Medicare Shared Savings Program saved a record $2.48 billion — its eighth straight year of savings — at $637 per beneficiary, with quality improving, not declining. After a decade of doubt, well-designed accountable care has its proof at scale.
Two-Sided Risk: The Engine
The single most important finding is about what kind of value-based care works. Two-sided risk — arrangements where ACOs can lose money if they overspend, not just share in savings if they underspend — is the engine of MSSP performance. ACOs in the higher-risk Level E and Enhanced tracks delivered more than two-thirds of all savings in 2024, generating $5.4 billion of the $6.6 billion total, and two-thirds of ACOs now operate under downside risk (Accountable for Health; Fierce Healthcare). The pattern is unambiguous: ACOs with real money at stake save far more than those in upside-only arrangements.
The mechanism is intuitive. An ACO that only shares in savings has a modest incentive to manage cost; an ACO that will be penalized for overspending has a powerful one, and it invests accordingly in the care management, data infrastructure, and network discipline that actually reduce spending. Upside-only models let organizations dabble in value-based care without committing; two-sided risk forces the operational seriousness that produces results. This is why CMS has steadily pushed ACOs toward downside risk, and why the 2024 data validates that push — the savings are concentrated exactly where the risk is.
The Performance Gap
Beneath the strong averages lies wide variation, and it is instructive. While 75% of ACOs earned performance payments, a quarter did not, and the savings are heavily concentrated in the higher-risk, more committed organizations. Some ACOs — physician-led groups and sophisticated value-based-care companies like Aledade — saved enormous sums (Aledade's ACOs alone saved over $1 billion in 2024), while others barely broke even. This dispersion reflects the reality that value-based care is hard operational work, not an automatic payoff: success requires investment in care coordination, risk stratification, data infrastructure, and network management that not every organization makes or makes well.
The performance gap carries a strategic lesson. Value-based care is not a financing trick that any provider can adopt and profit from; it is a different operating model that rewards organizations built for it and punishes those that treat it as a side activity bolted onto fee-for-service. Physician-led ACOs have tended to outperform hospital-led ones, plausibly because hospitals face a conflict — reducing admissions hurts their fee-for-service revenue — that physician groups do not. The winners are those whose whole orientation is population health; the laggards are those straddling two payment worlds.
[{"Metric":"Total savings","Value":"$2.48B (record; 8th straight year)"},{"Metric":"Per beneficiary","Value":"$637 saved"},{"Metric":"Earned payments","Value":"75% of 476 ACOs; $4.1B in payments"},{"Metric":"Two-sided risk","Value":"Level E/Enhanced = $5.4B of $6.6B total savings"}]
MSSP 2024: the value-based-care verdict
The Headwinds
The strong results coexist with real headwinds that temper the optimism. The first is the benchmark ratchet: an ACO's savings are measured against a spending benchmark that is itself influenced by the ACO's prior performance, so an ACO that saves money sees its future benchmark lowered, making each successive year's savings harder to achieve — the program can, in effect, penalize its own success. The second is the difficulty of attribution and selection: skeptics note that some apparent savings may reflect which patients an ACO ends up with rather than how it manages them, though the sustained quality gains argue against pure selection. The third is the investment barrier, especially for small and rural providers, who may lack the capital and infrastructure to take on downside risk even though that is where the savings are.
These headwinds matter because they shape who can participate and for how long. The benchmark problem in particular threatens the program's durability — if saving money makes future saving harder, the most successful ACOs face diminishing returns that could eventually push them out, which is precisely the opposite of what the program intends. Refining the benchmark methodology to reward sustained performance without ratcheting it away is among the most important technical fixes for keeping value-based care viable as it scales, and it is a recurring theme in the policy debate over the program's future.
ACO REACH and the Broader Landscape
Beyond MSSP, CMS's ACO REACH model pushes value-based care further — emphasizing health equity and requiring providers to take on substantial financial risk. ACO REACH has operated at meaningful scale (in 2023 it included 132 ACOs with 23 million beneficiary-months of alignment, generating $1.64 billion in gross and $694.6 million in net savings to CMS), serving as the program where the most risk-tolerant organizations operate (CMS). CMS has announced that REACH will end after 2026, succeeded in 2027 by the 10-year LEAD Model. Together, MSSP and REACH represent CMS's bet that accountable care, with escalating downside risk, is the future of Medicare payment — a bet the 2024 results increasingly justify. CMS has set a goal of having all Medicare beneficiaries in an accountable-care relationship, and the growing participation and savings suggest the trajectory is real.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether the savings trend continues to grow as more ACOs move into two-sided risk, or plateaus; whether CMS sustains its push toward downside risk despite provider resistance to the financial exposure; whether the benchmark-setting methodology (the perennial weak point — benchmarks that are too generous overstate savings, too harsh drive ACOs out) is refined; whether physician-led models continue to outperform hospital-led ones, with implications for who should lead value-based care; and whether the model extends beyond Medicare into Medicaid and commercial markets at similar scale. The 2024 results are the strongest evidence yet that value-based care works when structured with real risk — the question now is how far and how fast it spreads.
Bottom Line
The 2024 ACO results are the clearest evidence to date that value-based care delivers: a record $2.48 billion in Medicare savings, $637 per beneficiary, eight consecutive years of savings, and quality improving alongside cost reduction. The decisive lesson is that two-sided risk is the engine — ACOs with real downside exposure generated the overwhelming majority of savings, validating CMS's push toward downside risk and revealing that upside-only arrangements let organizations dabble without delivering. But the wide performance gap shows value-based care is hard operational work, not an automatic payoff, rewarding organizations genuinely built for population health (often physician-led) over those straddling fee-for-service. After a decade of doubt, the model has its proof of concept; the open questions are how aggressively CMS pushes risk, how it sets benchmarks, and how far accountable care spreads beyond Medicare. The era of asking whether value-based care works is giving way to the harder question of how to scale what the evidence now shows does.

######## policyAnalysis-eco-002 | Healthcare Private Equity Exit Multiples Compress as Interest Rates Stabilize
Private equity poured into health care over the past decade on a simple thesis: buy fragmented providers, add leverage, cut costs, and sell at a profit. As interest rates rose and the easy-money era ended, that thesis collided with reality — and the result has been a wave of distress and bankruptcy in PE-owned health care, alongside a growing policy backlash. This analysis examines the PE model in health care, why it has run into trouble, the bankruptcies exposing its risks, the consequences for patients and clinicians, and the regulatory response now taking shape.
The Private-Equity Model in Health Care
The PE playbook in health care is consistent: acquire physician practices, staffing firms, nursing homes, or other providers — often rolling up many small ones into a larger platform — finance the purchase heavily with debt loaded onto the acquired company, extract value through cost-cutting and sometimes asset stripping (such as selling off real estate and leasing it back), and exit by selling at a higher multiple within a few years. The financial engineering is the point: returns come substantially from leverage and multiple expansion, not only from operational improvement. For a while, in a low-interest-rate environment with rising health care valuations, it worked spectacularly.
The structural problem is that this model loads risk onto the operating company while extracting returns for the investors. The debt sits on the provider's balance sheet, the real estate that once provided stability may be sold off, and the pressure to hit return targets drives cost-cutting that often lands on staffing. When conditions are favorable the model generates outsized returns; when they turn, the leveraged, asset-stripped company is dangerously fragile — which is exactly what happened.
The PE healthcare model loads debt onto the acquired provider while extracting returns for investors. In good times it generates outsized returns; in bad times the leveraged, asset-stripped company is dangerously fragile. Rising rates turned good times to bad — and the bankruptcies followed.
Why the Thesis Broke
Two forces broke the PE health care thesis simultaneously. First, interest rates: the model's heavy reliance on debt made it acutely sensitive to borrowing costs, and as rates rose sharply, the leverage that amplified returns on the way up amplified distress on the way down — debt service ballooned, refinancing got harder, and the 'frothy' exit environment that let firms sell at high multiples evaporated. Moody's found that PE-backed companies defaulted at twice the rate of non-PE-backed companies in 2024 (Moody's; PESP). Second, rising labor costs: the post-pandemic surge in wages hit companies whose model depended on squeezing labor, eroding the margins the cost-cutting strategy was built on.
The combination was lethal for the most aggressively financialized companies. A provider already stripped of its real estate, loaded with debt, and run thin on staffing had no cushion when borrowing costs and labor costs both rose. The model that looked like financial genius in a zero-rate world looked like recklessness once money had a price again — and the bankruptcies that followed were not bad luck but the predictable failure mode of high leverage meeting higher rates.
The Bankruptcies
The distress has been concrete and high-profile. Half of the ten physician-practice-management companies that filed for bankruptcy in 2024 were PE-backed, and the trend continued: in July 2025, Genesis Healthcare — once the largest skilled-nursing operator in the U.S., operating across 17 states — filed for bankruptcy after years of deterioration shaped by asset stripping, high-risk borrowing, and regulatory violations, burdened with more than a billion dollars in debt (PESP; Fortune). The Steward Health Care collapse, which left communities scrambling to keep hospitals open, became the defining cautionary tale of PE hospital ownership. These were not marginal players; they were major operators whose failures disrupted care for large numbers of patients.
The bankruptcies matter beyond the balance sheet because when a PE-owned provider fails, patients and communities bear the consequences — hospitals close, nursing homes destabilize, and the access that the financial engineering quietly hollowed out disappears entirely. The Steward and Genesis collapses demonstrated that PE's financial risk-taking is not contained to investors; it is transmitted directly to the patients who depend on the providers PE bought.
The Consequences for Patients and Clinicians
Even short of bankruptcy, PE ownership reshapes care in documented ways. Because cost-cutting frequently targets labor, PE-acquired practices show patterns of laying off experienced staff, shortening doctor-patient visit times, and higher turnover, while physicians report loss of clinical autonomy and pressure to meet financial targets (Center for American Progress; Georgetown). The financial logic — maximize throughput and margin — sits in tension with clinical priorities, and the evidence increasingly links PE ownership to higher costs and, in some studies, worse quality or safety indicators. The model's returns, in other words, can come partly at the expense of the care.
[{"Element":"The model","Detail":"Buy + leverage + cost-cut/asset-strip + exit at higher multiple"},{"Element":"What broke it","Detail":"Rising rates (debt-sensitive) + rising labor costs"},{"Element":"The fallout","Detail":"PE-backed default 2x rate (2024); Genesis, Steward collapses"},{"Element":"Patient impact","Detail":"Closures, thinner staffing, shorter visits, autonomy loss"}]
PE in health care: the model and its failure
The Regulatory Response
The collapses have spurred a policy backlash focused on transparency and oversight. Massachusetts, galvanized by the Steward disaster in its own backyard, passed a comprehensive law (HB 5159) in 2025 requiring hospitals and provider organizations to annually disclose private-equity or REIT involvement and subjecting PE entities to the state's 'material change' review for significant transactions (PESP). Other states and federal agencies (the FTC, DOJ, and HHS have launched inquiries into PE in health care) are moving toward greater scrutiny of PE ownership, transactions, and the asset-stripping practices that leave providers fragile. The regulatory direction is toward visibility — forcing disclosure of who actually owns and controls a provider — and toward reviewing the transactions that load risk onto care delivery.
The challenge is that PE's involvement in health care is now vast and entrenched, and oversight is fragmented across states and agencies. Transparency requirements illuminate the problem but do not by themselves prevent the leveraged, asset-stripping deals that create the risk; harder questions — whether to limit certain transactions, restrict asset stripping, or hold investors accountable for the providers they bankrupt — remain largely unaddressed. The Steward and Genesis collapses created political will, but translating it into rules that meaningfully change PE behavior is the unfinished work.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether the bankruptcy wave continues as more PE deals struck in the low-rate era come due for refinancing or exit at higher rates; whether states follow Massachusetts toward mandatory PE disclosure and transaction review, and whether federal agencies (FTC/DOJ/HHS) move from inquiry to enforcement; whether oversight extends beyond transparency to actually constraining asset stripping and excessive leverage; and whether the documented care and cost effects of PE ownership harden into a regulatory case for limiting it in health care. The era of unexamined PE expansion in health care is closing; what replaces it depends on whether the backlash produces real constraints or just more reporting.
Bottom Line
Private equity's decade-long expansion into health care rested on a leveraged, cost-cutting, asset-stripping model that generated outsized returns in a low-rate world and became dangerously fragile when rates and labor costs rose — producing a wave of distress and high-profile collapses (Steward, Genesis) that transmitted PE's financial risk directly to patients and communities. The model's returns came partly at the expense of staffing, autonomy, and arguably care, and its failures closed hospitals and destabilized nursing homes. A regulatory backlash is building, led by states like Massachusetts toward disclosure and transaction review, but it has so far illuminated the problem more than it has constrained the practices that cause it. The unresolved question is whether policy moves from transparency to genuinely limiting the financial engineering that makes PE-owned health care fragile — because until it does, the conditions that produced the recent collapses remain in place, and the next wave of refinancing pressure will test them again. The deeper lesson transcends private equity: it is about what happens when a financing model optimized for investor returns is applied to an enterprise whose product is patient care. The interests can align — capital can fund growth and efficiency — but the PE structure, with its leverage and short hold periods and asset stripping, systematically privileges the investor's return over the institution's durability, and in health care the institution's durability is the community's access to care. That misalignment is the real subject of the policy reckoning now underway, and resolving it requires deciding how much financial risk society is willing to let investors load onto the providers people depend on.

######## policyAnalysis-eco-003 | Nursing Shortage Economics: Travel Nurse Rate Normalization and Long-Term Workforce Gaps
The nursing workforce crisis is, at its core, an economic one — a story of turnover, vacancy, and the staggering cost of replacing nurses faster than they can be retained. After the pandemic-era explosion in travel-nurse spending, rates have normalized, but the structural shortage remains, and the economics of how hospitals staff their floors have been permanently reshaped. This analysis examines the cost of turnover, the travel-nurse correction, the persistent structural shortage, and why retention — not recruitment — is the economic key.
The Cost of Turnover
Nurse turnover is one of the largest controllable costs in a hospital, and its scale is often underappreciated. The average cost of losing a single bedside RN reached about $60,090 in 2024 — covering recruitment, temporary coverage, orientation, and the productivity ramp of a replacement — and for a typical hospital, RN turnover adds up to between $4.2 million and $6.2 million in annual losses, averaging around $5.19 million (Becker's; NSI). With a national RN turnover rate of 16.4% in 2024 and a vacancy rate of 9.6%, and an average 83 days to recruit an experienced RN, the churn is both constant and expensive. Every nurse who walks out the door costs roughly $60,000 to replace, and hospitals are losing them faster than they can backfill.
This reframes the nursing crisis from a pure supply problem into an economic one: a hospital bleeding millions annually to turnover is not primarily short of nurses in the abstract — it is failing to keep the ones it has, at enormous and largely avoidable cost. The turnover figure is the number that should focus every hospital's workforce strategy, because it quantifies what poor retention actually costs.
Every bedside RN who quits costs a hospital about $60,000 to replace, and turnover drains a typical hospital $4-6 million a year. The nursing crisis is not only a supply problem — it is an expensive retention failure, and the turnover cost is the number that should drive workforce strategy.
The Travel-Nurse Correction
The most dramatic recent shift is the normalization of travel nursing. During the pandemic, travel-nurse rates exploded as hospitals competed desperately for scarce staff, and the cost was crushing. That bubble has deflated: by late 2024, average travel-nurse pay had settled to roughly $2,106 per week (about $109,512 annualized), well below the pandemic peaks, and travel-nurse revenue fell about 37% in 2024 as hospitals dramatically reduced their reliance on contract labor (Betternurse; industry data). U.S. hospitals still spent roughly $1.7 billion on travel nurses in 2024 — a large sum, but a sharp decline from the pandemic emergency.
The correction matters because the pandemic travel-nurse economics were unsustainable and distorted the entire labor market — staff nurses left permanent jobs for travel premiums, hospitals paid multiples of normal wages, and the cost contributed directly to the operating losses that strained hospitals. The normalization is a return toward sanity, but it has left a permanently altered landscape: nurses learned their market power, hospitals learned the cost of over-reliance on contract labor, and the baseline expectation for nursing compensation and flexibility shifted upward. The bubble deflated, but it did not simply reset to 2019.
The Structural Shortage Persists
Beneath the travel-rate normalization, the underlying shortage is real and durable. Over 138,000 nurses have exited the workforce since 2022, and surveys suggest nearly 40% intend to leave by 2029; meanwhile demand has outpaced supply, producing a national shortfall estimated around 295,800 nurses, with McKinsey projecting a gap of 200,000-450,000 RNs (Nurse.org; industry projections). The drivers are structural: an aging nursing workforce nearing retirement, an aging population increasing demand, burnout accelerating departures, and a training pipeline constrained by a shortage of nursing faculty and clinical placement capacity that turns away qualified applicants. Normalized travel rates do not solve any of this; they just remove the acute pricing distortion from a chronic shortage.
The pipeline constraint deserves emphasis because it is the rate-limiter. The U.S. does not lack people who want to become nurses — it lacks the capacity to train them, turning away tens of thousands of qualified applicants annually for lack of faculty and clinical slots. A shortage that is fundamentally a training-capacity problem cannot be solved by recruitment or wages alone; it requires expanding the pipeline's throughput, which is slow and expensive. This is why the shortage is projected to persist for years even as compensation rises.
Why Retention Is the Economic Key
Putting the pieces together points to a clear strategic conclusion: retention, not recruitment, is the economic key. With each departure costing ~$60,000 and the supply pipeline structurally constrained, a hospital cannot recruit its way out of the problem — there are not enough new nurses, and replacing the ones it loses is enormously expensive. The math overwhelmingly favors keeping existing nurses: a retention investment that prevents even a few departures pays for itself many times over against the turnover cost, and it does not depend on a pipeline that cannot expand fast enough. Yet hospitals have historically underinvested in retention relative to recruitment, treating turnover as a cost of doing business rather than the controllable, multimillion-dollar drain it is.
The retention levers are known and comparatively cheap against the turnover cost: schedule flexibility and control (consistently the top-ranked factor in nurse satisfaction), reduced administrative burden (a driver of burnout), career-ladder and education programs, manageable patient ratios, and a culture that addresses the moral distress driving nurses out. None is free, but all are inexpensive relative to $60,000 per avoided departure — which is why the economically rational response to the nursing crisis is to spend on keeping nurses rather than on the travel agencies and recruitment that replacing them requires.
[{"Metric":"Turnover cost","Value":"~$60,090 per RN; $4.2-6.2M/hospital/yr"},{"Metric":"Turnover / vacancy (2024)","Value":"16.4% turnover; 9.6% vacancy; 83 days to recruit"},{"Metric":"Travel correction","Value":"~$2,106/wk; travel revenue -37% (2024); $1.7B spent"},{"Metric":"Structural shortage","Value":"~295,800 shortfall; 138k+ exited since 2022; pipeline capacity-constrained"}]
Nursing workforce economics
What to Watch
Key signals: whether hospitals shift investment from recruitment and travel labor toward retention as the economics dictate; whether the nursing-education pipeline expands (more faculty, more clinical placements) to address the throughput constraint that caps supply; whether travel rates stay normalized or spike again with the next demand shock; whether the projected wave of nurse departures by 2029 materializes or is moderated by retention efforts; and whether new care models (virtual nursing, team-based care) meaningfully reduce the demand for bedside RNs. The acute travel-rate crisis has passed, but the structural shortage and the turnover economics that define it will shape hospital finances and staffing for years.
Bottom Line
The nursing workforce crisis is fundamentally economic: turnover costs roughly $60,000 per nurse and drains a typical hospital $4-6 million a year, the pandemic travel-nurse bubble has deflated but permanently shifted the labor market, and a structural shortage of nearly 300,000 nurses persists — driven by an aging workforce, burnout, and a training pipeline that cannot expand fast enough. The inescapable conclusion is that retention, not recruitment, is the economic key: a hospital cannot recruit its way out of a constrained pipeline, and keeping existing nurses is dramatically cheaper than replacing them. The retention levers — flexibility, reduced burden, career growth, manageable ratios — are inexpensive against the turnover cost, yet historically underused. The hospitals that treat nurse retention as the multimillion-dollar financial priority it is, rather than as a soft HR concern, will be the ones that stabilize their workforce and their finances; those that keep paying to replace the nurses they fail to keep will keep bleeding the cost. The broader policy implication is that the nursing shortage will not be solved by any single lever — it requires expanding the training pipeline (the supply constraint), funding retention (the cost lever), and redesigning care models to use scarce nursing time more efficiently (virtual nursing, team-based care), all at once. But for an individual hospital deciding where to spend its next dollar, the turnover math points unambiguously at retention, and the hospitals that internalize that arithmetic will out-compete those that keep treating their nurses as replaceable and their turnover as inevitable. None of this is novel insight — the turnover cost has been measured for years — yet the persistent gap between what the economics dictate and what hospitals actually do suggests that retention remains chronically undervalued precisely because its payoff (a departure that never happens) is invisible, while a new hire is concrete. Closing that perception gap may be the most important and least expensive workforce intervention available.

The nursing crisis is fundamentally economic: turnover costs ~$60,000 per RN and drains a typical hospital $4-6M/year, the pandemic travel-nurse bubble has deflated (rates ~$2,106/wk, revenue -37% in 2024) but permanently shifted the market, and a structural ~295,800 shortfall persists behind a capacity-constrained training pipeline. The conclusion: retention, not recruitment, is the economic key. This analysis covers turnover cost, the travel correction, the shortage, and retention.

######## policyAnalysis-eco-004 | Medicare Advantage Risk Adjustment Recalibration: Financial Impact on Health Plans
Medicare Advantage now covers more than half of all Medicare beneficiaries, and how the government pays those private plans turns on a technical mechanism most people never see: risk adjustment. A recalibration of that formula — the V28 model — is reshaping the economics of the entire MA market, squeezing plan margins, triggering market exits, and testing whether the program's rapid growth can continue. This analysis examines how MA risk adjustment works, the V28 changes, the market disruption they are causing, and the larger fight over whether MA is overpaid.
How Risk Adjustment Works
Medicare pays MA plans a fixed amount per enrollee, adjusted for how sick each enrollee is — the sicker the documented patient, the higher the payment, so that plans are not penalized for enrolling people with complex needs. That adjustment runs on the CMS Hierarchical Condition Category (HCC) model, which translates a patient's diagnoses into a risk score that scales their payment. The logic is sound in principle: pay more for sicker patients. But it creates a powerful incentive — the more diagnoses a plan documents, the higher its payments — which has driven aggressive 'coding intensity,' where MA plans document far more conditions than traditional Medicare would for clinically similar patients, inflating risk scores and payments.
The V28 Recalibration
V28 is CMS's effort to rein in that inflation. The recalibrated CMS-HCC model rebases the formula on more recent data and trims or removes coefficients for conditions that were being aggressively (and sometimes dubiously) coded, reducing the risk scores those diagnoses generate. CMS phased it in over three years — 33% in payment year 2024, 67% in 2025, and 100% in 2026 — and projected the transition would reduce average MA risk scores by about 3.12%, representing roughly $11 billion in net savings to the Medicare Trust Fund (CMS; MedPAC; Health Affairs). In plain terms, V28 pays MA plans less for the same patients by discounting the coding behavior CMS judged excessive.
Medicare Advantage risk adjustment pays plans more for sicker patients — a sound idea that created a powerful incentive to document more diagnoses. V28 is CMS clawing back the resulting 'coding intensity': ~3.12% lower risk scores, ~$11B in Trust Fund savings, phased to 100% by 2026.
The Coding-Intensity Mechanism
To understand the V28 fight, it helps to understand coding intensity, the practice it targets. Because MA payment scales with documented diagnoses, plans have strong financial incentive to ensure every qualifying condition a patient has is documented — and to find conditions that fee-for-service Medicare, with no such incentive, would not bother to capture. The tools are well-known: in-home 'health risk assessments' that exist largely to document diagnoses, retrospective chart reviews that comb records for additional codes, and analytics that flag patients for further diagnosis-hunting. The result is that MA enrollees appear measurably sicker than identical fee-for-service beneficiaries — not because they are, but because their conditions are more thoroughly documented, inflating risk scores and payments.
This is the heart of the overpayment critique: a meaningful share of MA's higher per-patient cost reflects documentation intensity rather than genuine illness, so the government pays more for MA enrollees than it would for the same people in traditional Medicare. V28's coefficient changes specifically target the conditions most associated with this gaming — trimming the payment value of diagnoses that were being aggressively coded with little corresponding care. CMS has also moved to exclude certain chart-review-derived diagnoses from risk scoring. The mechanism matters because it frames V28 not as cutting payment for sick patients but as discounting a documentation practice that inflated payment without improving care — which is why proponents call it a correction rather than a cut.
The Market Disruption
V28's revenue squeeze, arriving alongside rising medical costs, has destabilized a market accustomed to steady growth. Plans have responded by exiting markets and trimming benefits: the forced disenrollment rate (enrollees whose plans leave their market) jumped to nearly 6.9% in 2025 and is projected to reach about 10% for 2026 — roughly 2.9 million enrollees facing coverage termination as plans withdraw (AJMC). For a program that grew by promising rich supplemental benefits (dental, vision, low premiums), the contraction is a jarring reversal, and it lands on beneficiaries who chose MA for those very benefits.
Notably, though, the pass-through to beneficiaries has so far been more muted than plans warned. Research found that only about 17-24% of the anticipated reduction in plan revenues was passed through to beneficiaries as reduced benefits or higher cost-sharing in 2024-2025 (Health Affairs) — suggesting plans absorbed much of the squeeze through their own margins rather than fully passing it on, at least initially. This undercuts the industry's argument that V28 directly and proportionally harms seniors, while still leaving real disruption in the markets plans exited.
The Overpayment Fight
Underneath V28 is a fundamental, contested question: is Medicare Advantage overpaid? MedPAC and many analysts have long argued that MA costs Medicare more than traditional fee-for-service for equivalent patients — driven substantially by coding intensity and favorable selection — and that the program is overpaid by a meaningful margin, making reforms like V28 a correction rather than a cut. The insurance industry counters that MA delivers more value (care coordination, supplemental benefits, capped out-of-pocket costs) for the money and that payment reductions threaten the benefits seniors rely on. The V28 fight is a proxy for this larger battle over whether MA's growth has been built partly on overpayment that should be recovered.
The politics are unusually fluid. After finalizing V28's phase-in, CMS in its 2027 rate notice gave MA plans a roughly 2.5% payment increase and declined to implement a further proposed risk-adjustment update — a move read as a concession to the industry after sustained lobbying (Healthcare Dive; Center for Medicare Advocacy). This back-and-forth reflects the program's political weight: MA covers a majority of Medicare beneficiaries and the industry is a formidable lobby, so even evidence-based payment corrections face intense pressure. The trajectory of MA payment policy is being negotiated in real time between CMS's cost-control mandate and the industry's political clout.
[{"Dimension":"Mechanism","Detail":"HCC model scales payment by documented diagnoses → coding-intensity incentive"},{"Dimension":"V28 change","Detail":"~3.12% lower risk scores; ~$11B Trust Fund savings; 100% phased by 2026"},{"Dimension":"Market effect","Detail":"Plan exits; forced disenrollment ~6.9% (2025) → ~10% (2026, ~2.9M)"},{"Dimension":"Pass-through","Detail":"Only ~17-24% of revenue cut passed to beneficiaries (2024-25)"}]
MA risk adjustment: V28 and its effects
What to Watch
Key signals: whether the V28 squeeze continues to drive plan exits and benefit reductions or stabilizes as plans adjust; whether CMS holds the line on coding-intensity corrections or continues conceding to industry pressure (as the 2027 rate notice suggested); whether the overpayment evidence translates into durable policy or is repeatedly softened; how chart-review and other coding practices CMS has targeted evolve; and whether MA's majority-of-Medicare position makes its payment politically untouchable regardless of the evidence. The fundamental question — whether MA is overpaid and by how much — will keep driving the payment fight, with V28 just the current front.
Bottom Line
Medicare Advantage's economics turn on risk adjustment, and the V28 recalibration is CMS's attempt to claw back the coding-intensity inflation that critics say has overpaid the program — reducing risk scores about 3.12% for roughly $11 billion in Trust Fund savings, phased to full effect by 2026. The squeeze has destabilized a market built on growth, driving plan exits and rising forced-disenrollment rates, though plans have so far absorbed most of the revenue cut rather than passing it fully to beneficiaries. Beneath it lies the unresolved fight over whether MA is overpaid, with MedPAC and analysts saying yes and a powerful industry saying the payments buy real value — a fight CMS's 2027 concessions show is far from settled. As MA covers a majority of Medicare beneficiaries, how this plays out is among the most consequential questions in U.S. health financing, and V28 is only the current battle in a longer war over what private Medicare plans should be paid. The stakes extend beyond the federal budget: because MA now covers more than half of Medicare beneficiaries, getting its payment right (or wrong) affects the solvency of the Medicare Trust Fund, the benefits tens of millions of seniors receive, and the competitive dynamics of the entire insurance industry. Few technical formulas carry as much weight as the HCC risk-adjustment model, and few policy fights better illustrate how a sound principle — pay more for sicker patients — can be turned into a mechanism for overpayment by actors with the incentive and sophistication to game it, and how hard it then is, politically, to claw that overpayment back.

######## policyAnalysis-eco-005 | Hospital Price Transparency Compliance: Three Years After CMS Enforcement
For decades, the prices hospitals charge were among the best-kept secrets in the American economy — negotiated in private, invisible to the patients who paid them. Federal price-transparency rules set out to change that, requiring hospitals to publish their prices. Years in, the rules have produced data but not yet a functioning market. This analysis examines what the transparency rules require, the tightening enforcement, the persistent gap between disclosure and usable information, and the unresolved question of whether transparency actually lowers prices.
What the Rules Require
The Hospital Price Transparency rule requires hospitals to publish their standard charges — including the payer-specific negotiated rates that were historically secret — in two forms: a comprehensive machine-readable file (MRF) containing all items and services, and a consumer-friendly display of common 'shoppable' services. The requirements have tightened over time: beginning July 1, 2024, the MRF must conform to a CMS-specified template, layout, and data dictionary; and beginning January 1, 2025, hospitals must include charge-affecting modifiers and an estimated allowed dollar amount whenever a charge is expressed as a percentage or algorithm (CMS; HFMA). Hospitals must also attest that the published information is true, accurate, and complete.
The progression of these requirements tells the story of the rule's central struggle: it is not enough to require hospitals to publish prices; the data has to be standardized, complete, and accurate enough to actually use. Each tightening — the template mandate, the modifier and allowed-amount requirements, the attestation — addresses a way hospitals had complied in letter while producing data that was useless in practice. The rule has spent years evolving from 'publish something' toward 'publish something usable.'
Hospital price transparency's core struggle isn't getting hospitals to publish prices — it's getting them to publish prices that are standardized, complete, and accurate enough to actually use. Each rule tightening closes another way hospitals complied in letter while producing unusable data.
Tightening Enforcement
Enforcement has sharpened considerably. CMS released a validator tool to check MRF compliance and built an automated auditing system that makes noncompliance harder to hide and penalties harder to avoid. The fines have teeth and are being levied: in March 2025 CMS fined Northlake Behavioral Health System $257,180 for template and metadata violations and missing negotiated charges, and in May 2025 it imposed a $309,738 penalty on Arkansas Methodist Medical Center for ongoing noncompliance after multiple reviews (CMS; Wise Carter). The maximum penalties scale with hospital size and days of noncompliance, reaching into the millions per year for large hospitals. The early era of lax enforcement — when many hospitals simply ignored or minimally complied with the rule — is giving way to a regime of automated detection and real financial consequences.
The Disclosure-vs-Usability Gap
Publishing prices and creating a usable market are very different things, and the gap between them is the rule's central disappointment so far. Even where hospitals comply, the data has often been incomplete, inconsistently formatted, or hard to interpret — which is precisely why the GAO has argued that enforcement should focus on pricing-data quality, not just whether a file exists (HFMA/GAO). A machine-readable file of negotiated rates is only useful if it is accurate, complete, and comparable across hospitals; a file that is technically present but riddled with gaps or non-standard formats lets a hospital claim compliance while frustrating the comparison shopping the rule was meant to enable.
This is why the rule keeps tightening toward data quality. The underlying recognition is that transparency theater — publishing a file that satisfies the letter of the rule but cannot be used to compare prices — defeats the purpose, and that the hard work is not mandating disclosure but ensuring the disclosed data is good enough to drive the price competition that is the rule's actual goal. Years in, the data quality is improving under enforcement pressure but is not yet uniformly good enough to power a functioning shopping market.
The Variation It Exposed
Whatever its effect on prices, transparency has succeeded at one thing: documenting the staggering, indefensible variation in what hospitals charge. The data has revealed that the same service — an MRI, a colonoscopy, a delivery — can cost wildly different amounts not just between hospitals but between payers at the same hospital, and that there is often little relationship between price and quality. A hospital may charge one insurer several times what it charges another for an identical procedure, and its cash price may be lower than some negotiated rates. These disparities, long suspected, are now documented in the hospitals' own published files, and they undercut any claim that prices reflect cost or value rather than negotiating leverage.
This exposure has value independent of whether individual patients shop. It arms employers and payers with evidence for negotiation, fuels research and journalism that pressures outlier hospitals, and informs policy debates about price regulation, site-neutral payment, and provider consolidation by making the scale of the problem undeniable. Even if transparency never produces a consumer shopping market, forcing the variation into the open changes the politics of hospital pricing — it is harder to defend a price that has been publicly shown to be many times what the hospital accepts from another payer for the same service. The disclosed data is becoming an evidentiary foundation for the broader fight over hospital prices, which may prove more consequential than any individual patient's shopping.
Does It Lower Prices?
The ultimate test — whether transparency actually lowers what patients and payers pay — remains largely unproven, and honesty requires saying so. The theory is appealing: expose the enormous variation in negotiated rates (the same service can cost wildly different amounts at different hospitals, or even for different payers at the same hospital), and patients will shop, payers will negotiate harder, and prices will converge downward. There is some evidence of modest price moderation in markets with sophisticated employers and benefit consultants who use the data, but consumer-facing price shopping remains rare, and the broad price-lowering effect the rule's proponents envisioned has not clearly materialized.
The obstacles are structural. Most health care is not shoppable in the moment (you do not comparison-shop an emergency), insured patients are insulated from price by their coverage, and even motivated shoppers struggle with data that is incomplete and hard to compare. Transparency may work more through the back end — payers and employers using exposed rates to negotiate — than through individual patient shopping. The realistic assessment is that price transparency is a necessary foundation that has not yet, on its own, delivered the price competition its advocates promised, and may require complementary policies (and far better data) to do so.
[{"Dimension":"Requirements","Detail":"Machine-readable file (all charges) + shoppable-services display; CMS template (2024); modifiers/allowed amounts (2025)"},{"Dimension":"Enforcement","Detail":"Automated auditing; real fines (Northlake $257k, Arkansas Methodist $310k, 2025)"},{"Dimension":"Data-quality gap","Detail":"GAO: enforce on data quality, not just file existence"},{"Dimension":"Price effect","Detail":"Unproven; modest moderation in sophisticated-employer markets; little consumer shopping"}]
Hospital price transparency: status
What to Watch
Key signals: whether enforcement and the data-quality requirements produce files complete and standardized enough to enable real comparison; whether payers and employers (more than individual patients) use the exposed rates to negotiate prices down; whether evidence of an actual price-lowering effect emerges as data quality improves; and whether transparency is paired with complementary tools (site-neutral payment, out-of-network protections) that address the structural reasons transparency alone has underdelivered. The rule has moved from publish-something to publish-something-usable; whether usable data finally produces price competition is the test the next few years will run.
Bottom Line
Hospital price transparency has succeeded at forcing into the open the negotiated rates that were once secret, and enforcement has sharpened from lax to automated with real penalties. But publishing prices and creating a functioning price market are different achievements, and the rule has delivered the first far more than the second: the data has often been incomplete or unusable, which is why each rule revision tightens toward data quality rather than mere disclosure. The ultimate goal — lower prices through competition — remains largely unproven, obstructed by the structural reality that most care is not shoppable and insured patients are insulated from price. Transparency is a necessary foundation that has not yet, by itself, bent the price curve, and likely works more through payer and employer negotiation than individual shopping. Its eventual value depends on whether the data becomes good enough to use and whether it is paired with the complementary policies that address what transparency alone cannot.
