######## policyAnalysis-equ-001 | Social Determinants Screening in Clinical Settings: Evidence for Population Health ROI
Most of what determines health happens outside the clinic — in housing, food, transportation, and income. Health systems have responded by screening patients for these social determinants of health (SDOH), and in 2024 the practice moved from optional to expected as CMS tied screening to hospital quality measurement. This analysis examines what SDOH screening is, the evidence on whether it improves outcomes, the gap between screening and actually resolving needs, and what it takes to make it more than a box-checking exercise.
From Optional to Expected
Screening for social needs used to be a discretionary good-practice. As of 2024, CMS has made it a measured expectation: hospitals face a five-domain inpatient screening measure covering at least food insecurity, housing instability, transportation, utilities, and interpersonal safety, with NCQA scoring both whether systems screen and whether they intervene (CMSA; Findhelp). Positive responses map to ICD-10 Z-codes (Z55-Z65), and CMS encourages putting those codes on claims so it can analyze how social conditions drive cost. The shift matters because measurement plus payment is what turns a nice idea into standard operating procedure.
The largest real-world evidence comes from CMS's Accountable Health Communities (AHC) Model, which screened more than a million people across 28 communities and navigated those with needs to community resources. Its headline finding: food insecurity was the single most common health-related social need — a useful corrective to the assumption that housing or transportation dominate, and a guide to where screening most often leads (HCI; Camden Coalition).
SDOH screening crossed a threshold in 2024: CMS now measures it, NCQA scores both screening and intervention, and payers are attaching dollars to social-risk documentation. That changes it from a values statement into an operational requirement — and raises the stakes on whether screening actually leads anywhere.
The Screen-Without-Solve Problem
The central failure mode of SDOH screening is screening that goes nowhere. It is straightforward to ask a patient whether they have enough food; it is hard to do something about it. Many programs began by handing patients a printed resource guide — which is barely better than nothing, because the patient still has to navigate a fragmented social-services system on their own. The field's response is the closed-loop referral: an automated, tracked connection to a food bank, housing agency, or transportation service, with confirmation that the patient actually received help (Findhelp). The distinction is everything — an open-loop referral documents a need; a closed-loop referral resolves one.
The data exposes the gap. In programs that screen well, a large share of patients screen positive for at least one social need, a smaller share receive a referral, and a still smaller share successfully connect to the resource within a useful window. Each step loses people, so a program measured only on screening completion can look successful while changing nothing. Honest SDOH programs measure the whole funnel — screened, referred, connected, need resolved — not just the first step.
[{"Step":"Screen","Detail":"Ask the five domains; positive responses → Z-codes"},{"Step":"Refer","Detail":"Connect to a community resource — open-loop (guide) vs. closed-loop (tracked)"},{"Step":"Connect","Detail":"Patient actually reaches the resource — the biggest drop-off"},{"Step":"Resolve","Detail":"The underlying need is met — the only step that changes health"}]
The SDOH screening funnel (where programs leak)
The Data and Trust Question
SDOH screening also raises a question health systems are only beginning to grapple with: what happens to the data, and does collecting it help or harm the patient? Recording that someone is food-insecure, unstably housed, or experiencing interpersonal violence creates sensitive information that, once in the record, can be seen widely and could in principle be used against the patient — affecting how they are perceived, or surfacing in contexts (custody, immigration, insurance) where it was never meant to go. Patients are not naive about this, and some decline to disclose needs precisely because they distrust where the information will travel.
This matters operationally because under-disclosure undermines the whole exercise: a screening tool only works if patients answer honestly, and they will only answer honestly if they trust both the asker and the system behind them. That trust is easier to build when the person screening is someone the patient already trusts — a community health worker rather than an intake form — and when the system can actually offer help in return, so disclosure feels like a path to assistance rather than surveillance. SDOH screening, in other words, is as much a trust problem as a workflow problem, and systems that treat it as pure data collection tend to get incomplete and skewed data.
Does It Improve Outcomes?
The evidence is genuinely mixed and worth stating honestly. The logic is strong and the AHC model showed that navigation can connect people to resources, but rigorous proof that screening-and-referral programs improve hard health outcomes or lower total cost is still developing — the AHC evaluation found benefits more clearly for some needs and metrics than others, and effects depend heavily on whether the referral loop actually closes. Newer work shows that machine-learning models drawing on existing data can identify social needs more accurately than survey questions alone (AUC ~0.77 vs ~0.58), pointing toward better targeting — but identifying a need is not the same as meeting it. The defensible reading: screening is a necessary first step whose value is entirely contingent on the intervention that follows.
What Makes It Work
Programs that move outcomes rather than just document need share features. They invest in closed-loop referral infrastructure and the navigators or community health workers who actually do the connecting. They target the highest-yield needs — food insecurity first, given its prevalence — rather than screening broadly and acting narrowly. They build community partnerships so there is somewhere real to refer patients to, recognizing that a referral is worthless if the food bank or housing program is itself overwhelmed. And critically, they operate under value-based contracts that reward resolving needs, because under fee-for-service the work of closing a social-needs loop generates cost without revenue — the same wrong-pocket problem that limits prevention generally.
Who Should Do the Screening
A practical question that shapes whether screening works is who asks the questions and in what setting. A social-needs questionnaire handed to a patient at registration, divorced from any relationship, tends to produce low completion and guarded answers. The same questions asked by a trusted clinician, nurse, or community health worker who can immediately offer a connection to help produce more honest disclosure and a warmer handoff to resources. Setting matters too: the emergency department captures patients who avoid primary care but is a chaotic place to surface sensitive needs, while primary care offers continuity but misses the disconnected. The strongest programs match the screening modality to the population and pair the screen with a person, not just a form.
This connects SDOH screening to the broader workforce question. Because resolving social needs is labor-intensive, the screening strategy and the staffing strategy are inseparable: a system that screens at scale without the navigators or community health workers to act is generating documented needs it has no capacity to meet. The decision of who screens is therefore also a decision about whether the organization is prepared to do something with the answer.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether CMS's measurement expands from screening rates toward intervention and resolution measures, since that is what would force closed-loop investment; whether the evidence base matures from process metrics toward demonstrated outcome and cost effects; whether community-resource capacity keeps pace with referral volume (screening that floods under-funded social services helps no one); and whether value-based contracting spreads enough to fund the navigation labor that makes screening actionable. The trajectory of SDOH screening will be set less by the screening tools than by whether the system builds the capacity to act on what they find.
Bottom Line
SDOH screening is now an operational expectation, and that is progress — naming social needs is a precondition for addressing them. But screening is only as valuable as the intervention it triggers, and the field's defining risk is becoming very good at documenting needs it does not resolve. The programs that matter measure the full funnel, invest in closed-loop referral and the navigators who staff it, partner with adequately-resourced community organizations, and operate where payment rewards resolution rather than volume. SDOH screening is a means, not an end; judged as an end, it is theater, and judged as a means, its value depends entirely on what the system is built to do next.

The AHC Model found food insecurity the most common social need across 1M+ people. ######## policyAnalysis-equ-002 | Algorithmic Bias in Clinical AI: Detection Methods and Mitigation Strategies
Clinical algorithms — from risk scores to lab-result formulas to AI models — are supposed to be objective. Many are not. A series of landmark findings has shown that widely used clinical tools systematically disadvantage Black and other minority patients, and in 2024 the federal government made identifying and mitigating that bias a legal obligation. This analysis examines the canonical examples, the mechanisms that produce bias, the new regulatory duty under Section 1557, and what mitigation actually requires.
The Canonical Examples
Three cases anchor the field. The watershed was Obermeyer and colleagues' 2019 Science study, which found that a commercial algorithm used to manage the care of millions of patients systematically under-estimated the illness of Black patients — because it used historical healthcare spending as a proxy for health need, and less had historically been spent on Black patients with the same conditions. The model faithfully learned a pattern of unequal access and reproduced it as lower predicted need (Science, 2019).
The second is race-corrected eGFR, the kidney-function formula that for years adjusted Black patients' results upward on the assumption of higher muscle mass — making their kidneys look healthier than they were, delaying diagnosis, specialist referral, and transplant eligibility. After evidence of harm, the National Kidney Foundation and American Society of Nephrology recommended removing race from the equation. The third is pulse oximetry: the ubiquitous finger device overestimates blood-oxygen saturation in patients with darker skin, because its calibration data came largely from light-skinned subjects — a hardware bias that contributed to under-treatment, including during COVID-19 (PMC; FDA).
The pattern across the canonical cases is the same: a tool built on unrepresentative data or a flawed proxy encodes existing inequity and then dispenses it as objective fact. The bias is not in the math; it is in what the math was trained on and asked to predict.
How Bias Gets In
Algorithmic bias in medicine arises through a few recurring mechanisms. The first is a biased proxy: using a measurable stand-in (spending) for the real target (need) when the proxy itself reflects inequity — the Obermeyer mechanism. The second is unrepresentative training or calibration data: a model or device built mostly on one population performs worse on others — the pulse-oximetry mechanism. The third is race-based correction: explicitly building race into a formula on contested biological assumptions — the eGFR mechanism. The fourth is label and access bias: when the outcome a model learns from (a diagnosis, a procedure) is itself shaped by who historically had access to care, the model learns the access gap as if it were a clinical fact.
The common thread is that none of these is a coding error to be debugged. Each reflects a choice about data, proxies, or assumptions — which means bias cannot be prevented by better engineering alone. It requires deliberate evaluation of how a tool performs across subgroups, before and after deployment, by people empowered to act on what they find.
[{"Mechanism":"Biased proxy","Example":"Spending used for 'need' (Obermeyer 2019)"},{"Mechanism":"Unrepresentative data","Example":"Pulse oximetry calibrated on light skin"},{"Mechanism":"Race-based correction","Example":"Race-adjusted eGFR delaying kidney care"},{"Mechanism":"Label/access bias","Example":"Models learning historical access gaps as need"}]
Mechanisms of clinical algorithmic bias
The De-Racing Debate
Removing race from clinical algorithms is the right move in cases like eGFR, but the field has learned it is not a simple universal rule, and the nuance matters. Race is a social construct, not a biological one, so building it into a formula as if it were a genetic variable is usually wrong — and in eGFR it caused direct harm. But race in a dataset sometimes acts as a proxy for real, unmeasured factors (exposure, access, environmental burden), so naively deleting it can occasionally make a model less accurate for the very group it was meant to help. The sophisticated position is not 'race in' or 'race out' but: never use race as a biological input or a justification for differential treatment, and where race appears to carry predictive signal, find and use the real underlying variable (income, neighborhood, exposure) instead of the racial proxy.
This is why mitigation is harder than it sounds and cannot be reduced to a slogan. It requires understanding why a variable is in a model and what it stands for — work that demands clinical, statistical, and equity expertise together. The danger is that the legitimate push to de-race algorithms becomes mechanical, with organizations stripping race to satisfy a rule without doing the harder work of replacing it with the genuine causal factors, potentially trading a visible bias for a hidden one.
The New Legal Duty: Section 1557
In 2024 this stopped being only an ethics issue and became a compliance one. The HHS Office for Civil Rights' final rule under Section 1557 of the ACA (published May 6, 2024) explicitly prohibits discrimination through 'patient care decision support tools' — a deliberately broad term spanning flowcharts, clinical formulas, and AI/ML models. It cites race-corrected algorithms (eGFR, pulmonary-function equations), population health risk-prediction tools, and biased devices like pulse oximeters as examples of potential violations (Health Affairs; National Health Law Program).
Critically, the rule imposes affirmative, ongoing duties: covered entities must make reasonable efforts to identify decision-support tools that use a protected characteristic as an input and to mitigate the risk of discrimination from each tool's use. The general prohibition took effect May 1, 2025. This converts bias mitigation from optional best practice into a regulatory obligation backed by civil-rights enforcement — a significant shift in incentives for health systems and the vendors that sell them algorithms.
Section 1557's 2024 rule reframes algorithmic bias as discrimination, not just bad engineering. A health system can now be liable not only for building a biased tool but for using one without checking — which makes subgroup evaluation a compliance requirement, not a nicety.
What Mitigation Requires
Effective mitigation operates at three stages. In development: representative training data, subgroup performance reporting, and avoiding biased proxies and unjustified race correction. In validation: mandatory pre-deployment testing of how a tool performs across race, ethnicity, sex, age, and socioeconomic proxies — not just overall accuracy, which can hide subgroup failure. In deployment: ongoing post-market surveillance stratified by subgroup, because a model's performance drifts as populations and practice change. The recurring lesson is that overall accuracy is not enough; a model can be highly accurate on average and dangerously wrong for a subgroup, and only disaggregated evaluation catches it.
The Vendor Accountability Gap
A practical problem complicates the new legal duty: most health systems do not build their algorithms — they buy them, often as opaque components embedded in EHRs, imaging software, and risk-stratification platforms. Section 1557 places the obligation on the covered entity using the tool, but the entity frequently cannot see inside it: vendors treat training data, performance-by-subgroup, and model logic as proprietary, leaving the buyer legally responsible for discrimination it has no practical way to detect. This is the algorithmic equivalent of being told to inspect a sealed box.
Closing this gap requires pushing accountability up the supply chain. Buyers increasingly need to demand subgroup-performance documentation, validation data, and bias-audit results as a condition of purchase, and 'model cards' or nutrition-label-style disclosures are emerging to standardize what vendors must reveal. Until that transparency is routine, the legal duty and the practical ability to meet it are mismatched — the entity holds the liability while the vendor holds the information. How that tension resolves, through contracting norms or regulation reaching the vendors themselves, will determine whether the Section 1557 rule produces real audits or just paperwork attesting to boxes no one can actually open.
What to Watch
Key signals: how aggressively OCR enforces the Section 1557 algorithm provisions and what compliance norms emerge; whether health systems build real algorithm-inventory-and-audit programs or treat the rule as paperwork; whether the de-racing of clinical formulas (eGFR, pulmonary function, others) continues and reaches practice uniformly; whether the FDA tightens device-bias requirements (as it has begun to for pulse oximetry); and whether AI vendors furnish the subgroup-performance documentation buyers now need to meet their own legal duty. The combination of high-profile evidence and a new legal hook makes this one of the faster-moving equity fronts in health care.
Bottom Line
Clinical algorithmic bias is well-documented, harmful, and now legally actionable. The canonical cases — Obermeyer's risk algorithm, race-corrected eGFR, pulse oximetry — show a single pattern: tools that encode existing inequity and present it as objective. The 2024 Section 1557 rule turns mitigation into an affirmative duty, requiring health systems to find and fix discriminatory decision-support tools or face civil-rights liability. Meeting that duty means subgroup evaluation at development, validation, and deployment — because overall accuracy hides the failures that matter. The era of treating clinical algorithms as neutral by default is over; the question now is whether systems build the audit capacity the new obligation requires.
Sources

PMC — Mitigating Racial and Ethnic Bias in Clinical Algorithms: Scoping Review and Policy Implications
FDA — Performance Evaluation of Pulse Oximeters (skin-pigmentation bias)
Health Affairs Forefront — Section 1557 Rule Mandates Identification and Mitigation of Discriminatory Clinical Algorithms

Algorithmic Bias in Clinical AI: Detection Methods and Mitigation Strategies

######## policyAnalysis-equ-003 | Rural Healthcare Access: Facility Closures, Workforce Gaps, and Broadband Infrastructure
Rural Americans live sicker and die younger than their urban counterparts, and the gap is widening. The cause is not a single failure but a compounding one: hospitals close, the clinicians who remain are spread thinner, whole service lines like obstetrics disappear, and the broadband that might bridge the distance is itself missing. This analysis quantifies each layer of the rural access crisis — facility closures, workforce shortages, the collapse of maternity care, and the digital divide — and assesses the federal responses now in play.
The Closure Crisis
The most visible symptom is the disappearing hospital. The University of North Carolina's Sheps Center, which maintains the authoritative count, documents 153 rural hospital closures since 2010; counting facilities that converted away from inpatient care, acute inpatient access has ended in 182 rural communities over that period (Sheps Center). The pipeline of risk is larger still: the Chartis Center for Rural Health's 2025 analysis found 432 rural hospitals vulnerable to closure and roughly 46% of all rural hospitals operating with a negative margin — the weakest financial position the sector has been in since tracking began (Chartis, 2025).
The damage is geographically concentrated in the South and Midwest, and within those regions in counties that are sparsely populated, lower-income, and disproportionately Black — meaning closures fall hardest where baseline health is already worst. And the harm radiates beyond healthcare: a rural hospital is frequently one of its county's largest employers, so a closure removes both medical access and economic anchor at once.
A rural hospital closure is rarely just the loss of beds. It severs emergency stabilization, erases local jobs, and forces every future emergency — a heart attack, a car crash, a difficult birth — into a longer drive that the clock does not forgive.
Why Rural Hospital Economics Break
The closures are not the result of bad management so much as a structural economic trap. Rural hospitals combine low patient volume with high fixed costs — an emergency department, imaging, and 24/7 staffing cost roughly the same whether they serve 5 patients a day or 50 — so when volume falls below the level that covers those fixed costs, losses are mechanical (Saving Rural Hospitals). Layered on top is an unfavorable payer mix: Medicare and Medicaid covered about 72% of rural inpatient discharges in 2023, and both typically reimburse below the cost of care, leaving rural hospitals with little commercial revenue to cross-subsidize the shortfall (Commonwealth Fund, 2026).
The numbers are stark. MedPAC pegged the Medicare fee-for-service operating margin at roughly negative 12% in 2024; more than 700 rural hospitals lost money that year, and nearly 400 had losses of 5% or more (MedPAC; Commonwealth Fund). With thin reserves and little negotiating leverage against commercial insurers, a single bad year — a service-line loss, a labor-cost spike, a Medicaid rate that lags inflation — can push a marginal rural hospital from precarious to insolvent. This is also why Medicaid expansion matters so much: it converts uninsured, uncompensated patients into paying ones, and rural hospitals in non-expansion states run measurably worse margins as a result.
The Workforce Gap
Even where facilities survive, the clinicians do not follow the need. Rural areas have about 39.8 primary care physicians per 100,000 residents, against 53.3 in urban areas — roughly a quarter fewer doctors for a population that is older, poorer, and more burdened by chronic disease (Rural Health Information Hub). The maldistribution is starker for specialists and for behavioral health, where rural counties frequently have no psychiatrist within a long drive at all. The result is longer waits, longer travel, and care deferred until it becomes an emergency.
This is partly a pipeline problem — physicians tend to settle near where they train, and training is overwhelmingly urban — and partly an economics problem: rural practice often means lower reimbursement, less call coverage, and professional isolation. It is why scope-of-practice expansion for nurse practitioners and physician assistants, loan-repayment programs tied to rural service, and community-paramedicine models have all moved to the center of the rural workforce debate. None individually closes the gap.
Maternity Care: The Sharpest Edge
Nowhere is the access collapse more acute, or more dangerous, than in childbirth. By 2022, more than half of rural hospitals — 52.4% — no longer offered obstetric services, up from 43.1% in 2010, as 238 rural hospitals shed labor-and-delivery units over that period (Penn LDI). The downstream geography is stark: roughly 59% of rural counties are now classified as maternity care deserts, leaving an estimated several million women of childbearing age to travel long distances for prenatal and delivery care (March of Dimes; GAO, 2023).
The clinical consequences are not theoretical. When the nearest delivery unit is an hour or more away, the rate of out-of-hospital and pre-term births rises, prenatal visits fall, and obstetric emergencies — hemorrhage, eclampsia — arrive at emergency departments that are not equipped to manage them. Maternity-unit closures are concentrated in exactly the low-income, higher-minority rural counties that already have the worst maternal outcomes, so the closures widen an existing disparity rather than creating a new one.
[{"Dimension":"Hospital closures (since 2010)","Rural":"153 closed; 182 communities lost inpatient care","Source":"Sheps Center"},{"Dimension":"Hospitals vulnerable to closure","Rural":"432; ~46% running negative margins","Source":"Chartis 2025"},{"Dimension":"Primary care physicians / 100k","Rural":"39.8 (vs 53.3 urban)","Source":"RHIhub"},{"Dimension":"Hospitals without obstetric care (2022)","Rural":"52.4% (up from 43.1% in 2010)","Source":"Penn LDI"},{"Dimension":"Maternity care deserts","Rural":"59% of rural counties","Source":"March of Dimes"},{"Dimension":"Lack 100/20 Mbps broadband","Rural":"~28% (vs ~2% urban)","Source":"NTIA / FCC"}]
Rural access gaps at a glance
The Cost of Distance
When access disappears, distance becomes a clinical variable. Rural EMS call times average 92.8 minutes across all severity levels, against 74.1 minutes nationally, and the interval from a 911 call to an ambulance arriving on scene roughly doubles — about 14 minutes in rural settings versus 7 nationally (American College of Surgeons, 2025). Each rural hospital closure makes this worse: studies find closures add roughly 2.6 minutes to EMS transport time and over 7 minutes to total activation time, with the next-nearest hospital averaging about 12 miles farther away (PMC, 2020).
Those minutes are not abstract. Rural residents are significantly more likely than urban residents to die following traumatic injury, the fatality rate from a vehicle crash in a rural area is nearly double the urban rate, and survival from out-of-hospital cardiac arrest is lower — outcomes driven substantially by travel time to definitive care (RHIhub). In time-critical emergencies, the distance created by closures and the absence of nearby trauma capability is itself a cause of death.
The Digital Divide Undercuts the Obvious Fix
Telehealth is the intuitive answer to distance — and for many services it genuinely works — but it runs into the rural access crisis's quietest layer: connectivity. Roughly 28% of rural Americans lack access to fixed broadband at the 100/20 Mbps benchmark, against about 2% of urban residents; coverage is roughly 72% rural versus 98% urban, and in 32 states the urban-rural broadband gap actually widened in 2024 (NTIA; FCC; Route Fifty, 2024). Where the signal is weak, remote monitoring devices fail, video visits drop, and the very tool meant to compensate for the missing hospital cannot be deployed.
This is why broadband is now treated as health infrastructure. The $42.45 billion federal Broadband Equity, Access, and Deployment (BEAD) program, administered by the NTIA through the states, is the largest attempt yet to close the gap (NTIA; Brookings). But construction timelines are long, and prior subsidy programs have shown that connectivity can fade once funding ends — so broadband investment is necessary for telehealth-based rural access strategies but is years, not months, from delivering them.
Telehealth can substitute for the hospital that closed only where the broadband exists to carry it. In much of rural America, the two failures overlap — the place that lost its hospital is also the place that cannot get online.
What Is Being Tried
Federal and state responses fall into four buckets. On facility survival, the Rural Emergency Hospital (REH) designation — created by Congress and effective in 2023 — lets a struggling rural hospital drop inpatient services in exchange for enhanced outpatient and emergency payments, preserving emergency access where full inpatient care is no longer viable; uptake has been cautious. On payment, Medicaid expansion remains the single largest lever, because rural hospitals in non-expansion states carry far more uncompensated care and run worse margins than those in expansion states. On workforce, the tools are loan repayment, rural residency slots, scope-of-practice expansion, and telehealth-enabled specialty support. On connectivity, BEAD and related programs aim to make telehealth feasible in the first place.
Each addresses one layer, and none addresses the interaction among them — which is the core analytical point. A broadband grant does not help a county whose hospital has closed and whose physicians have left; a new REH preserves an emergency room but not obstetrics or specialty care; Medicaid expansion improves margins but does not by itself rebuild a maternity unit once the staff have scattered. Rural access is a system failure, and piecemeal fixes produce piecemeal results.
The Compounding Nature of the Crisis
The defining feature of rural access decline is that the layers feed one another, producing a downward spiral that no single intervention reverses. A hospital running negative margins cuts its least-profitable service line first — almost always obstetrics, which is expensive to staff around the clock for relatively few deliveries. Losing obstetrics drives young families away and removes a reason for a primary-care physician to stay, deepening the workforce gap. Fewer clinicians and services mean lower volume, which worsens the fixed-cost math that threatened the hospital in the first place. If the hospital ultimately closes, EMS transport times lengthen, the county loses one of its largest employers, the local tax base erodes, and the population that remains skews older and sicker — raising the share of Medicare and Medicaid patients and making any successor facility even harder to sustain.
Broadband sits awkwardly across this spiral: it is the one fix that could partially substitute for lost physical access through telehealth, but it is least available exactly where the spiral has run furthest. The communities that most need a virtual bridge to specialists, mental-health providers, and monitoring are the ones least likely to have the connectivity to build it. That overlap is why rural health and rural broadband policy have converged, and why neither succeeds alone.
What to Watch
Several indicators will show whether the trajectory bends. The first is the closure and conversion count: whether the REH pathway and any new rural funding slow the loss of inpatient and emergency access, or merely convert closures into a different label. The second is the maternity-desert map: whether obstetric-unit losses level off or continue, since this is the gap with the most immediate mortality stakes. The third is broadband buildout versus usage: not just miles of fiber laid under BEAD, but whether rural telehealth utilization actually rises and persists after the subsidies end. The fourth is the expansion gap: whether the remaining non-expansion states act, given that their rural hospitals remain the most financially exposed in the country.
Bottom Line
Rural health access is not deteriorating along one axis but several at once — facilities, workforce, maternity care, and connectivity — and the layers reinforce each other, which is why single-program fixes keep underperforming. The data is unambiguous: 153 hospital closures since 2010 and 432 more at risk, a quarter fewer primary care physicians than urban areas, a majority of rural hospitals no longer delivering babies, and more than one in four rural residents without adequate broadband. The federal response — REH designations, BEAD, the standing pressure for Medicaid expansion — is real but uncoordinated. The communities that recover will be the ones where payment, workforce, and connectivity interventions land together rather than in isolation; the ones that do not will keep converting the abstraction of 'access' into the concrete reality of a longer, sometimes fatal, drive.

Rural health access is failing on four reinforcing fronts at once — 153 hospital closures since 2010 with 432 more at risk, a quarter fewer primary care physicians than urban areas, a majority of rural hospitals no longer delivering babies, and more than 1 in 4 rural residents without adequate broadband. ######## policyAnalysis-equ-004 | Language Access in Healthcare: Legal Requirements and Implementation Gaps
Roughly one in twelve U.S. residents has limited English proficiency (LEP), and when they cannot communicate with their clinicians, care gets worse and more dangerous. Federal law has long required meaningful language access in health care, and a 2024 rule sharpened those obligations. This analysis examines the legal framework, the patient-safety stakes, the persistent implementation gaps, and why language access is both a civil-rights duty and a quality-of-care issue.
The Legal Framework
Two overlapping authorities govern language access: Title VI of the Civil Rights Act, which bars national-origin discrimination by recipients of federal funds, and Section 1557 of the ACA, which applies the same principle to health programs. The Section 1557 final rule effective July 5, 2024 sharpened the requirements: covered entities must take reasonable steps to provide meaningful access to people with LEP — and, newly, to their companions with LEP, such as a parent making decisions for a child (American Translators Association; HHS OCR).
The standard is specific about who counts as a qualified interpreter: a proficient bilingual person able to interpret accurately and impartially, who knows specialized medical vocabulary, preserves tone and meaning, and follows interpreter ethics. OCR has been explicit that relying on a bilingual employee who simply self-identifies as proficient does not meet the standard — a common shortcut that the rule forecloses. Services must be timely, free of charge to the patient, and delivered in a way that protects privacy.
Language access is not a courtesy — it is a civil-rights requirement under Title VI and ACA Section 1557. The 2024 rule's message is blunt: a qualified medical interpreter is mandatory, free to the patient, and a bilingual staffer pressed into service does not satisfy the law.
Why It Is a Patient-Safety Issue
Language barriers are not merely an inconvenience; they cause harm. LEP patients experience higher rates of adverse events and medical errors than English-proficient patients, because miscommunication corrupts every step of care — history-taking, diagnosis, medication instructions, and especially informed consent. A patient who cannot understand the risks of a procedure cannot meaningfully consent to it, and a patient who misunderstands discharge instructions is more likely to be readmitted. The technical density of medicine makes precise interpretation essential: a small mistranslation of a dose or a symptom can have outsized consequences.
This reframes language access from a compliance burden into a quality-and-safety intervention. The same interpreter requirement that satisfies Section 1557 also reduces errors, improves adherence, and supports the informed consent that is both ethically and legally required. Systems that treat interpretation as a cost to minimize are, in effect, accepting a higher rate of preventable harm for their LEP patients.
The Implementation Gap
The law is clear; practice lags. Many systems offer qualified interpretation reliably for the top one or two languages in their area but fall off sharply for less common languages — exactly the patients most at risk of being left without meaningful access. The common failure modes are predictable: using untrained bilingual staff or family members (including, dangerously, children) as ad hoc interpreters; treating telephonic interpretation as a universal substitute when video or in-person is clinically necessary; and failing to translate vital written documents. Each shortcut is cheaper in the moment and noncompliant — and unsafe.
The structural drivers are cost and convenience. Qualified interpretation, especially for rare languages and at all hours, is genuinely expensive and logistically hard, and there is no direct reimbursement for it in most settings, so it lands as an unfunded mandate that pressured systems under-resource. That funding gap is the core implementation problem: the obligation is firm but the financing is not, so the burden falls hardest on the safety-net providers serving the most LEP patients.
AI Translation: Promise and Peril
Machine translation and AI interpretation are arriving fast and could, in principle, address the hardest part of language access — the long tail of less-common languages for which a qualified human interpreter is rarely available at 3 a.m. The promise is real: instant, scalable access in dozens of languages at a fraction of the cost. But the peril is acute precisely because medicine is unforgiving of error. A mistranslated dose, a missed negation ('do not take'), or a culturally garbled explanation of risk can cause direct harm, and AI translation still makes exactly these kinds of confident mistakes — with no interpreter in the loop to catch them or to convey the tone and context a human would.
The responsible posture is therefore cautious and bounded: AI translation may be acceptable for low-stakes, routine communication (wayfinding, appointment logistics) but is not yet a substitute for a qualified human interpreter in high-stakes encounters — diagnosis, consent, complex instructions — where the cost of a subtle error is highest. As with clinical AI generally, the technology is a tool that requires human oversight in the situations that matter most, and treating it as a cheap replacement for interpretation in those settings would trade a compliance and safety obligation for a hidden risk.
What Closes the Gap
Progress comes from treating language access as infrastructure rather than ad hoc accommodation. That means contracted access to qualified interpreters across many languages (increasingly via video remote interpreting, which extends scarce interpreters efficiently while preserving the visual cues telephone loses); systematic identification and recording of each patient's preferred language so the need is known before the encounter; translation of vital documents; and staff training so clinicians know how to work with an interpreter and when an ad hoc substitute is unacceptable. Some states are also moving to reimburse interpretation through Medicaid, which addresses the financing gap that drives the shortcuts.
The Business Case Beyond Compliance
Framing language access purely as a legal mandate undersells it and, perversely, encourages the minimal compliance that produces unsafe shortcuts. The fuller case is that meaningful interpretation pays for itself in avoided harm and waste. LEP patients with poor communication have longer stays, higher readmission rates, more diagnostic testing (ordered to compensate for an unclear history), and higher rates of the adverse events that drive malpractice exposure — all of which cost money. Qualified interpretation reduces those downstream costs, and for a system bearing total-cost risk under value-based contracts, the interpreter is a cost-saving investment rather than an expense to minimize.
This reframing matters because it changes who within an organization champions language access. As a compliance line item it competes for scarce budget and loses; as a quality-and-safety and total-cost intervention it aligns with the incentives of risk-bearing systems and quality leaders. The systems that do language access well tend to be the ones that have stopped treating it as a regulatory cost and started treating it as part of delivering safe, effective care to a meaningful share of their patients — which, given that roughly one in twelve residents has LEP, it plainly is.
What to Watch
Key signals: how OCR enforces the sharpened Section 1557 language-access requirements after the June 2025 full-implementation deadline; whether more states add Medicaid reimbursement for interpretation to relieve the unfunded-mandate problem; whether video remote interpreting and, cautiously, AI-assisted translation expand access for rare languages without sacrificing the accuracy that safety requires; and whether systems extend reliable access beyond their top languages to the long tail of LEP patients who are currently least served. The tension throughout is between a firm legal duty and the cost of meeting it well.
Bottom Line
Language access is simultaneously a civil-rights obligation and a patient-safety necessity, and the 2024 Section 1557 rule tightened the first while the evidence on adverse events underscores the second. The law requires qualified, free, timely interpretation — not bilingual staff pressed into service — and now explicitly covers patients' companions. The persistent gap is implementation, driven by the cost of doing it well and the absence of direct reimbursement, which pushes systems toward unsafe shortcuts for exactly the patients most at risk. Closing it means building interpretation into the system as infrastructure and financing it — most promisingly through Medicaid — so that meaningful access is the default rather than the exception, especially for the long tail of less-common languages. New tools like video remote interpreting and, cautiously, AI translation can extend reach, but only as a complement to qualified human interpreters in the high-stakes encounters where a subtle error costs the most. The organizations that get this right will be those that treat language access not as a regulatory cost to minimize but as a core component of safe care for the substantial share of patients who cannot fully communicate in English — because the alternative is knowingly accepting a higher rate of preventable harm for the patients least able to advocate for themselves.
Sources

HHS OCR — Dear Colleague Letter on Section 1557 Language Access

AHA — OCR Clarifies Language Access Requirements (2024)

######## policyAnalysis-equ-005 | Community Health Worker Integration: Certification, Reimbursement, and Outcomes
Community health workers (CHWs) — trusted frontline members of the communities they serve who bridge patients and the health system — are one of the most evidence-backed and cost-effective tools for improving outcomes among high-risk and underserved populations. Yet they have long been hamstrung by unstable funding. A wave of Medicaid policy change is now putting CHWs on firmer financial footing. This analysis examines the evidence, the reimbursement shift, the certification questions, and the risks of formalizing a role whose value comes from its informality.
Why CHWs Work
The CHW's effectiveness comes from a quality the formal health system lacks: trust rooted in shared community. A CHW often speaks the patient's language, shares their background, and lives in their neighborhood — which lets them reach people the clinic cannot, understand the social context driving a patient's health, and provide the follow-through (reminders, navigation, coaching, connection to resources) that busy clinicians cannot. The evidence is robust: randomized trials of CHW programs for Medicaid beneficiaries with chronic disease have shown prevented hospitalizations and savings on the order of $2,500 per enrollee per year, alongside improved chronic-disease control and primary-care engagement (peer-reviewed RCTs; CHCS).
That combination — better outcomes and lower cost — is rare in health care, and it is why CHWs feature prominently in nearly every serious strategy for health equity, rural access, and SDOH. They are the human infrastructure that makes population health and social-needs programs actually work, because someone has to do the outreach the registry identifies and close the referral the screening triggers.
CHWs are one of the few interventions that improve outcomes and lower cost at once — RCTs show roughly $2,500 saved per Medicaid enrollee per year. Their value comes from trust the formal system cannot manufacture: they are of the community they serve.
The Reimbursement Shift
Historically, CHW programs lived on grants — soft money that disappeared when the grant ended, killing effective programs for lack of a sustainable payment model. That is changing. A wave of state Medicaid action has created pathways to reimburse CHW services: by early-to-mid decade, community-based organizations in more than 24 states could bill Medicaid for CHW services through state plan amendments or other mechanisms, with continued expansion (Milbank; NASHP). This is the single most important development for the field, because it converts CHWs from a perpetually-threatened pilot into a sustainable, billable service.
The shift is uneven, though. Medicaid reimbursement rates vary widely by state, and several states that launched CHW reimbursement have seen low uptake — often because providers judge the rates too low to cover the actual cost of the service. South Dakota, for instance, raised its rate (to roughly $64.86/hour) in response to feedback that the original rate did not work. The lesson: creating a billing pathway is necessary but not sufficient; the rate has to actually cover the cost, or the policy produces a benefit on paper that no one uses.
[{"Dimension":"Coverage","Detail":"24+ states have a Medicaid CHW reimbursement pathway (and growing)"},{"Dimension":"Certification","Detail":"State plan amendments typically require state-sponsored certification/training"},{"Dimension":"Rate adequacy","Detail":"Rates vary widely; low rates drive low uptake (e.g., SD raised to ~$64.86/hr)"},{"Dimension":"Evidence","Detail":"RCTs: ~$2,500/enrollee/yr saved; fewer ED visits and hospitalizations"}]
CHW Medicaid reimbursement: the state-by-state reality
Where CHWs Fit in the System
CHWs are not a freestanding intervention; they are the connective tissue that makes other strategies work, which is why they recur across this entire analysis layer. The SDOH screening discussed elsewhere only changes outcomes if someone closes the referral loop — that someone is often a CHW. The chronic-disease registry only prevents admissions if someone does the outreach it identifies — frequently a CHW. Rural access strategies lean on CHWs and community paramedics to extend a thin clinical workforce. Maternal-health programs use them to reach and advocate for at-risk mothers. In each case the CHW is the human who turns a system's intention into a patient's reality.
This cross-cutting role is also the strongest argument for sustainable financing. A CHW workforce funded by fragile grants cannot anchor permanent population-health, SDOH, and equity programs; one funded through Medicaid reimbursement can. The Medicaid shift therefore matters beyond CHWs themselves — it underwrites the labor layer that an entire generation of value-based and equity initiatives quietly depends on. Underfund it, and those initiatives lose the workforce that operationalizes them, no matter how well-designed they are on paper.
The Certification Tension
Reimbursement brings a trade-off the field is actively wrestling with. To bill Medicaid, states generally require CHWs to complete a state-sponsored certification or standardized training — which enhances credibility, ensures core competencies, and integrates CHWs into the health system. Oregon and Massachusetts built early certification programs along these lines. But there is a real tension: the CHW's effectiveness derives partly from being of the community rather than of the institution, and over-professionalizing the role — heavy training requirements, clinical language, institutional gatekeeping — risks screening out exactly the trusted community members who make CHWs work, or turning them into junior clinical staff who have lost the community rootedness that was the point.
The balance the best programs strike is to certify for core competencies and safety without erecting barriers that exclude community members, and to keep CHWs supervised and supported without absorbing them into the clinical hierarchy. Formalization is necessary for sustainability; the risk is that it quietly destroys the informality that created the value.
The Workforce Pipeline
Sustainable financing creates demand for CHWs faster than the pipeline can supply them, which is the next constraint the field will hit. Building a CHW workforce is unlike building a clinical one: the qualifications that matter most — community trust, lived experience, language and cultural concordance — are not produced by a training program and cannot be recruited from outside the community without losing the very thing that makes a CHW effective. This makes CHW workforce development inherently local: programs have to grow from the communities they serve, which is slower and less scalable than hiring clinicians off a national market, but is also the source of the role's value.
It also creates a career-ladder question. CHW roles have historically been low-paid and high-burnout, with little advancement, which limits retention of exactly the experienced workers programs most need. As Medicaid financing stabilizes the role, the systems that retain CHWs will be those that build genuine career pathways, supervision, and compensation that reflects the role's documented value — rather than treating CHWs as cheap, interchangeable labor. The reimbursement shift is the opportunity to professionalize the role's support and stability without professionalizing away its community character — the same balance the certification debate raises, applied to careers rather than credentials.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether more states adopt CHW Medicaid reimbursement and, crucially, whether they set rates high enough to drive real uptake rather than nominal coverage; whether certification requirements strike the balance between competency and community access or tilt toward over-professionalization; whether CHW programs are deployed where the evidence is strongest (chronic-disease management for high-risk Medicaid populations) or diluted across lower-yield uses; and whether rural and other underserved areas — where CHWs may matter most and the workforce is thinnest — can build and sustain programs under the new financing. The opportunity is real; execution and rate adequacy will decide whether it is realized.
Bottom Line
Community health workers are a rare win-win — better outcomes and lower cost, proven in randomized trials — and the spread of Medicaid reimbursement is finally putting them on sustainable financial footing after decades of grant dependence. The remaining work is execution: setting reimbursement rates that actually cover the cost so the billing pathway gets used, calibrating certification to ensure competency without screening out the community members whose trust is the whole point, and deploying CHWs where the evidence is strongest. Done well, the Medicaid shift could institutionalize one of the most effective equity interventions in American health care; done poorly — low rates, over-professionalization — it could formalize the role while hollowing out the trust that made it work. The deciding factors are unglamorous: whether states set rates that cover real costs, whether certification certifies competence without excluding community members, and whether systems build careers rather than churn through cheap labor. Get those right and CHWs become permanent infrastructure for equity, rural access, SDOH, and chronic-disease management alike; get them wrong and a proven intervention is institutionalized into ineffectiveness. Few health-policy choices offer this much proven return for this little money — which is exactly why the execution details deserve more attention than they usually get.
Sources
Milbank Memorial Fund — Medicaid Reimbursement for Community Health Worker Services (model SPA, 2025 update)
NASHP — State Community Health Worker Policies: 2024-2025 Trends
Center for Health Care Strategies — Leveraging Community Health Workers to Address Rural Health Care Needs
MARC — Community Health Workers in Medicaid: A Summary of State Plan Amendments
2026-03-03T08:00:00.000Z
community-health-worker-certification-reimbursement-outcomes
Community health workers improve outcomes and cut cost at once — RCTs show ~$2,500 saved per Medicaid enrollee per year — and a wave of Medicaid reimbursement (now 24+ states) is finally making them financially sustainable after decades of grant dependence. But low rates drive low uptake, and over-professionalizing certification risks destroying the community trust that makes CHWs effective. This analysis covers the evidence, the reimbursement shift, and the certification tension.
Community Health Worker Integration: Certification, Reimbursement, and Outcomes

######## policyAnalysis-pol-001 | Vermont's Act 167: Universal Primary Care and Its Fiscal Implications
Vermont is attempting something no other state has: deliberately restructuring its entire hospital system through global budgets, on the premise that the current model is mathematically unsustainable. Act 167 of 2022 is the legislative engine, and the 2024 Oliver Wyman report it commissioned laid out the stark arithmetic. This analysis examines what Act 167 does, the financial crisis driving it, the global-budget endgame it sets up, and why Vermont has become the most-watched natural experiment in state health reform.
What Act 167 Is
Passed by the Vermont Legislature in 2022, Act 167 funded the Green Mountain Care Board (GMCB) to lead a deep effort on hospital sustainability — analyzing the state's hospital finances and demographics, engaging communities, and, critically, developing all-payer value-based and global payments to Vermont hospitals (GMCB; Vermont Health Care Reform). It is not a one-off study; it is the framework for converting Vermont's hospital financing from fee-for-service toward fixed budgets. A central requirement is a timeline: the GMCB is to establish global hospital budgets for one or more non-critical-access hospitals by hospital fiscal year 2027 and for all Vermont hospitals by hospital fiscal year 2030.
Act 167 also commissioned the analytical foundation: a $1,050,000 fixed-price contract with Oliver Wyman to assess hospital sustainability, which produced the September 2024 report that has anchored the debate. And the framework continues to evolve — Act 68 of 2025 moved statewide health-care-transformation planning and coordination to the Agency of Human Services, and in 2025 all 14 Vermont hospitals were engaged in statewide and regional planning conversations (Vermont Business Magazine; legislative reports).
Act 167 commits Vermont to a hard timeline most states would not dare set: global budgets for at least one non-critical-access hospital by FY2027 and for all 14 hospitals by FY2030 — a deliberate, legislated transition away from fee-for-service for an entire state's hospital system.
The Crisis Driving It
Vermont did not choose global budgets out of ideology but out of arithmetic. The Oliver Wyman report examined Vermont's demographic projections and hospital finances and found a system under severe strain: an aging, shrinking working-age population, hospital finances that threaten the affordability of private insurance, and a structure the analysis indicated is not sustainable on its current trajectory. Vermont also has among the nation's highest health-insurance costs relative to income — the 'affordability crisis' that recurs throughout the state's health-policy debate. The premise of Act 167 is that incremental adjustment cannot fix a structurally unsustainable system, so the financing model itself has to change.
This is the logic of global budgeting: under fee-for-service, a hospital's revenue depends on volume, which both rewards unnecessary care and punishes a hospital when a shrinking population reduces demand. A global budget instead gives a hospital a fixed annual revenue regardless of volume, which stabilizes finances in a declining-population state and, in theory, flips the incentive from doing more to keeping people healthy. For a small, rural, aging state where several hospitals are financially fragile, that stability is the central appeal.
The Global-Budget Endgame
Vermont's destination is a system in which hospitals are paid fixed budgets by all payers rather than per service. The model draws explicitly on Maryland, the only state with a mature statewide hospital global-budget system, and on the Pennsylvania Rural Health Model's rural-specific application. The theory is that fixed budgets stabilize fragile hospitals, remove the incentive for volume-driven overtreatment, and let the state manage total hospital spending — addressing both the sustainability and affordability problems at once.
The execution risk is substantial, which is why the timeline matters. Setting a global budget requires deciding what each hospital's revenue should be — a politically and technically fraught determination — and getting all payers (Medicare, Medicaid, commercial) aligned, which is why Vermont has historically operated under an all-payer model agreement with the federal government. Done well, global budgets could make Vermont's system sustainable and affordable; done poorly, a budget set too low could starve a needed hospital, and one set too high locks in excess cost. The FY2027 and FY2030 deadlines force the state to make these hard calls on a clock.
[{"Element":"Lead body","Detail":"Green Mountain Care Board (transition to AHS under Act 68 of 2025)"},{"Element":"Analytical basis","Detail":"$1.05M Oliver Wyman hospital-sustainability report (Sept 2024)"},{"Element":"Endgame","Detail":"All-payer global hospital budgets"},{"Element":"Timeline","Detail":"≥1 non-CAH by FY2027; all 14 hospitals by FY2030"}]
Act 167: the framework
The Risks and the Critics
Act 167's ambition invites real objections, and taking them seriously is part of judging the experiment fairly. The first risk is regulatory capacity: setting fair global budgets for 14 distinct hospitals requires deep analytic capability and political insulation, and a board that gets the numbers wrong can do serious harm — a budget set too low to a hospital a community depends on is a closure by another name. The second is the all-payer alignment problem: global budgets only work if all payers participate, which depends on a federal agreement for Medicare and cooperation from commercial insurers; if any major payer opts out, the model leaks. The third is the service-mix question — a hospital under a fixed budget has an incentive to shed expensive, low-margin services (obstetrics, behavioral health), exactly the services rural communities can least afford to lose, unless the budget explicitly protects them.
Critics also note that global budgeting controls hospital cost but not necessarily total cost, since care can shift to non-hospital settings (the cost-shift Maryland experienced), and that a fixed budget can dull a hospital's responsiveness to genuine increases in community need. None of these is necessarily fatal — each can be addressed in design — but they are the reasons global budgeting is hard, and the reasons Vermont's execution, not just its intent, will determine whether the experiment succeeds. The legislated timeline is a forcing function, but a deadline does not make the underlying budget-setting any easier.
Why Everyone Is Watching
Vermont matters far beyond its small size because it is testing, in real time and at full state scale, whether global budgeting can rescue a structurally challenged hospital system. Maryland proved statewide global budgets can slow hospital spending growth, but Maryland is large and urban; Vermont is small, rural, and demographically declining — closer to the conditions facing much of rural America. If Vermont can stabilize fragile rural hospitals and bend its affordability curve through global budgets, it offers a template for the many states facing rural-hospital crises. If it cannot — if budgets prove impossible to set fairly or fail to control cost — that is a cautionary lesson of equal value. Either way, the next several years of Vermont's experiment will inform the national debate about whether fee-for-service can be replaced at scale.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether Vermont hits the FY2027 first-hospital and FY2030 all-hospital global-budget deadlines or slips them; how the budgets are set and whether the process can fairly value a hospital's revenue without starving essential services or locking in excess; whether the Act 68 transition to AHS strengthens or disrupts the effort; whether the all-payer alignment (especially federal Medicare participation) holds; and ultimately whether the model stabilizes Vermont's fragile hospitals and improves affordability, or proves too hard to execute. As the most ambitious state hospital-payment reform in the country, Vermont's trajectory is the one to watch for anyone tracking the future of hospital financing.
Bottom Line
Act 167 is Vermont's bet that a structurally unsustainable hospital system cannot be patched and must be re-financed — replacing fee-for-service with all-payer global budgets across all 14 hospitals by FY2030. The Oliver Wyman analysis supplied the grim arithmetic that justifies the gamble: an aging, shrinking state with unaffordable insurance and fragile rural hospitals. Global budgets offer real promise — stability for declining-volume hospitals and a brake on total cost — but the execution is genuinely hard, requiring fair budget-setting and all-payer alignment on a legislated clock. Vermont is now the country's foremost natural experiment in whether global budgeting can rescue a hospital system at state scale, and its success or failure will shape how every rural-heavy state thinks about the future of hospital payment. What makes Vermont's bet distinctive is its honesty about the stakes: rather than pretend incremental fixes can save a system the Oliver Wyman numbers show is failing, the state has committed, in statute and on a deadline, to rebuilding the financing model itself. That is either visionary or reckless depending on execution — and the next five years will tell which. For a country full of states watching their own rural hospitals slide toward the same cliff, Vermont is running the experiment they may all eventually have to run.
Sources

Vermont Health Care Reform — FAQ: Act 167 Hospital Transformation Report
Vermont Business Magazine — GMCB, Oliver Wyman statements regarding Act 167 final report
GMCB — Hospital Global Budget Technical Advisory Group

2026-01-15T08:00:00.000Z

Vermont's Act 167 (2022) commits the state to replacing fee-for-service with all-payer global hospital budgets — for at least one hospital by FY2027 and all 14 by FY2030 — on the premise, documented by a $1.05M Oliver Wyman report, that its aging, shrinking, unaffordable system is structurally unsustainable. This analysis covers the framework, the crisis driving it, the global-budget endgame, and why Vermont is the nation's most-watched state hospital-payment experiment.
Vermont's Act 167: Universal Primary Care and Its Fiscal Implications

######## policyAnalysis-pol-002 | PBM Reform Legislation: State-Level Transparency Requirements in 2025-2026
Pharmacy benefit managers (PBMs) sit at the center of the U.S. prescription-drug supply chain, negotiating with manufacturers, building formularies, and reimbursing pharmacies on behalf of insurers and employers. For most of their history they did this with little public scrutiny. That has changed: between aggressive Federal Trade Commission investigation, a wave of state legislation, and — as of early 2026 — a major federal statute, PBM regulation has become one of the most active fronts in American health policy. This analysis maps what the law now requires, what the evidence says it accomplishes, and where the binding legal constraints remain.
Why PBMs Drew Regulatory Fire
The case for intervention starts with concentration. The FTC's interim staff report found that the three largest PBMs — CVS Caremark, Cigna's Express Scripts, and UnitedHealth's OptumRx — processed nearly 80% of the roughly 6.6 billion prescriptions dispensed at U.S. pharmacies in 2023, and the top six processed more than 90% (FTC, 2024). That concentration is paired with vertical integration: each of the Big Three is owned by, or owns, a major insurer and its own pharmacies. The agency's second interim report found that pharmacies affiliated with the Big Three captured 68% of specialty-drug dispensing revenue in 2023, up from 54% in 2016 (FTC, 2025).
The FTC also documented how that market power converts into margin. It found the Big Three marked up numerous specialty generic drugs dispensed through their affiliated pharmacies by hundreds and in some cases thousands of percent, generating more than $7.3 billion in revenue above the drugs' estimated acquisition cost between 2017 and 2022, and reimbursing affiliated pharmacies at higher rates than unaffiliated ones on nearly every specialty generic studied (FTC, 2025).
The structural critique is not that PBMs charge for their services, but that an opaque, vertically integrated oligopoly can set the prices it pays itself, steer volume to its own pharmacies, and keep the difference — with neither the plans that hire them nor the pharmacies they pay able to see the spread.
How a PBM Actually Makes Money
Understanding the reform debate requires understanding the business model, because each regulatory tool targets a specific revenue stream. PBMs earn from at least five distinct sources. First, rebate retention: PBMs negotiate rebates from manufacturers in exchange for favorable formulary placement, pass some share to the plan sponsor, and may keep a portion — along with separate 'manufacturer administrative fees' for processing those rebates. Second, spread pricing: the PBM bills the plan more than it pays the pharmacy and keeps the difference; on a single prescription that spread might be a few dollars, but across billions of claims, and amplified on specialty drugs, it is substantial (Navitus, 2024). Third, per-claim administrative fees, typically a few dollars per script. Fourth, direct and indirect remuneration (DIR) fees — clawbacks recouped from pharmacies weeks or months after the sale, often tied to opaque 'performance' criteria (Pharmacy Times). Fifth, and increasingly the largest, profit from the PBM's own affiliated retail, mail-order, and specialty pharmacies.
The reform tools map onto these streams precisely. Spread-pricing bans attack the second; rebate pass-through mandates attack the first; delinking attacks the incentive structure beneath both by severing pay from price; anti-steering and any-willing-provider rules attack the fifth. This is why disclosure alone accomplishes little — it leaves every revenue stream intact and merely describes them.
The Rebate Trap and List-Price Inflation
The most consequential distortion is structural: because rebates are calculated as a percentage of a drug's list price, a PBM that retains any share of rebates has an incentive to prefer the drug with the highest list price and largest rebate — even when that drug carries the highest net cost to the payer and the highest out-of-pocket cost to the patient at the counter (Paragon Institute). This 'rebate trap' rewards list-price inflation. The canonical illustration is insulin: an analysis of Eli Lilly's Humalog found its list price rose 27% between 2015 and 2019 while its net price actually fell 10% — the gap captured by the supply chain, not the manufacturer (Pharmaceutical Commerce).
Analysts quantify the aggregate distortion as the 'gross-to-net bubble' — the gap between drugs' list prices and their net prices after rebates and discounts — which reached roughly $334 billion in 2023 (Drug Channels, 2024). In February 2026, the FTC secured a settlement with Express Scripts over allegations that it inflated insulin costs by steering formulary placement toward rebate size rather than net price, the agency's first concrete enforcement action flowing from its PBM investigation (FTC, 2026).
The rebate system quietly inverts the incentive a buyer's agent is supposed to have. A PBM paid as a percentage of list price does better when the sticker price is higher — which is why 'delinking' pay from price, not merely disclosing it, became the reform that mattered.
The Independent-Pharmacy Squeeze
The clearest real-world signal of stress is at the pharmacy counter. Independent and community pharmacies report being reimbursed below their drug acquisition cost on a meaningful share of prescriptions, a dynamic that is unsustainable at scale. Industry trackers recorded more than 3,000 net U.S. pharmacy closures in 2024, with independents disproportionately represented, deepening 'pharmacy deserts' in rural and underserved urban areas (Drug Topics, 2024; Economic Liberties, 2025).
This is the political engine behind reform. Pharmacy closures are visible, local, and bipartisan in their impact, which is why PBM bills have advanced in red and blue states alike and why the issue finally cleared Congress after years of stalling.
The State Legislative Wave
State action is now near-universal. According to the National Academy for State Health Policy, all 50 states have enacted at least some PBM regulation, and the pace accelerated sharply in the most recent cycles — 20 states enacted 33 PBM bills in 2024 alone (NASHP, 2025; MultiState, 2025). The substance clusters into a few recurring tools.
[{"Mechanism":"Spread-pricing ban","What it does":"Bars PBMs from charging plans more than they pay pharmacies and pocketing the difference","Example":"Florida SB 1550 (2023)"},{"Mechanism":"Rebate pass-through","What it does":"Requires manufacturer rebates be passed to plan sponsors or patients (often 100%)","Example":"Florida SB 1550"},{"Mechanism":"Delinking","What it does":"Prohibits tying PBM pay to a drug's price; mandates flat service fees","Example":"California SB 41; Colorado (2025)"},{"Mechanism":"Reimbursement floor","What it does":"Requires pharmacies be paid at least their acquisition cost","Example":"Arkansas Act 900"},{"Mechanism":"Licensure & disclosure","What it does":"Requires state registration and reporting of pricing, fees, and rebates","Example":"Most 2024-2025 statutes"}]
Common state PBM-reform mechanisms
The 2025 cycle pushed beyond transparency toward structural change. California's SB 41 and Colorado's delinking law both attack the core incentive problem directly by prohibiting PBM compensation from being tied to a drug's list price and requiring flat-fee models instead — a more aggressive intervention than disclosure mandates because it changes how PBMs make money, not merely what they must report (MultiState, 2025).
Does Transparency Actually Lower Costs?
The honest answer is: partially, and not by transparency alone. Disclosure requirements improve oversight but are readily absorbed by sophisticated firms, which have responded to spread-pricing bans by shifting revenue into administrative and 'service' fees that fall outside the regulated category. That is precisely why the policy frontier moved from transparency to delinking and reimbursement floors — rules that constrain the economics rather than merely illuminate them. The GAO's review of selected states' PBM oversight similarly cautioned that reporting regimes vary widely in scope and enforcement, limiting their comparability and bite (GAO, 2024).
Transparency is necessary but not sufficient. A PBM that must disclose its spread can eliminate the spread and recover the margin through a renamed fee. Reforms that bind — delinking, flat fees, acquisition-cost floors — are the ones the industry has fought hardest.
The ERISA Ceiling on State Power
State PBM laws operate under a federal ceiling: the Employee Retirement Income Security Act (ERISA), which preempts state laws that 'relate to' employer-sponsored benefit plans. Because self-insured employer plans cover a large share of privately insured Americans and are shielded from state insurance regulation, ERISA preemption is the central legal vulnerability of every state PBM statute.
Two cases define the current boundary. In Rutledge v. Pharmaceutical Care Management Association (2020), the Supreme Court ruled 8-0 that ERISA did not preempt an Arkansas law setting minimum pharmacy reimbursement rates, reasoning that a law which merely raises costs or alters incentives — without dictating plan benefit design — is permissible cost regulation (Rutledge v. PCMA, 2020). But in PCMA v. Mulready, the Tenth Circuit held in 2023 that ERISA and Medicare Part D preempted parts of an Oklahoma law that dictated pharmacy-network design, including 'any willing provider' and anti-steering provisions; the Supreme Court declined to review that ruling on June 30, 2025, leaving it in force (Epstein Becker Green, 2025; Wagner Law Group, 2025).
States may regulate what PBMs pay and how they disclose it; they may not dictate the network and benefit design of an ERISA plan. Rutledge opened the door; Mulready marked its frame.
Federal Reform Finally Lands
For years, federal PBM reform passed the House or cleared committee only to fall out of year-end spending deals — most notably when bipartisan provisions were stripped from the December 2024 continuing resolution. That changed in early 2026. The Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026, signed into law on February 3, 2026, enacted the most consequential federal PBM reform to date (Sidley Austin, 2026).
Its centerpiece is delinking in Medicare Part D: beginning January 1, 2028, PBM compensation for Part D plans must take the form of a 'bona fide service fee' — a flat dollar amount reflecting the fair-market value of services — rather than a percentage of a drug's price or rebates. The law also imposes detailed transparency reporting on Part D PBMs, covering utilization, pricing, affiliated-pharmacy activity, and manufacturer contracts, with annual reports due beginning July 1, 2028 (AJMC, 2026; Managed Healthcare Executive, 2026). By moving delinking from the states into federal Medicare law, Congress sidestepped the ERISA problem for a large slice of the market that states could never have reached.
What This Means for Stakeholders
For employers and plan sponsors, the immediate task is contractual: the combination of state delinking laws and federal Part D reform makes transparent, pass-through, flat-fee PBM arrangements both more available and more defensible, and sponsors that have not re-examined their PBM contracts are likely leaving money on the table and carrying fiduciary risk. For independent pharmacies, reimbursement floors and anti-steering rules are existential, but their durability depends on surviving ERISA challenge — which, post-Mulready, means rate rules are safer ground than network mandates. For PBMs, the era of unexamined spread is closing; the strategic response is migrating to fee-for-service models and defending the remaining gray zones in court.
What to Watch Through 2028
Several concrete indicators will reveal whether this regulatory wave changes outcomes or merely rearranges them. The first is fee migration: if PBM administrative and 'service' fees rise as spread and rebate retention fall, the reforms will have relocated margin rather than reduced it — a pattern already visible after early spread bans. The second is the gross-to-net bubble: a genuine effect of delinking should show up as slowing list-price inflation and a shrinking gap between list and net prices, which analysts expect to begin deflating as net-price contracting spreads. The third is pharmacy survival: if reimbursement floors and anti-steering rules hold, the rate of independent-pharmacy closures and the spread of pharmacy deserts should stabilize. The fourth is litigation: every aggressive state statute will be tested against the Rutledge–Mulready line, and the boundary between permissible rate regulation and preempted network design will keep moving in the circuit courts.
Finally, watch the federal effective dates. The Consolidated Appropriations Act's Part D delinking does not bind until January 1, 2028, with transparency reporting following in July 2028. That lag gives PBMs eighteen-plus months to restructure, and gives analysts a natural experiment: Medicare Part D under delinking versus a commercial market still governed by the patchwork of state law and ERISA limits. The comparison will be the clearest evidence yet on whether delinking lowers net drug spending or simply changes who captures the margin.
Bottom Line
PBM regulation has matured from a transparency project into a structural one. The evidence that concentration and vertical integration inflate drug costs is now documented by the federal government's own competition regulator; states have responded with an escalating toolkit that culminated in delinking; and Congress, after repeated failures, wrote delinking into Medicare Part D in 2026. The unresolved questions are enforcement capacity, the ERISA limits on state reach, and whether flat-fee economics genuinely lower net drug spending or simply relocate PBM margin once more. Those are the metrics worth watching as the 2028 federal effective dates approach.

Pharmacy benefit manager regulation has matured from a transparency project into a structural one — FTC investigation, an escalating wave of state delinking laws, and federal Medicare Part D delinking in the 2026 Consolidated Appropriations Act.
