######## policyAnalysis-pol-003 | Medicaid Redetermination Aftermath: Enrollment Cliff and State Response Strategies
When the COVID-19 public health emergency ended, so did the rule that had kept anyone from being dropped from Medicaid for three years. The result was the largest coverage event in the program's history: the 'unwinding,' in which every Medicaid enrollee in the country had to be re-evaluated for eligibility. This analysis examines what happened, why so many people lost coverage for paperwork rather than ineligibility, the enormous variation between states, and the lasting lessons about how enrollment systems either protect or shed eligible people.
What the Unwinding Was
During the pandemic, the Families First Coronavirus Response Act gave states extra federal funding in exchange for maintaining continuous Medicaid enrollment — nobody could be disenrolled regardless of changes in income or circumstance. That protection swelled Medicaid enrollment to record levels. When it ended in spring 2023, states restarted eligibility redeterminations for the entire caseload at once, a logistical undertaking with no precedent. The process stretched across roughly 18 months through 2024.
The aggregate outcome: more than 25 million people were disenrolled — about 31% of completed renewals — while roughly 56 million (69%) were renewed (KFF). Yet despite that staggering churn, total Medicaid/CHIP enrollment ended up about 10 million higher than pre-pandemic levels, because the program had grown so much during the continuous-enrollment period. The unwinding did not shrink Medicaid below its old baseline; it discharged the pandemic-era surge, but did so in a way that swept up many people who were still eligible.
The unwinding's defining failure: of the 25+ million people disenrolled, roughly 69% lost coverage for procedural reasons — missed paperwork, outdated addresses, unreturned forms — not because they were found ineligible. Most coverage loss was administrative, not eligibility-based.
The Procedural-Disenrollment Problem
The most important and damning statistic is this: nearly seven in ten disenrollments (69%) were procedural, meaning the person lost coverage because of a paperwork failure — a form not returned, mail sent to an old address, an unanswered request for information — rather than a determination that they earned too much to qualify (KFF). Only about 31% were disenrolled because they were actually found ineligible. This means a large share of the 25 million were people who likely still qualified but fell through an administrative crack, becoming uninsured or churning back onto Medicaid weeks or months later after a gap in coverage.
Procedural disenrollment is not a neutral administrative event. A gap in coverage means interrupted medications, deferred care, and surprise bills, and the churn of leaving and re-enrolling imposes costs on patients, providers, and the program alike. Children were a particular concern, which is why, beginning January 2024, states are required to provide 12 months of continuous eligibility for children regardless of mid-year changes — a structural fix aimed squarely at the churn the unwinding exposed.
The Enormous State Variation
The single clearest finding of the unwinding is that outcomes depended overwhelmingly on how a state ran its process, not on its population. Disenrollment rates ranged from over 50% in states like Montana (57%), Utah, Idaho, Oklahoma, and Texas down to under 20% in North Carolina, Maine, Oregon, California, and Connecticut (KFF). A nearly threefold spread in the share of enrollees who lost coverage cannot be explained by differences in who was actually eligible; it reflects differences in administrative design. Two neighboring states with similar populations could produce opposite outcomes purely on the strength of their eligibility systems and the choices they made about how aggressively to pursue automatic renewal and how much grace to extend before terminating coverage.
The mechanism behind the variation is largely the ex parte renewal — the automatic, data-driven renewal that confirms eligibility from existing records (income, tax, benefit data) without making the enrollee fill out anything. Ex parte rates ranged from 90% or higher in Arizona, North Carolina, and Rhode Island to 11% or less in Pennsylvania and Texas (KFF). A state that can automatically renew 90% of its caseload barely troubles its enrollees; a state that automatically renews only 11% forces nearly everyone through a paperwork gauntlet, and predictably loses far more eligible people to procedural drops. Administrative capacity, not policy generosity, was destiny.
[{"Metric":"Total disenrolled","Value":"25M+ (~31% of completed renewals)","Source":"KFF"},{"Metric":"Procedural share","Value":"~69% of disenrollments (vs 31% ineligible)","Source":"KFF"},{"Metric":"Net enrollment","Value":"~10M above pre-pandemic levels","Source":"KFF"},{"Metric":"Disenrollment range","Value":"57% (MT) to <20% (NC, ME, OR, CA, CT)","Source":"KFF"},{"Metric":"Ex parte renewal range","Value":"90%+ (AZ, NC, RI) to ≤11% (PA, TX)","Source":"KFF"}]
The unwinding by the numbers
Where the Disenrolled Went
Losing Medicaid does not necessarily mean becoming uninsured, and the unwinding's true human cost depends on where the 25 million landed. Some were genuinely no longer eligible and moved to employer coverage or subsidized ACA marketplace plans — and the marketplaces, aided by enhanced subsidies, absorbed a meaningful share, with special enrollment pathways created to catch people transitioning off Medicaid. For these people, the unwinding was a coverage transition, disruptive but not catastrophic. The concern is the others: those who were procedurally dropped while still eligible, and those who fell into the gap with no affordable alternative.
The data on final destinations is still firming up, but the structural worry is clear. People dropped for paperwork often do not immediately enroll elsewhere — they discover they are uninsured at the pharmacy counter or the ER, after a gap. Children, despite the new continuous-eligibility protection, were disenrolled in large numbers during the process. And in non-expansion states, an adult who loses Medicaid may have no subsidized option at all, falling into the coverage gap. The unwinding's coverage losses are therefore unevenly consequential: a managed transition for some, a hard fall into uninsurance for others, with the difference driven heavily by state policy and the same administrative capacity that drove the disenrollments in the first place.
The Lasting Lessons
The unwinding was, in effect, a natural experiment in enrollment-system design, and its lessons outlast the event. First, administrative friction is coverage policy: a renewal process that relies on enrollees returning paperwork will shed eligible people, and the more it can renew automatically from data, the more it protects them. States that invested in ex parte capacity, data-sharing, and modern eligibility systems kept eligible people covered; states that relied on returned mail did not. Second, the procedural-disenrollment problem is fixable through design — continuous eligibility for children, robust ex parte processes, multiple contact channels, and grace periods all reduce avoidable loss.
Third, the event reframed Medicaid 'enrollment' as something the system does to people as much as something people do. Whether an eligible person stays covered depends less on their own diligence than on whether their state's system is built to keep them — a profound point for a program covering more than 80 million Americans, and one that should inform every future eligibility process.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether states sustain the ex parte and continuous-eligibility improvements forced by the unwinding or let them lapse; whether the children's 12-month continuous-eligibility requirement measurably reduces child coverage churn; whether the people procedurally disenrolled found other coverage (employer, ACA marketplace) or became uninsured; and whether future policy changes (work requirements, more frequent redeterminations) re-introduce the administrative friction the unwinding showed is so costly. The unwinding's core lesson — that enrollment-system design determines who stays covered — will be tested again with every change to Medicaid eligibility rules.
Bottom Line
The Medicaid unwinding disenrolled more than 25 million people, but its defining feature was that roughly 69% of those losses were procedural — eligible people dropped for paperwork, not ineligibility — and that outcomes varied threefold between states based on administrative design rather than population. The clearest lesson is that enrollment friction is coverage policy: states that could renew automatically from data protected their enrollees, while those reliant on returned forms shed them. The structural fixes — ex parte renewal, continuous eligibility for children, modern eligibility systems — are known. Whether they are sustained, and whether future eligibility changes heed the unwinding's evidence, will determine how many eligible Americans keep the coverage they qualify for. The unwinding should be remembered not as a one-time pandemic cleanup but as the clearest demonstration in a generation that the design of an enrollment system — how much it automates, how much paperwork it demands, how many chances it gives — is itself a determinant of who has health coverage. Any policy that adds redetermination frequency or paperwork requirements is, on this evidence, a coverage-reduction policy whether or not it is labeled one; and any state serious about covering its eligible population should be investing in the ex parte and continuous-eligibility infrastructure the unwinding proved decisive.
Sources

KFF — Medicaid Enrollment and Unwinding Tracker

Public Health Mandates
2026-02-05T08:00:00.000Z
medicaid-redetermination-aftermath-state-response
The Medicaid 'unwinding' disenrolled 25M+ people as pandemic continuous-enrollment ended — but ~69% lost coverage for procedural/paperwork reasons, not ineligibility, and disenrollment ranged threefold across states (57% in Montana to under 20% elsewhere) based on administrative design. The driver was ex parte renewal capacity (90%+ in some states, ≤11% in others). This analysis covers what happened and the lasting lesson that enrollment friction is coverage policy.

######## policyAnalysis-pol-004 | Certificate of Need Laws in 2026: Expansion, Contraction, and the Rural Hospital Exception
Certificate of Need (CON) laws require health care providers to prove to a state board that a community 'needs' a new facility, bed, or major piece of equipment before they can build it. Born of 1970s cost-control theory, they survive in most states and have become one of the most ideologically charged debates in health policy. This analysis examines what CON laws do, the evidence on their effects on cost and access, the accelerating repeal movement, and why the rural question — where both sides claim the moral high ground — is genuinely unresolved.
What CON Laws Do and Why They Exist
Under a CON regime, a provider cannot add hospital beds, open an ambulatory surgical center, buy an MRI scanner, or launch a new service line without first obtaining state approval by demonstrating community need — often over the objections of existing providers who can intervene in the process. The original 1970s rationale was cost control: in a world where more capacity meant more (often unnecessary) utilization and spending, limiting supply was supposed to restrain costs. As of early 2025, 35 states plus Washington, D.C., still operate some form of CON program (Mercatus; Cicero Institute).
The theory has aged poorly. The core premise — that restricting supply restrains cost — runs against basic competition economics, and critics argue CON does the opposite: by letting incumbents block competitors, it entrenches local monopolies and raises prices. That critique has moved CON from a technocratic cost-control tool to a target of bipartisan-ish reform, championed by free-market groups but increasingly joined by rural-access advocates.
The Evidence on Cost
The cost evidence runs against CON. KFF found health care costs roughly 11% higher in CON states than non-CON states, and studies of repeal show spending by physicians and hospitals falling — by around 4% within five years of repeal in some analyses — with one model finding unhealthy individuals in CON states spent about 12% more than their counterparts in non-CON states (KFF; Mercatus). The mechanism is the one competition economics predicts: where incumbents can block new entrants, prices stay higher and supply stays constrained. The weight of the economic literature is that CON laws raise costs rather than lower them — the opposite of their founding purpose.
Certificate of Need laws were created to lower health-care costs by restricting supply. The evidence suggests they do the opposite: costs run roughly 11% higher in CON states (KFF), consistent with the competition theory that letting incumbents block rivals entrenches higher prices.
The Rural Paradox
The hardest part of the CON debate is rural, and it is genuinely contested. Defenders argue CON protects rural hospitals: by preventing a competitor from skimming the profitable services (imaging, surgery) that cross-subsidize a rural hospital's money-losing essentials (the ER, obstetrics), CON keeps fragile rural hospitals viable. Vermont, for instance, credits CON with sustaining its rural hospitals. The fear is that repeal lets cherry-picking ambulatory surgical centers open and bleed the rural hospital of its margin, hastening closure.
But the recent evidence cuts the other way. A 2024 study found that repealing CON for ambulatory surgical centers led to a 44-47% increase in ASCs overall and roughly doubled them in rural areas (92-112%) — without causing hospital closures; researchers found no evidence that CON repeal is associated with rural hospital closures, and some models showed repeal associated with fewer service reductions (Pacific Legal; STAT; University of Chicago analysis). In other words, the data suggests CON restricts rural access (fewer facilities, fewer services) more than it protects rural hospitals from closure. The protective story is intuitive; the access-restricting story is what the causal evidence increasingly supports.
[{"Question":"Cost","Finding":"~11% higher in CON states; spending falls after repeal (KFF/Mercatus)"},{"Question":"Access","Finding":"Repeal → more ASCs (+44-47%; ~2x in rural) without hospital closures"},{"Question":"Rural protection claim","Finding":"Little causal evidence CON prevents closures; may reduce access"},{"Question":"Status (2025)","Finding":"35 states + DC retain CON; repeal momentum (NC, TN)"}]
CON laws: the evidence
Why the Evidence Doesn't Settle It
If the cost and access evidence runs against CON, why do 35 states keep it? Part of the answer is politics: CON creates a powerful constituency of incumbent providers who benefit from blocking competition and lobby fiercely to preserve it, while the beneficiaries of repeal (future entrants, patients who would gain access) are diffuse and unorganized. The repeal fight is therefore a classic case of concentrated benefits versus dispersed ones, and the incumbents usually have the better-funded voice in the state legislature.
Part is genuine uncertainty. The literature, while leaning against CON, is not unanimous — some studies find minimal cost effects, and others tie CON to reduced access for specific services like hospice or dialysis, complicating any clean verdict. And the rural-protection argument, even if the causal evidence is weak, is not crazy: it is genuinely true that a cherry-picking ASC can erode a rural hospital's margin, even if the data so far does not show repeal causing closures. The honest position is that CON's costs are fairly well-established while its rural effects remain contested — which is exactly why the safeguarded, monitored repeals like Tennessee's are valuable: they will generate the causal evidence the debate has lacked.
The Accelerating Repeal Movement
Reform is moving faster than at any point in decades. North Carolina is on track for near-total CON repeal by January 2025; Tennessee has set a path to repeal by December 2027, paired with requirements that newly-freed ASCs serve Medicaid and charity patients and a six-year assessment to monitor effects; and states like New York have loosened CON review to speed provider investment (Becker's ASC). The Tennessee model is notable for pairing repeal with safeguards and measurement — an attempt to capture the access and cost benefits of repeal while addressing the legitimate equity concern that freed providers will serve only well-insured patients.
What Repeal Does and Doesn't Touch
It is worth being precise about CON's actual scope, because the debate often inflates it. CON governs the supply side — who may build, expand, or add equipment — but it does nothing about the demand and price dynamics that drive most health spending: it does not set prices, does not address hospital or insurer consolidation, and does not touch the fee-for-service incentives that reward volume. So even a full repeal, while it may lower costs at the margin and expand facility access, is not a systemic cost-control measure. Treating CON repeal as a major affordability lever overstates it in both directions — opponents overstate the harm and proponents overstate the benefit.
This matters for prioritization. A state with limited political capital for health reform has to weigh CON repeal against bigger levers — site-neutral payment, antitrust enforcement against provider and insurer consolidation, price transparency with teeth, and payment-model reform. CON repeal is a reasonable, evidence-supported deregulation, but it is a modest piece of the affordability puzzle, and selling it as a transformational fix sets up disappointment. The honest framing is that CON repeal removes an anticompetitive barrier that the evidence says raises costs and restricts access at the margins — worth doing, but not a substitute for confronting the larger structural drivers of health spending.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether the wave of repeals (NC, TN, others) produces the access gains and cost reductions the evidence predicts, or the rural-hospital harms defenders fear — the Tennessee six-year assessment will be a key natural experiment; whether repeal states pair deregulation with safeguards (Medicaid/charity-care requirements) to prevent cream-skimming; and whether the remaining CON states reform, double down, or hold. The CON debate is one of the rare health-policy questions where a real-time, multi-state natural experiment is unfolding, so the evidence base should sharpen considerably over the next few years.
Bottom Line
Certificate of Need laws are a 1970s cost-control idea that the evidence has largely turned against: costs run higher in CON states, repeal is associated with lower spending and more facilities, and the rural-protection rationale — CON's strongest remaining defense — finds little support in causal studies, which instead suggest CON restricts rural access more than it prevents closures. A real repeal movement is now underway, with North Carolina and Tennessee leading and building in safeguards and measurement. The genuinely open question is the rural one, and the next few years of multi-state natural experiments should answer it. For now, the weight of evidence favors reform — paired, sensibly, with the charity-care and monitoring safeguards that address the legitimate fear that deregulation serves the well-insured first. The broader lesson is a caution against supply restriction as a cost-control strategy: CON embodied the intuitive but mistaken idea that less capacity means less spending, when in competitive markets the opposite tends to hold. As states unwind these laws, the policy worth watching is not merely whether they repeal but whether they replace CON's blunt entry barrier with smarter tools — transparency, targeted support for essential rural services, and obligations on new entrants — that pursue access and affordability without protecting incumbents from competition.
Sources

STAT — Certificate of need laws hurt, not help, rural health care, expert says
Pacific Legal — Certificate of Need Reform: Answering the Fears (ASC repeal evidence)
Becker's ASC — The certificate-of-need shakeup: states updating policies (NC, TN, NY)

Certificate of Need Laws in 2026: Expansion, Contraction, and the Rural Hospital Exception

######## policyAnalysis-pol-005 | Surprise Billing Enforcement: Two Years Under the No Surprises Act
The No Surprises Act ended one of the most hated features of American health care: the surprise out-of-network bill that ambushed patients who had done everything right. On patient protection, it has largely worked. But the machinery built to settle the resulting payment fights between providers and insurers — the Independent Dispute Resolution (IDR) process — has been overwhelmed, gamed, and repeatedly re-litigated. This analysis examines what the law fixed, why its arbitration system buckled, who is winning, and the reforms now reshaping it.
What the Law Fixed
Effective January 2022, the No Surprises Act (NSA) protects patients from surprise out-of-network bills in the situations where they had no real choice: emergency care, and care from out-of-network clinicians (anesthesiologists, radiologists, assistant surgeons) at in-network facilities. The patient now pays only their normal in-network cost-sharing; they are taken out of the middle. This consumer protection is the law's clear success — patients are no longer billed thousands of dollars for an out-of-network anesthesiologist they never chose. The hard part was never protecting patients; it was deciding who pays what once the patient is removed from the dispute.
The IDR System Overwhelmed
To settle provider-insurer payment disputes, the NSA created Independent Dispute Resolution — a 'baseball-style' arbitration in which each side submits an offer and an arbiter picks one. The system was built for a trickle and hit a flood. CMS originally estimated about 17,000 disputes per year; by 2024 there had been more than 5 million disputes since the law took effect — roughly 300 times the projection (CRS; CMS). The backlog ballooned: even after closure rates improved from 29% at the end of 2022 to 85% by mid-2025, nearly 500,000 disputes remained outstanding (Georgetown CHIR).
The volume was not random. A few provider groups — often private-equity-backed staffing firms in exactly the specialties the NSA targeted — drove a disproportionate share of disputes, treating IDR as a revenue strategy. 'Batching' rules, meant to let similar claims be bundled efficiently, were exploited to flood the system, which is why later reforms tightened batching (now capped at 50 line items with specific eligibility rules). The arbitration system designed as a backstop became a high-volume business process.
CMS expected about 17,000 No Surprises Act disputes a year. It got more than 5 million in under three years — roughly 300x the estimate — overwhelming an arbitration system that was never designed for industrial-scale use.
Who Is Winning
The IDR outcomes have tilted sharply toward providers. Providers and facilities prevailed in roughly 85% of determinations in 2024, with insurers winning only about 15%, and over roughly 2.5 years through 2024 providers extracted more than $2.2 billion above the applicable in-network rates through arbitration (HFMA; Commonwealth Fund). A major reason is the arbitration's reference point: arbiters weigh the 'qualifying payment amount' but also consider providers' billed charges, and the structure has in practice favored the higher provider offers. The result is that the NSA, while protecting patients, has functioned as a mechanism for certain provider groups to win above-market payments from insurers — costs that ultimately flow back into premiums.
This is the law's central irony. It succeeded at its consumer-protection goal but created a payment-determination system that may be inflating the very costs that drive premiums, partially offsetting the savings surprise-billing protection was supposed to deliver. Patients are protected at the point of service; the system-level cost question is murkier and tilts on how arbitration is structured.
[{"Dimension":"Patient protection","Result":"Success — patients removed from surprise out-of-network bills"},{"Dimension":"Dispute volume","Result":"5M+ disputes vs ~17,000/yr projected (~300x)"},{"Dimension":"Backlog","Result":"~500k outstanding (mid-2025) despite 85% closure rate"},{"Dimension":"Win rate","Result":"Providers ~85% / insurers ~15%; providers +$2.2B over in-network"}]
No Surprises Act: the scorecard
The Private-Equity Dimension
A specific dynamic explains much of the IDR flood and is worth isolating: the role of private-equity-backed physician staffing. The out-of-network billing the NSA targeted was, in significant part, a deliberate business model — PE-owned staffing firms placing out-of-network clinicians (emergency physicians, anesthesiologists) into in-network hospitals precisely to generate surprise bills and leverage. When the NSA removed the patient from that equation, those same firms pivoted to using IDR at industrial scale to extract from insurers what they could no longer extract from patients. The disproportionate share of disputes filed by a handful of large filers reflects this strategy.
This reframes the IDR problem as not merely an administrative-capacity failure but a market-power one. The NSA changed the venue of a payment fight without changing the underlying incentive of well-capitalized actors to maximize out-of-network revenue, and those actors simply optimized against the new rules. It is a recurring pattern in health policy: a reform that addresses a symptom (the surprise bill) without addressing the structural incentive behind it (the out-of-network revenue model) invites sophisticated players to find the next available lever. The batching limits and eligibility tightening are, in effect, an attempt to close the levers one by one — a reactive posture against actors who will keep probing for the next one.
The Reforms Reshaping It
The IDR process has been under near-constant legal and regulatory revision. Courts repeatedly struck down aspects of the rules (especially how arbiters should weight the qualifying payment amount), forcing CMS to rewrite them. In 2025-2026, CMS moved to streamline the system: tightening and clarifying batching and eligibility to cut ineligible and duplicative filings, building a payer registry and improved dispute portal, and — notably — cutting the administrative fee from $115 to $15 per party to lower the barrier for legitimate disputes while other changes aimed to reduce the flood (MedCity News; Becker's Payer). The through-line is an attempt to make IDR function as the targeted backstop it was meant to be rather than the overwhelmed, gamed process it became.
What It Got Right
Amid the arbitration mess, it is important not to lose the win, because the NSA's core design choice was genuinely smart. Earlier surprise-billing fixes had tried to cap charges or set prices directly, which providers fought ferociously and which raised hard questions about rate-setting. The NSA instead removed the patient from the dispute entirely and left providers and insurers to fight over the residual — a design that protected consumers immediately and unconditionally while deferring the harder payment question to a separate process. That separation is why the patient-protection half has worked so cleanly even as the payment half has struggled: the thing patients care about (not getting the bill) was decoupled from the thing the industry fights about (who pays what).
There is a transferable principle here for health policy generally: protections that shield individuals can often be implemented faster and more durably when they are decoupled from the contested questions of price and payment, rather than bundled with them. The NSA's patient protection succeeded precisely because it did not wait to resolve the payment fight. The cost is that the unresolved payment fight then has to be managed somewhere — and the NSA's experience shows that 'somewhere' had better be designed with adversarial sophistication in mind.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether the batching, eligibility, and fee reforms actually reduce the dispute flood and clear the backlog, or whether high-volume filers adapt around them; whether the provider win-rate and the resulting above-market payments persist or moderate as the rules are rewritten; whether the system-level cost effect (premium impact) becomes measurable; and whether the courts finally settle the contested question of how arbiters should weight the qualifying payment amount, which has driven much of the instability. The patient protection is durable; the payment machinery behind it remains a work in progress.
Bottom Line
The No Surprises Act is a genuine consumer-protection success — patients are out of the surprise-billing trap — wrapped around a payment-arbitration system that was badly under-designed for reality. CMS expected 17,000 disputes a year and got millions, the backlog hit half a million, and a handful of provider groups have used the process to win above-market payments totaling billions, costs that feed back into premiums. The ongoing reforms — batching limits, eligibility tightening, fee changes, court-driven rule rewrites — are attempts to tame the machinery without undoing the protection. The lesson is a familiar one in health policy: solving the visible problem (the surprise bill) is the easy part; designing the payment mechanism behind it, in a system where sophisticated actors will optimize against any rule, is where the real difficulty lives. The No Surprises Act will likely be remembered as a model for what consumer protection should look like — taking the patient out of a fight they never should have been in — and as a cautionary tale about building an arbitration mechanism without anticipating how well-capitalized players would weaponize it. The next iteration of payment-dispute policy will have to start from the assumption that the rules themselves are a game board, and design accordingly.

######## policyAnalysis-tech-001 | AI Clinical Decision Support: FDA Regulatory Framework for High-Risk Algorithms
Artificial intelligence is moving into clinical practice faster than the regulatory framework built to govern medical devices was designed to handle. The core mismatch is structural: traditional device regulation assumes a fixed product, but the whole point of modern AI is that it learns and changes. This analysis examines how the FDA regulates AI-enabled medical devices, the predetermined-change-control-plan innovation meant to resolve the learning-model problem, the gaps the framework still leaves, and what it means for developers and health systems.
The Regulatory Mismatch
The FDA's medical-device framework was designed for static products — a device is tested, cleared, and then stays the same until the manufacturer files a new submission for a significant change. AI/ML software breaks that assumption. An adaptive model is valuable precisely because it can be updated and improved as it sees more data, but under the traditional rules each significant modification would require a new regulatory submission — an impossibly slow cycle for software that might update frequently. The FDA has authorized roughly a thousand AI/ML-enabled devices to date, the vast majority through the 510(k) pathway, and the pace has accelerated — forcing the agency to adapt a framework never built for learning systems (FDA SaMD; PMC).
The result has been a multi-year effort to evolve device regulation toward the realities of software, without sacrificing the safety assurance that is the FDA's core mandate. The central innovation of that effort is the Predetermined Change Control Plan.
The PCCP Innovation
On December 3, 2024, the FDA published final guidance on Predetermined Change Control Plans (PCCPs) for AI-enabled device software — its key answer to the learning-model problem. A PCCP lets a manufacturer specify, at the time of initial authorization, exactly what future modifications it plans to make and how it will validate them, so that pre-approved changes can be implemented without a new marketing submission each time (McDermott; FDA). It has three components: a description of the planned modifications, a protocol for how each will be developed and tested, and an impact assessment of the risks. In August 2024 the FDA proposed extending the PCCP concept beyond AI to all device types, and in January 2025 it issued further draft guidance on AI device lifecycle management.
The PCCP is a genuinely clever regulatory design: it shifts the FDA's review from a one-time snapshot to a pre-authorized envelope of change, letting AI devices improve within agreed bounds while keeping the agency's safety oversight intact. In effect, the manufacturer commits up front to the rules of how its model may evolve, and the FDA approves the rules rather than re-approving every result. This is the regulatory equivalent of approving a process rather than a fixed product — the only approach that can keep pace with software that is designed to change.
The FDA's core problem with clinical AI: device rules assume a fixed product, but AI's value is that it learns and changes. The Predetermined Change Control Plan is the fix — the FDA approves an envelope of pre-specified, pre-validated changes up front, so the model can improve without a new submission each time.
[{"Element":"Pathways","Detail":"510(k) (most), De Novo, PMA — adapted for software"},{"Element":"PCCP (final Dec 2024)","Detail":"Pre-authorize planned modifications + validation; no new submission per change"},{"Element":"PCCP components","Detail":"Modification description · modification protocol · impact assessment"},{"Element":"2025 direction","Detail":"Lifecycle-management draft guidance; PCCP extended toward all device types"}]
How the FDA regulates AI devices
The Gaps the Framework Leaves
For all its sophistication, the framework has real gaps. The largest is that most clinical AI is not regulated by the FDA at all. The FDA regulates AI that functions as a medical device, but a great deal of consequential clinical AI — administrative tools, many clinical-decision-support functions that fall under regulatory exemptions, and the large language models being woven into documentation and workflow — sits outside or at the edge of FDA jurisdiction, governed lightly if at all. A hospital deploying an AI scribe or a risk-stratification tool embedded in its EHR may be using software that never passed FDA review.
The second gap is post-market reality. Even FDA-cleared AI must perform in the messy real world, on local populations that may differ from the training data, and the framework's post-market surveillance and real-world-performance monitoring remain comparatively weak — a model can be authorized and then drift, or underperform for a subgroup, with limited mechanisms to catch it. The third is the bias and equity dimension: FDA clearance does not guarantee a device performs equitably across race or geography, which is why the separate Section 1557 algorithm rule exists — a sign that device regulation alone does not cover the discrimination risk. Together these gaps mean FDA clearance is necessary but far from sufficient assurance that a clinical AI tool is safe and fair in a given setting.
The Generative AI Wildcard
The framework described above was built largely around 'narrow' AI — models that do one defined task, like flagging a finding on an image. Generative AI and large language models break even that adapted framework, because they are general-purpose, their outputs are open-ended and probabilistic, and they can 'hallucinate' plausible-sounding errors. A traditional device validation asks 'does this model accurately detect X?'; a generative model used to summarize a chart or draft a note has no such bounded task to validate, and its failure modes (confident fabrication) are different in kind from a narrow classifier's. The PCCP and device framework were not designed for this, and the FDA has been notably cautious about how, or whether, to regulate generative clinical AI.
This creates a widening regulatory vacuum exactly where adoption is fastest. Ambient documentation, chart summarization, and LLM-based decision support are spreading through health systems with little FDA oversight, governed mainly by the systems' own judgment and by general rules like the Section 1557 anti-discrimination provisions. The result is that the most rapidly-adopted and arguably highest-risk category of clinical AI is the least regulated — the inverse of what a risk-based framework would intend. Closing this gap, whether through new FDA authority, new legislation, or robust self-governance, is among the most pressing unresolved questions in health-AI policy.
What It Means for Developers and Health Systems
For AI developers, the PCCP is both an opportunity and a discipline: a well-constructed PCCP can dramatically speed the cadence of model improvement by pre-clearing a change envelope, but it requires sophisticated upfront work to specify and justify the modification protocol — rewarding developers who plan their model's evolution rigorously. For health systems, the key lesson is that the FDA's clearance is a floor, not a ceiling: a cleared device still needs local validation, post-deployment monitoring, and subgroup performance checks, because clearance does not certify performance on the system's own patients. And for the large category of clinical AI outside FDA jurisdiction, the health system itself is effectively the regulator — responsible for the diligence the FDA did not perform.
What to Watch
Key signals: how widely PCCPs are adopted and whether they meaningfully accelerate safe AI iteration; whether the FDA strengthens post-market surveillance and real-world-performance requirements to match the lifecycle-management direction of its 2025 guidance; whether the jurisdictional gap (the large share of clinical AI outside FDA review) is addressed through expanded authority or left to health systems and other rules; and how FDA device regulation interacts with the Section 1557 bias rule and any future federal AI legislation. The framework is evolving thoughtfully, but the pace of AI deployment is outrunning the pace of regulatory adaptation, and the gap between what is deployed and what is rigorously overseen is the space to watch.
Bottom Line
The FDA is adapting a device framework built for static products to AI that learns and changes, and the Predetermined Change Control Plan — finalized in December 2024 — is a genuinely clever solution: pre-authorize an envelope of validated change so models can improve without re-submission while safety oversight persists. But the framework leaves real gaps: most clinical AI falls outside FDA jurisdiction, post-market surveillance is weak, and clearance does not certify equity or local performance. The practical takeaway is that FDA clearance is necessary but not sufficient — health systems must treat every AI tool as requiring their own local validation and monitoring, and must recognize that for the large category of unregulated clinical AI, they are the regulator of last resort. The regulatory framework is maturing; the deployment of AI is maturing faster, and closing that gap is the central challenge of clinical-AI governance.
Sources
FDA — Artificial Intelligence in Software as a Medical Device
McDermott+ — FDA Issues Final Guidance on Predetermined Change Control Plans for AI-Enabled Devices
PMC — Machine Learning-Enabled Medical Devices Authorized by the FDA in 2024

This analysis covers the framework, the PCCP, and the gaps.
AI Clinical Decision Support: FDA Regulatory Framework for High-Risk Algorithms

######## policyAnalysis-tech-002 | EHR Interoperability: TEFCA Network Participation and Real-World Data Exchange
For decades, U.S. health data has been trapped in silos — your records at one hospital invisible to the next, faxes standing in for data exchange. TEFCA, the Trusted Exchange Framework and Common Agreement, is the federal effort to finally build a national 'network of networks' for health information. After years of slow setup it is now scaling rapidly, but the gap between connection and useful exchange remains the real test. This analysis examines what TEFCA is, its 2024-2025 inflection point, the persistent gap between participation and clinical value, and what determines whether it delivers genuine interoperability.
What TEFCA Is
TEFCA establishes a common legal and technical framework so that the many existing health-information networks can exchange data with each other, rather than operating as disconnected islands. Its central building block is the Qualified Health Information Network (QHIN) — a small set of federally-designated networks that agree to a common set of rules and connect to one another, so that a query in one can reach data in any of them. Think of QHINs as the backbone routers of a national health-data internet: connect to one QHIN, and you can in principle reach the data flowing through all of them (ONC; Contexture).
The framework reached a regulatory milestone on December 16, 2024, when HHS published the TEFCA final rule, effective January 15, 2025, codifying the requirements for QHINs and putting the framework on firmer legal footing after years of operating under sub-regulatory guidance. TEFCA has also expanded beyond its initial scope: it now supports exchange not just for treatment and individual access but for payment, health-care operations, public health, and government-benefits determination — broadening it from a clinical-records pipe toward a multi-purpose national data utility.
TEFCA is the long-sought 'network of networks' for U.S. health data — a small set of federally-designated QHINs that connect to each other so a query in one can reach data in all. After years of slow setup, 2024-2025 is its inflection point: a final rule, 11 QHINs, 71,000+ sites, and Epic onboarding ~280M records.
The 2024-2025 Inflection Point
After a slow start, TEFCA's scale is now growing fast. Over 71,000 sites or organizations are participating through 11 designated QHINs (including Epic Nexus, Oracle Health, eHealth Exchange, Health Gorilla, Commonwell, Surescripts, and others), and the volume growth is steep — Health Gorilla alone reported query volume rising from about 200,000 to more than 66 million by March 2025 (Healthcare IT News; ONC). The single most consequential development is Epic: Epic Nexus announced it would onboard the 'full Epic community' to TEFCA by the end of 2025, bringing an estimated 280 million patient records into the framework — and given Epic's dominant share of the EHR market, that alone transforms TEFCA's reach. Carequality's 2024 decision to align its widely-used framework with TEFCA further consolidates the national exchange landscape around it.
Technically, the framework is also advancing from older document-exchange standards toward modern FHIR-based exchange, with QHIN-to-QHIN FHIR exchange piloted in 2025 — a shift from exchanging static documents toward exchanging discrete, computable data elements, which is what makes data genuinely usable rather than merely transmissible.
The Gap Between Connection and Value
The hard truth beneath the impressive participation numbers is that connection is not the same as useful exchange. A network can be technically connected, and a query can technically be answered, while the clinical value remains limited — because the data returned is incomplete, arrives as an unstructured document a clinician has to read rather than computable data a system can use, or because query response rates from some participants are low. The history of health interoperability is littered with networks that achieved impressive connection statistics while clinicians still could not reliably get the right data at the point of care. TEFCA's participation milestones are necessary but not sufficient; the question is whether a clinician in an exam room actually gets a complete, usable picture of a patient seen elsewhere.
This is why the FHIR transition matters so much. Exchanging documents (the older model) moves data but leaves a human to extract meaning; exchanging FHIR resources moves discrete, structured data a system can compute on — feeding decision support, reconciling medications automatically, populating a registry. TEFCA's ultimate value depends less on how many organizations connect than on whether the exchange moves usable structured data, which is precisely the harder, still-maturing part of the project.
[{"Dimension":"QHINs","Detail":"11 designated networks (Epic Nexus, Oracle, eHealth Exchange, etc.)"},{"Dimension":"Participation","Detail":"71,000+ sites; Epic onboarding ~280M records by end-2025"},{"Dimension":"Regulatory","Detail":"Final rule Dec 2024, effective Jan 15, 2025"},{"Dimension":"The real test","Detail":"Usable FHIR data at the point of care, not just connection"}]
TEFCA: status and the real test
Privacy and the Expanding-Purpose Question
TEFCA's expansion beyond treatment into payment, health-care operations, public health, and benefits determination is a double-edged development. On one hand, it makes the network more useful and more sustainable — a pipe that carries only clinical records for treatment is narrower and harder to fund than one supporting many legitimate uses. On the other, every added purpose means more entities accessing more data for more reasons, which raises real privacy and appropriate-use questions: data a patient shared for treatment now flows for operations or benefits determination, contexts they may not have anticipated. National-scale exchange concentrates a vast amount of sensitive information into an interconnected system, and the broader the permitted purposes, the larger the surface for both legitimate concern and potential misuse.
This matters for trust, which is ultimately what determines whether interoperability succeeds. Patients who fear their data will travel to places they did not intend may opt out or withhold information, undermining the completeness that makes exchange valuable in the first place. TEFCA's governance therefore has to balance utility against privacy carefully — broad enough to be useful and sustainable, bounded enough that patients trust it. The expanded-purpose design is a bet that the utility is worth the privacy trade-off, and whether that bet holds depends on transparent governance and meaningful patient control over how their data is used.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether Epic's full-community onboarding and Carequality alignment push TEFCA to genuine critical mass, making it the default national exchange; whether QHIN-to-QHIN FHIR exchange moves from pilot to production, shifting the framework from document exchange to computable-data exchange; whether query response rates and data completeness improve so participation translates into clinical value; and whether the expanded exchange purposes (payment, operations, public health) raise privacy and appropriate-use questions as more entities access the data for more reasons. The participation trajectory is now strong; the value trajectory — usable data at the point of care — is the one to watch.
Bottom Line
TEFCA is finally delivering the national health-data 'network of networks' that two decades of interoperability efforts promised, and 2024-2025 is its inflection point: a codifying final rule, 11 QHINs, 71,000-plus participating sites, and Epic's decision to bring its entire community and an estimated 280 million records into the framework. That scale is real and consequential. But the enduring lesson of health interoperability is that connection is the easy part and usable exchange is the hard part — a clinician needs complete, computable data at the point of care, not just a network that technically answers a query. TEFCA's ultimate success therefore hinges less on its impressive participation numbers than on the FHIR transition and data quality that turn connection into clinical value. The plumbing is being laid at last; whether clean, usable water flows through it is the question the next few years will answer. If TEFCA succeeds, it would quietly resolve one of the longest-running failures in U.S. health care — the inability of a patient's information to follow them — and unlock everything that depends on complete data, from care coordination to population health to the clinical AI that needs comprehensive records to work well. If it stalls at connection without achieving usable exchange, it joins the long history of interoperability efforts that moved data without delivering value. The stakes are high precisely because so much else in modern health care silently assumes the seamless data flow that TEFCA is, at last, actually attempting to build. For a health system or technology builder, the practical implication is to engage now — connect through a QHIN, prepare for FHIR-based exchange, and design workflows around the assumption that comprehensive external data will increasingly be available — while remaining clear-eyed that the value will arrive gradually as data quality and response rates mature, not the moment a connection is made.

######## policyAnalysis-tech-003 | Cybersecurity in Health Systems: Ransomware Trends and Regulatory Response
Healthcare has become one of the most targeted and most vulnerable sectors for ransomware, and the February 2024 Change Healthcare attack proved how a single compromise can paralyze much of the national health system. This analysis examines the scale of the threat, the Change Healthcare attack as a case study in systemic fragility, the costs of an attack, why healthcare is so exposed, and the regulatory response now taking shape.
The Scale of the Threat
Healthcare ransomware has escalated from an IT nuisance to a patient-safety and national-infrastructure threat. 2024 was the costliest year on record for healthcare cybersecurity, with the average healthcare data breach reaching roughly $7.42 million per incident and total industry losses from ransomware downtime running into the tens of billions of dollars (industry analyses; HIMSS). The attacks are not just more frequent but more damaging, increasingly designed to maximize operational disruption — encrypting the systems hospitals need to function — because a hospital that cannot access its records or process payments is under maximum pressure to pay quickly.
HHS's Office for Civil Rights has reported substantial increases in large breaches involving hacking and ransomware — enough that it refocused its 2024-2025 HIPAA audits specifically on the Security Rule provisions most relevant to hacking and ransomware. The regulator's own audit priorities are a barometer of how serious the threat has become.
Change Healthcare: A Case Study in Fragility
The February 21, 2024 attack on Change Healthcare — a UnitedHealth Group subsidiary that processes a huge share of U.S. medical claims — is the defining cyber event in healthcare history. The ALPHV/BlackCat ransomware group encrypted and incapacitated major portions of Change's systems, and the breach ultimately affected an estimated 192.7 million people — roughly two-thirds of the U.S. population — making it the largest healthcare data breach ever recorded, with costs to UnitedHealth reported at over $1 billion (Nixon Peabody; AHA).
The deeper lesson was systemic fragility. Because Change Healthcare sits at a chokepoint in the national claims-and-payment infrastructure, its compromise rippled out to providers and pharmacies nationwide: claims could not be processed, pharmacies could not verify coverage, and providers went weeks without revenue, with many small practices pushed toward insolvency. A single vendor's breach became a national health-system disruption — exposing how dangerously concentrated and interdependent the sector's digital infrastructure has become. The vulnerability was not just Change's security; it was the entire system's dependence on one node.
The Change Healthcare attack hit an estimated 192.7M people — about two-thirds of the U.S. population — and cost over $1 billion, but its real lesson was systemic: one vendor at a chokepoint of the claims-and-payment system, compromised, paralyzed providers and pharmacies nationwide for weeks.
[{"Metric":"Change Healthcare breach","Value":"~192.7M people affected; largest ever; >$1B cost"},{"Metric":"Avg breach cost (2024)","Value":"~$7.42M per incident"},{"Metric":"Downtime","Value":"~17 days avg; ~$1.9M/day in losses"},{"Metric":"Regulatory response","Value":"OCR refocused 2024-25 HIPAA audits on hacking/ransomware"}]
The healthcare ransomware threat
The Cost of an Attack
The cost of a healthcare ransomware attack extends far beyond any ransom paid. Analyses estimate average downtime exceeding 17 days per attack, with daily losses near $1.9 million as patient services and revenue cycles seize up. But the financial figures understate the real toll: when a hospital's systems go down, care is delayed, ambulances are diverted, procedures are postponed, and lab and imaging results become inaccessible — a genuine patient-safety emergency, not merely a business interruption. Studies increasingly link ransomware disruptions to worse clinical outcomes, including at neighboring hospitals that absorb diverted patients. The ransom, in other words, is the least of it; the operational paralysis and its clinical consequences are the real cost.
Why Healthcare Is So Exposed
Healthcare's vulnerability is structural. The sector runs on a sprawling, interconnected mix of systems — EHRs, medical devices, billing platforms, and countless third-party vendors — with a vast attack surface and many legacy components that cannot easily be patched or replaced. Medical devices in particular often run outdated, unsupported operating systems because recertifying them is costly, creating known and exploitable weaknesses. Hospitals also operate 24/7 with low tolerance for downtime, which makes them more likely to pay quickly to restore operations — exactly the leverage ransomware exploits. And the data itself is valuable: health records are rich, hard to change (you cannot reissue a medical history like a credit card), and command high prices on illicit markets.
Rural and smaller hospitals are especially exposed, because they have the least IT security capacity and the thinnest margins to invest in defense, even as they run the same critical systems. The sector's concentration compounds the risk: as the Change Healthcare attack showed, dependence on a few large vendors and clearinghouses means a single breach can cascade far beyond the initial target.
What Hospitals Can Actually Do
Defense is hard but not hopeless, and the most effective measures are unglamorous fundamentals rather than exotic tools. The basics that prevent most attacks are known: multi-factor authentication everywhere (a huge share of breaches start with a compromised credential), network segmentation so a single compromised system cannot reach everything, rigorous and tested backups so a hospital can restore rather than pay, prompt patching, and staff training against the phishing that initiates most intrusions. None of this is novel; the gap is execution, especially at hospitals without the staff to maintain it. The Change Healthcare breach reportedly traced to a system lacking multi-factor authentication — a basic control whose absence enabled a billion-dollar catastrophe.
Beyond the basics, the systemic lesson points to two structural defenses. The first is reducing single-vendor dependence and building redundancy for critical functions (claims, pharmacy verification) so that one vendor's compromise does not halt operations — a lesson the entire sector learned the hard way from Change Healthcare. The second is incident-response planning that assumes an attack will succeed: hospitals that had practiced downtime procedures — how to deliver care and process essential functions without their systems — weathered disruptions far better than those that had not. The question is no longer whether a healthcare organization will be attacked but whether it has prepared to keep caring for patients when it is.
The Regulatory Response
Policy is responding, though arguably lagging the threat. HHS has moved toward stronger cybersecurity expectations — developing healthcare-sector cybersecurity performance goals and signaling that baseline security practices may shift from voluntary to required, with OCR's refocused HIPAA audits putting teeth behind the Security Rule. The direction is toward treating cybersecurity as a condition of operating rather than an optional IT investment. The open question is resources: mandating stronger security without funding it falls hardest on the rural and small hospitals least able to comply, which is why proposals increasingly pair security requirements with grant support for the providers that cannot otherwise afford the upgrades.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether HHS's cybersecurity performance goals become mandatory requirements and whether they are paired with funding for under-resourced hospitals; whether the Change Healthcare attack drives real diversification away from single-vendor chokepoints or the concentration persists; whether ransomware tactics continue shifting toward maximum operational disruption; and whether the documented clinical-harm evidence elevates cybersecurity from an IT issue to a patient-safety mandate in practice, not just rhetoric. The threat is escalating faster than defenses are being built, and the gap is the space where the next Change Healthcare-scale event is incubating.
Bottom Line
Healthcare ransomware has become a patient-safety and national-infrastructure threat, not merely an IT problem, and the Change Healthcare attack — affecting roughly two-thirds of Americans and costing over $1 billion — proved how a single compromise at an infrastructure chokepoint can paralyze the system nationwide. The costs are enormous and, more importantly, increasingly clinical: downtime delays care and worsens outcomes. Healthcare is structurally exposed through its sprawling attack surface, legacy systems, low downtime tolerance, valuable data, and dangerous vendor concentration, with rural and small hospitals most vulnerable of all. The regulatory response is moving toward mandatory baseline security, but its success depends on pairing requirements with resources for the providers that cannot otherwise comply. Until the sector reduces its concentration and funds defense where it is thinnest, the conditions that produced the Change Healthcare catastrophe remain firmly in place. The uncomfortable reality is that healthcare digitized rapidly over the past fifteen years — driven by EHR adoption and the data-exchange push — without a commensurate investment in securing what it digitized, leaving a sprawling, interconnected, data-rich system defended unevenly and, in many places, poorly. Ransomware actors have simply recognized that a sector which cannot tolerate downtime, holds extraordinarily valuable data, and often cannot defend itself well is the ideal target. Reversing that requires treating cybersecurity not as an IT line item but as core clinical infrastructure — as fundamental to patient safety as infection control — and resourcing it accordingly, especially at the rural and small hospitals where the gap is widest and the next cascade is most likely to begin.

Cybersecurity in Health Systems: Ransomware Trends and Regulatory Response

######## policyAnalysis-tech-004 | Remote Patient Monitoring Reimbursement: CPT Code Evolution and Adoption Barriers
Remote patient monitoring (RPM) — using connected devices to track a patient's vital signs at home and bill for managing the data — has grown from a niche service into a multi-billion-dollar category, propelled largely by Medicare reimbursement. But the gap between RPM's promise and its real-world results is wide, and a wave of fraud scrutiny is reshaping the rules. This analysis examines how RPM is reimbursed, the 2026 coding changes, the inconsistent evidence, the fraud problem, and what separates RPM programs that improve care from those that just generate billing.
How RPM Is Reimbursed
RPM's growth is fundamentally a reimbursement story. Medicare pays for RPM through a small set of CPT codes — principally 99453 (device setup and patient education), 99454 (the monthly device supply and data transmission), and 99457/99458 (the monthly treatment-management time), plus 99091 (Telehealth.HHS.gov; ThoroughCare). The economics are attractive: a program enrolling 100 patients, each receiving the minimum monthly management, can generate on the order of $110,000 in annual reimbursement. That payment pathway, opened and expanded since 2019, is what turned RPM from a clinical experiment into a fast-growing business, with the global RPM market valued around $43 billion in 2024 and projected to more than triple by the early 2030s, and tens of millions of Americans expected to use some form of RPM.
The reimbursement design, however, shaped RPM's growth in a specific way — toward volume. Because the codes pay for enrolling patients, supplying devices, and logging management time, the financial incentive rewards getting many patients onto monitoring and billing the monthly codes, which is not necessarily the same as improving outcomes. That misalignment is at the root of both the inconsistent evidence and the fraud problem.
The 2026 Coding Changes
CMS is adjusting the rules in ways that matter. Effective January 2026, two changes stand out: the long-standing requirement that a provider collect 16 days of data in a 30-day period before billing the device code is being relaxed, and new codes are being added to reimburse 10-19 minutes of data management (where previously the management codes required 20 minutes) (ThoroughCare; CM Health Law). These changes lower the threshold for billing RPM, which expands access for patients who do not generate 16 days of data or need less than 20 minutes of management — but they also lower the bar for billing generally, which cuts both ways given the fraud concerns.
RPM's reimbursement design rewards volume — enrolling patients, supplying devices, logging time — which is not the same as improving outcomes. That misalignment is the root of both RPM's inconsistent evidence and its growing fraud problem.
The Inconsistent Evidence
Despite RPM's intuitive appeal — catch deterioration early, prevent the hospitalization — the real-world evidence is genuinely mixed. Systematic reviews find trends toward reduced hospital admissions, shorter stays, and lower non-hospitalization costs in some studies, but RPM has produced inconsistent clinical and economic results when implemented in ordinary practice (PubMed systematic review). The crucial insight from the literature is where the failure happens: the bottleneck is not the monitoring device but what happens downstream — whether someone interprets the data, integrates it into workflow, and acts on it. A stream of blood-pressure readings that no one reviews and responds to changes nothing; the value is in the clinical response, not the data collection.
This explains the gap between RPM's billing growth and its outcome record. A program optimized to bill the codes can enroll patients and transmit data — satisfying the reimbursement requirements — without building the clinical workflow that turns data into intervention. The technology is necessary but radically insufficient; RPM that improves outcomes is a care-model change (someone watching, someone calling, someone adjusting treatment), not just a device shipment. Programs that treat RPM as a monitoring-plus-response system succeed; those that treat it as a billing opportunity do not.
Where RPM Genuinely Works
RPM's mixed evidence does not mean it never works — it means it works in specific conditions and care models, and fails when deployed indiscriminately. The strongest use cases share features: a condition where home data meaningfully predicts deterioration (heart failure, where weight and vitals signal decompensation; hypertension, where titration depends on real readings; COPD; diabetes), a high-risk population where the cost of a missed deterioration is high, and — critically — a clinical team structured to watch the data and respond. RPM for a stable, low-risk patient generates billing and little benefit; RPM for a high-risk heart-failure patient with a responsive care team can genuinely prevent admissions.
This is why RPM and value-based care are natural partners. Under fee-for-service, RPM's incentive is to bill the monthly codes; under a risk-bearing arrangement, the incentive is to use RPM to prevent the expensive admission the organization is now on the hook for — which aligns the technology with the downstream response that makes it effective. The organizations getting real value from RPM tend to be those deploying it within accountable-care or capitated models for genuinely high-risk patients, where the financial logic rewards prevention rather than monitoring volume. Matched to the right patients and the right payment model, RPM earns its place; deployed as a universal billing add-on, it does not.
The Fraud Problem
The volume incentive has produced exactly the abuse one would predict. An HHS Office of Inspector General report found troubling patterns in Medicare RPM billing: 28% of enrollees in RPM programs had no claim or record for the education or setup of their device, 23% had no record of the device supply itself, and 12% received no treatment-management services (OIG). In other words, a meaningful share of RPM billing showed signs that the services were not actually delivered as the codes require — patients billed for monitoring they may never have meaningfully received. The OIG urged additional oversight, and the 2026 rule changes arrive alongside heightened fraud-and-abuse scrutiny.
This is the predictable consequence of a payment model that rewards enrollment and billing more directly than outcomes. When a code pays for shipping a device and logging time, some actors will ship devices and log time without the surrounding care — and at scale, across a fast-growing market, that becomes a significant fraud surface. The fraud problem is not a reason to abandon RPM but a sign that the reimbursement design rewarded the wrong things, and that oversight has to catch up to a market that grew faster than its guardrails.
[{"Dimension":"Payment","Detail":"CPT 99453/99454/99457/99458 (+99091); ~$110k/yr per 100 patients"},{"Dimension":"2026 changes","Detail":"16-day data rule relaxed; new 10-19 min management codes"},{"Dimension":"Evidence","Detail":"Inconsistent — bottleneck is downstream interpretation/response, not the device"},{"Dimension":"Fraud (OIG)","Detail":"28% no setup, 23% no device supply, 12% no treatment management"}]
RPM: the reimbursement-reality gap
What to Watch
Key signals: whether the 2026 coding changes expand legitimate access or widen the fraud surface, and whether CMS pairs them with the oversight OIG urged; whether RPM reimbursement evolves to reward outcomes (e.g., within value-based contracts) rather than enrollment and time-logging, which would realign the incentive with clinical value; whether the evidence base sharpens as programs mature their downstream workflows; and whether RPM consolidates into the care models (chronic-disease management, post-discharge monitoring) where it has the strongest rationale rather than spreading as a billing play. The technology is proven to be capable of helping; whether it does depends on payment design and clinical execution, not the devices.
Bottom Line
Remote patient monitoring is a case study in how reimbursement design shapes a technology's trajectory — for better and worse. Medicare payment turned RPM into a multi-billion-dollar category and put monitoring within reach of tens of millions, but by rewarding enrollment, device supply, and logged time rather than outcomes, it produced inconsistent clinical results and a significant fraud problem that OIG has flagged. The decisive lesson is that RPM's value lives downstream of the device — in whether someone interprets the data and acts on it — so RPM that improves care is a care-model change, not a billing line. The 2026 coding changes lower billing thresholds in ways that cut both ways. The path to RPM fulfilling its promise runs through realigning payment toward outcomes and building the clinical response workflows that the device alone cannot provide — and through the oversight needed to ensure a fast-growing market delivers the care it bills for. RPM is neither the panacea its boosters claim nor the boondoggle its fraud headlines suggest; it is a genuinely useful tool whose real-world value has been distorted by a payment model that paid for the wrong things, and whose promise depends entirely on whether the system can realign the incentive toward the downstream clinical response that actually helps patients.

######## policyAnalysis-tech-005 | Generative AI in Healthcare Administration: Use Cases, Risks, and Governance Frameworks
Generative AI is entering health care faster than any prior clinical technology — drafting notes, summarizing charts, answering questions, and increasingly suggesting actions. Its capabilities are real, but so is a failure mode unique to it: the confident, plausible fabrication known as a hallucination. Because most generative AI falls outside FDA device regulation, the burden of governing it falls on health systems themselves. This analysis examines generative AI's healthcare uses, the hallucination risk, why governance is largely a local responsibility, and what a responsible framework requires.
Where Generative AI Is Being Used
Generative AI in health care clusters around administrative and documentation tasks where the burden is heaviest and the risk, at first glance, lowest. Ambient scribes (Nuance DAX, Abridge, and others) listen to a visit and draft the clinical note; other tools summarize long charts, draft prior-authorization letters, generate patient-friendly visit summaries, and answer clinician and patient questions. The appeal is the documentation burden that drives clinician burnout, and adoption has been extraordinarily fast — far faster than the governance frameworks meant to oversee it (CDC; PMC scoping review). The technology is being woven into clinical workflow in real time, often before institutions have decided how to govern it.
The Hallucination Problem
Generative AI's distinctive danger is that it can produce information that is fluent, confident, and wrong. Unlike a narrow classifier that returns a probability, a generative model writes plausible prose — and that prose can contain fabricated details, mis-attributed statements, or subtle clinical errors presented with the same authority as correct content. In health care this is acute: even minor factual deviations can scale into patient-safety hazards, and once incorrect information enters a clinical note it can propagate across the EHR and influence downstream decisions (NCBI; pharmaphorum). A hallucinated detail in a generated note is not a contained error; it becomes part of the record that other clinicians and systems trust.
The hallucination problem is also harder to govern than ordinary error because of how it manifests — not only as obvious falsehoods but as ambiguity, subtle bias reinforcement, or confident over-generalization that existing quality controls are poorly equipped to catch. A note that is 95% accurate and fluent is more dangerous than one that is obviously garbled, because the reviewer's guard is down. This is why generative AI cannot be governed like traditional software, where you test against expected outputs; its outputs are open-ended and its errors are designed to look like successes.
Generative AI's defining danger is the hallucination — fluent, confident, plausible fabrication. In health care a hallucinated detail doesn't stay contained: it enters the note, propagates across the EHR, and is trusted by every clinician and system downstream. A 95%-accurate fluent note is more dangerous than an obviously broken one.
Why Governance Falls to Health Systems
The regulatory reality is that most generative clinical AI is not overseen by the FDA. The FDA regulates software that functions as a medical device, but ambient documentation, chart summarization, and general-purpose clinical assistants largely fall outside that definition or in its gray zones, and the FDA has been cautious about how to regulate generative AI at all. The result is a governance vacuum exactly where adoption is fastest — the most rapidly deployed category of clinical AI is the least externally regulated. In that vacuum, the health system itself becomes the regulator: responsible for vetting, deploying, monitoring, and governing tools that no external body has cleared.
This is a heavy and unfamiliar responsibility for institutions that are used to buying validated, regulated technology. A hospital deploying an ambient scribe cannot point to FDA clearance as assurance of safety; it has to build its own governance — and many are deploying first and governing later, under competitive and burnout-relief pressure. The gap between the speed of adoption and the maturity of local governance is the central risk in generative clinical AI today.
The Coming Higher-Stakes Uses
Today's generative AI in health care is concentrated in lower-stakes administrative tasks, which is why the governance gap has not yet produced a high-profile catastrophe. But the trajectory is toward higher stakes. The same technology is moving from drafting notes toward suggesting diagnoses, recommending treatments, answering patients' clinical questions directly, and triaging. Each step up the acuity ladder raises the cost of a hallucination: a fabricated detail in a note is bad; a hallucinated drug interaction in a treatment recommendation, or a dangerous answer to a patient's direct question, is worse. The governance discipline being established now for documentation is the template that has to scale with the technology as it moves into clinical decision-making — and the window to establish that discipline is while the stakes are still comparatively low.
This is the strategic argument for taking generative-AI governance seriously before it is forced by a disaster. The institutions that build robust oversight for ambient documentation today — attestation, auditing, monitoring, clear ownership — will have the muscle and the frameworks in place when the same vendors push tools into diagnosis and treatment. Those that deploy documentation tools ungoverned will find themselves with neither the processes nor the institutional habits to govern the higher-stakes tools safely. Governance maturity, in other words, is a capability that has to be built ahead of need, because retrofitting it after a generative-AI tool has caused clinical harm is the worst time to start.
What Responsible Governance Requires
A responsible local framework for generative AI rests on a few pillars that recur across the emerging guidance. Mandatory human oversight is the non-negotiable foundation: a clinician must review and attest to AI-generated content, which is both a safety control and the basis for liability (the clinician, not the AI, owns the note). Beyond that: data provenance and explainability — knowing what the model was trained on and being able to trace its outputs; continuous monitoring rather than one-time validation, since generative models drift and fail in evolving ways; accuracy auditing against defined thresholds with attention to the specific failure modes (hallucinated medications, wrong allergies, omitted symptoms); and clear data governance over the sensitive conversational data these tools capture, protecting patient interests while enabling appropriate use (Nature Scientific Reports; Hastings Center).
The through-line is that generative AI must be deployed inside a quality-and-safety framework, not switched on and trusted. The tools that are used safely are the ones embedded in institutional processes — attestation, auditing, monitoring, governance committees — that treat the AI as a powerful but fallible assistant requiring oversight, exactly as a teaching hospital supervises a capable but inexperienced trainee.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether the FDA or new federal legislation extends meaningful oversight to generative clinical AI, or whether the governance burden stays with health systems indefinitely; whether standardized governance frameworks and 'model card' style disclosures emerge so institutions are not each inventing oversight from scratch; whether hallucination-detection and accuracy-auditing tools mature enough to make monitoring tractable at scale; and whether the documented patient-safety risks produce adverse events significant enough to force governance to catch up with adoption. The capability is racing ahead; whether the guardrails follow is the defining governance question of clinical AI.
Bottom Line
Generative AI is the fastest-spreading clinical technology in memory, valuable for relieving the documentation burden but carrying a failure mode — confident hallucination — that is uniquely dangerous in medicine because fabricated content propagates through the record and is trusted downstream. The defining governance problem is that most generative clinical AI sits outside FDA regulation, so the responsibility falls on health systems that are largely deploying first and governing later. Responsible use requires a local framework built on mandatory human attestation, provenance and explainability, continuous monitoring, accuracy auditing, and data governance — treating generative AI as a powerful but fallible assistant under supervision, never as an autonomous author. The technology will keep advancing into higher-stakes tasks; whether health systems build the governance to match is what will separate generative AI as a safe productivity gain from generative AI as a propagating source of clinical error. The unusual urgency here comes from the speed: unlike prior clinical technologies that diffused over years, generative AI is being adopted in months, which compresses the time available to get governance right and raises the cost of getting it wrong. The institutions that treat governance as a prerequisite rather than an afterthought are the ones that will capture the benefit without importing the risk.

Generative AI is the fastest-spreading clinical technology — drafting notes, summarizing charts — but carries a uniquely dangerous failure mode: confident hallucination that propagates through the EHR once it enters the record. Because most generative clinical AI sits outside FDA regulation, governance falls on health systems, which are mostly deploying first and governing later.
