The Structural Triad: Redesigning the US Healthcare Value Chain

######## telehealth-correction-economic-implications | The Telehealth Correction: Economic Implications of Expiring Waivers
The economics of telehealth were supposed to be settled by a cliff. The pandemic-era expansion of virtual care rested on temporary Medicare waivers — distant-site flexibility, payment parity, home-as-origin-site, audio-only coverage — and for years the field braced for the moment those waivers would lapse and reimbursement would snap back to the restrictive pre-2020 rules. The real story turned out to be stranger and, for providers, harder to plan around: not a single cliff, but a recurring brinkmanship cycle of short extensions that keeps the threat permanently alive without ever resolving it.
What Actually Happened to the Waivers
As of early 2026, the Medicare telehealth flexibilities have not expired. After a lapse-and-restore scramble around the January 30, 2026 deadline, Congress passed a straight extension of Medicare telehealth policy through December 31, 2027. That extension preserves the core flexibilities that make virtual care viable: all eligible Medicare providers can furnish telehealth, FQHCs and RHCs can serve as distant-site providers for non-behavioral services, the home counts as an originating site, and audio-only delivery remains payable — all through the end of 2027. Behavioral and mental-health telehealth flexibilities, notably, have been made permanent.
This matters enormously for the economic analysis, because it inverts the original premise. The threat to telehealth economics is no longer an imminent reimbursement collapse; it is chronic policy uncertainty. Providers are not falling off a cliff — they are being asked to make multi-year capital and staffing decisions against a reimbursement framework that Congress renews in one- and two-year increments, often at the last minute. The 'correction' is real, but it is a correction in planning confidence, not in current payment rates.
The Economics of Brinkmanship
Recurring short-term extensions impose a distinct and underappreciated cost: they suppress investment even when the underlying payment is intact. A rural health clinic deciding whether to build out a permanent telehealth program must weigh not today's parity payment but the risk that parity disappears in eighteen months. That uncertainty raises the effective hurdle rate on every telehealth investment, biasing organizations toward under-investment relative to what stable policy would justify. The damage of brinkmanship is done through deferred and foregone investment, not through a single dramatic rate cut.
This asymmetry falls hardest on exactly the providers telehealth was supposed to help most. Large, well-capitalized systems can absorb policy risk and build hybrid infrastructure on the bet that flexibilities will keep being renewed. Small independent and rural practices, operating on thin margins, cannot make multi-year bets against an expiration date, so they hesitate — and the access gains telehealth promised for rural and underserved populations stall. Policy uncertainty, in this sense, is regressive: it rations confidence to those who least need it and withholds it from those who do.
The correction is not a payment cliff — Congress extended Medicare telehealth flexibilities through December 31, 2027 (with behavioral-health telehealth made permanent). The real economic damage is chronic uncertainty: short, last-minute extensions raise the hurdle rate on telehealth investment and bias small and rural providers — the intended beneficiaries — toward under-investment.
The RHC and FQHC Reimbursement Question
Even with the flexibilities extended, rural health clinics and federally qualified health centers face a genuine reimbursement-design problem distinct from the expiration question. These facilities are paid under special methodologies, and the way telehealth services map onto those methodologies has not always delivered parity with in-person care. National rural-health advocates have flagged that RHCs in particular have operated for extended periods without telehealth reimbursement that fully matches the economics of in-person visits, creating ongoing financial strain regardless of whether the broad waivers are in place.
So the honest framing is two-layered. Layer one — the existential 'will Medicare pay for telehealth at all' question — is resolved through 2027. Layer two — the structural 'does the payment rate and methodology actually sustain a rural telehealth program' question — remains contested and is where the real long-term economics will be decided. A provider can be fully covered by the extended flexibilities and still find that the specific rate for a virtual visit does not cover its cost to deliver, which is the subtler margin pressure the original 'cliff' narrative obscured.
The Macro Stakes: Access, Not Just Margins
Beneath the provider-level economics sits a larger question about access. Telehealth was the single largest expansion of health-care access in a generation, and its value is concentrated in exactly the populations that face the deepest access barriers: rural residents far from specialists, behavioral-health patients in counties with no psychiatrist, homebound and mobility-limited seniors, and workers who cannot take half a day off for an in-person visit. For these groups, the policy question is not whether a hospital's telehealth margin is positive but whether the care exists at all.
That is why the brinkmanship cycle has costs that never show up on a balance sheet. When flexibilities are renewed only in short increments, the providers who hesitate to invest are disproportionately those serving the hardest-to-reach patients, so the access that erodes at the margin is the access that was hardest-won to begin with. Vermont, with one of the oldest and most rural populations in the country, is a clear example: a brittle telehealth policy environment threatens precisely the virtual-access model that an aging, geographically dispersed population most depends on. The economic case for policy permanence is ultimately an access case — stable reimbursement is what keeps the access gains of the last several years from quietly unwinding.
Hybrid Models as the Rational Response
Under chronic policy uncertainty, the economically rational provider response is a hybrid model that does not bet the organization on any single reimbursement regime. A hybrid practice uses telehealth where it adds the most value — chronic-disease check-ins, behavioral health, specialty access, triage — while preserving the in-person capacity and billing infrastructure to operate if virtual parity were ever to narrow. This diversification is more expensive to run than an all-virtual model, but it is robust to the policy volatility that defines the current environment.
The consolidation pressure the original analysis identified is real but should be attributed correctly. It is driven less by an imminent rate cut than by the fixed cost of operating a compliant, policy-resilient hybrid program — billing complexity, technology, and the capacity to absorb reimbursement risk. Larger systems amortize those fixed costs across more visits and more easily absorb the risk, which advantages scale. The policy fix that would most help independent rural providers is therefore not another short extension but permanent, parity-based telehealth payment, which would remove the uncertainty premium that currently pushes small practices toward selling or closing.
The Cost-Offset Argument for Permanence
A recurring objection to making telehealth flexibilities permanent is cost: if Medicare pays for virtual visits at parity, does total program spending rise? The honest answer is that it depends on whether telehealth substitutes for in-person care or adds to it. Where a video visit replaces an office visit, an emergency-department trip, or an avoidable admission, it is cost-neutral or cost-saving; where it represents entirely new utilization that would not otherwise have occurred, it adds cost. The policy design question is how to capture the substitution value while limiting purely additive utilization.
For the chronic-disease and behavioral-health use cases that dominate telehealth's value, the substitution-and-prevention case is strong: a timely virtual check-in that catches a deteriorating condition early can avert a far more expensive acute episode, which is exactly the kind of upstream management a chronic-disease-heavy system needs. This is why the permanence debate should be framed in terms of net system cost, not gross telehealth spend. Judged on whether it reduces total cost of care for the populations that use it most, durable telehealth payment looks less like a new entitlement and more like an investment in the lower-cost, prevention-oriented care model the system is trying to build — which strengthens, rather than undercuts, the economic case for ending the brinkmanship cycle.
What Would Actually Stabilize the Economics
The path to stable telehealth economics is straightforward to describe and hard to enact: make the core flexibilities permanent and set telehealth payment at a rate and methodology that sustainably covers the cost of delivering virtual care, including for RHCs and FQHCs. Permanence removes the uncertainty premium that suppresses investment; sustainable rate-setting resolves the second-layer margin problem. Together they would let providers — especially the small and rural ones — plan and invest with confidence, which is precisely what the on-again, off-again extension cycle prevents.
Until that happens, the telehealth 'correction' will keep being misdiagnosed. Each renewal deadline produces a wave of cliff coverage, the deadline passes with a short extension, and the underlying instability persists unaddressed. The accurate economic reading is that Medicare telehealth is funded through 2027 but governed by a process that taxes long-term investment through uncertainty — and that the durable solution is policy permanence and honest rate-setting, not another eleventh-hour reprieve.

The Telehealth Correction: Economic Implications of Expiring Waivers

######## telehealth-reimbursement-cliff-2026-rural-hospitals | The 2026 Telehealth Reimbursement Cliff: What Rural Hospitals Must Do Before the COVID-Era Waivers Expire V2
Telehealth's national policy story is about a recurring funding cliff. For rural hospitals, that cliff is not an abstraction — it is an existential question about whether a service line they have built their access strategy around will still be paid for next year. This analysis takes the rural-hospital lens on the telehealth reimbursement cliff: why rural systems are uniquely exposed, what the current extension does and does not secure, the planning problem the uncertainty creates, and how rural hospitals should manage a service whose funding keeps living deadline to deadline.
Why Rural Hospitals Are Uniquely Exposed
Most of Medicare's pandemic-era telehealth flexibilities — letting patients be seen at home, removing the rural-originating-site and geographic restrictions, allowing audio-only visits, and letting rural clinics serve as distant-site providers — were temporary waivers, and they have been extended in a series of short windows rather than made permanent. For rural hospitals this is more consequential than for urban systems, because rural providers built telehealth into the core of how they reach patients across distance: tele-specialty consults, remote monitoring, and virtual primary and behavioral-health visits are not a convenience layer but a substitute for the specialists and services the community does not have locally.
When the flexibilities lapse or are threatened, rural hospitals face the prospect of either absorbing the cost of telehealth they can no longer bill Medicare for, or cutting services their patients depend on. The audio-only provision is especially critical rurally, because it is the lifeline for patients without the broadband for video — disproportionately rural patients — so a cliff that takes audio-only with it cuts hardest exactly where alternatives are thinnest.
For urban systems, the telehealth cliff is a reimbursement nuisance. For rural hospitals that built their access strategy on virtual specialty and behavioral-health care, it's an existential question about whether the service line replacing absent local providers will still be paid for next year.
What the Current Extension Secures
The landscape is now split into durable and precarious halves. The Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 extended the general Medicare telehealth flexibilities through December 31, 2027 — a two-year window of relative certainty (CMS; Quarles). And crucially, the core behavioral-health telehealth flexibilities were made permanent by earlier Consolidated Appropriations Acts: no geographic restriction, the home as an eligible site, and audio-only delivery for mental-health care are now codified in statute (Telehealth.HHS.gov). For rural hospitals, the permanent behavioral-health carve-out is the most reassuring piece, since behavioral health is the highest-value rural telehealth use case.
But the non-behavioral flexibilities — which cover most of rural telehealth's volume — still expire at the end of 2027 absent further action, and they have lapsed before (briefly, during a 2025 funding impasse). So a rural hospital planning beyond 2027 is planning against a cliff, and the recurring nature of these extensions means the uncertainty is structural, not a one-time event. Rural systems have learned to expect a last-minute extension, but cannot bank on one.
The Planning Problem
The deepest harm of the cliff may not be any single lapse but the chronic uncertainty itself. Building a telehealth program — the technology, the staffing, the workflows, the patient relationships — requires multi-year investment, and no rational administrator commits capital to a service line whose reimbursement might vanish at the next funding deadline. The cliff therefore suppresses exactly the durable investment rural telehealth needs, keeping programs perpetually provisional. A rural hospital that would build robust tele-specialty infrastructure for a permanent benefit instead runs a minimal, hedge-everything version because it cannot trust the payment will persist.
This is the case for permanence beyond any single extension: the value of making the flexibilities permanent is not only continued payment but the investment certainty that unlocks serious program-building. And the cost evidence increasingly supports permanence — research finding telehealth substitutes for rather than adds to utilization undercuts the budget fear that has driven the short-term, cliff-edge approach. The rural-access case and the cost case now point the same direction; what is missing is the political resolution to make the non-behavioral flexibilities permanent rather than perpetually extended.
[{"Element":"Behavioral health","Status":"Permanent (CAA 2021/2023) — the durable piece"},{"Element":"Non-behavioral (geographic/home/audio)","Status":"Extended to Dec 31, 2027 — still a cliff"},{"Element":"Rural stake","Status":"Telehealth substitutes for absent local specialists/services"},{"Element":"Planning effect","Status":"Chronic uncertainty suppresses durable investment"}]
The telehealth cliff: rural exposure
The Broadband Dependency Underneath
The telehealth cliff has a quieter twin: even if reimbursement is secured permanently, rural telehealth only works where the broadband to deliver it exists. The two constraints interact and compound — a permanent payment guarantee is worthless in a community where the signal cannot carry a video visit, and the best broadband in the world is useless if Medicare will not pay for the visit. For rural hospitals, telehealth sustainability is therefore a two-front problem: the payment cliff and the connectivity gap, and progress on only one leaves the service stranded on the other.
This is why rural telehealth strategy cannot be separated from the federal broadband investment (BEAD) running in parallel: they are two halves of the same access infrastructure. A rural hospital evaluating its telehealth future has to track both the reimbursement timeline and the broadband buildout timeline in its market, because the service's viability depends on both arriving — and the audio-only provision exists precisely as the bridge for the period and the places where video-grade broadband has not yet reached, which is one more reason its permanence matters so much to rural systems.
How Rural Hospitals Should Manage It
Until permanence arrives, rural hospitals have to manage the cliff deliberately. The first move is to build aggressively on the permanent foundation — behavioral-health telehealth is durable and is the highest-value rural use case, so it is the safest place to invest. The second is to model the 2027 cliff explicitly in financial planning, with contingency scenarios for full extension, partial extension (e.g., rural-HPSA and audio-only only), and full sunset, so a lapse does not arrive as a surprise. The third is to diversify payer reliance: commercial and Medicaid managed-care coverage of telehealth does not depend on the Medicare waiver, so a program anchored partly in those payers is more resilient to a Medicare cliff. And the fourth is advocacy — rural hospitals and their associations are among the most credible voices for permanence, and the cliff persists partly because the case has not been pressed to resolution.
What to Watch
Key signals: whether Congress makes the non-behavioral flexibilities permanent before the December 2027 deadline or extends them again at the edge; whether audio-only payment survives, given its rural importance; whether the substitution-not-addition cost evidence reshapes the CBO scoring that has stalled permanence; and whether rural telehealth investment recovers if certainty arrives or stays minimal under continued uncertainty. The behavioral-health foundation is secure; the rest of rural telehealth's future hinges on whether policymakers convert a recurring cliff into a permanent benefit.
Bottom Line
The telehealth reimbursement cliff is a national policy quirk that lands as an existential threat on rural hospitals, which built their access strategy on virtual care substituting for absent local providers. The current state is bifurcated: behavioral-health telehealth is permanently secured — the most valuable rural piece — while the broader flexibilities are extended only through 2027 and have lapsed before. The deepest damage is the chronic uncertainty, which suppresses the durable investment rural telehealth needs. With the cost evidence now favoring permanence and the rural-access case strong, the constructive path is clear: make the flexibilities permanent. Until then, rural hospitals should build on the permanent behavioral-health base, plan explicitly for the cliff, diversify payers, and press the case — because a service line this central to rural access should not have to be re-justified every funding deadline. The broader point is that telehealth has graduated from pandemic stopgap to permanent rural infrastructure in everything but its financing, and the policy lag is now the main obstacle. A rural hospital can build the technology, train the staff, and earn patient trust in months; what it cannot do is manufacture the multi-year payment certainty that justifies the investment. Closing that gap — converting the recurring cliff into a permanent benefit, and pairing it with the broadband that makes telehealth physically possible — is the single most valuable thing federal policy could do for rural telehealth, and the evidence to justify it is already in hand. For now, rural telehealth survives on borrowed time and renewed deadlines — a precarious footing for infrastructure that millions of rural patients now depend on for access to the specialists and behavioral-health care their communities cannot otherwise provide.

The 2026 Telehealth Reimbursement Cliff: What Rural Hospitals Must Do Before the COVID-Era Waivers Expire V2

######## uninhabitable-math-vermont-healthcare-cliff | The Uninhabitable Math: Decoding Vermont's $2.4 Billion Healthcare Cliff
For decades, American health care ran on a silent social contract: commercial-insurance premiums would rise indefinitely to cover the operating losses hospitals booked on Medicare and Medicaid. Vermont's Act 167 investigation documented, in unsentimental financial terms, that this contract has expired in the state. What the report describes is not a cyclical downturn but a structural decoupling — expense growth has detached from any revenue capacity Vermont can plausibly muster.
Simple solutions will not work... Higher taxation is unlikely to be a viable solution either due to the potential risk of driving residents and businesses out of state.
The Decoupling at the Center of the Report
The independent consultant Oliver Wyman, engaged under Act 167, concluded after more than 230 meetings across all of Vermont's hospital service areas — involving over 3,100 participants and roughly 100 organizations — that Vermont's health system is not sustainable on its current trajectory and that many hospitals face near-term solvency risk. The finding is not that costs are merely high; it is that the rate of cost growth has separated from the state's ability to pay, so that even a healthy economy cannot grow revenue fast enough to close the gap.
This is what makes the math 'uninhabitable.' In a normal market, demand falls as price rises, restoring balance. In Vermont health care, demand is demographically locked in — an aging, rural population needs care regardless of price — while the revenue base (commercially insured workers and their employers) is shrinking as premiums climb. The two lines do not converge; they diverge, year after year, which is the formal definition of an unsustainable system.
Why Premiums Can No Longer Carry the Load
The cross-subsidy worked while the commercial pool was large and growing. It is now contracting under its own price. On Vermont Health Connect, the individual exchange, premiums have roughly doubled or more in recent years. Payers have told the legislature that Vermont's regulated insurance markets have been losing money, citing hospital prices and medical spending as the principal drivers. In 2023, Blue Cross and Blue Shield of Vermont paid out about $1.11 in claims for every dollar of premium collected — a structural loss, not a one-year anomaly.
Each premium increase that is meant to shore up hospital revenue instead accelerates the erosion of the pool that funds it: employers thin benefits, individuals drop coverage or forgo care, and the commercial base that the whole cross-subsidy depends on gets smaller and sicker. The Medicare Advantage market tells the same story — for 2026, all but one Medicare Advantage carrier exited Vermont after concluding federal payments did not match local costs, stranding tens of thousands of seniors. When private payers withdraw because the numbers do not work, it is a market signaling that the price of Vermont care has outrun what any payer will fund.
Why Taxation Is Not the Escape Hatch
The intuitive response to a public-good shortfall is to tax. The Act 167 report explicitly closes that door, warning that higher taxation risks driving residents and businesses out of a small state with a mobile tax base and an already-high cost of living. Economically, this is the cost-shifting trap: a tax does not reduce the cost of producing care, it merely moves the bill from premium-payers to taxpayers — often the same households — while adding the risk of out-migration that shrinks the tax base itself.
This is why the report frames the problem as requiring structural transformation rather than a new funding source. There is no untapped pool of payers waiting to absorb the gap. Commercial premiums are already at the edge of the pool's tolerance, public payers are already among the lowest in the nation, and taxation threatens the state's economic competitiveness. Every financing lever is near its limit, which is exactly why the report insists the answer must be to lower the cost of care itself, not to find someone new to pay the existing cost.
The 'uninhabitable math' is the simultaneous exhaustion of all three financing levers: commercial premiums (collapsing the pool that funds them), public reimbursement (already among the nation's lowest), and taxation (risking out-migration from a small, mobile state). When every payer is maxed out, the only remaining variable is the cost of producing care.
What 'Lowest in the Nation' Public Payment Really Means
Vermont hospital leaders repeatedly note that the state's public-payer reimbursement is among the lowest in the country, and this is central to the arithmetic. If Medicare and Medicaid already pay near the floor, there is no slack on the public side to absorb more of the load — the public payers cannot be squeezed further to relieve the commercial pool, and the federal government is not poised to raise Vermont's rates. The cross-subsidy was, in effect, the state's improvised workaround for chronically thin public payment, and that workaround has now run out of room.
This closes the last conceptual escape route. One might imagine that if commercial premiums and state taxes are exhausted, the answer is simply to shift more cost back onto Medicare and Medicaid — but those payers are already at the bottom of the national distribution, and their payment rates are set federally, beyond Vermont's control. With the commercial pool collapsing, the public payers floored, and taxation foreclosed, the arithmetic genuinely has no remaining payer to absorb the gap. That exhaustion of payers is the precise mechanism that converts a difficult budget into an uninhabitable one, and it is why the report treats structural cost reduction not as one option among several but as the only option the math leaves standing.
The Structural Reforms the Math Forces
If revenue cannot rise, expense must fall — not through indiscriminate cuts that strip access, but through structural redesign. Vermont's central tools are hospital global budgets and reference-based pricing, which Vermont planned to pursue alongside the federal AHEAD model (pairing hospital global budgets with statewide cost-growth targets) until it withdrew from AHEAD in July 2026; both now rest on state law, Act 68 of 2025. A global budget caps a hospital's total annual revenue and forces it to operate within that envelope, flipping the incentive from maximizing billable volume to keeping a population healthy at lower total cost. Reference-based pricing attacks the price side directly by benchmarking payments to a defined standard rather than to ever-escalating chargemaster rates.
These reforms are hard and contested, and their savings are not guaranteed. But they are the only category of response the math actually permits, because they target the cost of care rather than reshuffling who pays for it. The Act 167 report's value is in foreclosing the comfortable alternatives — shop harder, raise premiums, raise taxes — and leaving policymakers facing the one genuinely difficult question that the arithmetic supports: how to deliver the care an aging Vermont needs using meaningfully fewer resources.
The Demographic Multiplier Behind the Numbers
The cliff is steep because Vermont's demographics multiply every cost pressure. Vermont is among the oldest states in the nation — roughly 22 percent of residents are 65 or older and about 28 percent are 60-plus — and an older population consumes substantially more health care per person, concentrated in exactly the high-cost categories (chronic disease, hospitalization, long-term care) that the system struggles to fund. The workforce that delivers that care is aging in parallel: roughly one in three Vermont primary-care physicians is over 60, and half the state's nurses are over 48, so supply is contracting precisely as demand rises.
This demographic multiplier is why Vermont hits the wall before larger, younger states do. The same national forces — chronic-disease growth, medical inflation, workforce shortages — are amplified by an age structure that pushes demand up and a labor structure that pulls supply down at the same time. The state has already lost roughly 500 nursing-home and assisted-living beds to closures and staffing shortages, producing hospital discharge bottlenecks in which patients wait days, weeks, or months for an appropriate care setting. Those stranded patients occupy expensive hospital beds they no longer need, a vivid illustration of how the demographic squeeze converts directly into wasted cost the budget cannot absorb.
Reading the Cliff Honestly
It is worth being precise about what the 'cliff' is and is not. It is not a prediction that Vermont hospitals will all close tomorrow; it is a statement that the current financing structure cannot continue and that, absent structural change, the system degrades through solvency crises, service closures, and lost access — the long-term-care bed losses and discharge bottlenecks already visible in the state. The cliff is the point at which incremental fixes stop working and only structural redesign remains.
That reframing is the report's real contribution. By converting an emotional sense of crisis into dated, dollar-denominated arithmetic, Act 167 makes the trade-offs explicit and the easy answers visibly inadequate. The math is uninhabitable only on the current path; it becomes habitable the moment Vermont accepts that the variable it must change is the cost of producing care, and builds the payment and delivery structures — global budgets, prevention, right-sized services — that can actually change it.

Vermont's Act 167 investigation (Oliver Wyman: 230+ meetings, 3,100+ participants, all of Vermont's hospital service areas) found the commercial cross-subsidy has expired: premiums on Vermont Health Connect have doubled+, BCBSVT paid $1.11 per premium dollar in 2023, and all but one Medicare Advantage carrier exited for 2026. With premiums, public reimbursement, and taxation all maxed out, the only remaining lever is the cost of care — driving global budgets and reference-based pricing, now under state law (Act 68) after Vermont's July 2026 withdrawal from AHEAD.
The Uninhabitable Math: Decoding Vermont's $2.4 Billion Healthcare Cliff

######## vbc-fundamentals-module-2-policy-pillar | Value-Based Care Fundamentals — Module 2: The Policy Pillar — Laws, Rules, and the Regulatory Architecture That Makes (or Breaks) Value-Based Care
Imagine you want to start a new kind of restaurant — one that charges customers a flat monthly fee to eat as many healthy meals as they need, rather than charging per dish. The idea makes sense nutritionally and economically. But before you open, you discover that local zoning laws require all food establishments to post per-item prices. The health department’s inspection checklist was designed for traditional menus. Your liquor license application has no category for subscription dining. None of these rules were written to block your idea — they were simply written when your idea did not exist. This is precisely the situation that value-based care innovators face in the American healthcare regulatory environment. The laws, billing codes, anti-kickback rules, and licensure frameworks that govern U.S. healthcare were almost entirely written for a fee-for-service world. Navigating, modifying, and sometimes working around them is not a bureaucratic inconvenience for VBC implementation — it is one of its defining challenges.
Why Policy Is a Pillar, Not Just a Background Condition
Healthcare is one of the most heavily regulated industries in the United States, and for good reason: the decisions made in healthcare settings involve life and death, profound information asymmetry between providers and patients, and the expenditure of trillions of dollars of public funds. The regulatory architecture that governs healthcare payment — Medicare and Medicaid rules, anti-kickback statutes, the Stark Law governing physician self-referral, state insurance laws, and HIPAA privacy regulations — was built incrementally over six decades, each layer added in response to a specific problem or abuse. The result is a regulatory environment of extraordinary complexity that can simultaneously protect patients and obstruct innovation, often in the same rule. For value-based care specifically, the policy environment matters in three distinct ways: it determines which VBC models are legally permissible, it shapes the financial terms under which VBC contracts can operate, and it establishes the data sharing authorities that VBC’s information infrastructure depends upon.
The good news for learners approaching this material is that you do not need to become a healthcare attorney to understand the policy landscape for VBC. What you do need is a working knowledge of four things: the major federal legislation that authorized and funds VBC programs, the agency — CMS and its Innovation Center — that designs and operates most federal VBC models, the specific legal waivers that allow VBC arrangements to operate outside standard fee-for-service rules, and the state-level Medicaid policy environment that governs VBC adoption for the Medicaid population, which accounts for about 18% of all U.S. healthcare spending and the majority of payer mix for rural and safety-net hospitals. This module covers all four in sequence, with enough practical depth that you can apply the concepts to your organization’s specific situation.
The ACA’s Innovation Center: The Engine of U.S. VBC Development
The Center for Medicare and Medicaid Innovation, universally known as CMMI, was established by Section 3021 of the Affordable Care Act in 2010 with a mandatory appropriation of $10 billion over ten years and a statutory mission to test, evaluate, and scale new payment and delivery models that reduce spending while preserving or improving quality. CMMI’s legal authority is significant: it can implement new payment models for Medicare and Medicaid without going through the standard notice-and-comment rulemaking process that governs most federal regulation, as long as the model operates within specific statutory parameters; expanding a model nationally additionally requires the CMS Chief Actuary to certify that expansion would not increase net program spending. This streamlined authority was deliberately designed to allow rapid policy experimentation — the legislative drafters recognized that VBC model design requires iteration and learning, and that subjecting every model modification to a 12-month rulemaking cycle would make meaningful innovation impossible.
Since 2011, CMMI has launched more than 50 distinct payment model pilots across Medicare and Medicaid, covering primary care transformation, specialty care bundling, accountable care organizations, direct contracting, and state-level all-payer models. The budget allocated per model varies enormously — from small demonstration projects covering a few thousand beneficiaries to large-scale national programs like the Medicare Shared Savings Program, which enrolled 11.2 million beneficiaries by 2025. CMMI’s mandate requires it to demonstrate that each model either reduces net Medicare spending, improves quality without increasing spending, or both. Models that fail to meet these thresholds can be modified or terminated. CMMI's own 2021 strategy review found that only six of its more than 50 models had produced statistically significant savings, and in March 2025 it announced it would end four models early, effective December 31, 2025 — Primary Care First, Making Care Primary, ESRD Treatment Choices, and the Maryland Total Cost of Care Model — citing evaluations and forecasts of increased spending; it is a record that reflects honest evaluation but has also drawn criticism from providers who invested substantially in model infrastructure before termination decisions.
CMMI’s authority to move faster than the standard rulemaking process is its most valuable feature and its most politically contested one. Every model CMMI launches is a hypothesis about how to organize care better. Some hypotheses are wrong. The question is whether the system is learning fast enough from both successes and failures.
MACRA: The Law That Made VBC Participation Effectively Mandatory for Physicians
If CMMI is the engine of federal VBC development, the Medicare Access and CHIP Reauthorization Act of 2015 — universally known as MACRA — is the law that turned VBC from an optional experiment into the default trajectory for physician payment in Medicare. MACRA was passed with rare bipartisan consensus, repealing the deeply unpopular Sustainable Growth Rate formula that had governed Medicare physician payments since 1997 and replacing it with a two-track system that explicitly links physician payment updates to VBC participation. Understanding MACRA’s structure is essential for any clinical or administrative leader whose organization bills Medicare for physician services, because it directly determines how physicians are paid and how much.
MACRA’s two tracks work as follows. Physicians who remain in traditional fee-for-service Medicare participate in the Merit-based Incentive Payment System, or MIPS, which adjusts their annual Medicare payments up or down by up to 9% based on performance across four categories: quality (30% weight), cost (30%), promoting interoperability (25%), and improvement activities (15%) — the weights in effect since the 2022 performance year. MIPS is designed as a transitional step — it maintains the fee-for-service base payment structure while adding a performance overlay that rewards quality and penalizes inefficiency. The alternative track, and the one that MACRA’s architects intended as the destination, is participation in an Advanced Alternative Payment Model, or APM, which includes CMS-approved VBC arrangements that meet specific risk and quality criteria. Physicians who generate a sufficient share of their Medicare revenue through APM participation become Qualifying APM Participants — originally earning a 5% lump-sum bonus (since reduced and, for 2025 performance onward, lapsed) and a higher fee-schedule update — and are exempt from MIPS reporting requirements — a substantial financial incentive to move toward more advanced VBC structures.
The MIPS-to-APM Progression: What It Looks Like in Practice
The practical effect of MACRA’s two-track architecture is to create a structured pathway from fee-for-service through MIPS participation toward Advanced APM engagement, with financial incentives escalating at each stage. A solo primary care physician in rural Wyoming who bills Medicare exclusively on a fee-for-service basis is automatically enrolled in MIPS and will receive payment adjustments — upward or downward — based on their quality reporting, EHR utilization, and cost performance. The same physician who joins an Advanced APM, such as a two-sided Medicare ACO, and receives at least 75% of their Medicare Part B payments (or sees 50% of their Medicare patients) through it qualifies as an Advanced APM participant, earns the higher QP fee-schedule update (the original 5% lump-sum bonus lapsed after the 2024 performance year), and escapes the MIPS reporting burden. MACRA’s architecture was designed to make this progression financially attractive enough that providers would voluntarily move toward advanced VBC structures over time, without the political controversy of making VBC participation legally mandatory.

MACRA Payment Track Comparison: MIPS vs. Advanced APM Participation, 2026
Policy Insight for Practice Leaders: If your organization’s physicians are currently in MIPS and scoring below 75, you are almost certainly paying more in administrative reporting costs than you are receiving in payment bonuses. A 2021 study of independent practices by Khullar and colleagues (JAMA Health Forum) put the cost of MIPS participation at $12,811 per physician per year in 2019, driven by about 200 hours of physician and staff time. Joining an ACO or Advanced APM eliminates this burden while generating shared savings upside. The question is not whether to make the move — it is when your organization has the infrastructure to do it responsibly.
The Stark Law and Anti-Kickback Statute: The Rules VBC Had to Work Around
Two of the most significant legal barriers to value-based care adoption are laws that were designed to protect patients from fraud: the Stark Law, which prohibits physician self-referral for designated health services when a financial relationship exists between the physician and the referred entity, and the Federal Anti-Kickback Statute, which prohibits offering or receiving anything of value to induce referrals of federal healthcare program business. Both laws were enacted in the 1980s and 1990s to address genuine abuses in fee-for-service medicine — physicians referring patients to laboratories or imaging centers they owned, and pharmaceutical companies paying physicians to prescribe specific drugs. They are important protections. They are also written in a way that, on their face, makes many value-based care arrangements technically illegal.
Consider a simple VBC arrangement: a hospital wants to share a portion of the savings generated by an ACO with the primary care physicians who successfully manage their attributed patient panels and reduce unnecessary hospitalizations. Under a strict reading of the Anti-Kickback Statute, this payment could be characterized as remuneration to physicians for reducing referrals to the hospital — which is exactly what the statute was written to prohibit when the financial relationship runs in the other direction. In November 2020, CMS (for the Stark Law) and the HHS Office of Inspector General (for the Anti-Kickback Statute) simultaneously published new rules — commonly called the Stark Law and AKS VBC Exceptions — that created explicit regulatory safe harbors for value-based arrangements meeting specific criteria. These exceptions are the legal foundation on which most current VBC contract structures rest, and understanding their existence (if not their full technical detail) is important context for any leader involved in VBC contract negotiation.
What the 2020 VBC Exceptions Actually Allow
The 2020 Stark Law and AKS VBC exceptions created three tiers of safe harbor protection based on the level of financial risk the arrangement assumes. The first tier, the “care coordination arrangement” exception, covers low-risk VBC arrangements where physicians and hospitals share non-monetary resources — care coordinators, technology platforms, data analytics tools — without direct financial remuneration flowing between them. The second tier covers arrangements where the parties share substantial downside financial risk, defined as the physician group being responsible for a meaningful share of losses if spending targets are not met. The third and most protective tier applies to arrangements where a physician entity is “fully at risk” — accepting capitation-level accountability for a defined patient population. The practical effect of this tiered structure is that more financially protective exceptions are available to VBC arrangements that accept more financial risk — a policy design that explicitly incentivizes providers to move toward more advanced VBC models in exchange for greater legal certainty.
State Medicaid Policy: Where VBC Gets Complicated Fast
Medicaid, the joint federal-state health insurance program for low-income Americans, covered about 76 million people together with CHIP at the end of 2025 and accounts for about 18% of all U.S. healthcare spending. For rural and safety-net hospitals, Medicaid is often the dominant payer — in some Critical Access Hospitals, Medicaid accounts for more than 40% of patient volume. Any serious discussion of VBC adoption must therefore grapple with Medicaid policy, and Medicaid policy is, by design, a state-level affair. Under the Social Security Act, the federal government sets minimum Medicaid standards and matches state spending at a regular rate (FMAP) that runs from a 50% floor to about 77% depending on state per-capita income, with a separate 90% match for the ACA expansion population — but states have substantial flexibility in how they design their Medicaid programs, including how they pay providers. This flexibility has produced a 50-state patchwork of Medicaid VBC approaches that range from nationally leading to years behind, often with little correlation to state wealth or healthcare system capacity.
States access this flexibility primarily through two mechanisms: Section 1115 waivers, which allow states to use Medicaid funds in ways not otherwise permitted by federal statute in exchange for CMS approval of a formal waiver application and evaluation framework, and Section 1915 waivers, which allow states to require Medicaid beneficiaries to enroll in managed care organizations rather than traditional fee-for-service Medicaid. As of 2025, 41 states (including DC) contract with comprehensive Medicaid managed care organizations, and many states use Section 1115 waivers to add VBC-oriented payment reform components. The remaining states continue to operate primarily fee-for-service Medicaid programs, creating a two-tier national Medicaid environment where the VBC infrastructure, data systems, and provider capabilities in a given state depend heavily on decisions made by state legislatures and Medicaid agencies that most healthcare professionals are only vaguely aware of.

State Medicaid VBC Adoption Landscape: Selected State Comparisons, 2025
The state variation in Medicaid VBC adoption documented in the table above is not simply a story of some states moving faster than others. It reflects fundamentally different political economies, administrative capacities, and provider infrastructure realities. Massachusetts’ MassHealth ACO Program, which by June 2023 enrolled more than 1.3 million members — over half of all MassHealth members — in its ACOs, required a decade of incremental managed care development, a sophisticated state health information exchange, and a robust community health worker infrastructure before global budgets were feasible. Mississippi, where about 65% of Medicaid beneficiaries are in MississippiCAN managed care and the rest remain in fee-for-service (Division of Medicaid 2024 Annual Report), faces not just a political environment less favorable to managed care but a provider infrastructure so financially fragile — as examined in the DSH module — that asking rural hospitals to accept downside financial risk before their basic solvency is secured would be policy malpractice. Understanding this context prevents the common mistake of treating state VBC laggards as simply lacking political will, when the more accurate diagnosis is often a missing infrastructure foundation.
The AHEAD Model: The Boldest Current Federal VBC Policy Experiment
The CMS AHEAD Model — announced in 2023 as the States Advancing All-Payer Health Equity Approaches and Development Model and renamed Achieving Healthcare Efficiency through Accountable Design after CMS redesigned it in September 2025 — represents the most ambitious federal VBC policy experiment currently active in the United States. AHEAD is fundamentally different from previous CMS models in one critical way: it operates across all payers simultaneously, not just Medicare. In the participating states — Maryland, Connecticut, Hawaii, Rhode Island, and downstate New York (Vermont signed in January 2025 but withdrew in July 2026) — CMS is working with state governments and commercial insurers to establish coordinated spending targets that apply to Medicare, Medicaid, and commercial insurance simultaneously. The goal is to eliminate the perverse incentive created by single-payer VBC models, where a provider that successfully reduces costs for its Medicare ACO population may simply shift utilization to its commercially insured population, producing savings for Medicare at the expense of commercial payer spending.
AHEAD’s architecture has three components that build on each other. Hospital Global Budgets establish annual spending targets for inpatient and outpatient hospital care, similar to the all-payer hospital global budgets Maryland has operated statewide since 2014, built on the all-payer rate-setting system it has run under a Medicare waiver since 1977. Primary Care Investment establishes a per-capita funding floor for primary care services in each participating state, addressing the underfunding of primary care that Module 1 identified as a structural driver of the U.S. spending-outcomes gap. And population health accountability — originally a state Health Equity Plan with disparity-reduction goals — was replaced in CMS's September 2025 redesign by statewide and hospital Population Health Accountability Plans focused on preventive care and chronic disease prevention, while the requirements to report quality by race and ethnicity and to submit demographic data were dropped and states must now adopt at least two policies promoting choice and competition. AHEAD’s early results will not be statistically meaningful until 2027 at the earliest, but its structural design represents the most coherent attempt yet to address the whole-system payment reform that health economists have argued is necessary for genuine transformation.
The AHEAD Model is not a pilot. It is a policy architecture for what the American healthcare payment system could look like at scale — one where no payer is an island, where primary care is funded as infrastructure rather than as a commodity, and where population health is a contractual obligation rather than an aspiration. Whether it works depends entirely on whether the participating states build the administrative and data infrastructure to make the targets meaningful.
Reading Policy Signals: How to Know What’s Coming Before It Arrives
One of the most practical skills a healthcare leader can develop is the ability to read federal policy signals early — to understand what CMS, Congress, and state Medicaid agencies are signaling about the future direction of payment policy before new rules take effect. This skill is valuable because VBC model participation requires organizational investment — in data systems, care management staffing, and actuarial capabilities — that takes 18 to 36 months to build. Organizations that wait until a rule is finalized to begin building readiness will always be behind. The following framework identifies the primary signals that healthcare leaders should monitor continuously as leading indicators of VBC policy direction.
CMS Requests for Information (RFIs) are formal public notices in which CMS invites stakeholder comment on a policy concept before publishing a proposed rule. An RFI on a new payment model concept typically precedes a proposed rule by 12–18 months, giving organizations that monitor them a substantial head start on understanding what is coming and beginning internal readiness assessments.
The Annual Physician Fee Schedule Proposed Rule, published every July for the following calendar year, contains the annual MIPS threshold adjustments, quality measure updates, and any modifications to Advanced APM qualifying criteria. Reading the proposed rule summary — not the full document, which runs to thousands of pages — each July is the single most efficient way to stay current on federal physician payment policy.
CMMI Model Termination Notices are official signals that a payment model has not met its statutory savings or quality thresholds. When CMMI terminates a model, it typically publishes evaluation findings that explain why. Reading these findings is often more instructive than reading the original model design documents, because they identify the specific infrastructure gaps, attribution problems, or market conditions that prevented the model from working — lessons directly applicable to understanding what makes other VBC models succeed.
State Medicaid Managed Care Request for Proposals are published by state Medicaid agencies when they are procuring new managed care contracts, typically on three-to-five year cycles. These documents specify the quality metrics, VBC incentive structures, SDOH screening requirements, and health equity goals that Medicaid managed care organizations and their provider networks will be held to. Organizations that serve Medicaid populations should read their state’s current managed care RFP as a direct statement of the performance expectations they will face in the next contract cycle.
Module 2 Summary: The Policy Environment Is Not Your Enemy — But You Must Understand It
The policy architecture governing value-based care in the United States is complex, layered, and still evolving — but it is not impenetrable to practitioners willing to invest in understanding its structure. The key institutions are CMS and its Innovation Center, which design and operate most federal VBC models; Congress, which authorizes and funds VBC infrastructure through legislation like MACRA and the ACA; and state Medicaid agencies, which determine how value-based principles are applied to the Medicaid population that drives solvency for most safety-net and rural providers. The key legal frameworks are MACRA’s two-track physician payment architecture, the 2020 Stark Law and Anti-Kickback VBC exceptions, and the Section 1115 and 1915 waiver authorities that give states flexibility to innovate within federal Medicaid rules.
The most important single insight from this module is that policy does not just regulate VBC — it enables it. The shared savings that MSSP ACOs distribute to physicians are legal because of CMMI’s model authority and the AKS safe harbor. The global budgets that Maryland hospitals operate under rest on the state’s all-payer rate-setting authority, first granted by a Medicare waiver in 1977 and continued today under its AHEAD agreement. The care coordination resources that a hospital shares with its independent physician partners are legally protected because of the 2020 VBC exceptions. Every advanced VBC arrangement you will encounter in your career rests on a specific legal and regulatory foundation. Understanding that foundation — at least at the level of knowing which questions to ask your legal team — is not optional for any leader who wants to move their organization toward value.
Identify your organization’s current MACRA track: are your physicians primarily in MIPS, in an Advanced APM, or in both? If primarily MIPS, what is the organization’s target timeline for APM qualification, and what infrastructure gaps stand between you and that threshold?
Review your state’s current Medicaid managed care contract or RFP. Identify the three quality metrics with the highest incentive weight and assess your organization’s current performance on each. The gap between your current performance and the contract’s quality bonus threshold is a direct measure of revenue at risk.
Determine whether your state is an AHEAD Model participant. If yes, begin engaging your state hospital association and state health department about the hospital global budget timeline and what data your organization will need to submit. If no, note that CMS's September 2025 redesign of AHEAD offered an opportunity for two additional states to participate starting in 2028 and/or 2029; all cohorts now run through December 31, 2035.
Schedule a conversation with your legal or compliance team specifically about the 2020 Stark Law and Anti-Kickback VBC exceptions. Ask them to identify any current or planned VBC arrangements at your organization that rely on these exceptions, and to confirm that the arrangements are structured to satisfy the applicable safe harbor criteria.
Module 2 Key Takeaway: The U.S. healthcare policy environment was built for fee-for-service, but it has been systematically modified over the past 15 years to enable value-based care. CMMI’s model authority, MACRA’s APM incentive structure, the 2020 VBC legal exceptions, and state Medicaid waiver programs together constitute a policy architecture that makes advanced VBC legally and financially feasible for most U.S. healthcare organizations. The organizations that understand this architecture and align their strategy to it are the ones that will be best positioned as CMS accelerates its stated goal of having 100% of Medicare beneficiaries in an accountable care relationship by 2030.

Module 2 of the HTR Academy Value-Based Care Fundamentals course examines the federal and state policy architecture that governs value-based care adoption in the United States. Building on Module 1’s conceptual foundation, this module explains in plain language how MACRA, the ACA’s Innovation Center, and state Medicaid waiver programs create the legal and financial scaffolding for VBC models, identifies the regulatory barriers that slow adoption for rural and safety-net providers, and equips learners to read and interpret the policy signals that will shape their organization’s VBC strategy through 2030. Value-Based Care Fundamentals — Module 2: The Policy Pillar — Laws, Rules, and the Regulatory Architecture That Makes (or Breaks) Value-Based Care

######## vbc-fundamentals-module-3-economics-pillar | Value-Based Care Fundamentals — Module 3: The Economics Pillar — Following the Money in Value-Based Care
Here is a question that sounds simple but is genuinely hard to answer: if a hospital successfully reduces its patients’ hospitalization rate by 15% through better care management, is that good news or bad news for the hospital’s finances? The instinctive answer is that it must be good news — fewer hospitalizations means healthier patients, which is surely the goal. But in a world where 40% of that hospital’s revenue still comes from fee-for-service Medicare and Medicaid payments, fewer hospitalizations means fewer inpatient admissions, which means fewer billed days, which means lower revenue. The hospital just cut its own income by doing its job better. This is not a hypothetical paradox — it is the central economic tension of the volume-to-value transition, experienced by virtually every health system that has seriously engaged with value-based care. Understanding this tension, and the financial mechanisms that VBC contract structures use to resolve it, is what this module is about.
The Financial Anatomy of a Shared Savings Contract
The Medicare Shared Savings Program is the most widely adopted VBC model in the United States, and shared savings is the financial mechanism at its core. Understanding how shared savings actually works — how the benchmark is set, how savings are calculated, and how they are distributed — is the foundation for understanding the economics of most VBC arrangements. The concept is straightforward: CMS calculates how much it would have expected to spend on your attributed patient population if they had remained in traditional fee-for-service Medicare, adjusted for their age, health status, and geographic cost factors. This projected spend is your “benchmark.” If your ACO’s actual spending on that population comes in below the benchmark, the difference is “savings.” You share those savings with CMS according to a pre-agreed formula — typically retaining 40–75% depending on your risk track and quality performance, with CMS keeping the remainder.
The devil, as always, is in the details. The benchmark calculation uses a three-year historical average of your patient population’s spending, adjusted upward by a national trend factor. For ACOs whose patient populations were already low-cost before joining the program, this creates an immediate structural problem: their benchmark is low, so even excellent care management produces modest absolute savings, making the shared savings payment insufficient to cover the care management infrastructure investment. This is called the “low benchmark problem,” and it is the primary reason that rural and safety-net ACOs, which often already operate lean systems with lower baseline utilization, struggle to generate shared savings large enough to justify Advanced APM participation costs. CMS has revised the benchmark methodology repeatedly in response to this problem — adding regional adjustments for agreement periods beginning in 2017, reworking them in the 2018 Pathways to Success rule, and, in the CY2023 Physician Fee Schedule rule, adding a prior-savings adjustment and an administratively set growth factor (the Accountable Care Prospective Trend) to blunt the ratchet effect — but the fundamental tension between historical benchmarking and equitable VBC participation for already-efficient providers remains unresolved.
Upside-Only vs. Two-Sided Risk: The Decision That Changes Everything
One of the most consequential decisions a provider organization makes when entering a shared savings arrangement is whether to accept only upside risk — where you share in savings if you beat the benchmark but owe nothing if you miss it — or two-sided risk, where you both share savings and repay losses if spending exceeds the benchmark. This choice has profound implications for the financial planning, working capital requirements, and organizational risk appetite of the participating entity. Upside-only arrangements (MSSP Track 1 and its successors) are appropriate for organizations beginning their VBC journey: they provide financial experience with population cost management without the catastrophic downside scenario of a major loss repayment obligation. Two-sided arrangements (the MSSP ENHANCED Track, and CMMI’s ACO REACH Model — the 2023 successor to Global and Professional Direct Contracting, which runs through December 31, 2026 and is to be succeeded by the Long-term Enhanced ACO Design (LEAD) Model from January 1, 2027) offer higher shared savings percentages — sometimes up to 80% of savings — but require the organization to maintain actuarial reserves sufficient to cover a loss scenario, typically modeled at 1–3% of benchmark spending.

Shared Savings Contract Economics: MSSP Track Comparison for a Hypothetical 5,000-Beneficiary ACO
The financial model in the table above illustrates why Two-Sided and Full-Risk tracks are substantially more attractive in their best-case financial outcome — but also why the actuarial reserve requirement creates a genuine barrier to entry for organizations operating at thin margins. For a health system with $30 million in unrestricted net assets, maintaining a $2.5 million actuarial reserve for ACO REACH participation represents a 8.3% allocation of available capital to a risk management buffer — a rational investment if leadership has high confidence in the organization’s ability to manage population cost, but a significant commitment that requires board-level approval and careful opportunity cost analysis against other capital needs.
Bundled Payments: When a Single Price Covers an Entire Episode of Care
Bundled payment models take a different economic approach to aligning incentives than shared savings. Rather than holding a provider accountable for the total cost of care across an entire attributed population, bundled payments establish a single target price for all the care associated with a specific clinical episode — typically defined as a procedure or hospitalization plus 90 days of post-acute care. If the sum of all actual payments during the episode falls below the target price, the lead provider (typically the hospital or physician group that performed the primary procedure) retains the difference. If actual costs exceed the target, the lead provider repays the excess. The economic logic is straightforward: bundled payments create a direct financial incentive for the hospital performing a hip replacement to care about which skilled nursing facility the patient is discharged to, how long they stay there, and whether they end up readmitted — because all of those costs are included in the bundle.
BPCI Advanced, CMS’s voluntary bundled payment model, ran from October 1, 2018 through December 31, 2025, with 29 inpatient and 3 outpatient clinical episode categories at launch, ranging from major joint replacement and cardiac procedures to pneumonia and chronic obstructive pulmonary disease hospitalizations. Its final evaluation (The Lewin Group for CMS, August 2026) found that participants reduced Medicare episode payments in every model year — by $743 per episode on average in Model Years 1 and 2 and by about $1,000 per episode in Model Years 3 through 6 — and that the model produced about $800 million in net Medicare savings across its eight model years: net losses in Model Years 1–3, when reconciliation payments to participants exceeded the spending reductions, were outweighed by net savings once CMS redesigned target pricing in Model Year 4 (2021). Readmission rates were similar to the comparison group, although patients in Model Years 7–8 reported less favorable changes in functional status. Critically, the savings came primarily from post-acute care — fewer patients discharged to skilled nursing or inpatient rehabilitation facilities, skilled nursing stays about three days shorter, and skilled nursing payments about $500 lower per episode — rather than from reductions in the procedural care itself, confirming the bundled payment hypothesis that the primary driver of unnecessary cost in high-volume episodes is the post-acute care tail, not the procedure. CMS concluded that BPCI Advanced did not meet its criteria for expansion, and carried its lessons into the mandatory Transforming Episode Accountability Model (TEAM), which began January 1, 2026.
The bundled payment evidence for joint replacement is about as clean as healthcare economics gets: when you make the hospital financially responsible for what happens after the patient leaves, patients spend less time in nursing homes, without more complications or readmissions, and cost Medicare less money. The same logic applies to any high-volume procedure with a predictable post-acute care pattern. The challenge is applying it to medical conditions where the post-acute trajectory is far less predictable.
Capitation: The Most Advanced — and Most Misunderstood — VBC Model
Capitation is the payment model that most completely aligns provider incentives with patient health outcomes, and the one that generates the most anxiety among providers who have not operated under it before. Under capitation, a provider or provider organization receives a fixed payment per enrolled member per month — the “PMPM” rate — and is responsible for delivering or arranging all covered healthcare services for that member, regardless of how much care they actually use. If the member uses more care than the PMPM covers, the provider absorbs the loss. If the member uses less, the provider retains the surplus. This structure creates the strongest possible financial incentive for prevention, care coordination, and early intervention — because every avoided hospitalization translates directly into retained revenue rather than lost fee-for-service income.
The anxiety around capitation is understandable but often misdirected. The concern most commonly expressed is that capitation incentivizes providers to withhold necessary care — to under-treat rather than over-treat. This concern is real and has historical basis: the managed care capitation models of the 1990s were frequently criticized for exactly this failure, and some HMO capitation arrangements did produce documented under-treatment in specific service categories. But the lesson of the 1990s HMO experience is not that capitation is inherently dangerous. It is that capitation without robust quality measurement, meaningful patient choice, and strong external accountability produces dangerous incentives. Modern capitation models — including Medicare Advantage plans and CMS’s ACO REACH program — include comprehensive quality metric frameworks with financial consequences for poor performance, minimum coverage requirements that prevent service restriction below evidence-based thresholds, and beneficiary rights to seek care outside the network when needed. The capitation model that exists in 2026 is substantively different from the managed care capitation of 1996.
How PMPM Rates Are Set: The Actuarial Core of Capitation
The financial success or failure of a capitation arrangement is largely determined before any patient is seen, at the point where the PMPM rate is negotiated or set. If the rate is set too low relative to the actual cost of providing care to the enrolled population, no amount of care management efficiency will produce a viable financial model. If the rate is set appropriately — reflecting the health acuity, demographic profile, and social complexity of the enrolled population — a well-managed capitated provider can generate a positive margin while delivering better-coordinated, lower-cost care than the fee-for-service alternative. The actuarial process of setting a PMPM rate involves risk adjustment models that account for enrollee diagnoses, functional status, and social determinants of health, applied to utilization cost data from comparable populations. For Medicare Advantage plans, CMS sets the base rate using the county-level fee-for-service benchmark and adjusts it upward or downward based on each enrollee’s Hierarchical Condition Category (HCC) risk score, a diagnosis-based model that attempts to predict expected healthcare utilization for that individual in the coming year.
The Volume-to-Value Transition: Why the Math Gets Hard Before It Gets Easy
The single most common financial mistake health systems make in VBC transitions is underestimating the revenue disruption of the transition period — the window, typically 18 to 36 months, during which care management investments have begun reducing utilization but VBC contract revenue has not yet scaled to replace the lost fee-for-service volume. Consider a regional health system generating $400 million in annual net patient revenue, with 30% of that revenue from inpatient admissions billed on a fee-for-service basis. The system joins MSSP and invests $2 million in care management infrastructure — care coordinators, a risk stratification platform, a post-discharge follow-up program — that begins generating measurable results in year one, reducing its attributed Medicare population’s hospitalization rate by 12%. That 12% reduction represents approximately 240 avoided admissions. At an average net revenue of $8,500 per admission, it also represents a $2.04 million reduction in fee-for-service inpatient revenue. The system’s MSSP shared savings payment for year one, based on total population savings, might be $1.4 million. Net financial impact of year one VBC investment: negative $640,000.
This math is not an argument against VBC. It is an argument for understanding VBC as a multi-year financial investment with a specific payback horizon, rather than a year-one revenue enhancement strategy. CMS’s own results show what is achievable at scale: in Performance Year 2024, Shared Savings Program ACOs generated gross savings of $651 per assigned beneficiary (shared between ACOs and Medicare) and net Medicare savings of $245, and 75% of the 476 participating ACOs earned performance payments. CMS publishes no standard figure for what ACOs spend on care management infrastructure, so the return on that investment has to be modeled from each organization’s own cost base. The problem is that most health system financial planning cycles operate on one-to-three year horizons, which are too short to capture the full economic benefit of VBC investment. Organizations that have successfully navigated the volume-to-value transition have done so by securing board approval for a five-to-seven year VBC investment thesis with explicit transition period loss projections, rather than attempting to justify each year’s VBC expenditure against that same year’s VBC revenue.
For CFOs and Finance Leaders: If your organization is evaluating ACO or Advanced APM participation, your financial model must explicitly project the transition period revenue displacement — the reduction in fee-for-service inpatient and ED revenue that will occur as care management succeeds. An ACO financial model that shows positive net revenue in year one almost certainly has a methodology error, either in the care management cost estimates or in the assumed savings rate. Realistic five-year models typically show a cumulative loss in years one and two, breakeven in year three, and meaningful positive return in years four and five. Present this model honestly to your board, or the VBC investment will be defunded the first time a quarterly loss appears.
Primary Care Investment as Economic Infrastructure
One of the most powerful and counterintuitive findings in health economics is that investment in primary care produces returns that are comparable to, and in many cases superior to, investment in specialty care infrastructure — not in terms of individual clinical case intensity, but in terms of total population health spending and outcomes. The economic mechanism is straightforward once articulated: primary care reduces the incidence of the expensive downstream events — hospitalizations, emergency visits, specialist procedures, complications of uncontrolled chronic disease — that account for the vast majority of total healthcare spending. The evidence is strongest on outcomes and quality: Baicker and Chandra (Health Affairs, 2004) found that states with more general practitioners per capita delivered higher-quality care to Medicare beneficiaries at lower spending, while states with more specialists spent more for lower quality; and Basu and colleagues (JAMA Internal Medicine, 2019) found that every 10 additional primary care physicians per 100,000 people was associated with a 51.5-day increase in life expectancy. Supply alone is not a cost-control lever, however — a 2009 Health Affairs analysis found that simply having more primary care physicians did not slow spending growth — which is why payment models that reward primary care for total cost matter.
The economic return on primary care investment is particularly relevant for value-based care organizations making resource allocation decisions. Under fee-for-service, a hospital has limited financial incentive to invest in expanding primary care capacity in its community — more primary care visits may mean fewer specialist referrals and fewer hospitalizations, which means less hospital revenue. Under a capitated or shared savings VBC model, the same investment in primary care capacity is a direct cost reduction strategy: every diabetic patient who sees their primary care provider quarterly and maintains target HbA1c levels is a patient less likely to generate a costly admission for a diabetic complication. The VBC payment model converts primary care from a cost center competing with hospital services for capital allocation into a return-generating infrastructure investment — which is the single most important realignment that value-based payment architecture produces.
