Care Management Interventions: What the Published Evaluations Found
The evidence in the table above carries an important lesson: the interventions that paid for themselves in trials were targeted ones — transition coaching for patients leaving the hospital, community health workers for patients with documented unmet social needs — while broad primary care payment models that spread enhanced payments across whole practice panels reduced hospital use without producing net Medicare savings. An organization implementing a care coordinator program without a functioning risk stratification system to identify which patients the coordinator should prioritize will see substantially lower returns — because care management applied uniformly across a population is far less efficient than care management targeted to the patients most likely to have high-cost events in the next 12 months. The technology readiness discussion in Module 4 is therefore directly connected to the economics of VBC investment: organizations that build population health data infrastructure before scaling care management programs generate significantly better returns than those that sequence these investments in reverse order.
Care coordination works when it is aimed at the right patients: its effectiveness depends on a functioning risk stratification system that identifies which patients are genuinely high-risk, not just high-cost in the prior year.
Post-discharge transition support has some of the strongest trial evidence in care management: in Coleman’s randomized trial of 750 older adults, transition coaching cut 30-day rehospitalizations from 11.9% to 8.3% and lowered six-month hospital costs, because the cost of coaching is small relative to the cost of a readmission.
Community health worker programs are the intervention most directly connected to social determinants of health — addressing food insecurity, housing instability, and transportation barriers that drive utilization patterns that clinical care alone cannot change — and Penn’s IMPaCT program returned $2.47 to Medicaid for every dollar invested, within a single fiscal year.
Advanced primary care transformation is the most comprehensive intervention, but the federal evidence is sobering: neither CPC+ nor Primary Care First produced net Medicare savings once their enhanced payments were counted, and CPC+ practices with incentives to control total cost of care, such as Shared Savings Program participants, did better at lowering costs — which is why primary care investment pays off most reliably inside a total-cost-of-care arrangement rather than as a stand-alone add-on.
The organizations that are winning in value-based care are not winning because they have better doctors or better patients. They are winning because they have better data, better targeting, and better financial discipline about which interventions they invest in and for which patient populations. VBC is fundamentally an information business with a clinical delivery layer on top.
Module 3 Summary: The Economics of VBC Reward Patience, Infrastructure, and Precision
The economics of value-based care are not complicated in their logic, but they are frequently misunderstood in their timing and their requirements. Shared savings arrangements reward population cost management, but only after a benchmark has been established and a minimum savings threshold has been crossed. Bundled payments reward episode cost efficiency, but require investment in post-acute care coordination infrastructure that most hospitals have historically not needed. Capitation rewards comprehensive population health management, but requires actuarial sophistication, quality measurement capability, and working capital reserves that are significant organizational commitments. All three model types have in common a multi-year investment horizon that is at odds with annual budget cycles, and a data infrastructure dependency that is at odds with the technology readiness of many rural and safety-net providers.
The economic case for making these investments nonetheless is visible in CMS’s own results: in Performance Year 2024, Shared Savings Program ACOs generated gross savings of $651 per assigned beneficiary and net Medicare savings of $245, with physician-led, low-revenue ACOs outperforming hospital-led ones ($319 versus $180 net per beneficiary) — returns that depend on care management infrastructure maturing and on the organization’s ability to identify and target high-risk patients. The organizations that have reached this performance level did not arrive there by treating VBC as a compliance exercise or a contract opportunity. They treated it as a fundamental redesign of how they organize and deliver care, supported by a financial model that explicitly anticipated and planned for the transition period losses that are the unavoidable cost of that redesign. Module 4 examines the technology infrastructure that makes this financial performance possible.
Review your organization’s current VBC contract portfolio and categorize each contract by model type: shared savings (upside only or two-sided), bundled payment, or capitation. For each contract, identify whether you have a financial model projecting per-beneficiary investment cost, expected savings rate, and net financial benefit over a five-year horizon.
Ask your CFO to quantify the annual fee-for-service revenue at risk from your care management programs — the inpatient admissions and emergency visits that your VBC interventions are intended to prevent, translated into net revenue impact. Compare this to your projected shared savings or capitation savings. If the net is negative, verify your model is using a five-year, not a one-year, planning horizon.
Review the evidence in the care management intervention table in this module and identify which intervention types your organization is currently investing in. For each intervention, assess whether you have a functioning risk stratification system that targets the intervention to high-risk patients rather than applying it uniformly across your attributed population.
Assess your organization’s actuarial reserve capacity for two-sided risk participation. If your unrestricted net assets are below $15 million, upside-only MSSP participation is the appropriate starting point. If your unrestricted net assets exceed $30 million and you have three or more years of MSSP experience, a formal analysis of ENHANCED track participation, or of CMMI’s LEAD Model (the ACO REACH successor beginning January 1, 2027), is warranted.
Module 3 Key Takeaway: The economics of value-based care favor organizations that plan in five-year cycles, invest in care management infrastructure before scaling VBC contract volume, and understand the specific financial mechanics of their contract type at the level of the benchmark calculation and the savings distribution formula. VBC is not a revenue strategy for the next fiscal year. It is a business model for the next decade — one that rewards the organizations that build the infrastructure, accept the transition period losses, and stay the course long enough for the compounding returns of mature care management to materialize.

Module 3 of the HTR Academy Value-Based Care Fundamentals course examines the financial mechanics of value-based care from first principles. It explains, in plain language, how money actually flows in the major VBC contract structures — shared savings, bundled payments, and capitation — how providers calculate whether a VBC arrangement is financially rational for their organization, what the volume-to-value transition problem means in practice for health system balance sheets, and what the economic evidence says about which VBC models generate durable savings versus which ones simply shift cost without reducing it. Learners will leave this module able to evaluate a VBC contract term sheet, identify the key actuarial concepts their finance teams are using, and understand the economic case for primary care investment as the highest-return infrastructure spend in healthcare.
Value-Based Care Fundamentals — Module 3: The Economics Pillar — Following the Money in Value-Based Care

######## vbc-fundamentals-module-4-technology-pillar | Value-Based Care Fundamentals — Module 4: The Technology Pillar — Data, AI, and the Digital Infrastructure That Makes VBC Work
Imagine trying to run a nationwide airline reservation system where every airport kept its flight records on paper, no two airports used the same form, and sharing records between airports required a fax machine and a three-day waiting period. Connecting passengers to flights would be technically possible but operationally chaotic — delays, lost records, missed connections, and dangerous errors would be routine, not exceptional. This is, only slightly exaggerated, the state of health information exchange in the United States as recently as 2019, and in many rural markets as recently as 2024. Value-based care requires something that American healthcare has never had at scale: a reliable, real-time, standards-based system for sharing patient health information across organizational boundaries. Without it, an Accountable Care Organization cannot know that its attributed Medicare patient was admitted to a hospital outside the network last Tuesday. A care coordinator cannot know that a high-risk patient missed their primary care appointment. A risk stratification algorithm cannot identify a patient approaching a high-cost event without complete longitudinal data about their diagnosis history, medication adherence, and recent utilization. Technology is not a nice-to-have for advanced VBC. It is the operating system on which the entire model runs.
The Three Technology Layers VBC Requires
The technology infrastructure that supports effective value-based care can be understood as three interdependent layers, each building on the one below it. The foundation layer is clinical data capture: the electronic health record system that documents every patient encounter, diagnosis, medication, order, and result in a structured, machine-readable format. The second layer is data integration and exchange: the health information exchange infrastructure that connects EHR systems across organizations so that complete patient data is available regardless of where care was delivered. The third layer is analytics and intelligence: the population health management platforms, risk stratification tools, and increasingly AI-powered predictive models that transform raw clinical data into actionable care management intelligence — identifying which patients are at highest risk of imminent high-cost events, which chronic disease patients are overdue for interventions, and which populations are generating the most preventable utilization. Each layer is necessary but not sufficient. Strong EHR implementation without HIE connectivity produces siloed data. HIE connectivity without analytics produces data that no one has the capacity to act on. Analytics without high-quality underlying data produces predictions that are wrong in ways that are hard to detect until they cause harm.
Electronic Health Records: The Foundation Layer
The United States made a massive federal investment in EHR adoption through the HITECH Act of 2009, which appropriated $27 billion in Medicare and Medicaid incentive payments to encourage providers to implement certified EHR systems and demonstrate “meaningful use” — a defined set of capabilities including electronic prescribing, clinical decision support, and patient portal access. Certified EHR adoption among non-federal acute-care hospitals rose from roughly 10% in 2008 to about 96% by 2021 (Health Affairs; ONC), one of the most rapid technology adoption curves in any industry, and it created the clinical data foundation that value-based care analytics requires. However, adoption rates mask significant quality variation that is directly relevant to VBC capability. Having a certified EHR does not mean having a fully configured, fully utilized EHR that generates high-quality structured data. Many providers, particularly smaller rural practices and critical access hospitals, implemented EHR systems at the minimum level required to qualify for HITECH incentive payments, resulting in installations that function primarily as billing and documentation tools rather than population health data platforms.
The VBC-relevant EHR capabilities that matter most are not the basic documentation features that HITECH required, but the advanced data quality and interoperability features that turn an EHR from a digital chart room into a population health intelligence system. These include structured problem lists and medication lists that use standardized coding (ICD-10 for diagnoses, RxNorm for medications, SNOMED CT for clinical concepts) rather than free-text notes; care gap reporting that identifies patients overdue for preventive services or chronic disease monitoring; patient panel management tools that allow care coordinators to view their entire assigned patient population sorted by risk level; and FHIR API connectivity that allows the EHR to exchange data with external systems using the standardized data format that the 21st Century Cures Act mandated in 2020. Federal data show the gap is real: in 2023, 70% of non-federal acute care hospitals engaged in all four domains of interoperable exchange (sending, receiving, finding, and integrating patient data), but only 43% did so routinely, and small, rural, critical access, and independent hospitals engaged less often than better-resourced hospitals (ONC Data Brief No. 71, 2024).
The 21st Century Cures Act: The Law That Changed EHR Interoperability Requirements
The 21st Century Cures Act, enacted in December 2016 with its implementing regulations finalized in 2020, made two changes to the EHR landscape that are directly relevant to VBC technology readiness. First, it prohibited “information blocking” — defined as any practice that unreasonably restricts the access, exchange, or use of electronic health information — and established civil monetary penalties of up to $1 million per violation for health IT developers, networks, and exchanges that engage in information blocking. This provision was specifically designed to address a well-documented pattern in which EHR vendors and some health systems restricted data sharing with competitors or external partners, creating artificial data silos that served commercial interests at the expense of care coordination. Second, it mandated that all certified EHR systems implement standardized FHIR (Fast Healthcare Interoperability Resources) application programming interfaces by 2022, enabling third-party developers to build population health applications that connect directly to EHR data without requiring custom integrations. The FHIR mandate is the technical foundation of the modern population health analytics market, as it allows risk stratification platforms, care gap tools, and quality reporting systems to access EHR data through a single standardized connection rather than a patchwork of proprietary integrations.
The 21st Century Cures Act’s information blocking prohibition changed the competitive dynamics of health IT in a way that CMS’s interoperability rules alone could not have. When health IT developers and exchanges face penalties of up to $1 million per violation for restricting data access, and providers face their own federal disincentives, the legal calculus of data hoarding changes overnight. The challenge now is not permission — it is infrastructure. Building the pipes to carry the data that is now legally required to flow is the work of this decade.
Health Information Exchange: The Data Sharing Layer
Health information exchange (HIE) refers to the electronic sharing of patient health information across organizational boundaries — between hospitals, physician practices, laboratories, pharmacies, and post-acute care providers that may use different EHR systems and operate in different ownership structures. For value-based care, HIE is not optional infrastructure. An ACO that cannot receive notifications when its attributed patients are admitted to or discharged from any hospital in its market cannot manage post-discharge follow-up effectively. A care coordinator who cannot see a patient’s complete medication list across all prescribers cannot identify dangerous drug interactions or medication non-adherence. A risk stratification algorithm that only has access to the patient’s encounters at one health system is working from an incomplete and potentially misleading clinical picture. The importance of HIE to VBC has been recognized in federal policy through TEFCA — the Trusted Exchange Framework and Common Agreement — which ONC published in January 2022 as the national framework for interoperable health information exchange.
TEFCA establishes a set of technical and legal standards that health information networks must meet to become Qualified Health Information Networks (QHINs), and creates a framework under which QHINs are required to share data with each other upon request, effectively creating a nationwide health information exchange network for the first time. By early 2026, eleven networks had been designated as QHINs — eHealth Exchange, Epic Nexus, Health Gorilla, KONZA National Network, and MedAllies (December 2023); CommonWell Health Alliance and Kno2 (February 2024); Netsmart; eClinicalWorks; Surescripts; and Oracle Health Information Network (November 2025). The coverage of these networks is extensive in urban and suburban markets but remains uneven in rural areas, where the combination of fewer participating providers, older EHR systems, and limited broadband infrastructure creates connectivity gaps that directly limit rural ACO and VBC participants’ ability to receive real-time patient data from non-network sources. The FCC’s Rural Health Care Program (including its Healthcare Connect Fund) and USDA’s ReConnect Program are the primary federal mechanisms for addressing rural broadband gaps, and rural providers’ requests to the FCC program routinely exceed its annual funding cap.
The capability gap between lower-resourced rural hospitals and better-resourced systems represents a structural VBC readiness disparity that policy interventions alone cannot close without significant capital investment. A rural Critical Access Hospital that lacks FHIR R4 API implementation, state HIE connectivity, and a population health platform is not technically capable of the patient data workflows that MSSP ACO participation requires — not because of organizational will or leadership quality, but because the digital infrastructure that enables those workflows has not been built. The federal programs designed to address this gap — including the FCC’s Rural Health Care Program, capped at about $724 million for funding year 2025, ONC’s Health IT Certification Program, and HRSA’s Rural Health Information Technology funding stream — are necessary but insufficient at their current funding levels to close the rural-urban technology gap within a policy-relevant timeframe.
AI and Risk Stratification: The Intelligence Layer
Risk stratification — the process of identifying which patients within a large population are at highest risk of future high-cost health events — is the intelligence function that turns population health data into actionable care management priorities. In a VBC model managing 10,000 attributed patients, a care management team cannot proactively reach out to every patient every month. They need a system that identifies the 300 or 400 patients most likely to have a preventable hospital admission, emergency department visit, or serious clinical deterioration in the next 90 days, so that care coordination resources can be concentrated where they will have the greatest impact. Traditional risk stratification relied on simple actuarial models — patients with the highest total spending in the prior year were assumed to be the highest risk in the coming year. These models are easy to implement but systematically misdirected: they identify patients who were recently sick rather than patients who are about to become sick, missing the early intervention window where care management is most effective and most cost-efficient.
Modern AI-powered risk stratification uses machine learning models trained on large clinical datasets to predict future utilization risk based on a much richer set of signals than historical spending alone. These models incorporate diagnosis trajectories (the sequence and pattern of diagnoses over time, not just their presence or absence), medication adherence signals derived from pharmacy data and EHR medication reconciliation records, laboratory value trends (a gradually rising HbA1c over three visits is a stronger predictor of near-term hospitalization than a single elevated value), social determinants of health flags derived from screening questionnaires and zip-code-level data, and behavioral health diagnoses and substance use disorder flags that are strongly predictive of high-cost medical utilization. Predictive accuracy is usually reported as the C-statistic — the probability that the model ranks a patient who will be hospitalized above one who will not — and models that add clinical EHR data to claims generally discriminate better than claims-only models, though published gains vary widely by population and outcome and should be validated locally before they drive care coordinator workloads.
The AI Bias Problem: When Risk Stratification Perpetuates Inequity
AI risk stratification tools carry a well-documented and serious equity risk: because they are trained on historical healthcare utilization data, they tend to encode the biases present in that data. A landmark 2019 study published in Science by Obermeyer et al. found that a widely used commercial risk stratification algorithm systematically underestimated the health needs of Black patients because it used healthcare costs as a proxy for health need — and because Black patients historically had lower healthcare costs not because they were healthier, but because structural barriers limited their access to care. The algorithm learned that low-cost patients were low-need patients, a correlation that held for white patients but produced systematic underestimation of Black patients’ clinical risk. The practical consequence was that Black patients were significantly less likely to be identified for high-intensity care management programs, perpetuating the very care access disparities that VBC is intended to address. This finding has been replicated for other demographic groups and other algorithm types, establishing AI bias as a genuine and important equity challenge in VBC technology deployment.
Algorithmic bias in risk stratification is a documented, real-world problem. Obermeyer and colleagues (Science, 2019) showed that a widely used commercial risk-prediction algorithm systematically underestimated the health needs of Black patients, because it was trained on historical cost data shaped by unequal access — the model learned to encode access barriers as lower predicted need. The lesson for VBC: a risk model trained on fee-for-service utilization in a world with documented access barriers will reproduce those barriers unless it is explicitly audited and recalibrated for equity.
Technology Governance Requirement: Any healthcare organization deploying an AI risk stratification tool in a VBC context has an ethical and, increasingly, a legal obligation to audit the tool for demographic performance disparities before deployment and on a regular basis thereafter. The audit should examine whether the model’s sensitivity and specificity — its ability to correctly identify high-risk and low-risk patients — varies significantly across racial, ethnic, and socioeconomic subgroups. If it does, the organization must either select a different model, recalibrate the existing model for its specific population, or supplement algorithmic risk scoring with clinical and social worker judgment for populations where the algorithm performs poorly.
Telehealth as a VBC Enabler: The COVID Legacy
The COVID-19 pandemic produced one technology outcome that has proven durably beneficial for value-based care: the rapid normalization of telehealth as a routine care delivery modality. Between March and June 2020, CMS issued emergency waivers that allowed Medicare to reimburse telehealth visits at parity with in-person visits, removed geographic restrictions that had historically limited telehealth to rural areas, and authorized audio-only visits for patients without video capability. Telehealth surged from about 0.2% of outpatient evaluation-and-management visits in February 2020 to a peak of roughly 50.7% in April 2020 — on the order of 78 times the pre-pandemic level (CDC MMWR; HHS ASPE) — demonstrating both latent demand and the clinical feasibility of delivering much of primary care and chronic disease management by video or phone. For value-based care organizations, telehealth is a care management efficiency multiplier: it lets care coordinators and physicians conduct follow-ups, medication reviews, and chronic disease check-ins at a fraction of the cost and scheduling friction of in-person visits, enabling more frequent contact with high-risk patients than a purely in-person model allows.
The scale of Medicare adoption is documented: the HHS Office of Inspector General found that more than 28 million Medicare beneficiaries — over 2 in 5 — used telehealth in the first year of the pandemic, using 88 times more telehealth services than the year before, with office visits making up just under half of that use. The same review found urban beneficiaries more likely than rural ones to use telehealth — a reminder that the rural populations with the most to gain also face the broadband and device barriers that limit uptake.
The policy question that followed the pandemic’s emergency waivers was whether CMS would make the telehealth flexibilities permanent. Congress answered with a series of short-term extensions rather than a permanent fix: the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2023 carried the flexibilities through 2024, later stopgap bills carried them into 2025, they briefly lapsed during the funding gaps of late 2025 and January 31, 2026, and the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 (H.R. 7148, enacted February 2026) extended them — including home as an originating site, audio-only visits, and FQHC/RHC distant-site services — through December 31, 2027. For rural VBC providers, the permanence of these telehealth payment rules is not a convenience issue — it is a care model viability issue. An ACO serving a rural population where 40% of attributed patients live more than 30 miles from the nearest primary care office cannot achieve the visit frequency that effective chronic disease management requires without telehealth as a structural component of its care model.
Building Technology Readiness: The Sequenced Investment Roadmap
For healthcare leaders assessing their organization’s VBC technology readiness, the most common mistake is attempting to deploy advanced analytics capabilities before the foundational data quality and connectivity infrastructure is in place. A population health platform fed by an EHR with poorly configured structured data will generate inaccurate risk scores. A care gap reporting system connected to an EHR that does not have standardized problem list coding will miss a significant proportion of the gaps it is supposed to identify. Technology investment in VBC should follow a deliberate sequence, with each layer validated before the next is added.
Phase 1 — Data Foundation (Months 1–6): Audit your EHR’s structured data quality. Assess the completeness and accuracy of your problem list, medication list, and care gap documentation. Implement standardized coding for diagnoses (ICD-10), medications (RxNorm), and procedures (CPT). This phase produces no new technology capability but is the single most important determinant of all subsequent analytics quality.
Phase 2 — Connectivity (Months 4–12): Activate your state HIE connection and configure admission-discharge-transfer (ADT) alert feeds so that care coordinators receive real-time notifications when attributed patients are admitted to or discharged from any participating hospital in your market. This is the highest-ROI single connectivity investment for ACO care management.
Phase 3 — Panel Management (Months 9–18): Implement a population health management platform that allows care coordinators to view and filter their attributed patient panel by risk level, care gap status, and recent utilization events. At this stage, risk stratification can be claims-based or simple rules-based — AI is not required for effective panel management at this phase.
Phase 4 — Predictive Intelligence (Months 18–36): Implement AI-powered risk stratification once your EHR data quality and HIE connectivity are sufficient to feed the model with the multi-modal data it requires for accurate predictions. Conduct a demographic equity audit of the model before deployment. Begin using model outputs to prioritize care coordinator outreach and track whether high-model-score patients who receive outreach have lower subsequent hospitalization rates than comparable patients who do not.
Phase 5 — Continuous Improvement (Ongoing): Establish a quarterly data quality review cycle that monitors EHR documentation completeness, risk model accuracy, care gap closure rates, and population health outcome trends. Use this data to identify where the care management workflow is breaking down — whether the issue is risk score accuracy, care coordinator capacity, patient engagement, or post-acute care coordination — and address the root cause rather than adjusting the technology.
Module 4 Summary: Technology Is the Operating System of Value-Based Care
The technology infrastructure required for effective value-based care is not exotic or cutting-edge by the standards of other industries. What VBC requires is reliable clinical data capture, standards-based data exchange, and analytics capable of turning population data into actionable care management intelligence. These are not science-fiction capabilities. They exist today in mature form at health systems like Kaiser Permanente, Intermountain Healthcare, and Geisinger. The challenge is not invention — it is diffusion. The rural Critical Access Hospitals that lack the foundational technology capabilities for advanced VBC participation are not being held back by the absence of technology. They are being held back by the absence of capital, broadband infrastructure, and technical implementation support that would allow them to deploy technology that already exists.
The federal investment framework for closing this gap — TEFCA for interoperability standards, the 21st Century Cures Act’s information blocking prohibition and FHIR mandate, the FCC’s Healthcare Connect Fund for rural broadband, ONC’s certification program for EHR capabilities, and HRSA’s rural health IT funding stream — represents the most coherent federal technology infrastructure investment in healthcare history. Whether it is sufficient to close the rural-urban VBC readiness gap within a policy-relevant timeframe is an open question that depends on appropriations decisions, state implementation capacity, and vendor market dynamics that are outside any single organization’s control. What is within your organization’s control is the sequenced technology investment roadmap described in this module, and the commitment to building VBC data infrastructure as a strategic priority rather than an IT project.
VBC technology infrastructure has three interdependent layers: clinical data capture (EHR), data integration and exchange (HIE and FHIR), and analytics and intelligence (population health platforms and AI risk stratification). Each layer depends on the quality of the layer below it, making data foundation investment the highest-priority first step.
The 21st Century Cures Act’s information blocking prohibition and FHIR API mandate are the legal and technical foundations of modern health IT interoperability. Health IT developers, health information exchanges, and networks that engage in information blocking face civil monetary penalties of up to $1 million per violation; health care providers face separate federal disincentives, such as losing Promoting Interoperability credit under MIPS, under a 2024 HHS rule.
AI risk stratification tools improve high-risk patient identification accuracy significantly compared to claims-based models, but carry documented equity risks from training data bias that require formal demographic audit before deployment and on an ongoing basis.
Telehealth is a structural care model component for rural VBC providers serving populations where in-person visit frequency sufficient for effective chronic disease management is geographically and logistically impossible. The permanence of post-pandemic telehealth reimbursement parity is a VBC infrastructure issue, not simply a convenience policy question.
Module 4 Key Takeaway: Technology readiness is the most concrete and actionable dimension of VBC preparation because it can be assessed objectively, sequenced rationally, and funded through a combination of organizational capital and federal programs. Before your organization takes on additional VBC financial risk, conduct a structured technology readiness assessment using the capability gap framework from this module and identify which phase of the five-phase roadmap you are currently in. Attempting to manage population cost without the data infrastructure to know who your highest-risk patients are and what is happening to them across the care continuum is the most common cause of VBC financial underperformance — and the most preventable.

Module 4 of the HTR Academy Value-Based Care Fundamentals course examines the technology infrastructure that value-based care requires to function — and what happens when that infrastructure is missing. This module explains, in plain language, what interoperable electronic health records, health information exchange, and AI-powered risk stratification tools actually do in a VBC context, why many rural Critical Access Hospitals remain technology-unready for advanced VBC participation, and how the federal interoperability agenda — including the 21st Century Cures Act, the CMS Interoperability Rule, and the TEFCA framework — is building the data infrastructure that VBC depends upon. Value-Based Care Fundamentals — Module 4: The Technology Pillar — Data, AI, and the Digital Infrastructure That Makes VBC Work

######## vbc-fundamentals-module-5-clinical-equity-pillars | Value-Based Care Fundamentals — Module 5: The Clinical and Equity Pillars — Does Value-Based Care Actually Work, and Does It Work for Everyone?
Everything in this course has been building toward the two questions that ultimately determine whether value-based care is worth pursuing: Does it actually improve clinical outcomes compared to fee-for-service? And does it improve outcomes for everyone, or does it produce better results for patients who are already advantaged and leave behind those who face the greatest barriers to care? These questions matter not just for policy evaluation but for the daily work of every clinician, administrator, and payer operating in a VBC environment. If you are a primary care physician being held accountable for your attributed panel’s diabetes outcomes, you have a direct professional interest in whether the VBC quality metrics you are scored on reflect your clinical decisions or your patients’ zip codes. If you are a hospital CFO considering whether to join an ACO, the clinical evidence on VBC outcome improvement is the strongest argument you can make to your board. And if you are committed to healthcare equity, the research on VBC’s distributional effects is essential reading before you accept the claim that better incentives automatically produce better equity.
The Clinical Evidence: What the Research Actually Shows
The clinical evidence base for value-based care’s effectiveness has grown substantially since the Medicare Shared Savings Program launched in 2012, and the overall picture is cautiously positive, with important qualifications. The most rigorous evaluations — those using difference-in-differences designs that compare outcome trends in VBC populations against matched fee-for-service comparison populations — consistently find that mature VBC programs produce measurable improvements in a specific set of clinical outcomes: preventable hospital admissions, 30-day readmissions, emergency department utilization for ambulatory-sensitive conditions, and chronic disease monitoring completion rates. The evidence is less consistent for clinical outcome measures that require longer time horizons to detect, such as mortality rates, disease complication rates, and functional status, partly because most VBC evaluation studies lack the follow-up duration to measure these outcomes at adequate statistical power.
The most cited peer-reviewed evaluation of MSSP clinical and spending performance, by McWilliams and colleagues in the New England Journal of Medicine (2016), compared 220 ACOs with local non-ACO providers using Medicare claims. ACOs that entered in 2012 reduced spending by about $144 per beneficiary — roughly 1.4% — by 2013, while the 2013 entry cohort showed no significant savings in its first year, an early sign that savings take time to develop. Savings were consistently larger in independent primary care groups than in hospital-integrated ones. Importantly, the savings were not accompanied by evidence of care withholding: ACO contracts were associated with improved performance on some quality measures — including fewer hospitalizations for COPD and asthma — and unchanged performance on the rest, suggesting that the spending reductions reflected better care management, not reduced access.
Where VBC Clinical Evidence Is Strongest — and Where It Is Weakest
The clinical evidence for VBC is not uniformly positive across all model types, patient populations, or clinical conditions. Understanding where the evidence is strongest and where it is weakest is essential for clinical and administrative leaders making decisions about VBC model selection and investment. Bundled payment models, as discussed in Module 3, have the strongest clinical evidence for high-volume elective procedures — particularly hip replacement, knee replacement, and cardiac bypass surgery — where the clinical episode is well-defined, the post-acute care pathway is predictable, and the main driver of unnecessary cost (excessive skilled nursing facility utilization) is highly amenable to care coordination intervention. BPCI Advanced's final evaluation (2026) found readmission rates similar to the comparison group rather than improved, and patients in the model's last two years reported less favorable changes in functional status, alongside the cost reductions documented in Module 3.
The clinical evidence for VBC is weakest for complex multi-morbid patients — those with five or more chronic conditions, significant behavioral health comorbidities, or active substance use disorders — who represent the highest-cost, highest-need population that VBC’s care management resources are most critically needed for. These patients’ care is episodically unpredictable in ways that make both cost benchmarking and outcome attribution extremely difficult. A patient with diabetes, heart failure, chronic kidney disease, severe depression, and housing instability may generate five hospitalizations in one year and zero in the next, not because of anything a care coordinator did or did not do, but because of a housing crisis, a medication supply disruption, or a mental health episode that no clinical intervention could have reliably prevented. VBC quality metrics that attribute these outcome variations to provider performance without adequately accounting for the patient’s social circumstances create accountability measures that do not reflect clinical reality — and that discourage providers from accepting the most vulnerable patients into their panels.

Clinical Evidence Strength by VBC Model Type and Patient Population, HTR Evidence Synthesis
The Equity Question: Does VBC Work for Everyone?
Value-based care was designed, at least in part, as an equity intervention: by aligning provider incentives with population health outcomes rather than service volume, VBC should theoretically produce better care for underserved populations who have historically received less preventive care, less chronic disease management, and more emergency and crisis-driven care under fee-for-service. The reality is more complicated. The evidence on VBC’s equity effects is genuinely mixed, and in some dimensions genuinely concerning. Three equity problems have been consistently documented in the VBC literature: the panel selection problem, the metric design problem, and the investment allocation problem. Understanding all three is essential for any leader who wants to build VBC programs that advance rather than undermine health equity.
The Panel Selection Problem: Who Gets Attributed
Under most ACO attribution methodologies, patients are attributed to the primary care physician who delivered the plurality of their primary care visits in the prior year. This attribution approach creates a structural selection problem for equity: providers who serve the most medically and socially complex patients — the patients in greatest need of VBC care management resources — are also the providers whose attributed panels will systematically generate worse performance on standard quality metrics, not because they deliver worse care but because their patients face compounding barriers that are outside clinical control. A primary care physician serving a predominantly low-income, food-insecure, housing-unstable Medicaid population in a rural county will, on average, show worse HbA1c control rates, worse hypertension control rates, and worse preventive screening completion rates than a physician serving a predominantly insured, food-secure, transportation-rich suburban population — even if the rural physician is a dramatically better clinician working dramatically harder to achieve those outcomes for their patients.
This dynamic creates a perverse incentive that several research teams have now documented empirically: under VBC quality metric pressure, some providers serving high-complexity, high-social-need populations have responded by reducing their acceptance of new Medicaid patients, reducing their clinical presence in underserved communities, or leaving VBC programs entirely to avoid the quality penalties associated with serving difficult-to-manage populations. A 2019 study in Health Affairs by Markovitz and colleagues found that because MSSP does not raise an ACO’s benchmark when its patients’ risk scores rise, ACOs have an incentive to shed increasingly sick patients — and beneficiaries at the 95th percentile of risk had a 21.6% chance of exiting MSSP attribution, against 16.0% for beneficiaries at the median. HHS’s 2016 Report to Congress on social risk factors likewise found that providers serving more dual-eligible beneficiaries fared worse under Medicare’s value-based purchasing programs. This is not a marginal finding. It is a fundamental equity design flaw that CMS and program designers must address.
SDOH Risk Adjustment: The Technical Fix for the Metric Design Problem
The metric design problem — the failure of standard VBC quality metrics to account for the social determinants of health that drive a large share of outcome variation — is the most technically solvable of VBC’s equity challenges, and it is receiving significant attention from CMS, researchers, and program designers. Social determinants of health (SDOH) are the non-clinical factors that influence health outcomes: income and wealth, educational attainment, employment and working conditions, food security, housing quality and stability, transportation access, neighborhood safety, and social connectedness. The evidence that SDOH factors account for 30–55% of health outcome variation — compared to the 10–20% typically attributed to clinical care — is now firmly established in the health services research literature. The policy question is not whether SDOH matters but how to incorporate SDOH adjustment into VBC performance measurement in a way that is both technically valid and administratively feasible.
CMS has moved toward social-risk adjustment in MSSP in steps. The CY2023 Physician Fee Schedule final rule (November 2022) added a health equity adjustment that awards up to 10 bonus points to the quality scores of high-performing ACOs serving large shares of dual-eligible, low-income-subsidy, or high Area Deprivation Index beneficiaries, and created advance investment payments for new low-revenue ACOs in underserved areas. The CY2025 final rule (November 2024) added a Health Equity Benchmark Adjustment that raises the spending benchmark for ACOs based on their proportion of beneficiaries enrolled in the Part D low-income subsidy or dually eligible for Medicaid. These adjustments acknowledge that high-need populations cost more to manage effectively and reduce the financial penalty for serving them. Health equity researchers argue they remain partial, because low-income subsidy and dual-eligible status are imperfect proxies for the full range of social complexity that drives outcome variation.

Social Determinants of Health: How Social Risk Shapes VBC Performance
Community Health Workers: The Human Infrastructure for VBC Equity
Community health workers — frontline public health workers who are typically members of the communities they serve, with lived experience of the social and health challenges those communities face — are emerging as the most powerful tool available for addressing VBC’s equity gap. Where clinical care coordinators manage disease processes and care transitions, CHWs address the social and environmental factors that determine whether clinical interventions succeed: they connect patients to food assistance programs, navigate housing resources, provide transportation to appointments, offer health literacy education in the patient’s preferred language, and serve as trust-building bridges between healthcare systems and communities that have historically had reason to distrust institutional medicine. The evidence on CHW effectiveness is now robust enough to justify significant investment: in a randomized controlled trial of Penn Medicine’s IMPaCT program (Kangovi et al., JAMA Internal Medicine, 2018), patients assigned a community health worker spent fewer total days in the hospital and reported better quality of care than controls, and a follow-up analysis (Health Affairs, 2020) estimated a return of $2.47 to a Medicaid payer for every dollar invested.
Despite this evidence base, CHW integration into VBC programs has been slower than the evidence warrants, primarily because of reimbursement barriers. Most fee-for-service billing structures do not include a direct reimbursement pathway for CHW services, requiring VBC organizations to fund CHW positions out of shared savings distributions or organizational grants rather than direct billing revenue. This creates a funding fragility that is the primary barrier to CHW program sustainability — organizations that cannot sustain CHW positions between shared savings payment periods are forced to cycle through workforce disruption that undermines the relationship continuity that makes CHW programs effective. CMS has taken several steps to address this barrier: the CY2024 Medicare Physician Fee Schedule created the first Community Health Integration codes (G0019, G0022) for CHW services furnished under a billing practitioner's supervision, and Medicaid coverage of CHW services, a state option since a 2013 CMS rule took effect in 2014, now exists in more than half of states (29 of 48 responding states in KFF's 2022 survey). The AHEAD Model has no dedicated health equity or SDOH investment fund; it gives each participating state up to $12 million in cooperative agreement funding, and CMS’s September 2025 redesign replaced the original Statewide Health Equity Plan with Population Health Accountability Plans focused on prevention and chronic disease. These policy changes are meaningful but still incomplete — Medicaid CHW coverage varies widely by state, and AHEAD support is geographically limited to the participating states (currently Maryland, Connecticut, Hawaii, Rhode Island and downstate New York).
Equity Action for VBC Leaders: If your organization does not have an active Community Health Worker program, the CHW ROI evidence and Medicare’s Community Health Integration billing codes make now the right time to build one. Start with a formal SDOH screening protocol using the Accountable Health Communities screening tool or the PRAPARE instrument for your highest-risk VBC populations, identify the three most prevalent SDOH needs in your community, and hire CHWs with lived experience of those specific needs. Do not hire case managers and call them CHWs. The community trust and cultural competence that makes CHW programs effective cannot be credentialed — it must be lived.
Rural and Geographic Equity: The Invisible Disparity
Healthcare equity discussions in the United States have historically focused primarily on racial and ethnic disparities, and for important reasons — the evidence of systemic racial inequity in healthcare access, quality, and outcomes is extensive and deeply troubling. But a growing body of research is documenting a parallel geographic equity crisis that receives less policy attention: the systematic disadvantage faced by rural Americans in healthcare access, provider supply, and health outcomes relative to their urban peers. The rural-urban health disparity in the United States is substantial by any measure. Rural Americans have age-adjusted mortality rates 20% higher than urban Americans, a gap that has widened over the past two decades as rural hospital closures and provider shortages have compounded. They are more likely to die from the five leading causes of preventable death — heart disease, cancer, unintentional injury, chronic lower respiratory disease, and stroke — and more likely to have those deaths classified as premature, occurring before age 80.
Value-based care has not, to date, served rural populations well. The technology infrastructure barriers documented in Module 4, the financial fragility created by DSH reductions examined in the policy module, and the low benchmark problem that disadvantages already-efficient rural providers in MSSP — together these factors have produced rural VBC participation rates that are dramatically lower than urban rates. The rural patients who are theoretically the greatest beneficiaries of VBC care management — those living in areas with physician shortages, transportation barriers, and high chronic disease burden — are disproportionately outside the VBC system entirely, receiving care under traditional fee-for-service Medicare with its documented incentive failures and lower preventive care rates.
Building an Equity-Centered VBC Program: What It Actually Requires
Building a VBC program that genuinely advances health equity rather than simply performing equity in its marketing materials requires design choices that are sometimes in tension with the financial optimization logic that drives most VBC strategy discussions. Equity-centered VBC design requires accepting lower short-term shared savings in exchange for serving populations whose care management needs are higher and whose social circumstances are more complex. It requires investing in CHW infrastructure before reimbursement for it is guaranteed rather than waiting for dedicated funding. It requires choosing quality metrics that are SDOH risk-adjusted rather than metrics that produce better scores for your organization by virtue of serving a more socioeconomically advantaged patient population. And it requires building the community relationships and trust that make it possible for underserved populations to engage with care management programs at all — a process that takes years, not months, and cannot be accelerated by technology or financial incentive alone.
Adopt the CMS Accountable Health Communities screening tool or PRAPARE instrument as a standard clinical workflow for all attributed VBC patients, not just those identified as high-risk by your risk stratification model. SDOH data that is not systematically collected cannot be incorporated into risk adjustment, care planning, or community investment decisions.
Disaggregate all quality metric performance data by race, ethnicity, income level, and geography before reporting aggregate performance to leadership or payers. Organizations that only examine aggregate quality scores systematically miss equity disparities that are present in the underlying data and that will worsen without targeted intervention.
Advocate explicitly for SDOH risk adjustment in every VBC contract negotiation with payers and in every public comment submission on CMS rulemaking. The policy change required to fix the metric design problem will not happen without sustained, evidence-based advocacy from providers who are experiencing its consequences directly.
Audit your attributed panel acceptance practices to determine whether your organization is, consciously or unconsciously, steering away from the most socially complex patients in response to quality metric pressure. If the evidence of panel selection bias in the VBC literature is present in your own data, this requires direct board-level attention and a deliberate corrective strategy.
Partner with community organizations — food banks, housing nonprofits, legal aid organizations, faith communities — as formal members of your care management team rather than as external referral destinations. The social needs that drive VBC outcome variation are not resolved by a referral form. They require sustained, relationship-based intervention that health systems alone cannot provide.
Course Conclusion: What Value-Based Care Can and Cannot Do
Across five modules, this course has examined value-based care through every lens that HTR’s analytical framework provides: the economic incentive logic that makes it necessary, the policy architecture that makes it legal and operational, the technology infrastructure that makes it analytically feasible, the clinical evidence that determines whether it actually works, and the equity design requirements that determine whether it works for everyone. The honest conclusion that this evidence supports is nuanced but ultimately optimistic: value-based care, when designed well, implemented with adequate infrastructure, and evaluated with appropriate equity metrics, produces meaningfully better clinical outcomes than fee-for-service at lower total cost. The international comparisons, the peer-reviewed MSSP evaluations, the BPCI Advanced bundled payment evidence, and the mature ACO performance literature all point in the same direction. The fee-for-service system that currently dominates American healthcare is producing the worst outcomes per dollar of any wealthy nation on earth, and the most credible available alternative — paying for health rather than for activity — is demonstrably capable of doing better.
What value-based care cannot do, on its own, is eliminate the social determinants of health that account for 30–55% of health outcome variation. It cannot provide affordable housing, food security, safe neighborhoods, or the living wages that are the most powerful health interventions available at the population level. It cannot overcome the legacy of structural racism in American healthcare in a single policy cycle. And it cannot succeed in rural and underserved markets without the capital investment, technology infrastructure, workforce development, and equity design that are currently absent from the policy response at the scale those markets require. These are not arguments against value-based care. They are arguments for building it with sufficient ambition, equity intentionality, and long-term commitment to match the scale of the problem it is trying to solve. The U.S. healthcare system can do better. Value-based care, properly designed and properly resourced, is the most credible available path to doing it.
Review the SDOH screening data your organization currently collects, or does not collect, for your attributed VBC populations. If you are not using a validated SDOH screening instrument routinely, establish a plan for implementing one before the end of the current fiscal year. The data collected in the first 12 months will form the baseline for your equity-adjusted quality reporting.
Examine your organization’s VBC quality metric performance disaggregated by race, ethnicity, and income level. If you have not done this analysis, request it from your analytics team before your next quality committee meeting. You cannot address equity disparities you have not measured.
Identify the three community organizations in your market best positioned to address the most prevalent SDOH needs in your attributed patient population, and schedule introductory conversations about formal care management partnership before the end of this quarter. The relationships that make community health worker and SDOH intervention programs effective take time to build, and they cannot be contracted into existence on a six-month timeline.
Review your organization’s VBC contract language with payers for the presence of SDOH risk adjustment provisions. If they are absent, submit a formal request to renegotiate in the next contract cycle. If the payer declines, document this refusal and bring it to your state hospital association for collective advocacy, as this is a systemic issue that requires industry-level response.
Course Completion Reflection: You have now completed the HTR Academy Value-Based Care Fundamentals course. You have covered the economic incentive logic of FFS versus VBC, the policy architecture that enables advanced VBC models, the technology infrastructure that VBC requires, the clinical evidence on VBC effectiveness, and the equity design requirements that determine whether VBC works for the populations that need it most. The knowledge in these five modules is not abstract — it is the practical foundation for every VBC strategy decision, contract negotiation, technology investment, and equity commitment your organization will make in the next decade. Use it. Share it with your colleagues. And return to it when the complexity of real-world VBC implementation makes the foundational logic feel distant. The complexity is real. So is the opportunity.

Module 5, the final module of the HTR Academy Value-Based Care Fundamentals course, examines the two most important questions about value-based care in practice: does it produce better clinical outcomes than fee-for-service, and does it produce those outcomes equitably across racial, ethnic, geographic, and socioeconomic lines? Drawing on the best available clinical evidence from MSSP, BPCI Advanced, and Medicare Advantage programs, and on the growing equity research literature that has examined VBC’s distributional effects, this module provides an honest assessment of where VBC has delivered on its clinical promise, where it has fallen short, and what the specific design features — SDOH risk adjustment, community health worker integration, health equity plan requirements — that are necessary to make VBC work fairly for the populations that need it most.

######## vermont-health-economics-roadmap-2026 | The Economic Architecture of Vermont’s 2026 Health Transformation: From Silos to Liquidity Fabrics
Vermont's 2026 health transformation is usually discussed as a financing problem — global budgets, cross-subsidies, the Act 167 cliff. But the financial reforms cannot work without a parallel transformation of the state's health-data architecture. Capping a hospital's revenue and asking it to manage a population's total cost of care is impossible if the patient data needed to manage that population is trapped in vendor-locked silos. The economic architecture and the data architecture are two sides of the same reform, and Vermont's roadmap has to engineer both.
The Strategic Imperative: Data Liquidity
A central goal of Vermont's 2026 health-data strategy is to sharply increase real-time clinical data liquidity — the speed and completeness with which a patient's record can move to wherever care is delivered — and to reduce the 'friction tax' of manual records retrieval across the state's rural health districts. In the legacy 'silo' model, data sits in proprietary, vendor-locked repositories, and moving it requires faxes, phone calls, and manual reconciliation. Every such handoff is administrative cost with no clinical value, and in a global-budget world that cost comes straight off the hospital's fixed envelope.
This reframes interoperability from an IT nicety into an economic necessity. Under fee-for-service, a hospital had little financial reason to invest in sharing data that might send a patient elsewhere. Under a fixed budget tied to population health, the same hospital has a direct financial stake in knowing — instantly — what is happening to its attributed patients across the entire care continuum, because missed information becomes avoidable admissions, duplicated tests, and preventable cost. Data liquidity is how a budgeted system sees the population it is now responsible for.
Why the National Standards Backdrop Matters
Vermont is not building this in a vacuum. Federal interoperability policy — the ONC/ASTP standards (USCDI, the FHIR-based TEFCA framework) and CMS's Interoperability and Patient Access rule — is steadily forcing health data toward standardized, API-accessible exchange. Vermont's roadmap aligns the state's health information exchange to these national rails rather than inventing a parallel standard, which lowers integration cost and lets the state inherit a national network effect instead of funding its own.
The economic logic is the same one that governs any infrastructure: standardization converts a tangle of expensive point-to-point connections into a shared utility. Just as a common rail gauge made a national rail network cheaper than a patchwork of incompatible lines, a common data standard makes statewide health-data exchange cheaper than the silo-to-silo integrations Vermont pays for today. The 'friction tax' the roadmap targets is, in large part, the cost of having no common gauge.
In a global-budget system, data liquidity is not an IT line item — it is a determinant of solvency. A budgeted hospital is financially responsible for a population's total cost of care; if it cannot see that population's data in real time, it cannot manage the cost, and the avoidable admissions and duplicated tests come straight out of a fixed revenue envelope.
The Build-vs-Inherit Decision
A core economic choice in the roadmap is whether to build bespoke state infrastructure or inherit shared, standards-based services. The strategic-pivot work around the Vermont Health Information Exchange points toward inheriting and integrating wherever possible — using CRISP-style shared services and national networks rather than maintaining a fully custom stack. For a small state, the build option is rarely justified: the fixed costs of building and maintaining health-data infrastructure are enormous, and Vermont's population is too small to amortize them efficiently.
This is straightforward economies-of-scale reasoning. Health-data infrastructure has high fixed costs and low marginal costs per additional patient, so cost per patient falls sharply with scale. Vermont, with one of the smallest state populations in the country, sits on the wrong end of that curve when it builds alone and on the right end when it joins a larger shared utility. The roadmap's instinct to inherit national rails and regional shared services is the correct response to the state's scale disadvantage.
The Administrative-Cost Prize
The economic stakes of the data architecture are large because administrative cost is one of the biggest and most addressable sources of waste in U.S. health care. National health expenditure reached $5.3 trillion in 2024 (18.0 percent of GDP), and a substantial share of that spending — by most estimates a meaningful fraction of total cost — goes to administration, billing, and the manual reconciliation work that fragmented data makes necessary. Unlike clinical care, administrative friction can be reduced without taking anything away from patients, which makes it the most politically and ethically attractive target in a cost-constrained system.
For Vermont, this is where the data roadmap and the financial cliff intersect most directly. The state cannot raise revenue, so it must lower cost, and administrative friction is the category of cost that can be cut without rationing care. A standards-based liquidity fabric that automates data exchange attacks exactly this category — replacing faxes, phone calls, and manual records retrieval with automatic, machine-readable exchange. The savings are bounded by how much administrative work the architecture can eliminate, but in a system where every other lever is exhausted, even bounded, non-rationing savings are disproportionately valuable, because they are among the only savings available that do not come at a patient's expense.
From Silos to a Liquidity Fabric
The roadmap's organizing metaphor — moving from 'silos' to a 'liquidity fabric' — is more than branding. A silo architecture imposes a cost on every transaction: each time data must cross an organizational boundary, someone pays to move it manually. A liquidity fabric drives that per-transaction cost toward zero by making exchange automatic and standardized, which is exactly the kind of administrative cost reduction a cost-constrained system needs. In a system where every payer is at its limit, eliminating avoidable administrative friction is one of the few sources of genuine, non-rationing savings available.
Crucially, this is cost-saving, not cost-shifting. Reducing the friction tax does not move a bill from one payer to another; it eliminates work that produced no health. That distinction matters in the Act 167 context, where the report explicitly warns that shifting costs (via taxes or premiums) is not a solution. Data-architecture reform is one of the clearest available examples of genuine cost-saving — taking real resources out of the system without taking care away from patients.
Data as the Precondition for Prevention
The financial case for prevention — heading off expensive acute episodes by managing chronic disease upstream — depends entirely on data the silo model cannot deliver. To prevent a diabetic patient's avoidable hospitalization, a care team must know, continuously, that the patient's labs are drifting, that a prescription went unfilled, that an emergency-department visit happened across town. In a fragmented data environment, those signals arrive late or not at all, and prevention degrades into reaction. A liquidity fabric is what turns a population's scattered data exhaust into early-warning signals a care team can act on.
This links the data roadmap directly to the economics of an aging state. Vermont's demographics guarantee a heavy and growing chronic-disease burden, and chronic disease is the dominant national cost driver, projected to cost the United States as much as $47 trillion over 2024–2039. The only durable way to manage that burden within a fixed budget is to manage it upstream, and upstream management is a data problem before it is a clinical one. Investing in data liquidity is therefore not a cost center competing with care; it is the enabling infrastructure for the prevention strategy that the state's finances require — without it, the global-budget model has no operational means of bending the chronic-disease cost curve it is built to bend.
Sequencing and the Risk of Over-Engineering
The honest risk in any 'data sovereignty' roadmap is over-engineering — spending scarce capital building elegant infrastructure faster than the clinical and financial system can use it. The disciplined sequence is to tie each data-architecture investment to a concrete financial use case under the new payment models: first the data needed to operate global budgets and identify high-cost patients, then progressively richer capabilities as the payment reforms mature. Infrastructure built ahead of a use case is stranded capital, which a budget-constrained state cannot afford.
Read this way, Vermont's 2026 roadmap is best understood as the data half of a single reform whose financial half is Act 167. The financial reforms set fixed budgets and population accountability; the data reforms supply the liquidity that makes managing within those budgets possible. Neither works alone. The economic architecture of Vermont's transformation is the integration of the two — payment models that demand population management, and a data fabric that makes population management achievable at a cost a small, aging, rural state can actually sustain.

CMS — Interoperability and Patient Access rule
Green Mountain Care Board — Act 167 Hospital Transformation (global budgets context)
CMS — AHEAD Model (state cost-growth targets + hospital global budgets)
Health Affairs — National Health Care Spending Increased 7.2% in 2024 (cost backdrop)
